1. Prehistoric India

Introduction: Geological and Climatic Context of the Indian Subcontinent

The Indian subcontinent possesses one of the deepest and richest prehistoric records in the Old World, spanning nearly two million years. The study of India's Stone Age is crucial for UPSC because it establishes the foundational human occupation of the subcontinent and reveals early technological, cultural, and cognitive developments. The prehistoric period is broadly divided into the Palaeolithic (Old Stone Age), Mesolithic (Middle Stone Age), and Neolithic (New Stone Age), followed by the Chalcolithic (Copper-Stone Age) cultures that bridged prehistory and the Indus Valley Civilization.

The geological backdrop of Indian prehistory is the Pleistocene epoch (c. 2.6 mya to 11,700 BP), characterised by a series of glacial-interglacial cycles that dramatically altered sea levels, monsoon patterns, and vegetation. During glacial maxima, sea levels dropped by as much as 120–130 metres, exposing the Sunda Shelf and creating land bridges between India and Sri Lanka, while the Persian Gulf was dry land. The Indian summer monsoon was the primary driver of habitability — strengthened during interglacials (warm periods) when the contrast between the heating of the Tibetan Plateau and the Indian Ocean was greatest, and weakened during glacials when the plateau was cooler. Palaeoclimatic records from Arabian Sea sediment cores (upwelling indices), speleothems from the Meghalaya caves, and the loess-palaeosol sequences of the Potwar Plateau show that periods of monsoon intensification (e.g., Marine Isotope Stage 5, c. 130–70 kya) corresponded with dense forest cover, expanded freshwater lakes, and more favourable conditions for hominin occupation. Conversely, the Last Glacial Maximum (LGM) (c. 24–18 kya) brought aridity, reduced tree cover, and the expansion of the Thar Desert, forcing human populations into refugia along the coastlines and the wetter foothills of the Himalayas. These cyclical environmental shifts governed the distribution of animal herds, the availability of raw materials for stone tools, and the migration routes of both animals and humans across the subcontinent.

The pioneering work of Robert Bruce Foote (who discovered the first Palaeolithic tool at Pallavaram in 1863), H.D. Sankalia (the doyen of Indian prehistory who excavated Nevasa, the Belan Valley, and established the first chrono-stratigraphic framework), and V.S. Wakankar (who discovered Bhimbetka in 1957) laid the foundation for Indian prehistoric studies. In recent decades, research by Shanti Pappu (Attirampakkam), K. Paddayya (Hunsgi-Baichbal), and Ravi Korisettar (Southern Neolithic) has transformed our understanding, applying modern geoarchaeology, luminescence dating, and microscopic wear analysis to problems once considered intractable. The prehistoric period is now understood as a dynamic, regionally diverse set of processes rather than a simple unilinear progression from simple to complex.

Palaeolithic Period (c. 2 mya – 10,000 BCE)

The Palaeolithic is subdivided into Lower, Middle, and Upper phases based on tool technology, climate, and hominin species. India preserves one of the most complete Palaeolithic sequences in the Old World, second only to Africa in its antiquity and diversity.

Lower Palaeolithic (c. 2 mya – 100,000 BCE)

The Lower Palaeolithic is characterised by two major technological traditions: the Acheulian (bifacial handaxes, cleavers, and large flakes made on quartzite, basalt, and dolerite) and the Soanian (a chopper-chopping tool tradition based on pebble cores). This dichotomy is one of the most debated issues in Indian prehistory. The Acheulian is found predominantly in the Peninsular India (south of the Vindhyas), where massive exposures of dolerite, basalt, and quartzite provided ideal raw material for large bifaces. The Soanian occurs in the Siwalik Hills and the Indo-Gangetic foothills, where river cobbles of quartzite were the only available raw material. Some scholars (H. de Terra, T.T. Paterson) saw the Soanian as a distinct cultural tradition brought by a separate wave of hominins; others (G. Corvinus, R.V. Joshi) argued it represents an adaptation to raw material constraints — that small, rounded cobbles simply cannot produce elegant handaxes, and the Soanian is functionally equivalent to the Acheulian rather than culturally different. The debate remains unresolved, though recent luminescence dating suggests the Soanian may be significantly younger (Middle Pleistocene, c. 400–100 kya) than the Acheulian, weakening the two-tradition hypothesis.

Attirampakkam (Tamil Nadu) — This open-air site on the Kortallaiyar River has produced the earliest evidence of hominin occupation in India. Excavated by Shanti Pappu and her team (Sharma Centre for Heritage Education), stone tools were found in a stratigraphic context dated by cosmogenic nuclide (26Al/10Be) burial dating to 1.5 million years ago (published in Nature, 2011). This date was a paradigm shift: previously, the earliest accepted date for Indian Acheulian was c. 500–600,000 years (based on the Bori volcanic ash sequence in Maharashtra, dated to 670,000 years by argon-argon methods). The Attirampakkam date pushed Indian prehistory back by nearly a million years, into the same timeframe as the earliest Acheulian sites in Africa (e.g., Olduvai Gorge, c. 1.76 mya; Kokiselei, West Turkana, c. 1.76 mya). The Attirampakkam tools are Mode 1 (Oldowan-like) industry — simple flakes, cores, and choppers predating the classic Acheulian handaxes found in the upper levels. This suggests that early hominins (likely Homo erectus) dispersed from Africa into India during the Early Pleistocene, before the development of the full Acheulian tradition. The site challenges the simple Out-of-Africa model by implying multiple hominin dispersals into Asia and a long, in situ technological evolution from Oldowan to Acheulian within the subcontinent. A 2017 study (Parks et al.) using palaeomagnetism at Attirampakkam has confirmed an age bracket of 1.5–1.0 mya for the lowest artefacts, with Acheulian tools appearing in levels dated to c. 1.07 mya.

Isampur (Karnataka) — This site, excavated by K. Paddayya, is an Acheulian quarry site where hominins extracted limestone slabs from an exposed outcrop, roughly shaped them into handaxe blanks on the spot, and carried the blanks elsewhere for final finishing. The site covers about 2.5 hectares and preserves thousands of finished and unfinished handaxes, cleavers, large flakes, and waste flakes in primary context. The most remarkable find is the remains of an extinct elephant (Elephas hysudrindicus) in association with stone tools — the butchered bones show cut marks from stone knives, providing direct evidence of large-game hunting or scavenging by Acheulian hominins in India, c. 1.2–0.8 mya. The Isampur quarry is among the earliest known specialised stone-tool production sites in the world.

Hunsgi-Baichbal Valleys (Karnataka) — This region, systematically explored by K. Paddayya from the 1970s onward, contains the densest concentration of Acheulian sites in India (over 200 sites within a 500 sq km area). The most significant discovery is the living floor at Hunsgi — a discrete layer (about 30 cm thick) preserving stone tools, debitage (waste flakes), animal bones (deer, cattle, wild pig, equid), and manuports (unmodified stones brought to the site) in their original positions, exactly as they were left by hominins c. 300,000–200,000 years ago. Three living floors were excavated, each containing 1,500–2,500 artefacts in areas of 30–50 sq m. These floors represent short-term camp sites — the arrangement of tools and bones suggests designated activity areas within the camp: a knapping area (where tools were made), a butchery area (with cut-marked bones), and a domestic area (with a cleared space for sitting). The floors have been interpreted as evidence of spatial organisation and planning depth among Acheulian hominins, challenging the stereotype of 'primitive' early humans as aimless wanderers. The site also demonstrates advanced knowledge of raw material sources — the majority of tools are made of limestone (derived from local outcrops within 1–3 km), but some tools are of basalt and dolerite brought from 12–15 km away, indicating territorial ranges of at least 15–30 km diameter. A circular stone structure — a ring of roughly dressed limestone blocks about 2.5 m in diameter — found at Hunsgi has been interpreted as the base of a hut or windbreak, possibly the earliest evidence of artificial shelter in India.

The Narmada Man — Discovered in 1982 by Arun Sonakia (Geological Survey of India) in the Narmada River alluvium near Hathnora (Madhya Pradesh), this is the only hominin fossil discovered from the Indian subcontinent in situ in Pleistocene deposits. The fossil is a partial calvarium (skull cap) — the cranial vault, missing the face and base. It was originally assigned to Homo erectus, but later studies suggest it may represent Homo heidelbergensis (the common ancestor of Neanderthals and modern humans in Africa and Europe) or an archaic Homo sapiens. The cranial capacity is estimated at 1,200–1,400 cc (within the range of both H. erectus and H. heidelbergensis), and the morphology shows a mix of primitive and derived features — a thick vault (primitive), but a raised parietal (more derived). The date is also disputed: the original association with a Middle Pleistocene fauna (including Bos namadicus and Elephas namadicus) suggested a date of c. 500,000 years BP, but recent ESR (electron spin resonance) dating of associated mammalian teeth has given an age of c. 236–288,000 years BP, placing it in the late Middle Pleistocene. A 2012 reanalysis (Sankhyan and Sahni) argued that the Narmada Man skullcap is actually part of a more robust Homo erectus population that persisted in India longer than in Africa or Europe. The fossil's taxonomic position remains unresolved, but it is critical evidence that India was inhabited by advanced hominins well before the arrival of Homo sapiens.

Singi Talav (Rajasthan) — This site in the Didwana dry lake basin has yielded evidence of fire in the form of burnt bones and charcoal fragments found in association with Acheulian tools in a stratigraphic context dated to c. 300,000 years BP. The burnt bones show clear signs of heating at controlled temperatures (200–400°C), ruling out natural bush fires. This represents one of the earliest instances of controlled fire use by hominins in Asia, comparable to the evidence from Zhoukoudian in China and Gesher Benot Ya'aqov in Israel. The control of fire was a transformative technology — it provided warmth during glacial periods, protection from predators, light for extended activity into the night, and, crucially, cooking, which increased the digestibility and nutritional value of food and may have been a key factor in the evolution of the larger brain characteristic of Homo.

Bhimbetka (Madhya Pradesh) — Discovered by V.S. Wakankar in 1957, Bhimbetka is a UNESCO World Heritage Site (since 2003) with over 700 rock shelters, of which about 400 contain paintings and 100 contain archaeological deposits. The site shows continuous human occupation from the Lower Palaeolithic through the Medieval period — an unbroken sequence unparalleled anywhere in the world. The lowermost deposits contain Acheulian handaxes and cleavers made on dolerite and quartzite, found in association with ancient streambed gravels. The wealth of the site lies in its stratigraphy — successive layers of occupation debris, each with distinct tool industries, provide a reference sequence for the entire Indian Stone Age. Bhimbetka is not merely a site but a region — the surrounding Ratapani Wildlife Sanctuary has dozens of other rock-shelter complexes with similar sequences, indicating a densely populated prehistoric landscape. Other Lower Palaeolithic sites of significance include Didwana (Rajasthan), a dry lake basin with Acheulian tools in situ; the Soan Valley (Pakistan), the type site of the Soanian tradition discovered by de Terra and Paterson (1939); the Belan Valley (Uttar Pradesh), where G.R. Sharma established the first stratigraphic sequence of the Indian Palaeolithic; and Bori (Maharashtra), where Acheulian tools were found sandwiched between two volcanic ash layers (basaltic tuff from the Toba eruption), providing a rare absolute date of c. 670,000 years BP.

Middle Palaeolithic (c. 100,000 – 40,000 BCE)

The Middle Palaeolithic marks a fundamental shift in stone tool technology: from large, heavy, hand-held bifaces to smaller, lighter, flake-based tools that were hafted onto wooden or bone handles. This period in India corresponds broadly to the arrival and spread of Homo sapiens (though the actual arrival date is hotly debated) and is a period of significant cognitive and behavioural change.

The characteristic technology is the Levallois technique — a sophisticated, multi-stage method of core preparation in which the core was carefully shaped on both faces to produce a predetermined flake of a specific size and shape with a single blow. The Levallois core is often described as a 'tortoise core' because of its dome-like shape. This technique implies advanced cognitive abilities: the knapper had to visualise the final product hidden within the core and execute a sequence of up to 20–30 preparatory flake removals before striking the final blow. The presence of the Levallois technique in India (attested at Nevasa, Bhimbetka, and Samnapur) demonstrates that the hominins of the subcontinent were cognitively comparable to contemporary Neanderthals and Homo sapiens elsewhere in the Old World. Tools of this period include scrapers (side, end, and concave), points (some with tangs for hafting), borers, and the distinctive handaxe-flake (a small, finely retouched handaxe that is often symmetrical and less than 10 cm long — sometimes called a "mini-handaxe").

The Nevasian industry — first identified by H.D. Sankalia at Nevasa (Maharashtra) on the Pravara River — is now recognised as a distinct Middle Palaeolithic tradition unique to the Indian subcontinent. The Nevasian is characterised by denticulates and notches — tools with serrated edges for cutting plant material — along with scrapers, points, and a minority of small bifaces. Unlike the contemporary Middle Palaeolithic of Europe (Mousterian, associated with Neanderthals) or Africa (Middle Stone Age), the Nevasian shows continuity from the preceding Acheulian — handaxes continue (albeit smaller and more refined), and many sites show Acheulian tools overlain by Middle Palaeolithic assemblages without any stratigraphic break or evidence of population replacement. This has been interpreted (by R. Korisettar and K. Paddayya) as evidence of in situ technological evolution — that Indian hominins gradually refined their tool-making skills over hundreds of millennia without being replaced by incoming migrants, which would be consistent with either local evolution or very early arrival of Homo sapiens.

Key Middle Palaeolithic sites include Nevasa (Maharashtra, type site of the Nevasian), Bhimbetka (upper shelters with rich Middle Palaeolithic assemblages), Samnapur (Madhya Pradesh, where an elephant skeleton was found butchered with Levallois flakes), the Luni Valley (Rajasthan, a dense concentration of open-air sites in the Thar Desert, studied by V.N. Misra and S.N. Rajaguru), the Belan Valley (Uttar Pradesh, where GR Sharma excavated a stratified sequence from Middle Palaeolithic to Neolithic), Yedurwadi (Karnataka), and the Jurreru Valley (Andhra Pradesh, near the Kurnool Caves). The site of Bhimbetka contains one of the most remarkable features of the Indian Middle Palaeolithic: the Auditorium Cave petroglyphs. These are cupules (cup-shaped depressions) and a spiral or meandering line ground into the dolerite bedrock, covered by Middle Palaeolithic sediment dated to c. 100,000 years BP or older. If this dating holds, these are the oldest known petroglyphs in the world — predating the El Castillo cave paintings (Spain, c. 40,000 BP) and the Blombos Cave engravings (South Africa, c. 77,000 BP) by tens of thousands of years. The cupules were made by pecking and grinding the rock surface, a technique that required considerable time and effort, indicating symbolic or ritual behaviour of a high order.

Upper Palaeolithic (c. 40,000 – 10,000 BCE)

The Upper Palaeolithic in India is associated with fully modern Homo sapiens and is characterised by the production of long, parallel-sided blade tools (blanks twice as long as they are wide, with parallel lateral edges), burins (chisel-like engraving tools used to work bone, antler, and ivory), and formal bone tools (points, awls, needles, harpoons). This period marks a revolutionary increase in technological efficiency: from a single prepared nodule of chert or chalcedony, a skilled Upper Palaeolithic knapper could produce 10–15 metres of cutting edge (compared to about 20–30 cm from a handaxe). The period also witnesses the first unambiguous evidence of symbolic behaviour — art, ornament, personal adornment, and complex burial practices.

Bhimbetka Rock Art — The rock shelters of Bhimbetka contain one of the largest and longest-running traditions of rock art in the world, with paintings spanning from the Upper Palaeolithic (c. 30,000 BP) through the Mesolithic, Chalcolithic, and into the Historic period (as late as the 7th–10th centuries CE). V.S. Wakankar identified several distinct stylistic periods:

  • Period I (Upper Palaeolithic) — Large, stylised, outlined figures of animals (bison, rhinoceros, elephant, tiger, wild buffalo) filled with geometric patterns (zig-zags, chevrons, cross-hatching). The pigments used were primarily manganese dioxide (producing dark purple-black) and limonite/hematite (giving deep red and yellow ochre). These paints were applied as thin washes, suggesting the use of a brush of chewed twigs or animal hair. The human figures are rare and schematic — stick-like, with triangular torsos. The most famous panel is the 'Bull Fight' scene — a large bison with a human figure in the background.
  • Period II (Mesolithic) — Smaller, more detailed, and more naturalistic figures than Period I. Animals are shown in profile with perspective (hind legs further apart than front legs). Human figures are more common, shown in dynamic action scenes — hunting with bows and arrows, running, dancing, and most famously, collecting honey from a bee hive (the earliest representation of honey collection in the world, at Shelter 2). The honey-collection scene shows a person on a ladder surrounded by bees, reaching for a honeycomb — a scene still depicted in contemporary Gond and Bhil folk art, suggesting a 10,000-year artistic tradition.
  • Period III (Chalcolithic/Early Historic) — Scenes of settled life: horse and elephant riders, chariots, warriors with shields and swords, musical instruments (drums, pipes), and religious or ceremonial processions. The colours shift to white (from kaolin or limestone) and green (from a copper compound), alongside the earlier reds and blacks.

The themes of Bhimbetka art are surprisingly consistent across millennia — hunting, dancing, music, ritual, and the natural world. The shelters that contain the best paintings (like Auditorium Cave, Shelter 5, and the 'Zoo Rock') are those with natural acoustic properties that amplify sound, suggesting that painting and performance were linked — these were spaces for communal rituals where the images on the wall served as a backdrop to singing, dancing, and storytelling.

Patne (Maharashtra) — Excavated by S.A. Sali, this Upper Palaeolithic site has yielded the most spectacular evidence of portable art from the Indian Stone Age: a collection of ostrich eggshell beads and a fragment of an ostrich eggshell engraved with a cross-hatched geometric pattern. The beads (over 100 were found) were made by chipping the eggshell into rough circles, drilling a central hole (probably with a stone drill rotated between palms), and stringing the beads. The engraved piece shows a deliberate, carefully executed pattern of overlapping triangles forming a lattice — sometimes described as "the earliest evidence of graphic art in India". The ostrich eggshells come from the extinct Indian ostrich (Struthio camelus indicus), which lived in the dry grasslands of Peninsular India during the Pleistocene and went extinct around 20,000 years ago (the end of the LGM). Ostrich eggshell beads of similar style have been found at Upper Palaeolithic sites in Africa, demonstrating that this was a widespread human symbolic practice. The presence of these beads at Patne, far from any known habitat of the bird, indicates long-distance exchange networks covering at least 300–500 km — the raw eggshells must have been traded or carried across the subcontinent. The Patne ostrich eggshell has been dated by radiocarbon to c. 25,000 BCE, making it one of the oldest securely dated examples of portable art in South Asia.

The Kurnool Caves (Andhra Pradesh) — A complex of limestone caves in the Kurnool district, including the Muchchatla Chintamanu Gavi, Belum Caves, and Sanyasula Gavi. Excavations by K.N. Prasad (1982) and later by M.L.K. Murty and R. Korisettar have yielded Upper Palaeolithic stone tools (blades, burins, scrapers) in association with charcoal and the bones of extinct Pleistocene fauna (including the Indian aurochs Bos primigenius namadicus, a large bovid, and the six-horned deer Hyaena carnivora). Radiocarbon dates from the charcoal suggest human occupation from c. 34,000 to 14,000 years BP. The Kurnool Caves are significant for several reasons: they contain the earliest well-dated evidence of Homo sapiens in Peninsular India (at least c. 34,000 BP); they show that early modern humans exploited both the plains (hunting large game) and the caves (as seasonal shelter); and the well-preserved charcoal and bone provide valuable palaeoenvironmental data. The Kurnool fauna includes the first evidence of crab-eating by early humans in India (crabshells found in association with hearths), suggesting a broader subsistence base than just large-game hunting.

The Toba Super-eruption (74,000 Years Ago) — The eruption of Mount Toba in Sumatra (c. 74,000 BP) was the largest volcanic event of the Quaternary period, about 1,000 times more powerful than the 1980 Mount St. Helens eruption. It ejected 2,800 cubic km of volcanic material, created a 100-km-wide caldera, and deposited a distinctive layer of volcanic ash (tephra) across the Indian subcontinent up to 6 metres thick in some areas. This ash layer, known as the Youngest Toba Tuff (YTT), is a critical marker horizon for dating Pleistocene sites across India — tools found beneath the tephra must be older than 74,000 years, and those above it, younger. The YTT has been identified at more than 100 sites across peninsular India, from the Son Valley (Madhya Pradesh) to the Jurreru Valley (Andhra Pradesh). The impact of the Toba eruption on human populations is debated. The volcanic winter hypothesis (Stanley Ambrose, 1998) argued that the eruption caused a 6–10-year volcanic winter, a 1,000-year cooling event (the 'Toba winter'), and a severe bottleneck that reduced the world human population to 5,000–10,000 individuals. However, Indian evidence challenges this catastrophic view. Excavations at Jwalapuram (Jurreru Valley, Andhra Pradesh) by Michael Petraglia revealed Middle Palaeolithic tools both below and above the YTT ash layer, with no change in technology or any evidence of population decline — the same hominins continued making the same tools after the eruption as before. This suggests that the Toba eruption, while devastating regionally (especially in Sumatra and Malaysia), did not cause a continent-scale human population crash in India. The Jwalapuram data has been used to argue that Homo sapiens had (or at least, some hominin population had) already expanded into India before the Toba eruption and that it survived the volcanic winter. The question of whether these pre-Toba Indian populations were Homo sapiens or archaic hominins (like the Narmada Man's population) remains one of the most exciting unresolved problems in Indian prehistory.

Mesolithic Period (c. 10,000 – 6,000 BCE)

The Mesolithic marks the transition from hunting-gathering to food production, set against the backdrop of profound environmental change: the end of the Pleistocene Ice Age and the onset of the Holocene warm period (c. 11,700 BP to present). The warming climate caused the retreat of the Himalayan glaciers, the filling of the Gangetic floodplains with alluvium, the expansion of tropical forests and grasslands, and the rise in sea levels that flooded the continental shelves, creating the present-day coastline. These changes opened up new ecological niches and forced human populations to adapt to new resources — the megafauna (woolly mammoth, woolly rhinoceros, giant bison) that had been the mainstay of Upper Palaeolithic hunters went extinct (in India, the Indian ostrich was one of the casualties), and humans shifted to a broader spectrum of smaller animals, fish, and plant foods.

The hallmark technology of the Mesolithic is the microlith — tiny, often geometric stone tools (lunates/crescents, triangles, trapezes, backed blades, points) that were produced by a technique known as the microblade punch technique: a punch was placed on the edge of a prepared core, and a small blade was detached by a sharp blow. These microliths (typically 1–4 cm long) were then hafted into wooden or bone handles or shafts using resin (often from the Shorea robusta sal tree) or beeswax, creating composite tools — arrowheads, spear barbs, and knives that were more effective, lighter, and easier to repair than the earlier bifaces. The development of the bow and arrow was arguably the most important technological innovation of the Mesolithic — it allowed hunting from a distance, greatly increasing success rates and safety. The composite tool principle — attaching multiple small, identical, replaceable parts to a wooden handle — was the same principle that would later be applied to the composite bow, the harpoon, and eventually to the modern machine tool.

Key Mesolithic sites include:

  • Bagor (Rajasthan) — Excavated by V.N. Misra, the largest Mesolithic site in India (about 20 acres), with five cultural phases spanning from c. 5500 BCE to the Medieval period. The site is situated on a small hillock surrounded by the floodplain of the Kothari River. Bagor yielded the earliest evidence of animal domestication in India (sheep and goat, c. 4500 BCE), along with abundant microliths (over 200,000 stone artefacts recovered), pottery (starting in Phase II, c. 3500 BCE), copper objects (Phase III, c. 2000 BCE, showing contact with Chalcolithic cultures), and structural remains (stone circles forming hut bases). The site also provided evidence of an elaborate burial complex with over 100 human burials, some with grave goods including shell bangles, carnelian beads, and copper ornaments.
  • Langhnaj (Gujarat) — In the Sabarmati River valley, excavated by H.D. Sankalia and D.H. Gordon. Notable for its burial practices: flexed burials with the head pointing north and the body lying on its side, accompanied by grave goods including shell bangles, beads of carnelian and agate, bone ornaments, and microliths. The faunal remains include wild cattle (Bos primigenius), deer (Cervus), pig (Sus), and an extinct species of equid (Equus hemionus khur, the Indian wild ass, but a larger extinct form). Radiocarbon dates place Langhnaj at c. 3500–2000 BCE, making it relatively late within the Mesolithic sequence.
  • Adamgarh (Madhya Pradesh) — This site is famous for the discovery of a dog burial — a complete dog skeleton (Canis familiaris) buried in a shallow pit, datable to c. 4000 BCE. This is the earliest evidence of dog domestication in India. The dog was a pariah-type animal, similar to the modern Indian village dog, and was buried with care, suggesting a close human-animal bond. The site also has rich rock-shelter paintings of the Mesolithic tradition.
  • Sarai Nahar Rai (Uttar Pradesh) — On the banks of the Ganga-Ghaghara interfluve, this site has yielded the earliest evidence of inter-personal violence (massacre) in Indian prehistory. Excavations by G.R. Sharma uncovered a burial pit containing 10 human skeletons (7 adults, 3 children), all showing signs of violent death — crushed skulls (blunt force trauma), an arrowhead embedded in a vertebra, and cut marks on limb bones. One skeleton (a young adult male) had five microliths (lunates) embedded in the pelvis, apparently from a single arrow or spear. The burials were not arranged in an orderly fashion — the bodies were thrown into the pit, some on top of others, suggesting a mass grave for victims of a raid or conflict over resources. Radiocarbon dating places the event at c. 6000–5000 BCE. This is among the earliest evidence of organised conflict anywhere in the world.
  • Mahadaha (Uttar Pradesh) — A large Mesolithic settlement (about 2 hectares) with well-defined habitation areas and a separate burial ground. Excavations by G.R. Sharma and J.N. Pandey found 31 burials, including multiple burials, a woman with a newborn child, and skeletons with ornaments of antler, bone, and shell. The site shows evidence of dietary diversity — the faunal remains include fish (catfish, carp), turtle, crocodile, wild boar, deer, and cattle, while the plant remains include wild rice, berries, and tubers. The skeletons from Mahadaha have been studied for paleopathology (evidence of healed fractures, dental caries, osteoarthritis, and a possible trephinated skull — a hole drilled in the skull, perhaps for therapeutic or ritual reasons).
  • Damdama (Uttar Pradesh) — The most prolific Mesolithic site in India in terms of human skeletal remains: over 100 individual burials excavated by D. Mandal and P.C. Pant. The burials show a remarkable variety of practices: extended burials (supine, legs straight), flexed burials (on side, knees drawn up), multiple burials (2–5 individuals together), and burials with distinct orientations (north-south, east-west). Many skeletons show evidence of dental modification — the anterior teeth (incisors) were deliberately filed or chipped into a V-shape, possibly as a rite of passage, a mark of group identity, or for cosmetic reasons. This practice is still found among some African and Australian Aboriginal groups. The Damdama skeletons also show high rates of dental caries (cavities), possibly because of the high carbohydrate content of the diet (wild rice, tubers). The bone chemistry analysis (carbon and nitrogen isotopes) of Damdama and Mahadaha skeletons by J.N. Lukacs and colleagues shows that the Ganga plain Mesolithic populations had a diet dominated by plant foods with a significant contribution from freshwater fish — a pattern of 'broad-spectrum' foraging that would eventually lead to agriculture. The large, well-organised cemeteries at these sites suggest territorial ownership — these were not nomadic bands moving across the landscape but groups that claimed exclusive rights to a particular territory and returned to the same settlement sites year after year. This is a critical step on the path to settled agricultural life.

The Transition to Agriculture: Lahuradewa — This site, on the Ghaghara River in Uttar Pradesh, has been excavated by Rakesh Tewari and has produced the earliest evidence of rice cultivation in the Indian subcontinent. Direct radiocarbon dating of rice grains found in association with microliths and Early Neolithic pottery has given dates of c. 7000 BCE, making Lahuradewa the oldest known site of rice culture in South Asia. The rice has been identified as Oryza sativa indica (a domesticated form of wild rice), distinct from the Chinese domesticated rice (Oryza sativa japonica). This suggests an independent domestication of rice in the Gangetic plains, separate from the Yangtze valley domestication event. The site shows a transition from wild to domesticated forms: the earliest levels (c. 7000 BCE) contain wild rice grains alongside hunting and fishing gear; by c. 5000 BCE, the rice grains are larger and more uniform (indicating selection for domesticated traits), and by c. 3000 BCE, fully domesticated rice with associated agricultural tools (hoes, mortars, pestles) is present. Lahuradewa is critical evidence that the transition to agriculture in India was not a simple diffusion from the Fertile Crescent — it was a regionally diverse process, with the Gangetic plains independently domesticating rice and the western subcontinent (Mehrgarh) receiving wheat and barley from West Asia.

Rock art continued to flourish during the Mesolithic. At Bhimbetka, the great panels of Period II show the full range of Mesolithic life: group hunting with bows and arrows, women gathering honey and fruits, men dancing in a line (possibly a ritual or ceremony), and animals depicted with remarkable naturalism (a tiger pouncing on a deer, a herd of deer running, a buffalo scratching its nose). The rock art of this period is often described as 'X-ray art' — the internal anatomy of animals is sometimes shown (ribs, heart, digestive system), suggesting intimate knowledge of game animals gained through butchering. The paintings are also notable for showing composite weapons (bows and arrows, spears with barbed tips) that match the microliths found in the archaeological deposits, confirming the link between the art and the material culture of the Mesolithic.

Key Fact for UPSC
Mesolithic sites in the Gangetic plains show evidence of the earliest rice cultivation at Lahuradewa (Uttar Pradesh), c. 7000 BCE, pushing back the antiquity of rice domestication in India significantly. The transition from hunting-gathering to agriculture was a gradual, regionally diverse process — not a sudden 'Neolithic Revolution' as once believed. The Bhimbetka rock shelter complex demonstrates the longest continuous human occupation in India, spanning from the Lower Palaeolithic to the Medieval period — a unique feature recognised by UNESCO world heritage status. The V.S. Wakankar discovery of Bhimbetka (1957) revolutionised Indian prehistory. Sarai Nahar Rai provides the earliest evidence of organised violence in India, and Damdama has yielded the largest collection of Mesolithic human skeletons (over 100 burials) in the subcontinent.

Neolithic Period (c. 7000 – 2000 BCE)

The Neolithic saw the full establishment of settled agriculture, domestication of plants and animals, ground and polished stone tools, pottery-making, and permanent villages. The Indian Neolithic has distinct regional variants, each with unique characteristics determined by local geography, climate, and cultural traditions. The term 'Neolithic Revolution' (coined by V. Gordon Childe, 1936) captured the transformative nature of this change: the shift from a food-collecting to a food-producing economy changed every aspect of human life — population density, social organisation, technology, ideology, and even human biology (the 'Neolithic Demographic Transition' brought higher birth rates and shorter stature due to a less diverse diet).

1. Mehrgarh (Balochistan, Pakistan) — c. 7000–2000 BCE, the earliest Neolithic site in South Asia. Excavated by Jean-Francois Jarrige (1974–1986) for the French Archaeological Mission in Pakistan, Mehrgarh provides a continuous sequence of five millennia of human occupation, from Pre-Pottery Neolithic to Chalcolithic, forming a direct precursor to the Indus Valley Civilization. The site is located on the Kachi Plain at the foot of the Bolan Pass, a strategic route linking South Asia with Central Asia and Afghanistan. The sequence is divided into eight phases:

  • Phase I (7000–5500 BCE) — Pre-Pottery Neolithic. Mud-brick houses (four-room rectangular structures with storage compartments). Cultivation of domesticated barley (Hordeum vulgare) and wheat (Triticum dicoccum and T. monococcum). Domesticated goats, sheep, and cattle. The earliest evidence of cotton cultivation in the world — cotton fibres (Gossypium sp.) found preserved in a copper bead, dating to c. 6000 BCE. The most astonishing discovery is the practice of dentistry: eleven drilled molars (with concentric grooves characteristic of a flint drill) were found in nine individuals, with evidence of healing indicating that the drilling was performed on living patients. This is the earliest known dentistry anywhere in the world, predating the next earliest (in Egypt, c. 3000 BCE) by three millennia. Burials contained grave goods — baskets coated with bitumen, beads of turquoise and lapis lazuli (from Badakhshan, Afghanistan), bone tools, and ornaments. The presence of lapis lazuli and turquoise proves that long-distance trade networks reaching into Central Asia were already operating by 6000 BCE.
  • Phase II–III (5500–3500 BCE) — Pottery Neolithic. The earliest pottery is coarse, hand-made, and sometimes basket-impressed. By Phase III, wheel-made pottery appears, painted with geometric designs (chevrons, triangles, cross-hatching) in black on a buff or red slip. The emergence of terracotta figurines, especially seated female figurines with elaborate headdresses (fan-shaped, with applied ornaments), prominent breasts, and pinched noses. These have been interpreted as 'Mother Goddess' figurines — the earliest evidence of the devotional tradition that would later become the goddess cults of Hinduism. Two types of burials appear: individual extended burials in mud-brick tombs, and jar burials for infants and children.
  • Phase IV–VII (3500–2600 BCE) — Chalcolithic transition. Copper appears, used for flat celts, knives, and mirrors. The pottery becomes finer, with elaborate painted designs including peacocks, pipal leaves, and antelopes. Stamp seals (steatite, square) appear with geometric motifs — precursors of the Harappan seals. Long-distance trade increases: lapis lazuli, carnelian, and marine shell are imported. The architecture becomes more elaborate, with large multi-room structures and a massive mud-brick platform that may have been a granary or administrative building.
  • Phase VIII (2600–2000 BCE) — Mature Harappan period. Mehrgarh becomes a small village (the focus shifts to the nearby urban site of Nausharo). The evidence shows that the Indus Valley Civilization did not appear suddenly but evolved out of the native Neolithic-Chalcolithic cultures of Balochistan, of which Mehrgarh is the most complete example.

2. Burzahom (Kashmir) — c. 3000–1500 BCE. This site in the Kashmir Valley is unique for its pit dwellings — circular or rectangular pits dug 2–4 metres into the ground, with a diameter of 2–6 metres. The pits had post-holes along the rim, indicating a superstructure of wooden poles covered with mud, thatch, or birch bark, with a conical or gabled roof. The pit-dwelling was well-adapted to the cold Himalayan winter, providing insulation against temperatures that dropped below freezing. The entrance was likely via a wooden ladder or sloping ramp. The pit floors were plastered with mud and occasionally burnt to harden them. The site gives its name to the Burzahom Neolithic culture, which has strong links with the Neolithic traditions of Central Asia and North China (the pit-dwelling tradition is widespread in the Yellow River valley of China, at sites like Banpo and Yangshao). The material culture includes polished stone axes (including shouldered axes, a type also found in China), bone tools (harpoons, needles, points, awls), and coarse grey pottery with mat impressions. The most famous find is a burial of a domesticated dog with its owner (c. 2300 BCE) — a complete dog skeleton placed in a grave alongside a human skeleton, with the dog at the man's feet, as if the dog was sacrificed to accompany its owner in the afterlife. This is the earliest known dog burial in South Asia. Burials also show the use of red ochre on the skeletons, a practice known from the European Palaeolithic and the Chinese Neolithic. The plant remains include wheat, barley, lentils, peas, and wild pistachio. The faunal remains include deer, wild goat, boar, and the Kashmir stag (hangul). The site also produced a unique stone slab with an engraving of a horned human figure (sometimes called the 'Burzahom shaman') — possibly representing a ritual specialist. The nearby site of Gufkral (meaning 'cave of the potter') shows a similar sequence with both pit dwellings and surface dwellings, and evidence of advanced pottery production.

3. Chirand (Bihar) — c. 2500–1500 BCE, located on the Ghaghara River in the Saran district. Chirand is the most important Neolithic site in the eastern Gangetic plains. The site covers an area of about 2 hectares and has a cultural deposit 2–3 metres thick. Excavated by B.P. Sinha and L.A. Narain, Chirand provides extensive evidence of rice cultivation — domesticated Oryza sativa (the Indian indica variety), along with wheat, barley, green peas, and moong (green gram). The animal remains include cattle, sheep, goat, pig, and the Indian rhinoceros (now extinct in the region, indicating a different climate with dense riverine forest and swamps). The bone tool industry is the most sophisticated of any Indian Neolithic site — beautifully polished arrowheads, fish hooks, harpoons, spear points, needles, borers, and scrapers, made from the long bones of deer and cattle. The pottery is distinctive: black-and-red ware (a precursor to the Chalcolithic black-and-red ware), painted in white or black with geometric patterns (simple lines, dots, concentric circles) and floral designs. The site also yielded terracotta beads and stone beads of agate and jasper. The importance of Chirand lies in its demonstration that the Gangetic plains were a centre of early rice agriculture independent of the western Neolithic traditions.

4. The Southern Neolithic Ash-Mound Complex — The Southern Neolithic (c. 3000–1000 BCE) of Karnataka and Andhra Pradesh is characterised by unique ash mounds — mounds of burnt cattle dung, some reaching 5–10 metres in height and 50–100 metres in diameter. Over 100 such ash mounds have been identified in the central and eastern Deccan. The most important sites include Budihal, Utnur, Kodekal, Kupgal, and Piklihal. The burnings were not accidental; they were repeated events, with each mound containing multiple layers of burnt and unburnt dung. Excavations at Budihal (by K. Paddayya) revealed a complete butchering floor adjacent to an ash mound: a stone platform with large stone choppers, cutting stools, and heavily cut-marked cattle bones, surrounded by ash and burnt clay. The interpretation (Paddayya, 1993) is that the ash mounds represent seasonal cattle corrals where animals were brought at the end of the monsoon grazing season for slaughter and feasting. After the animals were killed, the corral was deliberately set on fire (to clean the enclosure and drive away pests), the bones were smashed for marrow, and the meat was consumed in a communal feast. The next year, a new corral was built on the same spot, creating the mound. At Utnur (Andhra Pradesh), F.R. Allchin found an ash mound with a central post-hole (for tethering animals) and the remains of a mud-and-wattle fence, confirming the corral interpretation. At Kupgal, the ash mound is associated with a natural dolerite outcrop that produces a ringing sound when struck (a 'rock gong'), suggesting that the communal feasts were accompanied by musical/rhythmic performances. The Southern Neolithic also produced distinctive ground stone axes (including the characteristic Brahmagiri-type with a pointed butt and rectangular cross-section), stone bowls, and the earliest evidence of horse gram (Macrotyloma uniflorum) and millet (Setaria italica), crops adapted to the semi-arid Deccan climate. The principal excavated sites are Brahmagiri (excavated by Mortimer Wheeler, providing the first stratigraphic framework for South Indian archaeology), Hallur (excavated by M.S. Nagaraja Rao, one of the first radiocarbon-dated Neolithic sites in South India), and Piklihal (excavated by F.R. Allchin, rich in faunal remains).

5. The Neolithic-Chalcolithic Transition — The transition from the Neolithic to the Chalcolithic in India was not a single event but a gradual process that occurred at different times in different regions. In Balochistan (Mehrgarh), copper appears around 3500 BCE while the village continues uninterrupted. In the Deccan, the Neolithic and Chalcolithic overlap considerably — sites like Hallur have levels that are classified as 'Neolithic' by the presence of ground stone axes and 'Chalcolithic' by the presence of copper tools, sometimes in the same layer. The term Neolithic-Chalcolithic is used to capture this continuum. In the Gangetic plains, the transition is more abrupt: after the Neolithic of Chirand and Lahuradewa, there is a gap before the OCP and copper hoard cultures appear. This uneven transition reflects the patchy nature of early metallurgy, the availability of copper ore (the Khetri mines in Rajasthan were a major source, exploited from the Chalcolithic period onward), and the slow spread of metallurgical knowledge through trade networks.

6. The Spread of Rice Cultivation Across the Gangetic Plains — Rice (Oryza sativa indica) was domesticated in the Gangetic plains by at least 7000 BCE (Lahuradewa). From the eastern Gangetic plains (present-day Bihar, eastern Uttar Pradesh), rice agriculture spread in two directions: eastward into Assam, Bengal, and Southeast Asia (where it mixed with the japonica rice from China), and westward into the Indus Valley (where rice appears at Lothal and Rangpur only by c. 2000 BCE, in the Late Harappan period). The spread was not a simple diffusion of a pack of seeds — it required the adaptation of rice to different environments (deepwater rice in the floodplains, rain-fed upland rice on the higher ground, irrigated rice in the doabs). This adaptability made rice the staple of Indian agriculture and the demographic engine behind the expansion of the population into the eastern Gangetic plains during the second millennium BCE, which eventually provided the economic base for the Mahajanapadas (the first states of India) in the first millennium BCE.

Chalcolithic Period (c. 3000 – 700 BCE)

The Chalcolithic (Copper-Stone Age) represents the transition from stone to metal, with copper tools coexisting with stone. Chalcolithic cultures were regional, pre-urban or non-urban, and many were contemporaneous with or immediately preceded the Early Historic period. These cultures demonstrate the increasing complexity of society, with craft specialisation, inter-regional trade networks, and social stratification emerging. The term 'Chalcolithic' is used more confidently in India than in other regions because the subcontinent has rich copper deposits (Khetri in Rajasthan, Singhbhum in Jharkhand, Malanjkhand in Madhya Pradesh) that were exploited early.

Major Chalcolithic Cultures of India:

  • Ahar-Banas Culture (Rajasthan, c. 3000–1500 BCE) — Also called the Banas Culture after the Banas River. Known for black-and-red ware (BRW) made with a unique double-firing technique (oxidising and reducing atmosphere), which gives the pottery its distinctive bichrome appearance: the interior is black (from the reducing/oxygen-free atmosphere), and the exterior is red (from the oxidising atmosphere). The Ahar-Banas people cultivated wheat, barley, and rice, kept cattle, sheep, and goats, and used copper tools (flat celts, rings, bangles, and wire). The most spectacular site is Gilund, excavated by Umesh Singh and Gregory Possehl, which has revealed a massive, 54-room mud-brick complex covering 150 sq m, interpreted as a granary, storehouse, or administrative centre. The rooms are arranged in two parallel rows of 27 rooms each, separated by a long corridor — this is the largest single structure known from any Indian Chalcolithic culture. At Balathal (excavated by V.N. Misra and R.K. Mohanty), a massive fortification wall (5 m wide, of stone and mud-brick) encircling the settlement was found, along with a stone-paved road, a central hearth with traces of ritual offerings (animal bones and pottery), and evidence of copper smelting (furnaces, slag, crucibles). Balathal's fortifications suggest that conflict and the need for defence were already features of Chalcolithic life. The type site Ahar (excavated by R.C. Agrawala) gave the culture its name, and nearby Dhulkot and Ojiyana show similar fortified settlements. The Ahar-Banas culture was the most complex of the Indian Chalcolithic, with clear evidence of social hierarchy (the fortified citadel at Balathal vs. the unfortified village of Ahar), craft specialisation (dedicated potters, coppersmiths, and bead-makers), and inter-regional trade (copper from Khetri, carnelian and agate from Gujarat, marine shell from the Saurashtra coast).
  • Malwa Culture (Madhya Pradesh, c. 1900–1400 BCE) — Type site Navdatoli on the Narmada River, excavated by H.D. Sankalia and S.B. Deo. The Malwa culture is known for its fine painted pottery: black designs on a red slip, with geometric motifs (chevrons, triangles, wavy lines, concentric circles) and naturalistic motifs (peacock, deer, fish, tree, the sun). The pottery was made on a fast wheel and fired at high temperatures (800–900°C) in a kiln with controlled atmosphere. The subsistence base was agriculture: wheat, barley, lentils, peas, chickpeas, and linseed (for oil). Animal domestication included cattle, sheep, goat, pig, and the first firm evidence of the domestic horse in central India. Trade was extensive: copper came from Khetri and Malanjkhand, carnelian and jasper from the Narmada valley, and marine shell from Gujarat. The presence of a single Harappan seal at Navdatoli suggests contact with the Indus Valley. The site of Eran in the Malwa region has yielded evidence of a large mud-brick fortification, indicating that the Malwa culture too had an organised defence system.
  • Jorwe Culture (Maharashtra, c. 1400–700 BCE) — Type site Jorwe near Ahmednagar, named after the site excavated by H.D. Sankalia and S.B. Deo. The Jorwe culture is the best-studied Chalcolithic culture of India, thanks to the extensive excavations at Daimabad and Inamgaon by M.K. Dhavalikar and colleagues. The Daimabad bronzes are the most spectacular finds from this culture: a chariot (about 30 cm long), two elephants, a rhinoceros, and a buffalo, all cast in lost-wax technique (cire perdue) and dated to c. 2000 BCE. The bronze chariot, with its spoked wheels (the earliest known in India), a standing driver, and a coupling to two animals, testifies to advanced metallurgical skills. Four distinct phases of Jorwe culture have been identified at Inamgaon (c. 1400–700 BCE). Inamgaon has yielded a house of the chief (a large, centrally-located, multi-roomed mud-brick house with a raised platform against the wall, presumably where the chief sat), a granary (a thatched structure on stilts with 37 cylindrical storage bins, each lined with lime plaster to protect the grain from moisture and rodents), and a large mud-brick wall fortified with bastions, enclosing the site. The granary could store up to 115 cubic metres of grain — enough to feed 150–200 people for a year — indicating a substantial surplus and a redistributive economy. Burial practices included urn burials for infants and children (large globular urns, often decorated, with the body placed in a flexed position, accompanied by small pots and ornaments) and extended burials for adults (in pits, lying on the back, sometimes with grave goods such as pottery and copper ornaments). At Daimabad, a large mud platform with an apsidal plan (similar to the later Buddhist chaitya halls) was found, possibly a public or religious building. The Jorwe culture declined around 700 BCE, coinciding with the arrival of iron and the megalithic culture of the Deccan.
  • Kayatha Culture (Madhya Pradesh, c. 2500–2000 BCE) — The earliest Chalcolithic culture of the Malwa region, excavated by V.S. Wakankar and Z.D. Ansari. Kayatha is characterised by a distinctive buff-slipped pottery painted with linear designs in brown or black, along with a coarse grey ware. The Kayatha people used copper tools (flat celts, rings, and bangles), stone blades (many made of chalcedony and chert imported from the Narmada valley), and practised wheat, barley, and lentil cultivation. A mud fortification wall (6 m wide at the base) was found at Kayatha, making it one of the earliest known fortified sites of the Indian subcontinent. The Kayatha culture is contemporary with the late pre-Harappan and Mature Harappan periods, and some Kayatha pottery shows clear Harappan influence (the dish-on-stand shape, the reserved slip technique). It was succeeded by the Malwa culture around 1900 BCE.
  • Ochre Coloured Pottery (OCP) Culture (Ganga-Yamuna Doab, c. 2000–1500 BCE) — This culture is named after its characteristic pottery, which is covered with an ochre-coloured (orange-red) wash that rubs off on the hands. OCP sites are found in the western Gangetic plain, from present-day Bihar to Haryana. The pottery is handmade and wheel-made, with a thick red slip that flakes off. OCP is found at sites like Atranjikhera, Ambkheri, Saipai, Bisauli, Rajpur Parsu, and Ahichchhatra. The culture is often associated with Copper Hoards — caches of copper tools and weapons buried in the ground. The Copper Hoards include distinctive types: flat celts (axes with a rectangular cross-section), bar celts, harpoons with barbed points, swords with a midrib and antennae hilts (often called 'antennae swords'), anthropomorphic figures (a human-like shape with a hole in the head, possibly a ritual object or a symbol of a deity), and rings of various sizes. Over 200 such copper hoards have been found across the Ganga-Yamuna Doab, with the largest concentrations in the Saharanpur, Badaun, and Mainpuri districts of western Uttar Pradesh. The OCP culture has been seen as a link between the late Harappan and the early Vedic world — some scholars argue that OCP represents the eastward migration of the Harappans after the Ghaggar-Hakra dried up (c. 1900 BCE), while others see it as an independent indigenous culture of the Gangetic plain. The absence of excavated settlements (most OCP is known from surface finds and hoards) makes it difficult to characterise the culture fully. Recent excavations at Sinauli (Uttar Pradesh, 2018) by S.K. Manjul have revealed an OCP-culture burial with an antique bronze chariot, a copper helmet, and copper shields, dated to c. 2000–1800 BCE. The Sinauli find has reignited the debate on whether the OCP culture represents the material remains of the early Vedic period, since the chariots and weapons match descriptions in the Rigveda.
  • Painted Grey Ware (PGW) Culture (c. 1100–600 BCE) — The PGW is a fine, wheel-made grey pottery, painted with black geometric motifs (dots, dashes, concentric circles, wavy lines, swastikas, and the 'pipal leaf' motif). The pottery was made on a fast wheel, fired to a high temperature in a reducing atmosphere (giving the grey colour), and then decorated with black mineral paint. PGW sites are concentrated in the Ganga-Yamuna Doab and the eastern Punjab, with the most important being Hastinapur (excavated by B.B. Lal, 1950–52), Mathura, Ahichchhatra, Kurukshetra, Kampilya, Noh, Jakhera, and Bairat. The associated material culture includes iron tools and weapons (knives, celts, axes, spearheads, arrowheads, swords, and agricultural implements such as sickles and hoes), copper objects, glass beads, and terracotta figurines. The subsistence base was rice, wheat, barley, and millets, with domesticated cattle, sheep, goats, horse (well-attested in PGW levels), and pig. Horse bones and the iron horse-bit from Hastinapur confirm the importance of horse-riding in PGW culture. The PGW culture is associated with the later Vedic period (1000–500 BCE) and the Mahabharata. The sites of Hastinapur (the capital of the Kurus), Mathura (the capital of the Surasenas), Kampilya (the capital of the Panchalas), and Kurukshetra (the battlefield) are all PGW settlements mentioned in the Mahabharata. B.B. Lal's excavations at Hastinapur uncovered the PGW level (the 'Kuru kingdom' level) overlying a flood-destroyed OCP level (which he correlated with the earlier, flood-destroyed city of the Pandavas). The identification of the PGW with the Mahabharata is suggestive but not proven — the Mahabharata tradition certainly refers to the material culture of the PGW period, but the epic as we have it was composed and redacted over many centuries and may conflate the PGW period (c. 1000 BCE) with the early historical period (c. 500 BCE) when it was written down. The transition from the Late Vedic to the Early Historic period is visible in the PGW levels: at the end of the PGW period (c. 600 BCE), the pottery changes to the Northern Black Polished Ware (NBPW), iron tools become common (the 'Iron Age' proper), and urban centres (janapadas) emerge, marking the beginning of the historical period in the Gangetic plain.

The Antiquity of Iron in India — The date of the earliest iron in India is a subject of great importance for UPSC, as it marks the transition from prehistory to history in the Gangetic plain. Iron tools and weapons appear at the following well-dated early sites: Dadupur (Uttar Pradesh) — iron slag and iron tools in layers dated by radiocarbon to c. 1100 BCE; Raja Nala Ka Tila (Uttar Pradesh) — iron objects in a context dated to c. 1000 BCE; Malhar (Uttar Pradesh) — iron smelting furnaces and slag, with radiocarbon dates of c. 1000 BCE for the earliest iron level, with a continuous sequence of iron smelting for over 1,000 years. The site of Malhar, excavated by Rakesh Tewari (Director, U.P. State Archaeology Department), is particularly important because it provides evidence of a local, independent invention of iron smelting in eastern Uttar Pradesh, using local iron ore (from the Ganga alluvium) and a reduction furnace of a type unique to the region. Tewari (2003) argues that India's iron smelting was an independent invention, not a diffusion from the Near East (where iron appears c. 1200 BCE in Anatolia). This is a controversial claim — most scholars favour the diffusion model, arguing that iron was brought to India by the same trade networks that carried the PGW pottery and the technological complex of horse-breeding and chariot-making from Central Asia and Iran. However, the continuous sequence at Malhar, starting as early as 1000 BCE and developing into a fully-fledged iron industry by 800 BCE, is a strong argument for independent invention or at least very early assimilation of the technology. Other early iron-bearing sites include Atranjikhera (c. 1000 BCE, iron axes and sickles in OCP/PGW levels), Hastinapur (c. 900 BCE, a corroded iron sword), and Baranaga (c. 1000 BCE, iron smelting). By contrast, South India does not see iron until c. 700–600 BCE (in megalithic contexts at sites like Amaravati, Maski, and Brahmagiri), and the Deccan (Maharashtra) does not see it until c. 500 BCE. This north-to-south gradient is consistent with either diffusion from the Near East through the Gangetic plain or with independent invention in the north followed by gradual southward spread.

Evolution of the Indian Prehistoric Chronology — Contributions of Key Scholars

The chronology of Indian prehistory was built over more than a century through the work of pioneering scholars, each of whom added a major piece to the puzzle:

  • Robert Bruce Foote (1834–1912) — A British geologist of the Geological Survey of India, Foote discovered the first Palaeolith in India at Pallavaram (near Chennai) in 1863, only four years after the first Palaeolith was recognised in Europe (Boucher de Perthes in France, 1859). Foote went on to identify over 450 prehistoric sites across the Madras Presidency, Gujarat, the Deccan, and the Narmada Valley, assembling the first large collection of Indian Stone Age tools. His 1866 paper "On the occurrence of stone implements in lateritic formations in various parts of the Madras and North Arcot districts" is the foundational text of Indian prehistory. He was the first to note the lateritic (ferricrete) horizon as a key context for Lower Palaeolithic tools, and he developed a rudimentary typology of Indian handaxes and cleavers that remained in use for a century.
  • H.D. Sankalia (1908–1989) — The 'founder of modern Indian archaeology' and the first Indian professor of archaeology at Deccan College, Pune. Sankalia conducted the first systematic, stratigraphic excavations of Stone Age sites in India. His work at Nevasa (Maharashtra) established the Nevasian industry and the first chrono-stratigraphic sequence for the Indian Palaeolithic (Lower — Middle — Upper). He excavated Langhnaj (Gujarat), the first excavated Mesolithic site in India, and Navdatoli (Madhya Pradesh), the type site of the Malwa Chalcolithic culture. His textbook, Prehistory and Protohistory of India and Pakistan (1974), was the standard reference for a generation of students. Sankalia also directed the excavations at Inamgaon (with S.B. Deo and M.K. Dhavalikar), which produced the finest evidence of Chalcolithic settlement in the Deccan.
  • V.S. Wakankar (1919–1988) — An archaeologist and artist, Wakankar is best known for the discovery of Bhimbetka in 1957, when he saw the rock shelters from a moving train. He spent the rest of his life studying the rock art of Bhimbetka, developing the stylistic chronology (Periods I–V) that remains the foundation of Indian rock art studies. He also excavated Kayatha (the type site of the Kayatha Chalcolithic culture) and studied the rock art of the Vindhyas and the Chambal Valley. Wakankar was a passionate advocate for the protection of Bhimbetka, which was awarded UNESCO World Heritage status largely through his efforts.
  • Shanti Pappu (b. 1964) — A contemporary archaeologist who has revolutionised Indian prehistory through her work at Attirampakkam (Tamil Nadu). Using modern geoarchaeological techniques (micromorphology, grain-size analysis, heavy mineral analysis, cosmogenic nuclide burial dating), Pappu's team pushed back the date of the earliest human occupation of India to 1.5 million years ago, challenging the Out-of-Africa model. Her work is a model of scientific archaeology, combining geological analysis with artefact studies to reconstruct the landscapes of early hominin occupation in South India.
  • Other key contributors: K. Paddayya for his work on the Hunsgi-Baichbal Acheulian living floors and the ethnoarchaeology of the Deccan; G.R. Sharma for the Belan Valley sequence and the Mesolithic-Gangetic Neolithic excavations; M.K. Dhavalikar for the Jorwe culture and Inamgaon; V.N. Misra for Bagor and the Rajasthan Mesolithic; Rakesh Tewari for the early iron sites of the Gangetic plain (Malhar, Dadupur, Raja Nala Ka Tila); F.R. Allchin for the Southern Neolithic ash mounds; Jean-Francois Jarrige for Mehrgarh; and Ravi Korisettar for the Toba tephra studies and the Palaeolithic of the Jurreru Valley.
Historiographical Note
The three-age system (Stone-Bronze-Iron) was developed by Christian Jorgensen Thomsen (1836) for Danish museums. In India, Robert Bruce Foote (1863) discovered the first Palaeolithic tool at Pallavaram, Chennai. Modern research has problematised the rigid three-stage sequence — regional variations, overlapping technologies, and the gradual nature of transitions are now emphasised. The V.S. Wakankar discovery of Bhimbetka (1957) revolutionised Indian prehistory by revealing an unbroken sequence from Lower Palaeolithic to Medieval times. The transition from the Chalcolithic to the Iron Age in India was not uniform — iron appeared in the Gangetic plain by c. 1000 BCE (as evidenced at sites like Dadupur, Raja Nala Ka Tila, and Malhar) but was not used in South India until much later (c. 700–600 BCE). The Attirampakkam discoveries (2011) have pushed back the earliest date of Indian prehistory to 1.5 mya, challenging the Out-of-Africa model and establishing India as a key region for understanding early human dispersals.

Key Debates in Indian Prehistory

Indian prehistory is the subject of several major scholarly debates, many of which have direct relevance to UPSC because they touch on broader themes of human origins, migration, and the formation of Indian civilisation:

  • 1. The Out-of-Africa vs. Multi-Regional Model Debate (The Attirampakkam Challenge) — The dominant model for human origins is the Out-of-Africa (Replacement) Model, which holds that Homo sapiens evolved in Africa c. 200,000 years ago, dispersed into Eurasia c. 60,000–50,000 years ago, and replaced all existing hominin populations (including the Neanderthals and Homo erectus) without significant interbreeding. An alternative is the Multi-Regional Model, which holds that different regional populations of Homo erectus evolved in parallel into modern humans, with gene flow between them. The Attirampakkam date of 1.5 mya challenges the Out-of-Africa model in a different way: if the earliest Acheulian sites in India are as old as the earliest in Africa, then either hominins dispersed from Africa into India very shortly after developing Acheulian technology, or the Acheulian tradition developed independently in India and Africa from a common Oldowan base. Current evidence from Dmanisi (Georgia, 1.77 mya), Java (1.6 mya), and the Sangiran Dome (1.5 mya) shows that Homo erectus dispersed out of Africa very quickly, reaching Southeast Asia by 1.6 mya. Attirampakkam shows that India was part of this earliest dispersal. Acheulian tools from Olduvai Gorge (1.76 mya) and Kokiselei, West Turkana (1.76 mya) are still the oldest, but the gap with India (1.5 mya) is closing rapidly, and future discoveries may push back the Indian date further.
  • 2. The Acheulian-Soan Relationship — Are the Acheulian (handaxe) and Soanian (chopper-chopping tool) traditions the products of different hominin species, different cultural traditions, or different raw material constraints? The 'two-community' hypothesis (de Terra, Paterson, 1939) saw the Soanian as the work of a more primitive population in the Himalayan foothills. The 'raw-material' hypothesis (R.V. Joshi, G. Corvinus) argues that the Soanian is simply the Acheulian adapted to local pebble raw material. Recent dating suggests the Soanian may be much younger (Middle Pleistocene, c. 400–100 kya) than the Acheulian, implying the two traditions are not contemporaneous alternatives but rather sequential adaptations. However, Ambadevi (Maharashtra) and Adamgarh (MP) have yielded Soanian-like pebble tools in stratigraphic contexts that suggest they are contemporary with the Acheulian, keeping the debate open.
  • 3. The Date of the Narmada Man — The Narmada skullcap (Hathnora) is the only hominin fossil discovered in India, and its date and taxonomic position remain unresolved. The best estimates range from c. 500,000 years BP (faunal correlation) to c. 236,000–288,000 years BP (ESR dating of associated teeth). The skullcap has been assigned to Homo erectus, Homo heidelbergensis, and Homo sapiens by different scholars. The late date (c. 250,000 BP) would make it contemporary with Homo sapiens in Africa, raising the possibility that the Narmada hominin was a modern human, not an archaic one. A genetic study (aDNA) of the Narmada fossil could resolve the question, but DNA preservation in tropical Indian conditions may be too poor for extraction. Until more fossils are found, the Narmada Man will remain a tantalising puzzle.
  • 4. The Neolithic Transition — Independent Invention or Diffusion? — Two competing models explain the spread of agriculture in India. The Diffusion Model argues that wheat, barley, sheep, goats, and cattle were domesticated in the Fertile Crescent (c. 9500 BCE) and spread eastward through Iran, Balochistan, and into South Asia, reaching Mehrgarh by 7000 BCE. In this model, the Indian Neolithic is a secondary centre of agricultural origins, derived from the West Asian 'Neolithic package'. The Independent Invention Model argues that the Indian subcontinent had its own centres of plant and animal domestication: rice in the Gangetic plains (Lahuradewa, c. 7000 BCE), millets and horse gram in the Deccan (Hallur, c. 2500 BCE), and possibly cattle (zebu, Bos indicus) in Balochistan. The current consensus is a mixed model: wheat and barley arrived from West Asia via the Balochistan route, but rice was domesticated independently in the Ganga plain, and the Deccan millets represent a third, independent trajectory. The cattle domestication question is especially important: was the Indian zebu (Bos indicus) domesticated from the local wild population (Bos primigenius namadicus) independently of the domestication of the humpless taurine cattle (Bos taurus) in the Near East? Mitochondrial DNA studies (Kumar et al., 2011) suggest that Indian zebu cattle were domesticated locally, not imported from West Asia. This supports a model of multiple, independent origins for agriculture in India.
  • 5. The Date of Iron in India — The debate on whether iron was independently invented in India or diffused from the Near East remains active. The earliest iron in the Near East appears in Anatolia c. 1200 BCE, with the Hittite iron industry. The earliest iron in India appears at Dadupur, Raja Nala Ka Tila, and Malhar (Uttar Pradesh) at c. 1000–1100 BCE. The proponents of independent invention (Rakesh Tewari, 2003) point to the local iron ore sources, the unique furnace design at Malhar, and the continuous sequence from iron smelting to iron tool production that does not resemble the Near Eastern process. The diffusionists argue that the 100–200 year gap between Anatolia (1200 BCE) and India (1000 BCE) is too short for independent invention — the iron-smelting technology must have travelled through Iran and Afghanistan, reaching the Gangetic plain by the early first millennium BCE, carried by the same trade and migration networks that brought the horse, the chariot, the spoke wheel, and the PGW culture. The dates are close enough that either model is possible, and the question remains open pending further evidence.
  • 6. The PGW-Mahabharata Correlation — The identification of the PGW culture with the Mahabharata period is one of the most exciting but controversial correlations in Indian archaeology. B.B. Lal's excavations at Hastinapur (1950–52) uncovered a sequence of OCP (c. 2000–1500 BCE), followed by a flood deposit, followed by PGW (c. 1100–600 BCE), and then NBPW (c. 600–200 BCE). Lal proposed that the flood deposit at Hastinapur was the 'great flood' of the Mahabharata that destroyed the original city of the Pandavas, and that the PGW level was the city rebuilt by them. The identification is suggestive: Hastinapur is indeed the capital of the Kuru dynasty mentioned in the Mahabharata; the PGW culture has iron weapons, horse-drawn chariots, and rice agriculture, matching the epic's material culture; and the Mahabharata's detailed geography of the Kuru-Panchala region (between the Ganga and Yamuna) is exactly the heartland of the PGW culture. However, critics (R.S. Sharma, D.N. Jha) argue that the Mahabharata was composed over many centuries (c. 500 BCE to 400 CE) and that its most detailed descriptions of society and warfare refer to the early historic period (NBPW culture), not the PGW period. The epic's evidence cannot be used as a straightforward historical source for the PGW period. The UPSC exam expects students to know the PGW-Mahabharata link as a legitimate hypothesis but to recognise that it is not conclusively proven.

2. Indus Valley Civilization (2600-1900 BCE)

The Indus Valley Civilization (IVC), also called the Harappan Civilization after its type site Harappa, is one of the three great early civilizations of the Old World alongside Mesopotamia and Egypt. It flourished in the basins of the Indus River and the Ghaggar-Hakra (the palaeo-Saraswati), covering over 1.5 million sq km — larger than Egypt and Mesopotamia combined. Notable for advanced urban planning, standardized weights, maritime trade, and a still-undeciphered script, the IVC represents the First Urbanisation of the Indian subcontinent.

Discovery History: Unearthing a Lost Civilization

Unlike Egypt and Mesopotamia, the IVC was completely unknown until the 20th century. In 1826, British army deserter Charles Masson visited the ruined site of Harappa and described it as a “ruinous brick castle.” In 1853, Alexander Cunningham, first Director-General of the ASI, mistakenly identified it as a Buddhist site. The breakthrough came when John Marshall, ASI Director-General (1902–1928), recognized the distinctiveness of seals found at Harappa and ordered excavations.

Daya Ram Sahni began the first systematic excavations at Harappa in 1921, uncovering massive brick structures, pottery, and seals. In 1922, Rakhaldas (R. D.) Banerjee excavated Mohenjo-daro, finding the Great Bath, more seals, and recognizing the civilization’s pre-Buddhist antiquity. In 1924, Sir John Marshall formally announced the discovery of a new Bronze Age civilization in the Illustrated London News, pushing Indian antiquity back by over 2,000 years and placing it alongside Egypt and Mesopotamia.

Key archaeologists of the IVC discovery:

  • Daya Ram Sahni (1879–1939) — First excavator of Harappa (1921).
  • Rakhaldas Banerjee (1885–1930) — Discovered Mohenjo-daro (1922), the Great Bath, and the Pashupati seal.
  • Sir John Marshall (1876–1958) — Announced the IVC discovery (1924), published Mohenjo-daro and the Indus Civilization (1931), identified the Pashupati seal and Mother Goddess.
  • K. N. Dikshit (1889–1972) — Excavated further at Mohenjo-daro and Harappa; later DG of ASI (1938–1944).
  • N. G. Majumdar (1890–1938) — Excavated Chanhu-daro (1931) and Amri; established the pre-Harappan Amri culture.
  • M. S. Vats (1886–1959) — Directed Harappa excavations (1926–1934); published Excavations at Harappa (1940).
  • Sir Aurel Stein (1862–1943) — Explored Makran coast; discovered Sutkagendor, establishing western IVC limits.
  • Mortimer Wheeler (1890–1976) — DG of ASI (1944–1947); excavated Harappa (1946) and Mohenjo-daro (1950); proposed now-rejected Aryan Invasion Theory; identified granaries and fortifications.
  • S. R. Rao (1922–2013) — Excavated Lothal (1954–60), discovering the dockyard, bead factory, and Persian Gulf seal.
  • B. B. Lal (1921–2022) — Excavated Kalibangan (1961), discovering the ploughed field and fire altars; later DG of ASI (1968–1972).
  • J. P. Joshi (1931–2021) — Discovered Dholavira (1967) and excavated Surkotada (1970); DG of ASI (1994–1995).
  • R. S. Bisht (b. 1943) — Excavated Banawali (1975) and Dholavira (1990–2005); revealed the water management system and signboard.
  • Vasant Shinde (b. 1955) — Excavated Rakhigarhi (2013–16); led the aDNA study published in Cell (2019).

Chronology and Phases

PhaseTime PeriodKey Characteristics
Pre-Harappan / Early Food-Producingc. 7000–5000 BCEEarliest agriculture in the Indus region. Mehrgarh (Balochistan): mud-brick houses, wheat/barley, domesticated zebu cattle, earliest cotton cultivation (6000 BCE), steatite beads, simple pottery. Bridges Neolithic and Chalcolithic.
Early Harappan (Regionalization)c. 5000–2600 BCEMultiple regional cultures emerge. Hakra Ware (Cholistan, c. 4500–3500 BCE): handmade pottery, pastoralism. Kot Diji (Sindh, c. 3200–2600 BCE): massive defensive walls, stone foundations, pipal leaf pottery. Amri (Sindh, c. 3600–2800 BCE): geometric painted pottery, bull figurines. Sothi-Siswal (Rajasthan/Haryana, c. 3300–2600 BCE): ceramics at Kalibangan and Banawali lower levels. These cultures show increasing social complexity, craft specialization, early trade, and proto-writing symbols.
Mature Harappan (Integration)c. 2600–1900 BCEPeak of the civilization. Fully urban with grid-plan cities, standardized bricks (1:2:4 ratio), advanced drainage, Indus script (400–450 signs), binary-decimal weights, extensive inter-regional trade (Mesopotamia, Persian Gulf, Central Asia). Major sites: Mohenjo-daro, Harappa, Dholavira, Rakhigarhi, Lothal, Kalibangan. Sophisticated water management (Great Bath, Dholavira reservoirs, Lothal dockyard).
Late Harappan (Localization)c. 1900–1300 BCEGradual de-urbanization. Cessation of long-distance trade, abandonment of cities, loss of script and standardized weights. Regional cultures: Cemetery H (Punjab/Harappa), Jhukar (Sindh/Chanhu-daro), Rangpur IIB (Gujarat). Movement eastward to the Ganga-Yamuna Doab (Alamgirpur). The Ghaggar-Hakra dried up.
