Urban Planning and Architecture

Harappan urban planning represents the most sophisticated Bronze Age city design worldwide, with remarkable standardization across 1.5 million sq km.

The Grid-Iron System — Main streets ran north-south and east-west in a grid, dividing the city into rectangular blocks. Three street tiers: arterial streets (10–12m wide), subsidiary streets (3–6m), and narrow lanes (1–2m). Street corners were rounded for bullock cart traffic. Standardized burnt bricks in a precise 1:2:4 ratio were used across the entire civilization.

Citadel and Lower Town — Most cities had a raised citadel (west, on a 6–12m mud-brick platform with fortifications, containing public buildings) and a lower town (east, residential area on a 2–4m platform, surrounded by a wall with gateways). Dholavira uniquely had a three-part division: citadel, middle town (elite), and lower town (commoners).

The Great Bath (Mohenjo-daro) — 11.88 x 7.01 x 2.43 metres, lined with two brick layers sandwiching a 2.5 cm bitumen waterproofing layer. Accessed by stairs at north and south ends, surrounded by a colonnaded portico with rooms (changing rooms). Water supply from a well; outflow through a corbelled-arch drain. Likely used for ritual purification — prefiguring Hindu ritual bathing and Buddhist monastic pools. The earliest known public water tank in the ancient world.

Great Granary (Mohenjo-daro) — 50 x 27 metres, 27 brick blocks arranged in a 9x3 grid with ventilation shafts and narrow passageways for efficient loading. Possibly a state granary for tax grain or a public assembly building.

The Pillared Hall — 27.4m square with 81 brick pillars (9 rows of 9) supporting a flat roof, capable of seating 500–700 people. Likely a council chamber or assembly hall.

Dholavira’s Water Management — The most sophisticated hydraulic engineering of the Bronze Age: 2 dams across seasonal streams (Manhar and Luni), 16 reservoirs carved from bedrock (largest 80x30m, capacity ~2.5 million cu m), connected by stone-lined channels with sluice gates and silt traps, plus stepwells (the earliest known). The system could support 15,000–20,000 people in an arid region that today supports only a few thousand. The signboard (North Gate): 10 gypsum letters, each 37cm high, inlaid into a 3m-long wooden board on the city gate — the oldest signboard in the world. The stadium (280x45m with tiered seating) is the earliest known sporting facility.

Lothal Dockyard — Trapezoidal brick-lined basin 218 x 37m, 4m deep, connected to the Sabarmati by a 12m-wide inlet channel with a lock-gate mechanism (grooves for the wooden gate found intact). Could hold 30–60 large ships. The lock system maintained constant water level during low tide, showing sophisticated understanding of tidal hydraulics. Adjacent warehouse (12 rooms on a 3.5m-high platform).

Kalibangan Ploughed Field — A field surface with intersecting east-west (30cm spacing) and north-south (15cm spacing) furrows, showing cross-cultivation of two different crops simultaneously — the oldest such evidence in the world. The fire altars: 7 brick-lined pits (40x40x30cm) in a row on a mud-brick platform, containing ash, charcoal, terracotta cakes, and burnt animal bones — the earliest structured fire cult in India, possibly prefiguring Vedic yajna.

Houses — Standard house design: rooms arranged around a central courtyard (4–12 rooms), providing light and ventilation. Every house had a bathroom (brick-paved with sloping floor to a drain) connected to street drains. Many had their own well. Two-storey houses common. Entrances from side lanes (not main streets) for privacy. House sizes varied dramatically: elite houses 1,000–2,500 sq ft vs. single-room ‘barracks’ at Harappa just 3.5 x 2.5m, showing significant social hierarchy. At Mohenjo-daro, 700+ brick-lined wells served the population, one well for every three houses — unparalleled density in any ancient city.

Drainage System — The most advanced in the ancient world. Covered brick drains lined with gypsum plaster ran along every main street, connected from each house bathroom. Inspection holes (manholes covered with stone lids) at regular intervals for cleaning — the first known municipal sanitation system. Drains emptied into brick-lined soak pits or city drains. No contemporary civilization (Egypt, Mesopotamia) had anything comparable.

Society and Culture

Harappan society was hierarchical but less militaristic than Egypt or Mesopotamia. There are no palaces, royal tombs, or grand temples.

Dress and Ornaments — The Priest-King bust shows a man wearing a fillet headband with a circular forehead ornament, a trimmed beard, and a trefoil-patterned robe (the trefoil motif also appears on Sumerian priestly garments, suggesting shared symbolic vocabulary). Mother Goddess figurines show women with elaborate fan-shaped headdresses, piled hair, short skirts with girdles, bead necklaces (up to 12 strands), and massive ear ornaments. Both sexes wore: carnelian, agate, jasper, lapis lazuli, gold, silver, and copper beads; shell and copper bangles (the Dancing Girl has 25 on her left arm vs. 2 on her right, suggesting asymmetry was fashionable); finger rings, earrings, nose-studs (gold); and belts of beads. Cosmetics: copper and ivory kohl sticks, bronze tweezers, razors, ear-picks, ivory combs, bronze mirrors (Banawali).

Toys and Recreation — Remarkable range of terracotta toys: bullock carts with moving wheels (identical to those still used in rural India), rattles, whistles, miniature vessels, and terracotta animals (monkeys, dogs, sheep, elephants, the ubiquitous humped bull). Dice — cubical terracotta and ivory dice with dots arranged 1 opposite 2, 3 opposite 4, 5 opposite 6 (same as modern dice — the oldest in India). Gambling was popular — numerous dice and game pieces (conical, cylindrical) found at multiple sites. The Mahabharata’s dice game may reflect IVC origins.

Weights and Measures — Cubical chert weights with no markings, following a binary-decimal progression: ratios 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 160, 200, 320, 640, 1600, 3200, 6400, 8000, 12800. Base unit = 0.856 grams; largest = 10.9 kg (ratio 11,700). Consistency across all IVC sites within 2% margin of error — implying powerful centralized authority. A bronze measuring rod (Mohenjo-daro, 66.5 inches, divided into 10 units of 6.7 inches) suggests a standardized length unit (~52–53 cm cubit). A shell scale from Lothal has 1.7mm divisions for bead measurement.

Seals: Functions and Motifs

Typically square steatite seals (2–4 cm), engraved with an animal motif and 1–3 lines of Indus script, with a perforated boss on the back for stringing. Pressed into wet clay tags to seal goods — many tags have cord marks, showing they were attached to bundles for transit identification. Functions: trade/commerce (seals on goods for transit), administration (government officials controlling storage and tax), religious/amuletic (placed in graves, worn as amulets), identification (each seal unique to an individual/family/firm).

Motifs: Unicorn (bull-like with one horn in profile) — 60% of all seals (1,000 of 1,500+), likely a mythical or composite animal, always shown with a triple-branched ‘cult object’ (incense burner or feeding trough). Humped bull (zebu) — 10% of seals. Also buffalo, tiger, elephant, rhinoceros, gharial, antelope, and the composite Pashupati figure.

Economy: Agriculture, Trade, and Crafts

Agriculture

Intensive mixed farming combining crops and animal husbandry. Crops: wheat (Triticum compactum, T. sphaerococcum), barley (Hordeum vulgare), rice (Lothal, Rangpur — earliest in western India), cotton (Gossypium arboreum — India was the first region to domesticate cotton; earliest evidence from Mehrgarh c. 6000 BCE; cotton cloth on a silver vase at Mohenjo-daro), peas, dates, sesame (possibly first domesticated in IVC), mustard, millets, melons, grapes.

Irrigation: Floodplain agriculture after monsoon floods, canals (4km-long canal at Rakhigarhi — earliest in India), wells for garden plots, reservoirs at Dholavira with sluice gates for fields, dams across seasonal streams. Flood control: massive mud-brick platforms (up to 12m high at Mohenjo-daro) protected cities. Mohenjo-daro shows repeated flood deposits, indicating periodic catastrophic Indus flooding. Animal husbandry: zebu humped cattle (dominant — for ploughing, transport, dairy), water buffalo (milk, wet-field ploughing), sheep (wool), goats, pigs, camels, elephants, dogs. The horse (Equus caballus) is absent from the Mature Harappan — the scholarly consensus rejects the Surkotada horse identification.

Trade Networks

The IVC operated the most extensive trade network of any Bronze Age civilization, stretching from Mesopotamia to Central Asia to the Deccan.

Mesopotamian Trade and Meluhha — Mesopotamian texts (c. 2300–1800 BCE) refer to Meluhha (widely identified as the Indus region), Dilmun (Bahrain), and Magan (Oman). Sargon of Akkad (c. 2334–2279 BCE) boasted that “ships from Meluhha, Magan, and Dilmun docked at the quay of Akkad.” Gudea of Lagash imported carnelian and lapis lazuli from Meluhha. Ur-III texts record Meluhhan villages in Mesopotamia. An Akkadian cylinder seal of “Sul-ili, interpreter of the Meluhhan language” shows a resident Meluhhan community with their own interpreters. Indus seals found at Ur (14 seals), Kish, Tell Asmar, Nippur, and Susa. After 1900 BCE, Meluhha disappears from records, corresponding with IVC decline.

Exports: heat-treated carnelian beads (deep red, the Harappan monopoly), lapis lazuli (from Badakhshan, transshipped through Indus ports), timber (teak, deodar — Mesopotamia was wood-poor), ivory, peacocks (called Meluhhapi in Akkadian, “Meluhhan birds”), monkeys, tortoise shell, cotton textiles. Imports: silver (Iran/Taurus), tin (Central Asia through Shortughai), woolen textiles, perfume oils. The trade imbalance favoured the IVC — far more Indus goods reached Mesopotamia than vice versa.

The Persian Gulf Circuit: Dilmun (Bahrain) served as entrepot. Gulf-type circular button seals found at Lothal. Harappan waystations on the Makran coast (Sutkagendor). Coastal route: Gujarat → Makran → Oman → Bahrain → Euphrates to Mesopotamian cities.

Central Asian Network: The colony at Shortughai (Amu Darya, Afghanistan, c. 2500 BCE, 2000 km from Indus) controlled access to lapis lazuli (Sar-i-Sang, Badakhshan — the only major source in the ancient world) and tin (Ferghana/Zeravshan, essential for bronze). The colony had Harappan-style architecture, bricks, and seals. Internal trade: IVC exchanged carnelian, agate, and shell with Deccan Chalcolithic cultures (Jorwe, Ahar/Banas) for local copper and gold (Daimabad).

Craft Production

Bead-making — The most sophisticated IVC craft. Beads made from carnelian (heat-treated at 800–1000�C in reducing kilns to change iron oxide from brown haematite to red haematite), steatite (carved then fired to harden), agate, jasper, lapis lazuli, faience (first synthetic material in India — quartz+alkali fired at 1000�C), shell, copper, bronze, silver, gold, bone. The process: cutting → grinding on chert wheels → drilling (ernestite drill bits on bow-drills, with sand+water abrasive) → polishing → heat treatment (carnelian). Indus bead-makers drilled holes of 0.3mm diameter through 3cm beads — unmatched until the Roman period. Etched carnelian beads (white patterns created by alkali application and firing) were a Harappan invention exported to Mesopotamia.

Metallurgy — Copper (Khetri mines, Rajasthan; Oman), bronze (copper+10–12% tin from Central Asia), gold (Kolar, Karnataka), silver (Iran), lead (Aravallis). Techniques: lost-wax casting (Dancing Girl, Daimabad bronzes). Tools: axes, adzes, chisels, knives, daggers, arrowheads, spears, fishhooks, razors, mirrors, vessels. The Dancing Girl (10.8cm, Mohenjo-daro, 1926) — bronze nude female in contrapposto pose, 25 bangles on left arm, unique anatomical naturalism in Bronze Age art. The Daimabad bronzes (c. 1500 BCE): chariot with oxen, rhinoceros, elephant, buffalo — technically accomplished lost-wax castings from the final Harappan phase.

Pottery — Wheel-made red ware with black painted designs (geometric patterns, birds, fish, plants, rarely humans). Black-and-red ware also found. Forms: large storage jars, dishes-on-stand (hallmark Harappan form), goblets, beakers, bowls, perforated pottery (for straining/liquid cooking), miniature vessels, beaded rims, painted perforated jars.

Faience and Glass — Faience beads, bangles, pendants, and tiny figurines (monkeys, birds). A single dark-blue true glass bead from Lothal is the earliest evidence of glass-making in India.

Boat and Ship Technology — A terracotta boat model from Lothal shows a vessel with a mast (for a sail), steering oar, and cabin. A stone engraving from Mohenjo-daro shows a reed boat similar to traditional Indus donga boats. The dockyard at Lothal (218x37m with lock-gate) proves ocean-going ship construction. The hulls were likely wooden planks sewn with rope (technique still used on the Malabar coast until the 20th century). Monsoon winds allowed reliable seasonal voyages between Gujarat and the Gulf.

Religion and Belief Systems

Harappan religion is reconstructed from material remains — no temples, palaces, or texts. Religion appears domestic and personal, centred on household shrines, though the Great Bath and fire altars suggest some public ritual.

The Pashupati Seal Debate

The Pashupati seal (Mohenjo-daro, 3.56 x 3.53 cm steatite) shows a figure seated in a yogic posture (legs bent, knees outward, heels touching, thumbs on knees — mulabandhasana), with three faces (central + two profiles), a horned headdress with three branches, surrounded by four animals (elephant, tiger, rhinoceros, buffalo) with two deer at the feet. Ten Indus signs across the top.

  • John Marshall (1931): Identified as ‘Proto-Shiva’ (Pashupati — Lord of Animals). Arguments: yogic posture prefigures later yoga; three faces suggest the three-eyed Shiva; horned headdress may prefigure the trident/crescent; Lord of Beasts is one of Shiva’s epithets. This is the standard UPSC-accepted description.
  • Asko Parpola: A buffalo-horned deity connected to Near Eastern traditions, not specifically Shiva.
  • K. N. Shastri / D. Srinivasan: May depict a female deity (Mother Goddess) given prominent breasts visible on the figure.
  • Gregory Possehl: A deity or priest in ritual pose, but labelling it ‘Shiva’ is anachronistic. The posture may simply be a tailor’s seat common in the Near East.
  • Steve Farmer / M. Witzel: A ‘Lord of the Beasts’ figure common in pan-Eurasian art, with no specific connection to Hinduism.

UPSC expects students to describe it as ‘Proto-Shiva’ while noting the scholarly debate.

Mother Goddess Figurines

Hundreds of terracotta female figurines found across all IVC sites. Typically 10–20 cm tall, handmade (not wheel-thrown), with fan-shaped headdress (sometimes with horn-like projections), pinched nose, applique ornaments (necklaces, earrings, girdles, armlets), and emphasized feminine features (prominent breasts, wide hips, detailed genitalia — suggesting a fertility cult). Found in domestic contexts (every house at Mohenjo-daro had at least one), suggesting household deities for fertility and domestic prosperity — possibly the earliest form of the later Devi/Shakti tradition.

Tree and Animal Worship

Seals show the pipal tree (Ficus religiosa) with a horned deity within its branches and a kneeling worshipper. Another seal shows seven female figures (the Saptamatrikas) with a pipal tree. The pipal later became the most sacred tree in Hinduism and Buddhism (Bodhi tree). The unicorn appears with a triple-branched ‘cult object’ (incense burner or sacred feeding trough). Humped bulls and buffalo appear in reverential poses — the bull (Nandi) later became Shiva’s vehicle, the buffalo was associated with Yama and Durga (Mahishasura-mardini).

Fire Worship

The seven fire altars at Kalibangan (brick-lined pits 40x40x30 cm in a row on a mud-brick platform, containing ash, charcoal, terracotta cakes, and burnt animal bones) are the most important evidence of ritual fire worship. B.B. Lal argued they are structurally similar to Vedic fire altars (garhapatya, ahavaniya, dakshinagni). Fire altars also found at Lothal (single brick platform) and Banawali (brick-lined pit). If these are indeed yajna altars, they suggest continuity between Harappan and Vedic religion.

Lingam and Yoni Worship

Conical/cylindrical stone objects (10–30 cm tall) from Kalibangan, Harappa, and Dholavira have been interpreted as lingas. Ring-shaped stones found with them as yonis. The evidence is suggestive but not conclusive — they could be architectural elements or game pieces. If correct, this is the earliest evidence of the linga cult central to Shaivism.

Sacred Tanks

The Great Bath at Mohenjo-daro is monumental evidence of ritual purification through water — the same snana (ritual bathing) that is central to Hinduism (Ganges bathing), Buddhism (monastic pools), and Jainism. The stepped reservoirs at Dholavira prefigure later Hindu temple kundas (like Modhera’s Surya Kund) by 3,000 years.

Burial Practices

Harappan burial practices varied across sites and periods, providing the most direct evidence of beliefs about death.

  • Extended burial (most common at Rakhigarhi, Harappa, Mohenjo-daro) — Body laid in rectangular pit on back, head north/north-east, legs straight, arms at sides or crossed on chest. Grave goods: pottery (with food/drink), personal ornaments (beads, shell bangles, copper mirrors, combs), tools (copper knives, axes, fishhooks). Suggests belief in an afterlife requiring possessions.
  • Fractional burial — Body exposed and bones collected in large urns (long bones, skull, pelvis). Typical of Cemetery H (Harappa) and Late Harappan phase. May represent different cultural tradition (possibly Indo-Aryan).
  • Pot burial — Infants placed in large jars (two pots joined at mouth), often under house floors. Also seen at Lothal and Dholavira. Continuity from Neolithic Mehrgarh.
  • Post-cremation burial — Cremated bones and ashes placed in urns and buried. Evidence from Mohenjo-daro and Harappa. The earliest evidence of cremation in India, later the dominant Hindu practice.
  • Coffin burial — One wooden deodar coffin covered with reed matting from Harappa (contained a child with 80+ beads of carnelian, agate, steatite). The only known coffin from the IVC.
  • Joint burial — At Lothal, male and female skeletons in one grave in an embracing position. Only 3–4 such burials found — possibly sati or simultaneous death.

Cemetery R-37 (Harappa, Wheeler 1946): 37 burials, uniformly oriented (head north) with consistent grave goods (3–6 pots + ornaments per burial). The uniformity suggests status was not flamboyantly displayed in death (unlike Egypt). The overall pattern suggests a relatively egalitarian approach to death compared to other Bronze Age civilizations.

The Indus Script — The Undeciphered Enigma

The Indus script remains undeciphered after a century of effort. It appears on: (1) ~2,000 steatite seals; (2) ~100 copper tablets (Mohenjo-daro); (3) ~2,000 pottery graffiti; (4) The Dholavira signboard (the only large-scale public inscription).

Sign inventory: 400–450 distinct pictographic signs (600+ variants including compounds). The count places it between an alphabet (20–30 signs) and a logographic system (thousands of signs) — likely logo-syllabic (like Sumerian cuneiform). Direction: right to left (signs crowd on the left; scribes ran out of space). The Dholavira signboard shows boustrophedon (alternating direction per line, like ox-ploughing). Length: average 4–5 signs; max 17 (Dholavira signboard). No long continuous text — no bilingual, no ‘Rosetta Stone’ — the single greatest obstacle to decipherment.

The Dravidian Hypothesis (most widely accepted):

  • Proponents: Asko Parpola (Helsinki, 50+ years of work), Iravatham Mahadevan (standard concordance of Indus signs), Yuri Knorozov (deciphered Maya), F.C. Southworth, Michael Witzel.
  • Evidence: (a) River and place names in the Indus region have Dravidian etymologies (Narmada, Tapti, Godavari, Krishna); (b) The script’s agglutinative structure (suffixes added to roots) matches Dravidian grammar; (c) The Rakhigarhi aDNA (2019) shows genetic continuity between IVC and modern Dravidian-speaking populations; (d) Parpola reads the ‘fish’ sign as min (fish/star in Dravidian) — ‘fish+roof’ = min+ vara (‘merchant’ = ‘star-people’), and the ‘mountain goat’ sign as kuri (‘sheep’ also ‘mark/sign’ used as a suffix).
  • Other hypotheses rejected: The Sanskrit hypothesis (no horse/chariot in IVC, no steppe ancestry in aDNA); the Brahmi link (1500-year gap with no intermediate forms); the non-language hypothesis (Farmer, Sproat, Witzel 2004 — criticized because inscriptions are too long and structured for mere clan symbols, with compound signs consistent with language).

The script remains undeciphered without a bilingual text, but the Dravidian hypothesis is the best working model. Partial readings like min for ‘fish’ are widely accepted in scholarly circles.

Decline of the IVC — Causes and Debates

The decline (c. 1900–1300 BCE) was a gradual, multi-causal process lasting 500–600 years: abandonment of cities, end of trade, loss of writing and weights, collapse of craft specialization, fragmentation into regional styles (the ‘Localization Era’).

  • Climate Change (Monsoon Weakening) — The Leading Theory — Palaeoclimatic evidence from Arabian Sea sediment cores (reduced upwelling), Meghalaya cave speleothems (Mawmluh — oxygen isotopes show severe drought at 2200–1900 BCE), and Himalayan lake cores (Kotla Dahar — desiccation from 2200 BCE) shows the Indian summer monsoon weakened by 30–40% between 2200–1900 BCE. Rain-fed agriculture became unsustainable. The Ghaggar-Hakra was a monsoon-fed river that dried up completely (ISRO satellite studies have traced its palaeochannel and confirmed it was not glacier-fed). Eastern settlements (Rakhigarhi, Kalibangan, Banawali, Ganweriwala) were abandoned first, followed later by the western Indus-dependent cities.
  • Tectonic Activity — The Indus plain is tectonically active (Indian plate pushes into Eurasia at 40–50 mm/year). Tectonic movements altered river courses, caused earthquakes (Kalibangan shows earthquake damage), and led to catastrophic floods (Mohenjo-daro has multiple flood deposits up to 2m thick). The Indus River is notorious for shifting course — shifts of 15 km in a single flood have been recorded historically.
  • Ecological Degradation — Deforestation for brick-making (300+ million bricks at Mohenjo-daro alone = 30–50 sq km of forest burned), overgrazing, and soil salination from intensive irrigation in a semi-arid climate progressively reduced agricultural yields.
  • Trade Disruption — The collapse of the Akkadian Empire (c. 2150 BCE, itself due to climate crisis — the ‘4.2 kiloyear event’) and the Ur-III period decline reduced external demand for Harappan luxury goods, weakening the urban economy and the elite class that organized the cities.
Historiographical Controversy: The Aryan Theory
The Aryan Invasion Theory (Mortimer Wheeler, 1946) based on: (a) 37 skeletons at Mohenjo-daro claimed as massacre victims; (b) the Rigveda’s ‘Indra destroying cities’; (c) absence of horses in IVC vs. their presence in Vedic culture — is now completely rejected. The skeletons show no weapon injuries — they died from disease, floods, or were secondary burials. No invading culture’s remains found at IVC destruction levels. The Rakhigarhi aDNA study (2019) proved the IVC population had no Steppe ancestry — Indo-Aryan speakers carrying Steppe ancestry arrived in India after the IVC collapse, not as invaders causing it. The Aryan Migration Theory (gradual, small-scale migration after the IVC decline, c. 2000–1500 BCE) is the current scholarly consensus. For UPSC, present the Migration Theory while noting the Invasion Theory has been abandoned.

Late Harappan Regional Cultures (1900–1300 BCE)

  • Cemetery H Culture (Punjab, Harappa) — Large burial urns with geometric and bird motifs, fractional burials. Persisted until c. 1300 BCE. Possibly represents interaction with incoming Indo-Aryan populations.
  • Jhukar Culture (Sindh, Chanhu-daro) — Black-on-slate-grey pottery, simpler architecture (mud-brick replacing fired-brick), disappearance of seals and script. Post-urban phase in Sindh.
  • Rangpur IIB / Lustrous Red Ware (Gujarat) — Fine red pottery with glossy slip, continued bead-making (less refined), rural agricultural settlements. The Daimabad bronzes (c. 1500 BCE) belong to this final phase.

Legacy of the Indus Valley Civilization

The IVC’s influence on later Indian culture is profound, though only recognized since the 20th century.

  • Weights — The Indian tola (11.6g) and ratti (0.12g, seed of Abrus precatorius used by goldsmiths for millennia) derive from the Indus binary-decimal system (the ratti is ~1/7th of the Indus base unit 0.856g).
  • Bullock cart design — The hooded bullock cart with solid wooden wheels is virtually unchanged from Harappan terracotta models — 4,000 years of continuous design.
  • Bath culture — Every Harappan house had a bathroom with drainage, establishing a cultural value persisting today. Stepwells like Rani ki Vav trace their ancestry to Dholavira’s reservoirs.
  • Cotton — India was the first region to cultivate cotton, and Indian textiles dominated global trade for over 3,000 years — the IVC foundation.
  • Goddess worship (Shakti) — The Mother Goddess figurines are the earliest evidence of Devi worship, today one of the most vibrant Hindu traditions (Durga, Kali, Lakshmi, village goddesses).
  • Yoga — The Pashupati seal provides the earliest depiction of a yogic posture, predating Patanjali’s Yoga Sutras by 2,500 years.
  • Ayurveda — Neem, turmeric, and ginger found at IVC sites; trepanation (skull drilling) and drilled teeth from Neolithic Mehrgarh (7000 BCE) — the foundation of Ayurveda.
  • Tree worship — The pipal tree, shown with a deity on Harappan seals, remains the most sacred tree in India (Bodhi tree of Buddhism, village pipal platforms for community worship).
  • Lingam worship — Conical stone objects from Kalibangan/Harappa, if correctly identified, trace Shiva-linga worship to the IVC.
  • Embroidery traditions — The elaborate headdresses and decorated garments on terracotta figurines may be the earliest evidence of embroidered textile traditions (phulkari, chikankari, kantha).
  INDUS VALLEY CIVILIZATION — KEY FACTS
  
  TIME SPAN:    Pre-Harappan (7000-2600 BCE) | Mature (2600-1900 BCE) | Late (1900-1300 BCE)
  EXTENT:       1.5 million sq km | 1,000+ sites
  EASTERNMOST:  Alamgirpur (Meerut, UP)
  WESTERNMOST:  Sutkagendor (Makran coast, Pakistan)
  NORTHERNMOST: Shortughai (Badakhshan, Afghanistan)
  SOUTHERNMOST: Daimabad (Ahmadnagar, Maharashtra)
  
  KEY SITES IN INDIA: Dholavira, Lothal, Rakhigarhi, Kalibangan, Banawali, Alamgirpur, Daimabad, Surkotada
  POPULATION:        Estimated 1-5 million (peak)
  MAJOR DISCOVERIES: 1921 (Sahni/Harappa), 1922 (Banerjee/Mohenjo-daro), 1924 (Marshall/public), 1967 (Dholavira), 2019 (Rakhigarhi aDNA)
  STILL UNDECIPHERED: Indus Script (400+ signs, 4,000+ inscriptions)
Why IVC Matters for UPSC
Every UPSC GS-I paper has 1–3 IVC questions. Key areas: (1) Major sites and distinctive features (Dholavira, Rakhigarhi, Lothal, Kalibangan); (2) Chronology and the Saraswati debate; (3) Pashupati seal and Harappan religion debate; (4) Indus script and the Dravidian hypothesis; (5) Decline theories (climate change, Aryan Migration vs. rejected Invasion Theory); (6) Rakhigarhi aDNA evidence for Indo-Aryan debate. Common mistakes: calling the script ‘deciphered,’ stating Pashupati seal definitively shows ‘Shiva,’ claiming the IVC was ‘peaceful’ (it had fortifications and weapons), invoking the ‘Aryan Invasion’ as a cause of decline.

3. Vedic Age (1500-600 BCE)

The Vedic Age is the period between the arrival of Indo-Aryan speakers in the Indian subcontinent and the emergence of urbanisation in the Gangetic plain. Our knowledge comes primarily from Vedic literature. The period is divided into Early Vedic (Rigvedic) Period (c. 1500�1000 BCE) and Later Vedic Period (c. 1000�600 BCE). The Vedic age is the formative period of classical Hinduism � the caste system, foundational texts, and philosophical ideas that shaped Indian civilization. The current scholarly consensus is the Aryan Migration Theory.

The Aryan Debate � Major Theories

The 'Aryan Question' is the most contentious historiographical issue in Indian history � it touches on questions of national identity, linguistic origins, and the interpretation of ancient texts. Four major scholarly positions exist, each with distinct evidence bases and political implications. The UPSC syllabus expects candidates to understand all positions and the nature of the evidence for each.

Aryan Invasion Theory (AIT)

Proposed by Max M�ller (19th c.) and championed by Mortimer Wheeler (1940s), AIT held that a light-skinned 'Aryan race' invaded India c. 1500 BCE, conquering Dravidian inhabitants and destroying the Indus cities. Wheeler cited the 'massacre at Mohenjo-daro' (37 skeletons in upper levels) as evidence. Linguistic analysis of the Indo-European family (Sanskrit, Greek, Latin, Persian, Germanic, Celtic sharing a common ancestor � Proto-Indo-European) was the theoretical backbone. Max M�ller dated the Rigveda to c. 1200�1000 BCE. AIT was decisively rejected: the skeletons were from different levels, most showed no violence, the Rigveda has no memory of migration into India (it considers the Punjab the original homeland), and the theory was influenced by 19th-century racial ideologies.

Aryan Migration Theory (AMT) � Current Consensus

AMT (Steppe/Kurgan hypothesis) posits small-scale, gradual migration of Indo-European speakers from the Pontic-Caspian steppe into South Asia c. 2000�1500 BCE, bringing Indo-Aryan languages, the horse, spoke-wheeled chariot, and Vedic religious elements (Dyaus Pita = Greek Zeus = Roman Jupiter; Soma = Iranian Haoma). Key evidence: (1) Linguistic: shared Indo-European vocabulary for horses, chariots, pastoralism. (2) Archaeological: the Andronovo culture (c. 2000�1400 BCE) and the Bactria-Margiana Archaeological Complex (BMAC, c. 2300�1700 BCE) in Turkmenistan/Uzbekistan show horse-keeping, chariots, and Soma (Ephedra) use. BMAC material appears in the Swat Valley (Gandhara Grave Culture, c. 1700�1400 BCE) after c. 1900 BCE. The BMAC worshipped a goddess, used enclosed fire altars similar to Vedic yajna-vedi, and Ephedra (likely Soma) has been found in BMAC vessels at Togolok 21 (c. 2000 BCE). (3) Genetic: ancient DNA (Reich et al., 2017�2024) shows Central Asian Steppe ancestry (Ancestral North Indian � ANI) appearing in South Asia after c. 2000 BCE. Modern Indians are ANI + ASI (Ancestral South Indian) admixture. Steppe ancestry correlates with Y-haplogroup R1a-Z93 (40�60% in North Indian Brahmins, 10�20% in Dravidian South India, near 0% in isolated tribes), found in ancient Steppe DNA from Andronovo and Sintashta (c. 2100 BCE), confirming the Steppe origin.

Out of India Theory (OIT)

OIT argues Indo-European languages originated in India and spread outward. Proponents: Shrikant Talageri (The Rigveda: A Historical Analysis), David Frawley, B.B. Lal. Claims: Rigveda dates to c. 4000�3000 BCE; the Indus Valley was Vedic Aryan (Ghaggar-Hakra as Saraswati); horses and chariots existed in India before 2000 BCE (contested � 'horse' bones from Surkotada/Harappa are identified by zooarchaeologists as Equus hemionus khur, the wild ass). OIT is not accepted by mainstream scholarship � the linguistic, genetic, and archaeological evidence overwhelmingly supports Steppe origin. The 'astronomical dating' of the Rigveda (equinox positions) is circular (mythological rather than precise). However, the identification of the Ghaggar-Hakra as the Vedic Saraswati is now universally accepted (the dating implications remain debated).

The Horse and Chariot Argument

The Rigveda prominently describes the horse (ashva) and spoke-wheeled chariot (ratha), while the Indus Valley has yielded almost no evidence of either (contested 'horse' bones identified as ass/onager; the Daimabad copper model c. 1800 BCE is a solid-wheeled cart, not a spoked chariot). The earliest spoked chariots appear archaeologically in the Andronovo/Sintashta culture (c. 2100�1800 BCE, Urals/Central Asia) � the earliest chariot burials in the world. The horse-chariot complex remains a strong argument for Steppe migration.

Adi Dravida Theory

Holds that the Indus Valley was Dravidian-speaking and Aryans were late migrants who subjugated Dravidians. Origin: Bishop Caldwell (1856), later the Dravidian political movement (Periyar, DMK). Key arguments: Dravidian languages are autochthonous (no demonstrable external relations); the Indus script likely encodes a Dravidian language (Parpola, Mahadevan, Knorozov � the dominant view among epigraphists); the Rigveda contains Dravidian loanwords (phala, nira, kunda, mayura, karpasa, anna); the dasa likely refers to the indigenous population. Criticised for politicising history and overstating the 'race war' interpretation � 'dasa' in the Rigveda often refers to demonic enemies, not a specific ethnic group. The Dravidian substratum in Vedic Sanskrit is, however, accepted by mainstream scholarship.

Linguistic Palaeontology � The Indo-European Vocabulary

Linguistic palaeontology reconstructs the culture of the Proto-Indo-European (PIE) speakers by analysing shared vocabulary across Indo-European languages. The reconstructed PIE vocabulary includes words for: snow and winter (sneigwh-, gheim-), horse (ekwo- � Sanskrit ashva, Latin equus, Greek hippos), wheel (kweklo- � Sanskrit chakra, Greek kyklos, English wheel), axle (aks- � Sanskrit aksha, Latin axis), yoke (yug- � Sanskrit yoga, Latin iugum), copper/bronze (ayos- � Sanskrit ayas, Latin aes), cattle (gwow- � Sanskrit go, Latin bos, English cow), sheep (owi- � Sanskrit avi, Latin ovis), dog (kwon- � Sanskrit shvan, Greek kyon), and bee/honey (melit- � Sanskrit madhu, Greek meli, English mead). Importantly, there are no reconstructed PIE words for 'rice', 'tropical crops', 'elephant', 'tiger', 'monkey', 'coconut', or cotton � all things native to South Asia. The PIE homeland is therefore reconstructed as a temperate, inland region with horses, cattle, and wheeled vehicles � the Pontic-Caspian steppe, not India. Conversely, the Rigvedic vocabulary includes Dravidian loanwords for items native to India (phala � fruit, nira � water, mayura � peacock, karpasa � cotton, anna � rice), confirming that Indo-Aryan speakers encountered a Dravidian population when they entered India. This linguistic evidence is considered among the strongest arguments against OIT.

Genetic Evidence � Steppe Ancestry in India

The Reich Lab (2017�2024) traced three migrations into South Asia using ancient DNA: (1) Out-of-Africa migrants (c. 65,000 ya) � the ASI component. (2) Iranian farmers (c. 7000�6000 BCE) � agriculture, the other ASI component. (3) Eurasian Steppe migration (c. 2000�1500 BCE) � the ANI component, found at high levels in North Indian upper castes (30�50%) and decreasing southward (5�10% in Tamil Brahmins, ~0% in Paniya tribals). This correlates with Y-haplogroup R1a-Z93, found in Andronovo/Sintashta aDNA � confirming Steppe origin. A 2024 study by the Reich Lab and the Birbal Sahni Institute specifically analysed DNA from the Gandhara Grave Culture (Swat Valley, c. 1400�800 BCE) and confirmed the presence of Steppe ancestry in these early Indo-Aryan-associated populations. Genes are not identical to language or culture, but the correlation between Steppe ancestry, Indo-Aryan language distribution, and Vedic period timing strongly supports AMT.

Vedic Literature � Complete Classification

CategoryKey TextsContent and Significance
SamhitasRigveda, Samaveda, Yajurveda (Shukla and Krishna), AtharvavedaCore collections of hymns and mantras. The earliest layer (c. 1500�1000 BCE). Rigveda is the oldest Indo-European text (1028 hymns).
BrahmanasAitareya (Rigveda), Shatapatha (Yajurveda), Taittiriya (Yajurveda), Gopatha (Atharvaveda), Panchavimsha (Samaveda)Prose texts on ritual procedure. Shatapatha is the most important � contains the earliest reference to zero as a positional number.
AranyakasPart of each Veda � Aitareya, Shankhayana, Taittiriya, Brihad'Forest texts' � esoteric interpretations bridging Brahmanas and Upanishads.
Upanishads108 total, 13 principal: Isha, Kena, Katha, Prashna, Mundaka, Mandukya, Taittiriya, Aitareya, Chandogya, Brihadaranyaka, Shvetashvatara, Kaushitaki, MaitriPhilosophical treatises on Brahman, Atman, and Moksha � the foundation of Vedanta.
VedangasShiksha, Kalpa, Vyakarana, Nirukta, Chandas, JyotishaSix auxiliary sciences for correct Vedic understanding.
UpavedasAyurveda (medicine), Dhanurveda (military), Gandharvaveda (music), Sthapatyaveda (architecture)Applied knowledge systems from the Vedic tradition.

The Four Vedas � Detailed Analysis with Recensions (Shakhas)

1. Rigveda (c. 1500�1000 BCE) � Oldest and most important. Composed in the Punjab region. Contains 1028 hymns (suktas) divided into 10 books (Mandalas), 10,600 verses. Books II�VII are the oldest ('family books' attributed to Gritsamada, Vishwamitra, Vamadeva, Atri, Vasishtha, Bharadvaja). Book IX: dedicated to Soma. Books I and X are the latest (c. 1000 BCE). The Rigveda had 21 shakhas; only 5 survive: Shakala (the only complete recension � basis of all modern editions, edited by Max M�ller 1849�1874), Ashtaka, Baskala, Khandikaya, Shankhayana. The Purusha Sukta (X.90) describes the cosmic man whose body created the four varnas � a later interpolation (c. 1000�800 BCE). The Nasadiya Sukta (X.129) is a remarkable agnostic cosmogonic hymn questioning creation itself: 'The gods themselves are later than this creation, so who knows whence it arose?'

2. Samaveda � 'Veda of Melodies'. 1549 verses, most from the Rigveda (only 75 original), arranged by musical notation. 1000+ shakhas originally; 3 survive: Kauthuma, Ranayaniya, Jaiminiya. The seven notes (Sa, Re, Ga, Ma, Pa, Dha, Ni) are described. Foundation of Indian classical music (Hindustani and Carnatic traditions). The Udgatri priest who chants it was the highest-paid.

3. Yajurveda � 'Veda of Sacrificial Formulas'. Two recensions: Shukla (White) (Vajasaneyi Samhita, 40 chapters � contains Isha Upanishad as final chapter; only Madhyandina and Kanva recensions survive) and Krishna (Black) (Taittiriya Samhita, intermixed prose and mantra; 86 shakhas known, 4 survive: Taittiriya, Maitrayani, Kathaka, Kapishthala). Contains the Shiva Sankalpa Sukta.

4. Atharvaveda � 'Veda of Magical Formulas'. 20 books, 731 hymns, 6000 verses. Nine shakhas; only Shaunaka and Paippalada survive. Contains the Prithvi Sukta (hymn to Earth as a living goddess) and Skambha Sukta (cosmic pillar). Ayurveda traces its origins here � earliest references to the three doshas (vata, pitta, kapha). First appearance of the term 'Yoga'.

The Brahmanas

  • Aitareya Brahmana (Rigveda) � Story of Harishchandra; lists tribes and regions; earliest reference to Kuru and Panchala janapadas.
  • Shatapatha Brahmana (Yajurveda) � The most important Brahmana. Contains: Manu and the flood (earliest Indian flood myth); construction of fire altars with 10,800 bricks (earliest complex geometry); the concept of zero ('puchham'); geographical details of early janapadas; the eastward shift of Vedic culture.
  • Taittiriya Brahmana (Yajurveda) � Supplements the Taittiriya Samhita.
  • Panchavimsha Brahmana (Samaveda) � 25 books on ritual music and the mystical significance of numbers.
  • Gopatha Brahmana (Atharvaveda) � Late composition (c. 600�500 BCE), shows Upanishadic influence.

The Upanishads � The 13 Principal Mukhya Upanishads

The word 'Upanishad' means 'to sit down near' (a teacher). 108 are enumerated traditionally; 13 are principal (commented upon by Shankaracharya):

  • Isha (Yajurveda) � 18 verses. The Lord (Isha) pervades all. 'Perform prescribed actions; live a hundred years.'
  • Kena (Samaveda) � 'By whom impelled?' Brahman transcends the senses.
  • Katha (Yajurveda) � Nachiketa and Yama. Famous lines: 'Uttishthata, Jagrata' (Arise, awake) and 'The sharp edge of a razor.'
  • Prashna (Atharvaveda) � Six questions on creation and reality answered by Pippalada.
  • Mundaka (Atharvaveda) � Para vidya (higher knowledge) vs apara vidya (lower knowledge). 'As the spider emits and withdraws its web.'
  • Mandukya (Atharvaveda) � 12 verses on AUM. Four states of consciousness: waking, dreaming, deep sleep, turiya. Foundation of Advaita Vedanta (Gaudapada's commentary).
  • Taittiriya (Yajurveda) � Brahman as Sat-Chit-Ananda. Five koshas (sheaths): annamaya, pranamaya, manomaya, vijnanamaya, anandamaya.
  • Aitareya (Rigveda) � Creation of the world from the Self. Mahavakya: 'Prajnanam Brahma.'
  • Chandogya (Samaveda) � Longest and most important. Uddalaka's teaching to Shvetaketu through 9 examples (the banyan seed, salt in water). Mahavakya: 'Tat Tvam Asi.' Contains Satyakama Jabala story and Pravahana Jaivali's five-fire doctrine.
  • Brihadaranyaka (Yajurveda) � Longest Upanishad. Yajnavalkya-Maitreyi dialogue, Yajnavalkya-Gargi debate at Janaka's court. Mahavakya: 'Aham Brahmasmi.'
  • Shvetashvatara (Yajurveda) � Theistic. Rudra-Shiva as Supreme Lord (Ishvara). Yoga, bhakti, grace.
  • Kaushitaki (Rigveda) � Path of the soul after death.
  • Maitri (Yajurveda) � Late (c. 300 BCE�200 CE). Three gunas (sattva, rajas, tamas). Yoga practices.

The Vedangas

  • Shiksha (Phonetics) � Pratishakhyas for each Veda. Science of correct pronunciation and accent (udatta, anudatta, svarita).
  • Kalpa (Ritual Sutras) � Largest Vedanga. Shrauta Sutras (public sacrifices), Grihya Sutras (domestic rites), Dharma Sutras (law). Baudhayana, Apastamba, Gautama, Vasishtha Dharmasutras (c. 600�300 BCE) � the first systematic exposition of varna and ashrama.
  • Vyakarana (Grammar) � Panini's Ashtadhyayi (c. 500 BCE), 4,000 sutras in 8 chapters. The most complete grammar of any ancient language � a precursor to generative grammar (cited by Chomsky).
  • Nirukta (Etymology) � Yaska's Nirukta (c. 600�500 BCE), the world's first etymological dictionary.
  • Chandas (Metre) � Pingala's Chandashastra (c. 300�200 BCE) analyses Vedic metres using binary notation � the earliest binary numeral system (laghu/guru = 0/1). Key metres: Gayatri (24 syllables), Trishtubh (44), Jagati (48), Anushtubh (32).
  • Jyotisha (Astronomy) � Vedanga Jyotisha of Lagadha, the oldest Indian astronomical text. Five-year yuga cycle, 27 nakshatras.

The Upavedas and Epics

Upavedas: Ayurveda (medicine � Charaka Samhita c. 300 BCE, Sushruta Samhita c. 200 BCE); Dhanurveda (military); Gandharvaveda (music � Bharata's Natyashastra, c. 200 BCE�200 CE); Sthapatyaveda (architecture � Vastu Shastra). The Epics � Ramayana (Valmiki, c. 500�100 BCE) and Mahabharata (Vyasa, c. 400 BCE�400 CE, 100,000 verses � the longest epic poem in the world, seven times the Iliad+Odyssey) � are classified as Itihasa (history) and are often called the 'Fifth Veda'. The Mahabharata is set in the Kuru kingdom of Hastinapur in the Later Vedic period.

Early Vedic (Rigvedic) Period (1500�1000 BCE)

Geography � The Sapta-Sindhu and the Saraswati River

The Rigvedic people inhabited the Sapta-Sindhu (Land of Seven Rivers): five Punjab rivers (Jhelum-Vitasta, Chenab-Asikni, Ravi-Parushni, Beas-Vipasa, Sutlej-Sutudri) plus the Indus (Sindhu) and the Saraswati. The Saraswati is the most frequently mentioned river (50+ references) � described as 'flowing from the mountains to the sea' (VII.95.2). Geological/ISRO satellite studies confirm the Ghaggar-Hakra was a perennial river until c. 2500 BCE, fed by the Sutlej and Yamuna, and dried to a seasonal stream by c. 1900 BCE due to tectonic shifts and monsoon decline. The Nadi Sukta (X.75) lists rivers east to west: Ganga, Yamuna, Saraswati, Sutlej, Ravi, Chenab, Jhelum, Indus. The Ganga is mentioned once, Yamuna three times � indicating Vedic people had not yet moved into the Gangetic plain. The Rigvedic world stretched from eastern Afghanistan (Kubha-Kabul, Gomati-Gomal, Krumu-Kurram rivers) to the upper Gangetic plain.

Polity � The Battle of Ten Kings (Dasarajna)

The basic political unit was kula (family) → grama (village under gramani) → vis (clan under vispati) → jana (tribe) → rashtra (kingdom). The king (rajan) was a tribal chieftain elected by the tribe, removable for incompetence. Two assemblies checked his power: Sabha (council of elders, judicial functions in the sabhavati hall) and Samiti (general assembly deciding war/peace, electing the king). The vidatha was the oldest folk assembly for land distribution and religious functions. Women attended both assemblies. No standing army or bureaucracy.

The Battle of Ten Kings (Dasarajna) � the most important political event in the Rigveda (VII.18, VII.33, VII.83) � was fought on the Parushni (Ravi) River between King Sudas of the Bharata clan and a confederation of ten tribes: the Purus (most powerful), Yadus, Turvashas, Anus, Druhyus, Alinas, Bhalanas, Pakhtas (possibly ancestors of Pashtuns), Bhrigus, and Shimyus. Sudas won decisively with the aid of his priest Vasishtha, establishing Bharata dominance. The merger of the Bharata and Puru lineages later produced the Kuru tribe � the dominant power of the Later Vedic period. India's ancient name, Bharatavarsha, derives from the Bharatas.

Society � Tribes, Varna, Family, Women

Tribes: Major Rigvedic tribes include Bharatas, Purus, Yadus, Turvashas, Anus, Druhyus, Alinas, Bhalanas, Pakhtas. The Panis were wealthy merchants hostile to the Vedic people. The Dasas/Dasyus were non-Aryan peoples described as dark-skinned � the word 'dasa' later came to mean 'slave'. However, some Dasa chiefs (Balbutha, Taruksha) are praised as generous donors, indicating a complex relationship.

Varna: The varna system was nascent � based on occupation, not birth. The Purusha Sukta (X.90) mentions four varnas from the cosmic man's body but is widely accepted as a later interpolation (c. 1000�800 BCE). In the early Rigveda, 'varna' (literally 'colour') probably referred to the Arya-Dasa contrast, not the four-fold division. Inter-varna mobility was possible � Kavasha (son of an outcast woman) composed hymns; King Vishwamitra became a Brahmarishi through penance.

Family: Patriarchal and joint (kula headed by kulapa). Monogamy normal; polygyny among chiefs; polyandry not mentioned. Sons desired for funeral rites (shraddha). The gotra system (exogamous lineage traced to a rishi) was just emerging � only Brahmins initially.

Women: Relatively high status � could participate in Sabha/Samiti, choose husbands (svayamvara), remarry if widowed (niyoga � levirate), undergo upanayana (Vedic initiation), and compose hymns. Notable women: Apala (cured by Indra, composed VIII.91); Ghosha (daughter of Kakshivan, composed X.39�40 invoking the Ashvins for cure); Lopamudra (wife of Rishi Agastya, composed I.179 on marital/spiritual balance); Visvavara (female rishi). Gargi Vachaknavi challenged Yajnavalkya at Janaka's court (Brihadaranyaka Upanishad). Maitreyi chose spiritual knowledge over wealth. Child marriage was absent � girls married only after puberty.

Economy � Pastoral to Agricultural

Pastoral-cum-agricultural. Cattle were the primary wealth and standard of value � elaborate cattle raids (gavishthi, meaning 'desire for cows' = 'war'). Cow called aghnya ('not to be killed'). A wealthy man was gomat (possessor of cows). Barley (yava) the main crop; no rice or wheat yet. Copper (ayas) the main metal; iron (krishna ayas) appears only in the Atharvaveda. Land belonged to the tribe collectively. No coined money. Trade existed but limited � the Panis were hostile merchant-hoarders.

Religion � 33 Devas, Yajnas, No Idol Worship

Nature worship � 33 devas (11 heaven, 11 earth, 11 atmosphere). No temples, no idolatry, no image worship. The Rigvedic worldview was based on rita (cosmic order, truth) � a universal, impersonal law that governed both gods and humans. Maintaining rita through sacrifice (yajna) was the highest religious duty.

  • Indra (250 hymns � most) � Warrior-rain god, king of the gods, wielder of vajra (thunderbolt), slayer of Vritra (cosmic serpent releasing the monsoon). Indra's myth represents the thunderstorm breaking the drought. He is both a nature god (rain) and a war god (leading Aryans to victory).
  • Agni (200 hymns) � Fire god, priest-messenger between humans and gods, 'mouth of the gods'. Agni is the immortal guest in every home, the purifier, the witness to all actions. Without him, no sacrifice can reach the heavens.
  • Varuna � Guardian of rita (cosmic law). The most morally elevated deity � omniscient, all-seeing ('he knows the path of birds in the sky'), punisher of sin. Varuna's moral seriousness � he forgives sins to those who pray sincerely � gives the Rigveda a dimension of personal piety rare in ancient polytheism. The concept of rita is the earliest Indian formulation of universal law, binding even the gods.
  • Mitra � God of contract, friendship, harmony. Closely associated with Varuna (invoked as Mitra-Varuna). Represents the social aspect of cosmic law.
  • Ashvins � Divine twin horsemen, sons of the sun (Surya). Healers, rescuers from danger (restore sight, heal the lame, save shipwrecked sailors). Represent the dawn � the first rays of light.
  • Soma � Deified plant/ritual drink. 120 hymns (entire Book IX). Described as a divine intoxicant giving immortality, warriors invincibility, poets inspiration. Pressed between stones, filtered through sheep's wool, mixed with milk and honey. Botanical identity debated: Ephedra (strongest candidate � found in BMAC vessels at Togolok 21, c. 2000 BCE, and still used in Zoroastrian Haoma ritual), Amanita muscaria (hallucinogenic mushroom), Cannabis sativa, Peganum harmala (Syrian rue).
  • Other gods: Ushas (dawn goddess, 20 hymns � described as a young woman in a red chariot driving away darkness), Maruts (storm gods, companions of Indra, 180+), Vayu (wind god), Parjanya (rain god), Prithvi (earth goddess), Dyaus Pita (sky father � cognate with Greek Zeus, Roman Jupiter, Germanic Tyr/Tiu), Surya (sun god), Savitar (the impeller, god of the Gayatri Mantra), Rudra (fierce archer god, father of the Maruts, precursor to Shiva � minor in the Rigveda but given supreme status in the Shvetashvatara Upanishad).

Ritual: The central practice was yajna (sacrifice) before an open-air fire altar (vedi) with three fire pits: garhapatya (domestic fire, west), ahavaniya (offering fire, east), and dakshina (southern fire). Offerings of ghee, grain, animals (cattle, goats, horses), and Soma into the fire with mantra recitation. Four priestly roles: Hotri (Rigveda reciter, invokes the gods), Udgatri (Samaveda chanter, the highest-paid), Adhvaryu (manual acts of sacrifice, recites Yajurveda), Brahma (supervising priest, Atharvaveda). The Soma sacrifice was the most elaborate (9�16 priests). The Agnihotra (daily milk offering at sunrise/sunset) and Darsha-Purnamasa (new/full moon sacrifices) were basic obligations. No concept of karma or samsara in the Rigveda � the afterlife was the realm of Yama (the first mortal who chose death to show others the way). Heaven (svarga) for the virtuous who performed sacrifices; hell (naraka) for the wicked. The Nasadiya Sukta (X.129) is a remarkable agnostic cosmogonic hymn: 'There was neither non-existence nor existence... Who really knows? The gods themselves are later than this creation, so who knows whence it arose?'

The Gayatri Mantra � Rigveda III.62.10
Composed by the sage Vishwamitra, dedicated to Savitar (the sun god). The most sacred verse in Hinduism, chanted daily by Brahmins for millennia:

'Om. Bhur Bhuvah Svah. Tat Savitur Varenyam, Bhargo Devasya Dhimahi, Dhiyo Yo Nah Prachodayat.'

'Let us meditate on the most excellent effulgence of that divine Sun, who shall inspire our intellect.'

The three Vyahritis (Bhur � Earth, Bhuvah � Atmosphere, Svah � Heaven) encapsulate the entire Vedic cosmos.

Later Vedic Period (1000�600 BCE)

Geography � The Eastward Shift to the Gangetic Plain

The centre shifted from Sapta-Sindhu to the Ganga-Yamuna Doab � the Kuru-Panchala region (Haryana/Western UP). Reasons: (1) Drying of the Saraswati (c. 1900 BCE). (2) Introduction of iron (krishna ayas) c. 1000 BCE enabled clearing Gangetic forests. (3) Higher rainfall and fertile alluvial soil supported rice cultivation. The Shatapatha Brahmana and Aitareya Brahmana list janapadas: Kuru (Hastinapur), Panchala (Ahichchhatra/Kampilya), Kosala (Ayodhya), Videha (Mithila � King Janaka's court), Kashi (Varanasi), Magadha (south Bihar), Matsya (Jaipur), Avanti (Ujjain), Gandhara (Taxila).

Polity � Kingship Absolute, Rajasuya-Ashvamedha-Vajapeya

Kingship became hereditary and absolute. The Sabha and Samiti declined sharply � the Sabha became a restricted judicial council of Brahmin and Kshatriya nobles, and the Samiti lost its power to elect the king. The king (now called samrat � 'sovereign', or rajadhiraja � 'king of kings') claimed divine status and was addressed as 'deva' (god) in the Shatapatha Brahmana. The earlier tribal assemblies were replaced by a rudimentary court (the sabha now a royal council) and a bureaucracy (the ratnins � 'jewel-holders', 12 court functionaries including the purohita � chief priest, senani � army commander, gramani � village head, and sangrahitri � treasurer).

Three great sacrifices legitimised and enhanced royal power: Rajasuya � a year-long consecration ceremony involving anointment (abhiseka), a symbolic chariot race (the king had to win), a rigged dice game (the king had to win), the royal milking of a cow, and massive gifts to Brahmins. The king declared: 'I am the king! I am the lord! I am the sovereign!' Ashvamedha � the horse sacrifice, the most potent symbol of imperial sovereignty. A consecrated stallion roamed free for a year, accompanied by 400 warriors; any territory it crossed unchallenged became the king's domain (if challenged, war ensued). The climax involved the horse's sacrifice and the queen performing a ritual with the dead horse (interpreted as a fertility rite). Only the most powerful kings could perform it. Vajapeya � 'the drink of strength', a chariot race with ritual Soma drinking, intermediate in prestige between Rajasuya and Ashvamedha.

The janapadas (territorial polities, literally 'foothold of the tribe') now replaced the tribal janas. The Shatapatha Brahmana and Aitareya Brahmana list the major janapadas: Kuru (Hastinapur, the epicentre of Later Vedic culture), Panchala (Ahichchhatra/Kampilya), Kosala (Ayodhya), Videha (Mithila, Janaka's kingdom), Kashi (Varanasi), Magadha (south Bihar, emerging as a rival to Kuru orthodoxy), Matsya (Viratnagar/Jaipur), Avanti (Ujjain), and Gandhara (Taxila, which preserved older Vedic traditions). These would, by c. 600 BCE, evolve into the 16 Mahajanapadas of the Buddhist texts.

Society � Birth-Based Varna, Ashrama System, Gotra, Women's Decline

Varna: The varna system became rigid and birth-based. The Purusha Sukta provided divine sanction � Brahmins from the mouth, Kshatriyas from the arms, Vaishyas from the thighs, Shudras from the feet (fixed hierarchy). The Dharmasutras codified svadharma (specific duties), endogamy, and the exclusion of Shudras from Vedic study. Chandalas (untouchables) emerged at the bottom � performing polluting tasks, forced to live outside villages and announce their approach.

Ashrama System: Four stages of life: (1) Brahmacharya (student celibate, gurukula); (2) Grihastha (householder � the most important, supporting all other stages, pursuing artha and kama within dharma); (3) Vanaprastha (forest-dweller, austerity and meditation); (4) Sannyasa (renouncer, ochre robes, wandering bhikshu, focused on moksha).

Gotra and Marriage: Gotra system fixed � Brahmins claimed descent from 8 rishis (Vasishtha, Bhrigu, Kashyapa, Gautama, Atri, Vishwamitra, Jamadagni, Bharadvaja). Rules: gotra exogamy, varna endogamy. Anuloma marriage (man of higher varna + woman of lower) permitted but discouraged. Pratiloma marriage (man of lower varna + woman of higher) strictly forbidden. 16 samskaras marked the life cycle from conception (garbhadhana) to cremation (antyeshti).

Women's Status Declines Sharply: Excluded from Sabha/Samiti, denied upanayana (Vedic initiation), confined to domestic roles. The Aitareya Brahmana states: 'A daughter is a source of misery.' Child marriage became common (Grihya Sutras specify marriage before puberty). Widow remarriage declined and was banned. Sati appears for the first time (Vishnu Dharmasutra, Mahabharata). Women classified with children and Shudras in legal capacity � perpetually under male guardianship.

Economy � Iron, Rice, Coins, Guilds, NBPW

Iron (krishna ayas) transformed agriculture � the iron ploughshare (phala) enabled cultivation of heavy Gangetic clay soils. Rice (vrihi) became the staple, replacing barley. Panini (c. 500 BCE) mentions 8 types of rice, 6 types of barley. The king claimed a share (bhaga) � origin of land revenue (1/6th�1/12th). Coined money: nishka (gold ornament/coin) and shatamana (100 ratis, ~11.5g silver) appear in the Brahmanas � pre-coinage monetary stage. Guilds (shreni) emerged � craft specialists organised by trade. Northern Black Polished Ware (NBPW) � fine glossy pottery marking the transition to the Second Urbanisation � appears c. 700�600 BCE.

Religion � Prajapati, Rudra-Shiva, Vishnu, Karma-Samsara Emerges

The elaborate sacrificial cult peaked � and became inaccessible to common people (Brahmin monopoly on offerings). New deities: Prajapati (creator god, supreme in the Brahmanas � creates the universe through tapas and enters into all beings). Rudra-Shiva � the Shvetashvatara Upanishad identifies him as the Supreme Lord (Ishvara). Vishnu � now a major god (his three strides = the three worlds). Pushan � pastoral god, becomes god of the shudras.

The doctrines of karma and rebirth appear for the first time in the Upanishads. The Brihadaranyaka Upanishad: 'As a caterpillar reaches out to another blade, so the soul reaches out to another body.' The Chandogya Upanishad describes the five-fire doctrine (Pravahana Jaivali's teaching on the path of the soul determining the quality of rebirth). The ascetic ideal (renunciation for liberation) emerged as a reaction to Brahmanical ritualism � the Aranyakas and Upanishads teach that liberation comes through knowledge (jnana) and renunciation (vairagya), not sacrifice. The ashrama system precisely accommodated the tension between householder and ascetic ideals.

Philosophy � The Upanishadic Revolution

Key Upanishadic Thinkers and Dialogues

Yajnavalkya and Maitreyi (Brihadaranyaka) � The most famous philosophical dialogue in Indian history. Maitreyi asked: 'If I had the whole world filled with wealth, would it make me immortal?' Yajnavalkya replied: 'No. The life of the wealthy is just a life.' He taught her: 'It is not for the sake of the husband that the husband is dear, but for the sake of the Self. When the Self is seen, heard, and known, then all this is known.'

Yajnavalkya and Gargi (Brihadaranyaka) � At King Janaka's court, Gargi Vachaknavi engaged Yajnavalkya in a dialectical inquiry asking 'on what is each element woven?' through successive layers of reality until he replied: 'All this world is woven on the imperishable Brahman.' This illustrates the method of 'neti, neti' (not this, not this).

Uddalaka Aruni and Shvetaketu (Chandogya) � Uddalaka taught his son Shvetaketu the nature of Brahman through 9 examples (upamana): (1) The Clay and the Pot � by knowing clay, all clay objects are known. (2) The Banyan Seed � split a banyan fruit; inside, nothing visible � from that nothing, a great tree arises. (3) Salt in Water � salt dissolved in water is invisible but present everywhere. Each example culminates in: 'Tat Tvam Asi � That Thou Art.'

Shandilya Doctrine (Chandogya III.14) � Brahman as the inner Self: 'All this universe is Brahman. Peaceful, benevolent, and tranquil is that Self.' A theistic Vedanta bridging impersonal Brahman and personal Ishvara.

The Four Mahavakyas

  • 'Prajnanam Brahma' (Consciousness is Brahman) � Aitareya Upanishad.
  • 'Aham Brahmasmi' (I am Brahman) � Brihadaranyaka Upanishad.
  • 'Tat Tvam Asi' (That Thou Art) � Chandogya Upanishad.
  • 'Ayam Atma Brahma' (This Self is Brahman) � Mandukya Upanishad.

Atman-Brahman, Karma-Samsara-Moksha

  • Brahman � Ultimate reality, Sat-Chit-Ananda (Being-Consciousness-Bliss). Beyond all categories: 'Neti, Neti.' The ground of all existence.
  • Atman � The individual self, the pure witness-consciousness (sakshi-chaitanya). The Mahavakyas teach Atman = Brahman.
  • Karma � Impersonal law of cause and effect. Every action generates consequences determining future births.
  • Samsara � The cycle of birth-death-rebirth driven by karma and ignorance (avidya).
  • Moksha � Liberation attained only through knowledge (jnana) of Atman-Brahman identity, not through ritual. A jivanmukta (liberated while alive) is free from fear, desire, and attachment.
  THE ARYAN DEBATE � SUMMARY OF POSITIONS

  AIT (Max M�ller, Wheeler)
  └─ Aryan 'race' invaded c. 1500 BCE, destroyed IVC
  └─ Status: ❌ Rejected

  AMT / Steppe Hypothesis (Current Consensus)
  └─ Small-scale migration c. 2000-1500 BCE, cultural mixing
  └─ Evidence: Linguistics, Genetics (R1a-Z93), BMAC/Andronovo
  └─ Status: ✅ Mainstream academic position

  OIT (Talageri, Frawley, B.B. Lal)
  └─ PIE originates in India; Rigveda = 4000-3000 BCE; IVC = Vedic
  └─ Status: ❌ No mainstream support

  Adi Dravida (Caldwell, Periyar, DMK)
  └─ IVC = Dravidian; Aryans = late migrants/subjugators
  └─ Status: Dravidian substratum accepted; race war rejected

Comparative Table: Early Vedic vs Later Vedic

Vedic Chronology � The Dating Debates

The dating of the Vedic period is one of the most contested areas of Indian historiography, with different lines of evidence pointing to different conclusions. The main positions are:

  • Max M�ller (19th c.): Rigveda c. 1200�1000 BCE (he later revised to 1500�1000 and admitted it was 'a conjecture, not a scientific calculation'). His '200 years per layer' scheme (200 years for composition of the Vedas, 200 for the Brahmanas, 200 for the Aranyakas, etc.) became standard in Western textbooks, though he himself called it 'arbitrary and unscientific'.
  • Michael Witzel (Harvard, current mainstream): Rigveda c. 1400�1000 BCE. Key evidence: (a) The Mitanni treaty (Boghaz-Koi, Turkey, c. 1380 BCE) invoking Indra, Varuna, Mitra, and Nasatya (Ashvins) as divine witnesses � the earliest external attestation of Vedic gods. (b) Kikkuli's horse-training manual (c. 1340 BCE) using Indo-Aryan numerals (aika-, tera-, panza-, satta-, nawa- for one, three, five, seven, nine) and technical terms (vartana � 'turn'). The Mitanni were an Indo-Aryan ruling elite over a Hurrian population in Syria. Their presence proves Indo-Aryan speakers left the homeland by c. 1500�1400 BCE. Since the Mitanni names of gods and numerals are 'fossilised' forms (already fixed in a non-Indo-Aryan context), the Rigveda must represent an earlier, still-evolving tradition � hence a terminus ante quem of c. 1400 BCE for the common Indo-Aryan religious tradition. (c) Iron absence: the Rigveda knows only ayas (copper/bronze); krishna ayas (iron) appears in the Atharvaveda and Yajurveda. Since iron technology reached South Asia c. 1200�1000 BCE (firmly dated in the northern black polished ware sequence), the Rigveda must predate its widespread use.
  • David Frawley (OIT, 1990s�present): Rigveda c. 4000�3000 BCE based on astronomical references (the position of the equinoxes and solstices in Vedic texts, the statement that 'the sun rises in the east in Phalguna' corresponding to the Vernal Equinox in Orion c. 4000 BCE; the mention of the 7 rishis/Big Dipper constellation in the Shatapatha Brahmana). Rejected by mainstream scholarship because: (a) The references are ambiguous and can be read symbolically rather than as precise observations � precession cycles repeat every 26,000 years, so the same data could fit multiple periods. (b) The Rigveda describes a pastoral society with no knowledge of cities, writing, or urban administration � but the Indus Valley Civilization (c. 2600�1900 BCE) was thriving in the same region during Frawley's proposed dates. If the Rigveda were as old as claimed, it should mention cities, which it does not. (c) The linguistic evidence (PIE vocabulary pointing to a temperate steppe homeland, not India) is incompatible with an Indian origin at 4000 BCE.
  • The Saraswati River Argument: The Ghaggar-Hakra (Vedic Saraswati) dried to a seasonal stream by c. 1900 BCE (confirmed by ISRO satellite imagery, OSL dating, and borehole stratigraphy showing the river was perennial until c. 2500 BCE and declined c. 2500�1900 BCE). The Rigveda describes the Saraswati as 'flowing from the mountains to the sea' (VII.95.2) � a description that could only be accurate before c. 1900 BCE. OIT proponents use this to argue the Rigveda must predate 1900 BCE. Mainstream scholars respond: (a) Oral traditions can preserve accurate geographical memories for centuries or millennia. (b) The Saraswati is also a goddess in the Rigveda � its description may be idealised or mythological rather than literal. (c) The Rigveda also mentions the Ganga and Yamuna (which flow to a different ocean system), suggesting the text is a composite of different periods. The debate remains unresolved, but most scholars maintain a date of c. 1500�1000 BCE for the bulk of the Rigveda, with Book X (containing the Purusha Sukta) as late as 800 BCE.
Historiographical Debate: Aryan Origins
AIT (obsolete) → AMT (current consensus) → OIT (politically influential but academically marginal). UPSC candidates should know all three, their key evidence, and the historiographical context. The Rigveda itself records cultural interaction through Dravidian and Munda loanwords (300+), references to Dasas/Dasyus, and the absorption of indigenous elements into Vedic culture.

4. Mahajanapadas & Rise of Magadha (600-321 BCE)

The 6th century BCE was a transformative period in Indian history �" the Second Urbanisation (the first being the Indus Valley), marked by the rise of territorial states, coined money (punch-marked coins), long-distance trade routes (the Uttarapatha and Dakshinapatha), the shramana (renunciant) movements that rejected Vedic orthodoxy, and new philosophical systems. Buddhist and Jain texts mention 16 Mahajanapadas ('great territorial states') that existed in northern India from the 6th to 4th centuries BCE. These represented the transition from the tribal jana (people/clan) to the territorial janapada (foothold of the people) �" people were now identified by their region rather than their clan. This period saw the emergence of complex state structures, professional standing armies, regular taxation in cash and kind, written records (copper-plates, stone inscriptions, birch-bark manuscripts), and the first imperial ambitions that culminated in the Mauryan Empire. Archaeologically, this period is identified by the Northern Black Polished Ware (NBPW) �" a glossy, high-fired pottery found at Mahajanapada sites, associated with the emergence of urban centres and a money economy.

The Second Urbanisation: Economic and Material Context

The period from c. 600 BCE is called the Second Urbanisation because it saw the re-emergence of cities in the Indian subcontinent after the decline of the Indus Valley Civilization (the First Urbanisation, c. 2600-1900 BCE). For nearly a millennium (c. 1900-600 BCE), India was a land of villages and small towns, not cities. The re-urbanisation of the Gangetic plain was driven by three interlinked factors: the expansion of iron technology (especially iron ploughshares that allowed cultivation of heavy clay soils of the Gangetic basin), the rice surplus (rice is a high-yield crop that can support dense populations), and the development of long-distance trade networks connecting the Gangetic heartland to the northwest (Central Asia, Persia), the west coast (Saurashtra, Gujarat), and the Deccan plateau.

Northern Black Polished Ware (NBPW) is the ceramic hallmark of this period. It was manufactured from c. 700 BCE to 200 BCE and is found at nearly all Mahajanapada sites from the Indus valley to the Bengal delta. NBPW is a fine ware with a distinctive glossy, mirror-like surface ranging from jet-black to steel-grey, sometimes with silver or golden iridescence. The glossy finish was achieved by applying a slip of iron-rich clay and firing the pots at high temperatures (800-900°C) in a reducing atmosphere (controlled oxygen) �" a technologically sophisticated process. While NBPW was a luxury tableware used by elites, its presence indicates urban centres with specialised craft production, kiln technology, and social stratification. Key NBPW sites: Hastinapur, Kaushambi, Shravasti, Vaishali, Rajgir, Pataliputra, Champa, Ujjain, Varanasi.

Punch-marked coins (also called ahat coins, literally 'struck') appeared in the 6th century BCE as the first metallic currency of India. These were small silver or copper pieces of irregular shape, each stamped with symbols using punches �" hence the name. The symbols included the sun, the six-armed wheel, the bull, elephant, tree-in-railing, mountain, fish, bow-and-arrow, and various geometric patterns. The coins were issued by merchant guilds (sreni), cities (nigama), and states, suggesting a complex multi-issuer monetary system. The standard silver coin was the karshapana (weighing about 3.3 grams, approximately 32 rattis). The spread of coinage revolutionised the economy: taxes could be collected in cash (not just kind), trade could be conducted without barter, soldiers could be paid a regular salary (creating the first professional standing armies), and wealth could be accumulated in non-perishable form. The Nanda treasury's legendary wealth was stored in the form of these punch-marked coins.

Trade routes were formalised into two great highways that shaped Indian history. The Uttarapatha (Northern Route) ran from Shravasti/Kaushambi through Ahichchhatra, Hastinapur, Indraprastha (Delhi), Sakala (Sialkot), and Taxila, then through the Khyber Pass to Bamiyan, Balkh (Bactria), and Central Asia. The Dakshinapatha (Southern Route) ran from Kaushambi/Varanasi through Vidisha, Ujjain, Pratishthana (Paithan), and onward to ports on the west coast (Broach/Bharuch, Sopara, Kalyan). These routes carried silk, pepper, precious stones, ivory, timber, and textiles. The Ganga itself was the greatest highway of all �" a superhighway of water that could transport bulk goods (rice, timber, stone, metals) far more cheaply than land routes. Major river ports included Champa (the wealthiest port on the Ganga), Pataliputra, Kaushambi, and Varanasi. The synergy of riverine and overland trade made the Gangetic plain the economic heartland of India for the next two millennia.

Urban centres (the Mahajanapada capitals) were true cities, not mere villages. Pataliputra (Patna), according to Greek accounts in the 4th century BCE, was surrounded by a massive wooden palisade wall 15 km in perimeter with 570 towers and 64 gates. Kaushambi (near Allahabad) was a walled city of 6 sq km with a massive rampart still visible today. Taxila was a cosmopolis where Persian, Greek, and Indian cultures met. These cities had paved streets, drainage systems, multi-storey buildings (mentioned in Pali texts), organised markets, and distinct quarters for different crafts and trades (the potters' quarter, the weavers' quarter, the metalworkers' quarter �" each with its own gate). The concentration of population, wealth, and power in these cities created the conditions for the intellectual ferment of the age.

The 16 Mahajanapadas

The Anguttara Nikaya (a Buddhist Sutta Pitaka text) and the Bhagavati Sutra (a Jain Agama text) list the 16 Mahajanapadas with some variations. They were distributed across the Indus-Gangetic plain from eastern Afghanistan (Gandhara) to Bengal (Anga) and from the Himalayan foothills (Kamboja) to the Godavari valley (Ashmaka). Political organisation varied: some were monarchies (rajya or rajadhiraja �" hereditary kings with a council of ministers), others were gana-sanghas (republics or oligarchies, also called an-rajya or sreni, where power was vested in an assembly of clan elders who elected a chief). The most powerful kingdoms were Kosala, Magadha, Vatsa, and Avanti; the strongest gana-sanghas were the Vrijjis (the Lichchhavi confederacy at Vaishali), Mallas (at Kushinara and Pava), and the Shakyas (the Buddha's own clan at Kapilavastu). The Mahajanapadas can be grouped geographically: the north-western group (Gandhara, Kamboja �" influenced by Persian Achaemenid culture); the Indus-Punjab group (Kuru, Matsya, Shurasena); the Gangetic plain group (Panchala, Kashi, Kosala, Vatsa, Chedi, Malla, Shakya); the eastern group (Magadha, Anga, Vrijji); and the central-southern group (Avanti, Ashmaka).
