Rise of Magadha

Magadha (modern south Bihar, roughly the Patna-Gaya-Rajgir area) became the dominant power in the 6th-4th centuries BCE, eventually creating the first empire in Indian history. Its rise from a relatively minor Mahajanapada to the centre of a pan-Indian empire was due to a combination of factors that have been analysed by historians for centuries:

  • Geographical advantages: Located in the fertile middle Ganga basin with rich alluvial soil that produced abundant rice surpluses, supporting a large population and a standing army even in years of poor rainfall. The region was surrounded by the Rajgir hills providing natural defensive fortifications (the five hills of Rajgir: Vaibhara, Varaha, Vrishabha, Rishigiri, and Chaityaka). Most critically, Magadha had access to abundant high-quality iron ore from the hills of south Bihar (the Rajgir-Gaya iron belt), which gave Magadha a decisive technological advantage in weapons and agricultural tools �" Magadhan soldiers had better weapons and Magadhan farmers had better plowshares. The Ganga River, flowing along Magadha's northern border, provided a superhighway for trade, transport of troops, and communication.
  • Strategic location: Magadha was situated at the junction of the Uttarapatha (the northern trade route from the Gangetic plain to Taxila and Central Asia) and the Dakshinapatha (the southern route to Vidisha, Ujjain, and the Deccan). The Son, Ganga, and Ghaghara rivers provided water transport to Bengal in the east and the Doab in the west. Control of the riverine trade brought enormous customs revenue.
  • Dynamic rulers: A remarkable succession of aggressive and capable kings (Bimbisara, Ajatashatru, Shishunaga, Mahapadma Nanda) who systematically expanded territory through war, marriage alliances, diplomacy, and strategic assassination over 200 years without a single prolonged succession crisis.
  • Military innovations: Magadha pioneered the use of war elephants on a mass scale (the Nanda army reportedly had 3,000-6,000 elephants �" terrifying to cavalry-dependent northern armies); developed advanced siege warfare technology (Ajatashatru's rathamusala �" a wheeled chariot with a swinging mace to crush walls; and the mahashilakantaka �" a giant catapult that hurled massive stones); and maintained the first professional standing army paid in cash (rather than feudal levies of clan warriors), ensuring loyalty and discipline.
  • Administrative innovations: Magadha developed the most sophisticated tax administration of its time, with regular assessment of land revenue (1/6th of produce as bhaga), taxes on trade goods (sulka), a professional bureaucracy appointed by the king (rather than hereditary tribal chiefs), a secret police network (the raja-purushas), and a system of provincial governors reporting to the capital.
  • Cultural openness: Magadha was not a centre of Vedic orthodoxy (the eastern Gangetic plain was considered mleccha/degenerate by the orthodox Kuru-Panchala Brahmins). This made Magadha open to the new shramana movements of Buddhism and Jainism, which provided ideological legitimacy to the new monarchical state (the Buddha was a Kshatriya of the Shakya republic from the same region). The Magadhan kings patronised both religions, gaining the support of the merchant classes who were the main supporters of Buddhism and Jainism.

Social and Economic Context: The Urban Revolution and Guild System

The rise of the Mahajanapadas cannot be understood without examining the profound social and economic transformation of the Gangetic plain. This was a period of urban revolution �" the first such revolution since the Indus Valley. The Pali texts speak of six great cities (the Mahanagaras): Champa, Rajgir, Shravasti, Kaushambi, Varanasi, and Vaishali. The emergence of these cities created a new social class: the setthi-gahapati (banker-merchants and wealthy householders) who accumulated enormous fortunes from trade, moneylending, and land ownership. These merchants were the chief patrons of Buddhism and Jainism, which explains the rapid spread of these religions along trade routes.

Guilds (shreni) were a central institution of the urban economy. Each craft or trade was organised into a shreni with its own chief (the shresthin, from which the word 'seth' derives), its own rules, its own standard of weights and measures, and even its own militia. The shreni performed multiple functions: it regulated prices and wages, maintained quality standards, settled disputes among members, managed charitable trusts (to build rest-houses, hospitals, monasteries), and acted as a banker (accepting deposits and lending money at interest). Some guilds issued their own coins (the nigama-sabha coins). The guild system was so strong that guild chiefs were among the most powerful men in the city �" frequently consulted by kings and often serving as ministers. The Jatakas (Buddhist birth stories) contain numerous references to merchants who travelled with caravans of 500 wagons, navigating the trade routes from Varanasi to the Deccan or from Mathura to Taxila.

The rise of the shramana movements was both a cause and a consequence of this urban revolution. The old Vedic religion, centred on animal sacrifice, the authority of the Brahmins, and the world of the village, did not speak to the needs of urban merchants who wanted a moral framework for their commercial activities (honesty in weights, fair dealing, non-violence) and an ideology that valued their worldly success. Buddhism and Jainism provided exactly this: they rejected animal sacrifice, preached non-violence (ahimsa), taught that karma and ethical conduct mattered more than birth, and offered merchants a path to spiritual merit through gifts to the Sangha. The result was a symbiotic relationship �" merchants funded the Sangha, and the Sangha provided merchants with a prestigious identity, social networks, and even armed protection for their caravans through the monasteries along the trade routes.

The Haryanka Dynasty (c. 544-412 BCE)

Bimbisara (c. 544-492 BCE)

Bimbisara (also called Shrenika in Jain texts) was the first great Magadhan ruler and the founder of the Haryanka dynasty (named after his clan, the Haryanka). He came to the throne at age 15 and ruled for 52 years. Bimbisara expanded Magadha through a sophisticated three-pronged strategy.

First, matrimonial alliances were used with exceptional skill. He married three strategically chosen wives, each bringing significant diplomatic advantage. (1) Kosala-devi, the sister of King Prasenajit of Kosala (the most powerful monarch of the day), who brought the prosperous district of Kashi as dowry �" this single district alone was worth 100,000 gold coins annually in revenue and was never fully recovered by Kosala despite repeated attempts. (2) Chellana, a Lichchhavi princess of Vaishali (the daughter of Chetaka, the Lichchhavi chief), which gave Magadha influence over the powerful Vrijji republic and produced a son, Ajatashatru, who would inherit the throne. (3) Kshema, a princess of the Madra kingdom of Punjab, extending Magadha's diplomatic reach into the north-west. These marriages gave Bimbisara what no other Mahajanapada king had: a network of alliances that simultaneously neutralised the most powerful neighbouring kingdom (Kosala), gave him an heir with a claim to the strongest republic (Vrijji), and opened diplomatic channels to the Punjab.

Second, conquest was pursued with relentless focus. He annexed the eastern kingdom of Anga (capital Champa, a wealthy river port on the Ganga near present-day Bhagalpur). Anga was strategically vital: it controlled the eastern Ganga river trade, the copper and iron mines of the eastern Bihar region, and the route to Bengal. The annexation of Anga doubled Magadha's territory and gave it a monopoly on the Ganga river trade from Champa to the Son River. The prince of Anga, who was killed in the war, was the father of the future Jain Tirthankara Mahavira (in Jain tradition, the Anga king's son became a disciple of Mahavira after the conquest).

Third, administration was centralised at his capital Girivraja (Rajgir), surrounded by the five hills of Rajgir that provided near-impregnable natural fortification (the hills have only four passes, each guarded by a gate). He established a council of ministers (known as the nine ratnas or 'gems' of his court), including the famous physician Jivaka, who was both the king's personal doctor and the personal physician of the Buddha. Jivaka's story is remarkable: he was the abandoned son of a courtesan who was adopted by a prince, studied medicine at Taxila for seven years, and became the most celebrated physician of his time, treating Bimbisara, the Buddha, and Ajatashatru. The existence of a figure like Jivaka testifies to the sophistication of Magadhan society �" a professional medical class trained at a university (Taxila) and patronised by the court.

Bimbisara was a contemporary of both Gautama Buddha and Mahavira, and he patronised both traditions (though he was more inclined toward Buddhism in his later years). He is said to have been the first king to meet the Buddha and became a lay follower (upasaka). Under his patronage, the Buddhist Sangha flourished in Magadha. He built the Jivakarama Monastery at Rajgir for the Buddha, a gift of the physician Jivaka's mango grove. Bimbisara was overthrown and killed by his son Ajatashatru �" starved to death in prison, according to the Pali tradition �" after a reign of 52 years.

Ajatashatru (c. 492-460 BCE)

Ajatashatru (mentioned in Buddhist texts as Kunika, and in Jain texts as Udayin's father) killed his father Bimbisara (by starving him in prison, according to Pali texts) and pursued an even more aggressive expansion policy. His reign was marked by two major conflicts that defined the shape of the emerging Magadhan empire.

The first was the war with Kosala. King Prasenajit of Kosala (the brother-in-law of Bimbisara) was horrified by the patricide. He immediately deposed Ajatashatru and reclaimed the district of Kashi (which Bimbisara had received as dowry). After a protracted war with mixed fortunes, Prasenajit ultimately made peace and sealed the treaty by marrying his daughter Vajira to Ajatashatru, with Kashi once again given as dowry. The Kosala-Magadha rivalry thus ended with Magadha's victory �" Kosala was reduced from independent kingdom to subordinate ally.

The second and more famous conflict was the 16-year war against the Vrijji republic (the most powerful gana-sangha confederacy, centred at Vaishali). This was the first major documented war between a monarchy and a republic in Indian history and is recorded in detail in both Buddhist and Jain sources. The war is notable for several innovations. Ajatashatru's Brahmin minister Vassakara infiltrated the Lichchhavi council of 7,707 rajas and sowed discord among them, using the classic imperial tactic of 'divide and rule'. The Buddha himself had predicted to Vassakara that the Vrijjis, as long as they held their councils regularly and maintained unity, would be invincible �" this is recorded in the Mahaparinirvana Sutta. Unable to defeat the Vrijjis' determined citizen army in the field, Ajatashatru invested heavily in military technology. He built the rathamusala (a chariot with a rotating mace designed to crush fortifications by swinging a heavy beam on a pivot) and the mahashilakantaka (a giant stone-hurling catapult, essentially a massive crossbow or trebuchet that could breach the mud-and-brick walls of Vaishali). These are the first recorded use of dedicated siege engines in Indian history.

Ajatashatru also built a fort at the village of Pataligrama on the Ganga River, at the confluence of the Ganga and Son rivers. This fort was intended as a military base for controlling riverine traffic and as a forward post against the Vrijjis. The Buddha prophesied that this village would become 'the chief city of the region, a great centre of trade and commerce' �" a prophecy that was fulfilled when Pataligrama became Pataliputra (Patna), the capital of the Mauryan Empire and the greatest city of ancient India. The war ended with the complete defeat of the Vrijjis and the annexation of Vaishali by Magadha, eliminating the strongest republican obstacle to Magadhan expansion.

Ajatashatru is also famous for his religious patronage. He met the Buddha shortly before the Buddha's death (the Mahaparinirvana) and later built a chaitya on Gridhrakuta Hill (Vulture's Peak), the Buddha's favourite meditation spot at Rajgir. He convened the First Buddhist Council at the Sattapanni cave in Rajgir immediately after the Buddha's death (c. 483 BCE), presided over by Mahakassapa, with Ananda reciting the Dhamma and Upali reciting the Vinaya. This council fixed the Tripitaka (three baskets) in oral form.

Udayin and Later Haryankas (c. 460-412 BCE)

Udayin (Udayabhadra) (c. 460-444 BCE) succeeded Ajatashatru. He made the most important strategic decision of the period: he moved the capital from the hill-fortress of Girivraja (Rajgir) to the newly built city of Pataliputra at the confluence of the Ganga and Son rivers. The location was strategically superior for three reasons. First, it controlled both Gangetic riverine trade and the land routes to the north, south, and west �" Rajgir was tucked away in the hills and difficult to access, while Pataliputra was at the geographic centre of the expanding Magadhan state. Second, the river confluence provided natural water defences on two sides, making the city easier to defend than a hill fortress that could be besieged. Third, the flat Gangetic plain was far better suited for the administration of a growing empire than the narrow and isolated Rajgir valley. Udayin fought the Palaka king of Avanti for control of the Ganga-Yamuna Doab, a conflict that continued after his death. Later Haryanka rulers (Aniruddha, Nagadasaka) were weak, and the dynasty was overthrown by the Shishunaga dynasty, whose founder was a high official married to a royal Lichchhavi princess.

The Shishunaga Dynasty (412-344 BCE)

Shishunaga founded this short-lived dynasty after overthrowing the last Haryanka king, Nagadasaka. Shishunaga was originally a high official (amatya/minister) who served the Haryankas and was married to a Lichchhavi royal princess (connecting the new dynasty to the republic that the Haryankas had conquered). The Shishunaga dynasty is historically important for three achievements.

First, it finally destroyed the Avanti kingdom (the Pradyota dynasty of Ujjain), absorbing western Malwa into the Magadhan empire. This conquest gave Magadha control of the central Indian trade routes (the route to the Deccan via Ujjain) and eliminated the last major independent kingdom in the Gangetic basin. With the annexation of Avanti, Magadha now controlled the entire Gangetic plain from the Himalayan foothills to the Vindhyas, and from Bengal to Malwa.

Second, the second Shishunaga king, Kalashoka (also called Kakavarna Shishunaga, c. 383-372 BCE), convened the Second Buddhist Council at Vaishali (c. 383 BCE) to resolve a serious dispute over monastic discipline. The issue was the 'Ten Points' of relaxation proposed by the Vajjian monks of Vaishali, which included: (1) storing salt in a horn, (2) eating after the midday meal, (3) eating a second meal the same day after having already eaten, (4) holding the uposatha (monastic observance) in different places within the same parish, (5) performing official acts without the full assembly, (6) following a teacher's practice as binding even if it was not the Buddha's rule, (7) drinking buttermilk after meals, (8) drinking unfermented toddy (palm wine), (9) using a mat without a border (of proper size), and (10) accepting gold and silver. The council was attended by 700 monks and presided over by the elder Sabbakami. The orthodox party (the Sthaviravadins, 'Elders') rejected all Ten Points. The more liberal faction (the Mahasanghikas, 'Great Community') walked out, creating the first major schism in Buddhism. The Mahasanghikas later evolved into the Mahayana tradition, which would become the dominant form of Buddhism in East Asia. This schism had profound consequences: it ended the unity of the early Buddhist Sangha and led to the proliferation of 18 (or more) schools within Buddhism over the next few centuries. Kalashoka moved the capital permanently to Pataliputra, which now became the undisputed political centre of northern India.

Third, the Shishunaga period saw the formal consolidation of the Magadhan administrative system. The pattern established by the Shishunagas �" a centralised monarchy with the king as the supreme authority, a council of ministers (amatya-sabha), provincial governors (the kumara-amatyas, often royal princes), and a network of tax collectors and spies �" became the template that the Nandas and Mauryas would inherit and perfect. The last Shishunaga king was overthrown by his minister Mahapadma Nanda, the son of a Shishunaga king by a Shudra woman, who founded the Nanda dynasty.

The Nanda Dynasty (344-321 BCE)

Mahapadma Nanda (reigned c. 344-321 BCE) was described by the Puranas as 'the destroyer of all Kshatriyas' (sarva-kshatrantaka) �" he was the son of a Shishunaga king by a Shudra woman (hence of low-caste origin, which explains the Puranic hostility) and systematically overthrew the old Kshatriya dynasties (the Ikshvakus, Panchalas, Kasis, Kurus, Maithilas, and many others). This Shudra origin of the Nandas is historically significant �" it shows that social mobility was possible in the fluid conditions of the Mahajanapada period, contrary to the rigid caste stereotypes. The Puranas claim that Mahapadma was 'born of a Shudra woman' and that 'all kings will be of Shudra origin' thereafter �" a complaint that reflects the anxiety of Brahminical orthodoxy at their loss of patronage.

Mahapadma Nanda is sometimes considered India's first empire-builder. He created the first imperial structure that extended from the Brahmaputra River in the east to the Beas River in the west, and from the Himalayas in the north to the Vindhyas in the south. The Nanda empire was significantly larger than the Magadha of the Haryankas and Shishunagas �" it included the Kalinga region (Odisha) and parts of the central Indian plateau, and it seems to have established tributary relationships with the remaining gana-sanghas of the Punjab.

The Nandas were the wealthiest dynasty of ancient India. They accumulated an enormous standing army that amazed the Greek historians who wrote about Alexander's invasion. According to the Greek historian Curtius: the Nanda army consisted of 200,000 infantry, 20,000 cavalry, 2,000 chariots, and 3,000 war elephants. Diodorus Siculus and Plutarch give similar numbers (adding up to 6,000 elephants in some accounts). This army was the largest that any Greek writer had ever heard of �" it was the Nanda army's war elephants, waiting on the other side of the Beas River, that caused Alexander's troops to mutiny in 326 BCE. Alexander's men, after eight years of continuous campaigning, simply refused to face a force of thousands of elephants supported by hundreds of thousands of infantry. The mutiny on the Beas was one of the most consequential events in world history �" it saved India from Macedonian conquest and led Alexander to turn back, dying soon after in Babylon.

The Nanda wealth was legendary. The enormous Nanda treasury is repeatedly mentioned in Indian texts (the Mudrarakshasa, the Puranas, the Mahavamsa) as containing 'mountains of gold, silver, and precious stones.' This wealth came from two sources: regular taxation (the Nandas collected taxes in cash, not just kind, creating a thoroughly monetised economy but at oppressive rates �" they were the first Indian dynasty to demand cash payment of taxes rather than a share of the crop) and tribute from conquered kingdoms. The Nandas ruled from Pataliputra, which was now a magnificent fortified city surrounded by a 15-kilometre wooden palisade wall with 570 towers and 64 gates, protected by a wide moat connected to the Ganga River (this is described in detail by the Greek ambassador Megasthenes, writing in the 4th century BCE). The Nanda empire was the first Indian state that could be called a 'territorial empire' rather than just a 'regional kingdom.'

The last Nanda king, Dhana Nanda (called Xandrammes or Agrammes in Greek sources), was overthrown by Chandragupta Maurya with the strategic guidance of Chanakya (Kautilya, the Brahmin minister of the Nandas who had been publicly insulted by the king and swore revenge). The takeover was both a palace coup and a regional uprising. Chanakya, born in the Takshashila region, had been a teacher at Taxila before being humiliated by Dhana Nanda at Pataliputra. He then met the young Chandragupta (exiled from the Maurya clan of the Pipphalivana republic) and formed the partnership that would create the Mauryan Empire. The immense Nanda treasury, army, and administrative system were inherited intact by the Mauryas, providing the material foundation for the first pan-Indian empire. Thus the Nanda period, though brief, was the direct precursor of the Mauryan Empire �" the Nandas created the imperial structure, and the Mauryas expanded it.

The Gana-Sanghas (Republics)

The gana-sanghas were non-monarchical states ruled by an assembly of Kshatriya clan heads. The term gana means 'equal in rank' or 'group/troop,' while sangha means 'assembly' �" together they denote a state where sovereignty was vested in a corporate body rather than a single king. They were widespread in the Himalayan foothills (Shakyas, Mallas, Lichchhavis, Koliyas, Moriyas), Punjab (Yaudheyas, Arjunayanas, Kunindas, Sibis, Malavas, Trigartas), and Saurashtra.

The Lichchhavi republic at Vaishali was the most famous and most powerful gana-sangha, and it is the best-documented. According to the Buddhist texts, the Lichchhavi constitution was remarkably sophisticated for its time. The republic had 7,707 rajas �" a number that is not literal but signifies the entire warrior oligarchy (the 7,707 represents all adult Kshatriya males entitled to sit in the assembly). The actual governing body was a smaller inner council of 500-1,000 members who met in the santhagara (assembly hall) at Vaishali.

The decision-making process was conducted as follows. Meetings were held regularly (the Buddha praised the Vrijjis for 'holding full and frequent assemblies'). Each proposal was introduced by one of the gana-mukhyas (clan chiefs). Voting was conducted using wooden tokens called salakas (hence the officer who conducted the vote was the salaka-grahaka �" the 'vote-taker'). The vote-taker would collect the tokens and declare the majority decision. There were provisions for a quorum, for adjournment, and for voting by proxy. Decisions were binding on all members, and the raja (president/chief) was elected by the assembly and served for a fixed term. The Mallas of Kushinara and Pava had a similar system with two parallel assemblies that met simultaneously and coordinated their decisions.

The gana-sanghas were not democratic in the modern sense �" they excluded women, low-caste members, and non-Kshatriyas from political participation. The 'equality' was among the Kshatriya clan members only. But within that oligarchy, there was genuine procedural democracy �" voting, debate, public accountability, and elected leadership. This is why Indian historians have called Vaishali 'the world's first republic' and have compared the Lichchhavi constitution to the republican institutions of Greece and Rome.

The Jatakas and Buddhist texts contain fascinating details of republican life. The Lichchhavi rajas were known for their fierce pride, their martial prowess, and their luxurious lifestyle �" they wore fine clothes, rode chariots, and were attended by retainers. The Buddha's praise of the Vrijjis' democratic institutions (recorded in the Mahaparinirvana Sutta as the 'Seven Conditions of a Nation's Welfare') is one of the earliest political testaments to the value of assemblies, consultation, and collective decision-making in the Indian tradition: 'As long as the Vajjians hold full and frequent public assemblies, they may be expected to prosper and not decline.'

Why Did Republics Decline?
The gana-sanghas were gradually absorbed by the Magadhan expansion. The republics had inherent weaknesses: they were small, lacked standing armies (every warrior was a citizen-soldier), had decision-making that could be paralysed by faction, and were ill-suited to large-scale imperial warfare. The Magadhan monarchy's secret police and divide-and-rule tactics (as used by Vassakara against the Lichchhavis) exploited these weaknesses. By the Gupta period, most gana-sanghas had disappeared, replaced by monarchical feudatories. The Yaudheyas of Punjab survived as a republic until the 4th century CE, minting their own coins and electing their maharaja. The republican tradition, however, did not completely die �" the village assemblies (sabhas and samitis) of later periods and the self-governing guilds (shrenis) of medieval India carried forward some of the institutional memory of the gana-sanghas.

Persian and Greek Contacts

Two external forces impinged on the Mahajanapada world during this period: the Achaemenid Persian Empire (c. 550-330 BCE) and Alexander of Macedon (326-325 BCE). These contacts brought India into the wider political and cultural currents of the ancient world for the first time since the Indus Valley Civilization.

Achaemenid Conquest of Gandhara

The Achaemenid king Cyrus the Great (558-530 BCE) was the first Persian ruler to campaign in the Indian subcontinent, conquering the region of Gandhara and Kamboja (the Kabul valley, eastern Afghanistan, and the Peshawar valley) and incorporating them into the Persian Empire as the satrapy of Hindush (the 20th satrapy, according to the Behistun Inscription of Darius I, c. 520 BCE). The Persian historian Herodotus names Gandhara as one of the satrapies of the Achaemenid Empire, paying an annual tribute of 360 talents of gold-dust �" one of the richest satrapies in the empire.

The Achaemenid rule of Gandhara lasted from c. 550 to 330 BCE (over 200 years) and left deep cultural imprints. The Kharoshthi script (a modified Aramaic script, written right-to-left) was developed in Gandhara during this period for writing the Prakrit language �" it was used for inscriptions and documents in the north-west for centuries. Persian architectural motifs (the lotus design, the bell-shaped capital, the use of polished stone) entered India through Gandhara and influenced Mauryan art (the Ashokan pillars have clear Achaemenid inspiration). Persian administrative practices �" the use of a written chancellery, the maintenance of an imperial road system, the appointment of provincial governors (satraps) �" were observed by the Indian states that came into contact with the empire. Taxila (the capital of Gandhara) became a cosmopolitan city and a great centre of learning, where Persian, Greek, and Indian ideas mixed freely.

The Achaemenid presence in the north-west was also significant for what it did not do �" it did not expand east of the Indus. The Achaemenids controlled Gandhara and Sindh but never attempted to conquer the Gangetic plain. This left the Indian Mahajanapadas to develop independently, without foreign domination, which is one reason why Indian civilisation developed in a distinctive way compared to the Hellenised and Persianised Near East.

Alexander's Invasion (326-325 BCE)

Alexander of Macedon crossed the Indus River in 326 BCE after conquering the Achaemenid Empire. He defeated the local king of Taxila, Ambhi (Omphis), who submitted without a fight and even provided troops for Alexander's campaign. The famous battle of the Hydaspes (Jhelum River) was fought against King Porus (Purushottama) of the Paurava kingdom in the Punjab (between the Jhelum and Chenab rivers). Porus fought with exceptional courage, using his war elephants to devastating effect against Alexander's cavalry. Though defeated, Porus so impressed Alexander that he was reinstated as a satrap, ruling an even larger territory.

Alexander advanced eastward across the Punjab, subduing the independent gana-sanghas of the region (the Sibis, Agalassoi, Kathaians, Malloi, and others who put up fierce resistance). He reached the Beas River (Hyphasis) in the summer of 326 BCE, planning to cross into the Gangetic plain and conquer the Nanda Empire. It was at this point that his army mutinied. After eight years of continuous campaigning, the Macedonian soldiers had had enough. They had heard terrifying reports of the Nanda army �" 200,000 infantry, 20,000 cavalry, thousands of war elephants. The monsoon rains had started, the Beas was swollen, and the army refused to go further. Alexander's general Coenus pleaded with him to turn back. Alexander sulked in his tent for three days, but finally gave the order to retreat. He sailed down the Indus, fought his way through the Sindh tribes, and marched back to Babylon through the Gedrosian desert (Makran), losing more men to thirst and heat than to battle.

The impact of Alexander's invasion on India has been debated by historians. On the one hand, Alexander did not conquer any part of the Gangetic plain and his Indian expedition lasted barely 18 months. His death in 323 BCE and the collapse of his eastern empire meant that his conquests in the Punjab were quickly reclaimed by Indian rulers (Chandragupta Maurya conquered the Punjab from the Greek satrap Seleucus Nicator in 305 BCE). On the other hand, Alexander's invasion had three important consequences. First, it demonstrated the vulnerability of the gana-sanghas �" the fierce but disunited republics of the Punjab were no match for a disciplined professional army, a lesson that Chandragupta Maurya absorbed. Second, it weakened the north-western states and created a power vacuum that Chandragupta exploited. Third, it established the first direct Greek-Indian contact, which led to the Hellenistic influence on Indian art (Gandhara art), astronomy, and coinage in the subsequent period. Alexander's invasion also revealed the existence of the Nanda Empire to the Greek world �" the first news to reach Europe that there was a powerful empire in the interior of India, ruled by a king whose army could have crushed any Greek force.

UPSC Tip: Remember the Haryanka Chain
BIMBISARA (founded Haryanka through marriage + conquest)
→ AJATASHATRU (killed father, defeated Vrijji, built Pataliputra)
→ UDAYIN (moved capital to Pataliputra)
→ SHISHUNAGA (ousted Haryanka, annexed Avanti, Second Council)
→ NANDA (first empire, vast army, amassed wealth)
→ MAURYA (Chandragupta with Chanakya topples Nanda).

Mnemonic: 'Babies Actually Use Shiny New Masks' (Bimbisara, Ajatashatru, Udayin, Shishunaga, Nanda, Maurya)
  16 MAHAJANAPADAS (c. 600 BCE)

  PUNJAB/HARYANA: Kuru, Matsya

  → MAURYA (Chandragupta, 321 BCE)

5. Buddhism and Jainism (6th Century BCE)

The 6th century BCE was a period of intellectual ferment in India, marked by the rise of the Shramana (striver/renunciant) movement. Numerous heterodox teachers emerged, rejecting Vedic orthodoxy, the authority of the Brahmanas and Vedas, animal sacrifice, and the caste system. They preached a path of personal effort, asceticism, and individual liberation. Buddhist texts mention 62 schools of thought. The most influential included Gautama Buddha, Mahavira (24th Tirthankara), Purana Kassapa (amoralism), Makkhali Gosala (fatalism — Ajivika), Ajita Kesakambali (materialism), Pakudha Kacchayana (atomism), and Sanjaya Belatthiputta (scepticism). Only Buddhism and Jainism survived as major religions.

UPSC Key Insight
The 6th century BCE is the Axial Age (Karl Jaspers) — simultaneous philosophical efflorescence across the world: Confucius/Lao Tzu in China, Zarathustra in Persia, pre-Socratics in Greece, Hebrew prophets. India’s contribution was the Shramana tradition. Key causes: rise of urban centres (mahajanapadas), growth of trade (setthis), rigid varna system, excesses of Vedic sacrifice.

Buddhism — Detailed Exposition

Life of Gautama Buddha (c. 563-483 BCE) in Detail

Siddhartha Gautama was born in Lumbini (Nepal Terai, marked by an Ashokan pillar) as a prince of the Shakya clan (gana-sangha republic), hence Shakyamuni. His father Suddhodana was the elected chieftain; mother Maya died 7 days after birth; raised by step-mother Prajapati Gautami. The sage Asita prophesied he would become either a chakravartin king or a spiritual teacher. His father shielded him from all suffering, providing three palaces and every luxury.

The Four Sights (Nimitta): At 29, Siddhartha ventured out with his charioteer Channa. He saw an old man (old age), a sick man (disease), a corpse (death), and a serene ascetic (renunciation). These four sights shattered his complacency and revealed the reality of suffering.

Mahabhinishkramana (Great Renunciation): That night, he left his wife Yashodhara and newborn son Rahula (‘fetter’), woke Channa, saddled his horse Kanthaka, and rode to the river Anoma, where he cut his hair, exchanged robes for rags, and sent Channa back. He became a wandering ascetic.

Journey to Enlightenment: He studied under Alara Kalama (sphere of nothingness) and Uddaka Ramaputta (neither perception nor non-perception), but rejected both as inadequate. With five ascetics (Kondanna, Vappa, Bhaddiya, Mahanama, Assaji) at Uruvela (Bodh Gaya), he practised extreme asceticism for 6 years, nearly starving to death.

Enlightenment under the Bodhi Tree: Realising the futility of self-mortification (the Middle Way), he accepted rice-milk (payasa) from the village girl Sujata. His five companions abandoned him. Sitting under a pipal tree, he vowed not to rise until enlightened. He was tempted by Mara (the Evil One) and his three daughters (Tanha, Arati, Raga), but touched the earth (Bhumisparsha mudra) calling the earth goddess to witness his merit. After 49 days, at 35, he attained Nirvana — becoming the Buddha, the Awakened One.

First Sermon at Sarnath: The Buddha hesitated to teach, but the god Brahma Sahampati begged him. He walked 150 miles to the Deer Park (Mrgadava) at Sarnath and delivered the Dhammacakkappavattana Sutta (Setting in Motion the Wheel of Dharma) to his five former companions. Kondanna became the first bhikkhu. The Triratna (Three Jewels) was born: Buddha, Dharma, Sangha.

45 Years of Ministry: He travelled through Kosala, Magadha, and Vajji. Converted his father, step-mother (first bhikkhuni), son Rahula (first novice), and cousin Ananda (attendant who memorised all sermons). Chief disciples: Sariputta (wisdom), Moggallana (psychic powers), Mahakassapa (asceticism, presided over First Council), Anuruddha (divine eye). Devadatta, his cousin, attempted to kill him (threw a rock, unleashed elephant Nalagiri) and create a schism. Royal patrons: Bimbisara (donated Veluvana Bamboo Grove, first monastery), Prasenajit, Ajatashatru. Lay patron: courtesan Amrapali (donated mango grove). Merchant Anathapindika purchased Jetavana Grove with gold coins.

Mahaparinirvana at Kushinagar: At 80, he ate his last meal — sukaramaddava (truffle or pork) offered by the blacksmith Cunda — and fell ill. At Kushinagar, between twin sal trees, he lay in the lion posture and attained Mahaparinirvana. Last words: ‘Decay is inherent in all composite things. Work out your salvation with diligence.’ His body was cremated and relics divided into 8 portions for: Mallas of Kushinagar, Ajatashatru (Magadha), Licchavis (Vaishali), Shakyas (Kapilavastu), Mallas (Pava), Koliyas (Ramagama), Bulis (Allakappa), and a Brahmin (Vethadipa). The relics were enshrined in stupas. Ashoka later redistributed them into 84,000 stupas across his empire.

Core Buddhist Philosophy — Expanded Exposition

The Four Noble Truths (Arya-satyani): (1) Dukkha — all existence is suffering (birth, aging, sickness, death, separation from loved ones, not getting what one wants, the five skandhas). (2) Samudaya — the cause is tanha (craving for sensual pleasures, existence, non-existence). (3) Nirodha — cessation of craving leads to Nirvana (the unconditioned). (4) Marga — the Noble Eightfold Path.

The Noble Eightfold Path: Right View (understanding the Four Truths), Right Intention (renunciation, goodwill), Right Speech (no lying, slander, harsh words, gossip), Right Action (no killing, stealing, sexual misconduct), Right Livelihood (avoid butchers, arms/poison/slave traders, intoxicants), Right Effort (prevent/abandon unwholesome states, cultivate/maintain wholesome states), Right Mindfulness (four satipatthanas — body, feelings, mind, phenomena), Right Concentration (four jhanas/dhyanas). The path is grouped into three trainings: Shila (morality), Samadhi (concentration), Prajna (wisdom).

Anitya (Impermanence): The first of the Three Marks of Existence (tilakkhana): anitya, dukkha, anatman. All conditioned things arise and pass away. Nothing — body, feelings, thoughts, consciousness — is permanent. Clinging to impermanent things is the root of suffering.

Anatman (Anatta — No-Self): The most radical Buddhist doctrine. There is no permanent, unchanging self. A ‘person’ is a temporary aggregation of the Five Skandhas: Rupa (form), Vedana (feeling), Sanna/Samjna (perception), Sankhara/Samskara (mental formations — karmic impulses), Vinnana/Vijnana (consciousness). At death, the skandhas disperse; karmic energy propels a new continuum — like one flame lighting another. No ‘thing’ transmigrates, yet there is causal continuity. This is the Middle Way between eternalism (atman) and annihilationism (ucchedavada).

Kshanika Vada (Momentariness): Everything exists only for a single moment (kshana). A ‘thing’ is a series of discrete momentary events. The person of a moment ago is not the same as now, yet there is causal continuity. Criticised by Hindu philosophers (Kumarila Bhatta) as making memory and moral responsibility impossible.

Pratityasamutpada (Dependent Origination — 12 Nidanas): All phenomena arise dependently. The 12 links spanning three lives:

LinkSanskritMeaning
1AvidyaIgnorance (of the Four Truths)
2SamskaraVolitional formations/karmic impulses (conditioned by past ignorance)
3VijnanaConsciousness (first moment of new life)
4Nama-RupaName-and-form (body-mind complex)
5ShadayatanaSix sense bases
6SparshaContact (organ + object + consciousness)
7VedanaFeeling (pleasant, unpleasant, neutral)
8TrishnaCraving/thirst
9UpadanaClinging/attachment
10BhavaBecoming/existence (karmic momentum)
11JatiBirth
12Jara-MaranaAging-and-death (cycle repeats)

Breaking the chain at avidya (through wisdom) or trishna (through detachment) leads to cessation. Formula: ‘When this exists, that comes to be.’

Dhatu Theory: The person is analysed into six elements (dhatus): earth (solidity), water (cohesion), fire (heat), air (motion), space (cavity), and consciousness (knowing). The 18 dhatus (6 sense organs + 6 objects + 6 consciousnesses) map the cognitive process.

Pudgala Vada (Personhood Controversy): The Pudgalavadins/Sammitiyas argued there is a ‘person’ (pudgala) that transmigrates, inexpressible as identical to or different from the skandhas. Fiercely opposed by Theravadins and Sarvastivadins as a betrayal of anatman. Despite this, the Sammitiya school became extremely popular during the Gupta period — Xuanzang reported they constituted a majority of Indian Buddhists.

Karma & Rebirth: How Buddhism Differs: Actions driven by intention (cetana) shape future rebirths. Unlike Hinduism: no permanent soul transmigrates — rebirth is a causal continuum (like flame passing between lamps). Unlike Jainism: karma is mental (intention), not material — not a substance that ‘sticks’ to the soul but a law of cause and effect through consciousness-continuity.

Common UPSC Mistake
Don’t confuse: Anitya says things change; anatman says there is no permanent ‘thing’ that changes. They are related but distinct. Buddhist karma emphasises intention (cetana) — distinguishing it from both Hindu and Jain karma theories.

Five Precepts (Pancha Shila) and Ethics

Lay Buddhists (upasakas) undertake: (1) abstain from killing, (2) abstain from stealing, (3) abstain from sexual misconduct, (4) abstain from lying, (5) abstain from intoxicants. Monks follow 227 rules (Patimokkha) including complete celibacy and poverty (only 8 requisites: 3 robes, bowl, razor, needle, belt, water-strainer). The Uposatha fortnightly ceremony includes confession and Patimokkha recitation.

Sangha and Monasticism

Vinaya Pitaka Rules: The Patimokkha has 8 categories: Parajika (4 — defeat: sexual intercourse, stealing, killing, false claims; permanent expulsion), Sanghadisesa (13 — requiring formal meeting), Aniyata (2 — indefinite), Nissaggiya Pacittiya (30 — forfeiture + confession), Pacittiya (92 — confession), Patidesaniya (4 — acknowledgement), Sekhiya (75 — training/etiquette), Adhikarana-Samatha (7 — dispute resolution). Upasampada (ordination) requires age 20+, no debt/disease, parental consent.

Vassa (Rain Retreat): Three-month monsoon retreat (July-October) when monks stay in one monastery. Ends with Pavarana (invitation of criticism) and Kathina (offering of robes by laity).

Bhikkhuni Order: Buddha was reluctant to ordain women. Mahaprajapati Gotami (step-mother) led 500 women on a 150-mile barefoot journey to Vaishali. Ananda interceded; Buddha agreed but imposed 8 Chief Rules (Gurudharmas) placing nuns under monks’ authority. The order flourished but died out in Theravada by the 11th century. Revived controversially in modern times.

Laity (Upasaka): Provide dana (alms) to the Sangha, earning merit. The Sigalovada Sutta is the layperson’s code of conduct. Laity observe Eight Precepts on Uposatha days. Unlike Jainism, Buddhism honours a ‘middle path’ for laity — normal householder life is compatible with the Dharma.

Buddhist Councils in Detail

First Council (Rajagriha, c. 483 BCE): Convened 3 months after Buddha’s death at the Sattapanni Cave (Rajgir). Patron: King Ajatashatru of Magadha. President: Mahakassapa. 500 arhats assembled. Ananda recited the Sutta Pitaka; Upali recited the Vinaya Pitaka. Texts preserved orally — no writing.

Second Council (Vaishali, c. 383 BCE): 100 years later at Vaishali. Patron: King Kalashoka. President: Sabbakami. Dispute over 10 Points (Dasavatthuni) of monastic laxity (storing salt in a horn, eating after noon, accepting gold/silver, etc.). The orthodox Sthaviravadins declared all 10 unlawful. The Vajjiputta monks (later Mahasanghikas) refused and held a separate council. This was the first great schism: Sthaviravadins vs Mahasanghikas.

Third Council (Pataliputra, c. 250 BCE): At Ashokarama monastery, Pataliputra. Patron: Emperor Ashoka. President: Moggaliputta Tissa. Purpose: purify the Sangha of heretics. Tissa composed the Kathavatthu (Points of Controversy, refuting 219 heresies). The Abhidhamma Pitaka was formally closed. Ashoka dispatched missionaries to 9 regions: Kashmir/Gandhara (Majjhantika), Sri Lanka (Mahinda), Suvarnabhumi/Myanmar (Sona & Uttara), and Hellenistic kingdoms.

Fourth Council (c. 78-100 CE): Two separate councils. Theravada: at Aloka Vihara, Sri Lanka (Patron: Vattagamani Abhaya) — Pali Canon written on palm leaves for the first time. Sarvastivada: at Kundalavana, Kashmir (Patron: Kanishka; President: Vasumitra) — compiled the Mahavibhasha (Great Commentary). Associated with the formal Hinayana/Mahayana split.
