Buddhist Sectarian Evolution

18-20 Schools: Sthaviravada (conservative, arhat ideal, Pali canon — survives in Sri Lanka/SE Asia). Mahasanghika (liberal, transcendental Buddha, introduced Bodhisattva path, gave rise to Mahayana). Sarvastivada (‘all exists’, dharmas exist in past/present/future, dominant in Kashmir/Central Asia). Vibhajyavada (Moggaliputta Tissa’s school, precursor of Theravada). Sammitiya/Pudgalavada (accepted a ‘person’ — most popular in Gupta India). Sautrantika (rejected Abhidharma, relied only on Sutras, developed bija/seeds theory).

Mahayana Revolution (c. 1st cent. BCE onward): Key innovations: Bodhisattva ideal — postponing Nirvana to save others, practising Six Paramitas (dana, shila, kshanti, virya, dhyana, prajna); Trikaya doctrine (Nirmanakaya, Sambhogakaya, Dharmakaya); new sutras proclaimed as higher teachings. Major Mahayana sutras: Prajnaparamita (Diamond Sutra, Heart Sutra), Lotus Sutra, Avatamsaka Sutra, Lankavatara Sutra, Sukhavati-vyuha (Pure Land), Vimalakirti Nirdesha. Madhyamaka (Sunyavada): founded by Nagarjuna (c. 150-250 CE), demonstrated all phenomena are empty (shunya) of inherent existence. Yogacara (Vijnanavada): founded by Asanga and Vasubandhu (c. 300-400 CE), taught citta-matra (mind-only) and 8 consciousnesses including alaya-vijnana (storehouse consciousness). Vajrayana (5th cent. CE onward): Tantric Buddhism with mantras, mandalas, mudras, and the 84 Mahasiddhas (Tilopa, Naropa, Marpa, Milarepa). Dominant in Tibet, Bhutan, Mongolia.

Buddhist Art and Architecture

Early Buddhist art (3rd-1st cent. BCE) was aniconic — the Buddha represented by symbols: Bodhi tree, dharma wheel, footprints, empty throne, stupa, lotus, triratna. Best preserved at Sanchi and Bharhut stupas.

Stupas: Sanchi Stupa (MP) — best preserved, built by Ashoka, enlarged by Shungas, with four carved toranas (gateways). Bharhut Stupa (Satna, MP) — earliest narrative panels (2nd cent. BCE, now in Indian Museum, Kolkata). Amaravati Stupa (Andhra) — largest but destroyed; railings in British Museum and Chennai Museum. Nagarjunakonda Stupa (Andhra) — linked to Mahasanghikas. Dhamek Stupa (Sarnath, Gupta period) — marks the first sermon.

Ashokan Pillars: Monolithic sandstone pillars with Mauryan polish (now lost technique). The Lion Capital at Sarnath (India’s national emblem) has four lions on a circular abacus with dharma chakra. Others: Vaishali (single lion), Lauriya Nandangarh (lion), Rampurva (bull/lion), Sankisa (elephant).

Rock-cut Chaityas: Karle Chaitya (1st cent. BCE) — grandest, 38m x 14m, 37 pillars. Bhaja Chaitya (2nd cent. BCE) — unique horseshoe window. Kanheri Caves (109 caves, Mumbai). Ajanta Caves (30 caves, 2nd cent. BCE-6th cent. CE) — pinnacle of Buddhist cave art with magnificent fresco secco paintings of Jataka tales and Buddha’s life.

Buddha Image: Aniconic to Iconic: First anthropomorphic Buddha images appeared in the Kushan period (1st-3rd cent. CE) in two centres: Gandhara (Greco-Roman influence, Apollo-like Buddha) and Mathura (indigenous, round-faced, transparent robe). The Gupta School (4th-6th cent.) synthesised both — the Sarnath Buddha is the epitome: serene, transparent robe, delicate halo, dharma-chakra mudra. The Pala-Sena School (8th-12th cent., Bengal/Bihar) produced multi-armed tantric deities in black stone and bronze (Nalanda bronzes).

UPSC Art & Culture Connection
The aniconic-to-iconic transition is a key UPSC topic. Aniconic phase reflected reluctance to depict the liberated teacher. Iconic phase arose under Kushans due to: (1) Mahayana concept of Buddha as transcendent cosmic being, and (2) Greek anthropomorphic tradition in Gandhara. Mathura school developed independently from Yaksha traditions.

Buddhist Literature

Pali Canon (Tipitaka): (1) Vinaya Pitaka — monastic rules; (2) Sutta Pitaka — discourses in 5 nikayas (Digha, Majjhima, Samyutta, Anguttara, Khuddaka including Dhammapada, Theragatha, Jataka); (3) Abhidhamma Pitaka — 7 books of philosophy (Kathavatthu by Moggaliputta Tissa, Patthana on 24 causal relations). Jataka tales (547 birth stories) are the single most important source for Buddhist narrative art. Nidanakatha: fullest Pali biography of the Buddha. Milindapanha: dialogue between King Menander and Nagasena on the self (chariot simile). Mahavastu (Lokottaravadin): biography with Jatakas. Lalitavistara: Mahayana biography. Buddhacarita by Ashvaghosha (Kanishka’s contemporary): Sanskrit epic poem, the earliest complete biography. Visuddhimagga by Buddhaghosa (5th cent. CE): encyclopaedic manual of Theravada meditation and ethics. Dipavamsa and Mahavamsa (5th-6th cent. CE): Sri Lankan chronicles preserving Ashoka’s history. The Mahayana Sutras (Prajnaparamita in 8,000-100,000 lines, Lotus, Avatamsaka, Lankavatara, Sukhavati-vyuha) form a vast Sanskrit literature.

Spread of Buddhism

Ashokan Missions: After the Third Council, Ashoka sent Mahinda (his son) and Sanghamitta (his daughter, carrying a Bodhi tree branch) to Sri Lanka — establishing the oldest continuous Buddhist tradition. Majjhantika to Kashmir/Gandhara. Sona and Uttara to Suvarnabhumi (Myanmar/Thailand). Edicts in Greek and Aramaic in Kandahar show Hellenistic outreach. Sri Lanka: Theravada flourished under Devanampiya Tissa; Pali Canon first written at Aloka Vihara. Silk Road/Central Asia: Buddhism reached Gandhara, flourished under Indo-Greeks and Kushans, spread through Tarim Basin (Khotan, Kucha, Turfan). The Bamiyan Buddhas (55m, 38m) were the greatest Silk Road monuments. China: Arrived 1st cent. CE (Emperor Ming, 67 CE). Kumarajiva (344-413 CE) translated Lotus, Diamond, Madhyamaka texts. Xuanzang (602-664 CE) travelled to India, studied at Nalanda 5 years, returned with 657 texts — his records are an indispensable source. Yijing (635-713 CE) studied at Nalanda 10 years. Chinese schools: Chan/Zen (Bodhidharma), Pure Land, Tiantai, Huayan. Tibet: Two waves — (1) 7th-9th cent. under Songtsen Gampo and Padmasambhava (Nyingma order, Samye Monastery); (2) 11th-12th cent. under Atisha (Kadampa). Tibetan orders: Nyingma, Kagyu, Sakya, Gelug (Dalai Lamas). SE Asia: Myanmar (Anawrahta of Pagan), Thailand (Sukhothai adopted Sri Lankan Theravada), Cambodia (Khmer Empire, Jayavarman VII built Bayon), Indonesia (Borobudur, 9th cent., 504 Buddhas, 2,672 relief panels).

Buddhist Decline in India

Multiple causes: (1) Huna invasions (5th-6th cent.) — Mihirakula destroyed monasteries in Gandhara. (2) Gupta revival of Brahmanism — Puranic Hinduism revived; Buddha incorporated as 9th Vishnu avatar; Shankara (788-820 CE) refuted Buddhist philosophy. (3) Tantric degeneracy — Vajrayana practices alienated laity, blurred distinction from Hindu Tantrism. (4) Loss of royal patronage — after Pala decline (8th-12th cent.), Buddhism lost its support base. (5) Muslim invasions (1193 CE) — Ghurid Turks destroyed Nalanda, Vikramashila, Odantapuri, Somapura. Monks killed or fled to Nepal, Tibet, SE Asia. (6) Absorption into Hinduism — Buddha worshipped as Vishnu avatar; Buddhist practices integrated into Vaishnavism.

  BUDDHISM TIMELINE
  c. 563 BCE  — Buddha born in Lumbini
  c. 528 BCE  — Enlightenment at Bodh Gaya
  c. 483 BCE  — Mahaparinirvana; 1st Council (Rajgir)
  c. 383 BCE  — 2nd Council (Vaishali): Schism
  c. 250 BCE  — 3rd Council (Pataliputra): Missions
  c. 200 BCE  — Aniconic art (Sanchi, Bharhut)
  c. 100 BCE  — Mahayana Sutras appear
   78 CE      — 4th Council (Kashmir): Kanishka
  c. 150 CE   — Nagarjuna: Madhyamaka
  c. 300 CE   — Asanga & Vasubandhu: Yogacara
  c. 400 CE   — Gupta Buddha images (Sarnath)
  1193 CE     — Nalanda destroyed

Jainism — Detailed Exposition

Jainism traces its origins to 24 Tirthankaras (ford-makers). The first was Rishabhanatha (Adinatha), mentioned in the Rigveda. Historicity is reliably established for Parshvanatha (23rd, c. 877-777 BCE) who taught 4 principles (ahimsa, satya, asteya, aparigraha), and Mahavira (24th, c. 599-527 BCE) who added the 5th vow of brahmacharya (celibacy).

Life of Mahavira in Detail

Vardhamana Mahavira was born at Kundagrama (Vaishali district, Bihar) in 599 BCE (traditional date). His father Siddhartha was chieftain of the Jnatarika clan (Vrijji republic); mother Trishala was sister of Chetaka, the Lichchhavi chief. According to the Kalpa Sutra, Queen Trishala had 14 auspicious dreams (white elephant, bull, lion, goddess Sri, garlands, full moon, sun, flag, full vase, lotus lake, celestial ocean, chariot, jewel throne, smokeless fire) portending the birth of a Tirthankara.

30 years as prince: Svetambara tradition says he married Yashoda and had a daughter Priyadarshana. Digambara tradition says he never married. After his parents’ death (by sallekhana), he renounced at 30.

Great Renunciation and 12 Years of Meditation: He distributed his wealth, pulled out his hair in 5 handfuls (kevala-loch), wore no clothes (digambara — sky-clad), and wandered as an ascetic for 12 years. He endured extreme hardships: beaten by cowherds, pelted with stones, harassed by villagers — never retaliating. He stood motionless so that ants built mounds around his feet. He fasted for months. He had a 6-year association with Makkhali Gosala (Ajivika founder) before they parted ways.

Kevala Jnana (Omniscience): At 42, meditating under a sal tree at Jambhiyagrama, he attained Kevala Jnana — absolute, all-knowing knowledge. He had destroyed all four ghati (destructive) karmas. He was now an arhat — omniscient and free while still alive.

30 Years of Teaching: He organised a 4-fold sangha (monks, nuns, laymen, laywomen). His 11 Ganadharas included Indrabhuti Gautama (chief disciple), Sudharma, Arya Jambu. Chief nun: Chandana. He wandered through Anga, Magadha, Mithila, Kosala.

Nirvana at Pavapuri: At 72, he died at Pava (Pavapuri, near Rajgir) through sallekhana (voluntary fasting unto death). Jain Diwali commemorates his nirvana. The Jain calendar uses Mahavira Nirvana Samvat (527 BCE as epoch).

Jain Philosophy — Detailed Exposition

Anekantavada (Non-Absolutism): Reality cannot be captured by a single viewpoint. Two sub-doctrines: Syadvada — every proposition must be prefixed by ‘syat’ (in some way). Seven modes of predication (saptabhangi): (1) exists, (2) does not exist, (3) both (successively), (4) indescribable (simultaneously), (5) exists and indescribable, (6) does not exist and indescribable, (7) both and indescribable. Nayavada — 7 valid standpoints: naigama, samgraha, vyavahara, rju-sutra, sabda, samabhirudha, evambhuta. Anekantavada is frequently cited as a philosophical foundation for tolerance.

Ahimsa Paramodharma: Non-violence is the supreme religion, carried to its logical extreme. Monks sweep ground before walking, strain water, wear mouth-covers (muhpatti), never eat at night. The universe is full of jivas (souls) — every element (earth, water, fire, air, plants) has its own class of embodied souls. The Five Vows (mahavratas for monks, anuvratas for laity): Ahimsa (non-violence), Satya (truth), Asteya (non-stealing), Brahmacharya (celibacy — added by Mahavira), Aparigraha (non-attachment).

Karma Theory: Karma is a subtle material substance (karma-pudgala) that literally sticks to the jiva through kashayas (passions: anger, pride, deceit, greed). 8 Types of Karma: Ghati (destructive) — Jnanavaraniya (knowledge-obscuring), Darshanavaraniya (perception-obscuring), Mohniya (deluding, 28 sub-types), Antaraya (hindrance-producing). Aghati (non-destructive) — Vedaniya (feeling-producing), Ayushya (life-span), Nama (body-determining, 103 types), Gotra (status-determining). 14 Gunasthanas (stages of spiritual development) from mithyatva (wrong belief) to ayoga-kevali (final stage before nirvana). 6 Dravyas (substances): Jiva (soul), Pudgala (matter, smallest unit paramanu), Dharma (motion), Adharma (rest), Akasha (space), Kala (time). Bondage and Liberation: Samvara (stopping influx) through 5 samitis and 3 guptis; Nirjara (shedding karma) through 12 tapas (6 external: fasting, reduced eating, dietary restrictions, giving up tasty food, lonely places, mortification; 6 internal: expiation, confession, service, study, abandoning body, meditation).

  JAIN 9 TATTVAS
  1. Jiva (Soul)          6. Nirjara (Shedding karma)
  2. Ajiva (Non-Soul)     7. Punya (Merit)
  3. Asrava (Influx)      8. Papa (Demerit)
  4. Bandha (Bondage)     9. Moksha (Liberation)
  5. Samvara (Stopping)
  Goal: samvara + nirjara → destroy 8 karmas → Kevala Jnana → Moksha

Jain Monastic Order

Mahavratas (5 great vows for monks) are absolute: no violence, truthfulness, non-stealing, celibacy, non-attachment. Additional rules: no transport (walk barefoot), no fire, no money, no eating after sunset, no root vegetables. Digambara monks are naked with a peacock-feather broom; Svetambara monks wear white with a mouth-cover and alms-bowl.

Sallekhana (Santhara): Voluntary fasting unto death when the body can no longer follow Jain vows. Not suicide — it is a calm, deliberate renunciation undertaken with an acharya’s permission. Legally challenged in 2015 but remains central to Jain practice.

Major Acharyas: Bhadrabahu (c. 4th-3rd cent. BCE) — last shrutakevalin, author of Kalpa Sutra, teacher of Chandragupta Maurya. Sthulibhadra (3rd cent. BCE) — headed First Jain Council. Kundakunda (1st cent. BCE-CE) — most important Digambara acharya, author of Samayasara. Umaswati (2nd-3rd cent. CE) — author of Tattvartha Sutra (the only Jain text accepted by both sects). Haribhadra Suri (8th cent. CE) — prolific Svetambara scholar, author of 120+ works. Hemachandra (1088-1173 CE) — chief advisor of Chaulukya king Jayasimha Siddharaja, polymath grammarian/philosopher/historian, converted Kumarapala to Jainism.

Jain Councils

First Council (Pataliputra, c. 300 BCE): Under Chandragupta Maurya’s patronage, led by Sthulibhadra. Compiled the 11 Angas from oral tradition. The 12th Anga (Drishtivada) was declared lost because Bhadrabahu (who knew it) had migrated to Karnataka and refused to attend.

Second Council (Vallabhi, c. 453-467 CE): The most important Jain council. Under Devardhigani Kshamashramana. The Svetambara canon was written down on palm leaves for the first time after 800 years of oral transmission. Produced the final redaction of 45 texts (12 Angas, 12 Upangas, 10 Prakirnakas, 6 Chedasutras, 4 Mulasutras, Nandisutra, Avashyaka Sutra), written in Ardha-Magadhi Prakrit. After this, the Svetambara canon was closed. The Digambara tradition does not accept the Svetambara canon, relying instead on the Shatkhandagama (Pushpadanta and Bhutabali), Kasayapahuda (Gunadharacarya), and Kundakunda’s works, plus the Paramagama.

Jain Art and Architecture

Ellora Caves (Caves 30-34, Maharashtra): Jain caves from the Rashtrakuta period (6th-10th cent. CE). Indra Sabha (Cave 32) — two-storey with carvings of Tirthankaras and goddess Ambika (yakshini of Neminatha). Chhota Kailasha (Cave 30) — dedicated to Parshvanatha.

Dilwara Temples (Mount Abu, 11th-13th cent.): The most famous Jain temples. Vimala Vasahi (1031 CE, by Vimala Shah, minister of Bhima I) and Luna Vasahi (1230 CE, by Tejahpala and Vastupala). Built entirely of white marble with unparalleled carving: concentric lotus ceilings, delicate filigree pillars, 108 miniature Tirthankara images suspended from the dome.

Gommateshwara (Shravanabelagola, 981 CE): The tallest monolithic statue in India — 17.37m (57 ft) carved from single granite. Commissioned by Chavundaraya, general of the Ganga king. Depicts Bahubali, son of Rishabhanatha, standing motionless in meditation so long that vines grew around his legs. The Mahamastakabhisheka festival (every 12 years) anoints it with milk, ghee, yoghurt, saffron, sandalwood paste.

Other Sites: Sittannavasal (Tamil Nadu, Pandya period) — the most important Jain paintings in South India (fresco secco). Deogarh (UP) — Gupta to medieval temples with finest Jain sculpture. Osian (Rajasthan) — 15 Jain temples, Mahavira Temple (c. 780 CE) is the oldest in Rajasthan. Palitana (Shatrunjaya Hills, Gujarat) — 863 marble temples, most important Jain pilgrimage centre. Sammeta Shikhar (Parasnath Hill, Jharkhand) — 20 of 24 Tirthankaras attained nirvana here.

Royal Patrons of Jainism

Chandragupta Maurya allegedly abdicated and became a Jain monk. Kharavela of Kalinga (1st cent. BCE) — patronised Jainism (Hathigumpha inscription). Rashtrakutas (8th-10th cent.) — Amoghavarsha I wrote the Kavirajamarga, Krishna III patronised Ellora. Western Ganga dynasty (4th-10th cent.) — built Gommateshwara statue. Chaulukya dynasty (Gujarat, 10th-13th cent.) — Kumarapala converted by Hemachandra, made Jainism state religion, built Dilwara temples. Others: Kadambas, Hoysalas, Pandyas, Pallavas (Sittannavasal).

Contributions of Buddhism and Jainism

  • Social reform: Both rejected caste hierarchy. The Buddha: ‘Not by birth does one become a Brahmin, but by deeds.’ Jainism: all souls are inherently equal.
  • Language and literature: Both used Prakrit/Pali, breaking Brahmanical monopoly on Sanskrit. Both produced vast literatures (Pali canon, Jain Agamas).
  • Art and architecture: Stupas (Sanchi, Bharhut, Amaravati), cave temples (Ajanta, Ellora, Karle), Ashokan pillars, universities (Nalanda, Vikramashila). Jain marble temples at Dilwara, Palitana, monolithic Gommateshwara.
  • Institutions of learning: Nalanda (10,000 monks, 2000 teachers), Vikramashila (108 teachers), attracted students from China, Tibet, Korea, Central Asia.
  • Spread of Indian culture abroad: Buddhism carried Indian culture along the Silk Route to Central Asia, China, Tibet, Korea, Japan, Sri Lanka, SE Asia (Borobudur, Angkor Wat, Shwedagon).
  • Vegetarianism and ahimsa: Jainism permanently established ahimsa in Indian culture; vegetarianism among millions of Indians is a direct legacy.
Common UPSC Mistake: Buddhism vs Jainism
Don’t confuse: Buddhism rejects the soul (anatman); Jainism affirms infinite souls (jivas). The Buddha taught the Middle Way; Mahavira embraced extreme asceticism. Buddha accepted gods (trapped in samsara); Jainism is non-theistic (gods subordinate to Tirthankaras). Buddhism emphasises compassion (karuna); Jainism emphasises ahimsa. Buddhist karma is mental (intention); Jain karma is material (karma-pudgala).
Essay Application
For UPSC essays on ‘Tradition vs Modernity’ and ‘Non-violence in World Affairs’: Buddhism’s empirical approach (test the doctrine like gold) resonates with modern science. Jain anekantavada is a foundation for pluralism and tolerance. Ahimsa influenced Gandhi’s satyagraha, which inspired Martin Luther King Jr. and Nelson Mandela.
  BUDDHISM vs JAINISM — SNAPSHOT
  
  BUDDHISM                JAINISM
  Middle Way              Ascetic Way
  No soul (Anatman)       Infinite souls (Jiva)
  Compassion (Karuna)     Non-violence (Ahimsa)
  4 Noble Truths          9 Tattvas
  8-Fold Path             Five Vows
  No extreme penance      Extreme penance
  Pali Canon              Ardha-Magadhi
  
  BOTH: Rejected Vedas/sacrifices & caste hierarchy; taught in Prakrit;
        founded by Kshatriya princes; spread by royal patronage

Buddhist Influence on Indian Literature and Culture

Buddhist influence on Indian literature is immense: the Jataka tales influenced the Panchatantra and Hitopadesha. The Dhammapada is one of the most translated religious texts. The Theragatha/Therigatha contains some of the most moving personal poetry in world literature. The Milindapanha is a philosophical masterpiece of dialogue and simile. In architecture, the stupa form influenced the Hindu temple shikhara; the chaitya arch (gavaksha window) became a ubiquitous decorative motif. The mudras of Buddhist iconography (bhumisparsha, dhyana, abhaya, varada, vitarka, dharma-chakra) became standardised in Indian art. Buddhist universities at Nalanda, Vikramashila, and Odantapuri were the world’s first great residential universities, attracting scholars from across Asia.

Ajivika Sect — The Third Shramana Tradition

The Ajivikas (followers of Makkhali Gosala) were the third major Shramana sect alongside Buddhism and Jainism. Their central doctrine was niyati (fate/destiny) — all beings are powerless, everything is predetermined by cosmic destiny. Gosala was associated with Mahavira for 6 years before their doctrinal split (over Gosala’s claim that all beings reach liberation after exactly 84 lakh great kalpas). The Ajivikas flourished under Bindusara (Ashoka’s father) and survived until the 14th century CE in South India. Their rock-cut caves at Barabar (near Gaya) — donated by Ashoka — are the earliest surviving example of Mauryan cave architecture.

UPSC Study Tip
For UPSC Prelims, remember the key numbers: Buddhism — 4 Noble Truths, 8-Fold Path, 5 Precepts, 12 Nidanas, 5 Skandhas, 3 Marks of Existence, 4 Councils, 227 Monk Rules, 311 Nun Rules, 8 Relic Portions, 84,000 Stupas. Jainism — 24 Tirthankaras, 5 Vows, 7/9 Tattvas, 8 Karmas, 14 Gunasthanas, 6 Dravyas, 2 Councils, 12 Angas, 11 Ganadharas, 14 Auspicious Dreams, 12 Years of Penance.

6. Mauryan Empire (321-185 BCE)

The Mauryan Empire was the first pan-Indian empire, stretching from the Hindu Kush in the northwest to Bengal in the east, and from the Himalayas in the north to the Deccan plateau in the south. It was the largest empire in Indian history until the British Raj, covering approximately 5 million square kilometres � an area larger than the contemporary Mauryan Empire under Ashoka exceeded even the Achaemenid Persian Empire in its heyday. The Mauryan period marks the first time most of the Indian subcontinent was politically unified under a single sovereign. The empire was founded by Chandragupta Maurya in 321 BCE with the guidance of his chief minister Chanakya (also called Kautilya or Vishnugupta), the author of the Arthashastra � one of the most sophisticated treatises on statecraft, economics, and military strategy ever written. The Arthashastra, rediscovered in manuscript form by the Mysore librarian R. Shamasastry in 1905, comprises 15 books and 180 chapters covering everything from fiscal policy, diplomacy (the mandala theory of interstate relations), spy networks, urban planning, mining, forestry, and military strategy. It remains relevant today for understanding ancient Indian governance, taxation systems, and realpolitik.

Foundation and Sources

Our knowledge of the Mauryan Empire rests on a diverse array of Greek, Indian, and epigraphic sources. The Greek sources are anchored by Megasthenes' Indica � a detailed ethnographic and geographic account of India written by the Seleucid ambassador who resided at Chandragupta's court in Pataliputra. The original text is lost, but extensive fragments survive as quotations in later Greek and Latin authors: Strabo (Geography), Arrian (Indica), Pliny the Elder (Natural History), Diodorus Siculus (Bibliotheca Historica), and Aelian (De Natura Animalium). Megasthenes described the city of Pataliputra (with its wooden palisade, 570 towers, and 64 gates), the seven castes of India (a Greek misreading of the varna system), the absence of slavery among Indians (again a misreading � slaves existed but were treated differently from Greek chattel slaves), and the magnificent Mauryan court. His account, though coloured by Hellenistic ethnography and occasional inaccuracies, remains invaluable. Later Greek ambassadors, such as Deimachus (sent by Antiochus I to Bindusara's court) and Dionysius (sent by Ptolemy II Philadelphos to Ashoka's court), also contributed to the Greek knowledge of India.

Indian literary sources are equally important. The Arthashastra (rediscovered by R. Shamasastry in 1905, 15 books, 180 chapters) provides the theoretical blueprint of Mauryan governance � though scholars debate whether it reflects Chandragupta's actual administration or an idealised Kautilyan model. The Mudrarakshasa � a Sanskrit play by Vishakhadatta, composed around the 5th century CE � dramatises Chanakya's political machinations to overthrow the Nandas and install Chandragupta. Though a literary work composed centuries later, it preserves the core historical memory of Mauryan political intrigue. The Puranas (especially the Vishnu Purana and Vayu Purana) provide king-lists with regnal years, though their chronologies are schematic and sometimes contradictory. Buddhist texts � the Sri Lankan chronicles Mahavamsa and Dipavamsa (5th-6th centuries CE), the Ashokavadana (a Sanskrit Buddhist legend, c. 2nd century CE, preserved in Chinese and Tibetan translation), and the Divyavadana � provide rich narrative traditions about Ashoka's conversion, his building of stupas, the Third Buddhist Council, and the mission to Sri Lanka, though their historicity must be critically assessed. Jain texts (the 8th-century CE Rajavali Kathe, the Bhadrabahu Charita) preserve the tradition of Chandragupta's abdication and Jain monastic life. The epigraphic corpus of Ashoka's edicts � 14 Major Rock Edicts, 7 Pillar Edicts, 4 Minor Pillar Edicts, several Minor Rock Edicts, and 3 Cave Dedications � forms the single most important primary source. These edicts are the first deciphered texts in the Brahmi script (deciphered by James Prinsep in 1837) and they provide an unparalleled window into Ashoka's policies, beliefs, and administration. The empire collapsed 137 years after Chandragupta's accession.

Chandragupta Maurya (321-297 BCE)

Chandragupta Maurya's origins remain obscure. The Puranas and Buddhist texts describe him as belonging to the Moriya clan, a clan of the Shakyas (the Buddha's own clan). The name 'Maurya' is variously explained: Buddhist sources connect it to the peacock (mayura in Sanskrit) � the peacock-tamers (moraposakas) of the Moriya clan; Jain sources suggest he was the son of a royal concubine named Mura. The Mudrarakshasa portrays him as the son of a Nanda prince by a Shudra woman (which would make him low-born), while the Puranas claim he was a Kshatriya of the Moriya clan. This debate � peacock-tamers vs Kshatriya � continues among historians, with most modern scholars (Thapar, Allchin) favouring a humble origin from a non-royal Kshatriya clan, which Chanakya then elevated through political engineering.

With Chanakya's guidance, Chandragupta overthrew the unpopular Nanda king Dhana Nanda (the last of the nine Nanda kings, famous for his immense wealth � the Nandas are said to have accumulated 80 crores of gold coins) and captured Pataliputra. The Mudrarakshasa describes the complex political manoeuvres: Chanakya first allied with the Himalayan king Parvataka (possibly the same as Porus, Alexander's adversary), then defeated the Nanda armies. After victory, Chanakya outmanoeuvred Parvataka through a combination of diplomacy and assassination, securing sole power for Chandragupta. Whether historically accurate or not, the Mudrarakshasa captures the ruthlessness of Mauryan realpolitik � the same realism that fills the Arthashastra's chapters on diplomacy and covert operations.

Chandragupta then confronted the Greek satrapies left by Alexander's invasion. Seleucus Nicator, Alexander's successor who controlled the eastern Seleucid Empire from Babylon to the Indus, crossed the Indus around 305 BCE to reclaim the Macedonian territories. The war ended in a treaty � Seleucus ceded four vast territories to Chandragupta: Arachosia (modern Kandahar, Afghanistan), Gedrosia (Balochistan, Pakistan), Paropamisadae (the Kabul Valley), and Aria (Herat, Afghanistan). In addition, Seleucus gave his daughter in marriage to Chandragupta (establishing a diplomatic marriage alliance), and in return received 500 war elephants � which Seleucus immediately used at the Battle of Ipsus (301 BCE) against Antigonus Monophthalmus. Megasthenes was sent as the Seleucid ambassador to Chandragupta's court, producing the famous Indica.

Chandragupta's empire extended from Afghanistan to Bengal and from the Himalayas to the Narmada River. He established a highly centralised bureaucracy and a standing army of formidable proportions: according to Pliny's Natural History (citing Megasthenes), the Mauryan army numbered 600,000 infantry, 30,000 cavalry, 9,000 elephants, and 8,000 chariots � making it one of the largest standing armies of the ancient world. His administration was based on the principles of the Arthashastra, with a tightly controlled economy, a comprehensive tax system, and an elaborate spy network. The emperor was the ultimate authority, but he ruled through a well-defined hierarchy of ministers (mantrins), superintendents (adhyakshas), provincial viceroys (kumaras), district officers (sthanikas), and village headmen (gramikas). In his later years, according to Jain tradition, Chandragupta abdicated the throne, became a Jain monk under the sage Bhadrabahu, travelled to Shravanabelagola in Karnataka during a famine, and died there by sallekhana (ritual fasting unto death). The 8th-century CE text Rajavali Kathe and the Bhadrabahu Charita preserve this tradition, and to this day the hill at Shravanabelagola bears the name 'Chandragupta Hill' with a footprint said to be his.

Bindusara (297-272 BCE)

Chandragupta's son, called Amitraghata ('Slayer of Enemies') by Greek authors � the exact Indian name is unknown, but 'Bindusara' appears in the Puranas. He inherited a stable and vast empire and continued his father's expansionist policies southwards into the Deccan plateau as far as present-day Karnataka and Andhra Pradesh. The southern provinces were governed from Suvarnagiri ('Golden Mountain'), a regional capital in the Raichur Doab region, rich in gold mines. Bindusara's reign was largely peaceful and administrative rather than military � he maintained the bureaucratic system his father had built. He was a patron of the Ajivika sect, a shramana movement founded by Makkhali Gosala (a contemporary of the Buddha and Mahavira), which taught strict determinism (niyati) � the doctrine that everything is fixed by fate and individual effort is irrelevant. Bindusara's patronage of the Ajivikas is attested by the Barabar Cave dedications made by Ashoka after Bindusara's death � the caves were given to the Ajivika ascetics, perhaps in honour of Bindusara's religious preferences.

Bindusara maintained diplomatic relations with the Hellenistic world. He received the Greek ambassador Deimachus from the Seleucid king Antiochus I Soter (son of Seleucus Nicator). According to the Greek writer Athenaeus, Bindusara sent a letter to Antiochus asking him to purchase and send: sweet wine, dried figs, and a Sophist philosopher. Antiochus replied: 'We shall send you the figs and the wine, but in Greece it is not lawful to sell a philosopher.' This anecdote, preserved in the Deipnosophistae (c. 3rd century CE), not only confirms the diplomatic contacts between the Mauryan and Seleucid courts but also reveals the cultural curiosity of the Mauryan court towards Greek intellectual traditions. Bindusara's reign was long and stable, providing the secure foundation upon which Ashoka would later build.

Ashoka the Great (268-232 BCE - actual coronation 269 BCE)

Ashoka (Devanampriya Priyadarshi � 'Beloved of the Gods, of Gracious Mien') is the single most famous ruler in ancient Indian history � arguably in all of India's pre-modern history. He was not the eldest son of Bindusara � that was Susima, the crown prince. Ashoka was appointed governor of Taxila (the north-western provincial capital) where he suppressed a revolt and gained administrative experience. Later, he was posted as governor of Ujjain (the western capital), where he met and married Devi, a merchant's daughter from Vidisha � the mother of his children Mahinda and Sanghamitta. After Bindusara's death, a bitter succession war lasted four years (c. 272-269 BCE) before Ashoka prevailed. The Buddhist text Ashokavadana claims he killed 99 of his brothers, but this is likely a literary exaggeration drawn from a stock motif of fratricide in Indian dynastic legends � only two brothers, Susima and Vigatashoka, are named in reliable sources. His coronation (abhiseka) was finally performed in 269 BCE, four years after his de facto accession.

In the 8th year of his reign (c. 261 BCE), Ashoka conquered Kalinga (modern Odisha) � a rich, independent maritime kingdom on the east coast that had resisted Magadhan expansion since the time of Ajatashatru (c. 5th century BCE). Kalinga's strategic and economic importance was immense: it controlled the Bay of Bengal coastline, gave access to the sea-trade routes to Southeast Asia and Sri Lanka, and was rich in resources including elephants, textiles, and gems. The Kalinga War was the turning point of Ashoka's life and world history.

The Kalinga War (c. 261 BCE)

The 13th Major Rock Edict provides the only contemporary account of the war. The numbers are staggering: '150,000 people were deported, 100,000 were killed, and many more died from the aftermath.' The Edict records Ashoka's profound and unexpected remorse: 'Even the one-hundredth part or the one-thousandth part of all the people who suffered... would be considered deplorable now.' What makes this document extraordinary is that no other ancient conqueror � not Alexander, not Julius Caesar, not Ashoka's contemporary Qin Shi Huang � expressed remorse for the casualties of war. The 13th Edict continues: 'Even if a person living in the forest is wronged by someone, the Beloved of the Gods considers it deplorable.' Ashoka declared that 'the Beloved of the Gods now considers that conquest by Dhamma is the only true conquest.' He names the five Hellenistic kings who ruled the successor kingdoms of Alexander's empire � Antiochus II Theos of Syria (the Seleucid king, grandson of Seleucus Nicator), Ptolemy II Philadelphos of Egypt, Antigonus Gonatas of Macedonia, Magas of Cyrene, and Alexander of Epirus � and notes that Dhamma conquest had reached them, along with the unconquered peoples of the forest, the Cholas, the Pandyas, the Satiyaputras, and the Keralaputras in the far south. Ashoka renounced war forever, embraced Buddhism (he became an upasaka � a lay devotee � in the 8th year, and his devotion deepened over time; according to Sri Lankan chronicles, he later became a full bhikkhu and even entered the Sangha, though this is debated), and adopted the policy of Dhamma (Dharma � righteous conduct, moral law, not Buddhist doctrine per se but a universal ethical code). He became the only military ruler in world history to abandon armed conquest after a military victory.

Ashoka's Dhamma: Ideology and Practice

Ashoka's policy of Dhamma was not a state religion but a universal code of social ethics, carved into stone pillars and rock faces across the empire for all to read (14 Major Rock Edicts, Minor Rock Edicts, 7 Pillar Edicts, 4 Minor Pillar Edicts, 2 Tarai Pillar Edicts, 3 Cave Dedications � the entire corpus comprises over 40 separate inscriptions). The Dhamma may be understood as Ashoka's attempt to forge a unifying imperial ideology that transcended sectarian differences.

Key principles of Dhamma:

  • Ahimsa (Non-violence): Ashoka banned animal sacrifices, drastically reduced meat consumption in the royal kitchen (from thousands of animals daily to just two peacocks and a deer), and listed 25 categories of protected animals in the 5th Pillar Edict � including parrots, geese, bats, tortoises, porcupines, squirrels, swans, pigeons, and all four-footed animals that are neither useful nor edible.
  • Religious Tolerance: The 12th Major Rock Edict declares: 'All sects deserve reverence for one reason or another. By thus acting, a person exalts his own sect and at the same time does service to the sects of others.' This is the earliest known state endorsement of religious pluralism in world history.
  • Social Responsibility: Ashoka appointed a new cadre of officers � the Dhamma-mahamattas (officers of righteousness) � to enforce moral principles, promote welfare, supervise charitable institutions, and ensure justice. They were instructed to care for the welfare of prisoners, the aged, the infirm, and women.
  • Non-aggression in Foreign Policy: Ashoka renounced war and conquest. The 13th Edict explicitly declares that 'the Beloved of the Gods considers that conquest by Dhamma is the best conquest.' He established friendly relations with all neighbouring kingdoms, including the Hellenistic monarchies mentioned above. This was the world's first documented foreign policy based on non-violence.
  • Public Works and Welfare: Ashoka's edicts record that he dug wells, planted shade trees along roads (banyan and mango trees, every half-kos � about every 2 kilometres), established hospitals for both humans and animals (among the first animal hospitals in world history), built rest houses, and provided herbal medicines for both humans and animals across the empire and in neighbouring kingdoms.

The Edicts of Ashoka: Corpus, Languages, Scripts, and Geography

The Ashokan edicts constitute the oldest decipherable corpus of Indian inscriptions. They are written in three languages: Prakrit (the vernacular of the Mauryan court, in both Brahmi and Kharosthi scripts), Greek (in the Kandahar region � the Greek script was used for the Greek version of the edicts found at Kandahar and Shar-i-Kuna), and Aramaic (the administrative language of the Achaemenid Empire, which had ruled the northwest before the Mauryas � found at Taxila, Pul-i-Darunteh, and Laghman). Two scripts were used: Brahmi (written left-to-right, the parent script of almost all modern Indian scripts � Devanagari, Bengali, Tamil, etc.) and Kharosthi (written right-to-left, derived from the Aramaic alphabet, used in the northwest � Gandhara region). The edicts are not royal decrees issued from the capital � they are personally composed messages from Ashoka to his subjects, written in the first person ('Devamapiyana Piyedasi Lahika' � 'Thus says Devanampriya Priyadarshi'). They were placed at strategic locations: along major trade routes, at pilgrimage centres (Lumbini, Bodhgaya, Sarnath), at provincial capitals (Taxila, Ujjain, Tosali, Suvarnagiri), and at the borders of the empire.

The locations of the edicts define the geographical extent of Ashoka's empire. Major Rock Edicts are found at Girnar (Gujarat), Sopara (Maharashtra, near Mumbai), Dhauli (Odisha), Jaugada (Odisha), Kalsi (Uttarakhand, in the Yamuna valley near the Himalayas), Shahabazgarhi (Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Pakistan), Mansehra (Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Pakistan), Erragudi (Andhra Pradesh), and Maski (Karnataka). Pillar Edicts are located at Delhi-Topra, Delhi-Meerut, Lauriya-Araraj, Lauriya-Nandangarh, Rampurva (all in Bihar), and Allahabad (originally at Kaushambi). Minor Rock Edicts extend the reach further south � Brahmagiri, Siddapur, Jatinga-Rameshwara (all in Karnataka), Yerragudi (Andhra), and even Maski (where the edict uses the name 'Asoka' directly � the only edict to do so). The geographical spread confirms that Ashoka's empire extended from Afghanistan in the northwest to Karnataka in the south, and from Odisha in the east to Gujarat in the west.

Ashoka and Buddhism: The Third Buddhist Council and Missions

Ashoka's personal faith deepened over time. The Minor Rock Edict I (found at Brahmagiri, Maski, Rupanath, Sahasram) declares he became an upasaka (lay devotee) two and a half years before the edict was issued, but that his effort (prakrama) intensified in the following year. The Bairat Edict (found at Bairat, Rajasthan) is addressed to the Buddhist Sangha and recommends the study of seven specific Buddhist texts � including the Vinaya Samukase (the summary of monastic discipline), the Anagata-bhayani (the discourse on future dangers), and the Muni-gatha (the verses of the sage). This shows Ashoka's intimate familiarity with Buddhist doctrinal literature.

According to the Sri Lankan chronicles (Mahavamsa, Dipavamsa), Ashoka convened the Third Buddhist Council at Pataliputra around 250 BCE, under the presidency of the elder monk Moggaliputta Tissa (whom Ashoka had brought out of retirement). The council had two main purposes: (1) to purify the Sangha of heretical monks and false doctrines that had crept in as a result of the Sangha's rapid growth and state patronage, and (2) to compile and finalise the Buddhist canon (the Tipitaka or 'Three Baskets' � the Vinaya Pitaka on monastic discipline, the Sutta Pitaka on discourses, and the Abhidhamma Pitaka on philosophy). The council approved the Kathavatthu ('Points of Controversy'), a text by Moggaliputta Tissa refuting heretical views. This council did not include the Mahasanghika school (the precursors of Mahayana Buddhism), marking a significant schism between the orthodox Theravada and the more liberal Mahasanghika traditions.

After the council, Moggaliputta Tissa and Ashoka decided to send missionaries (dhammadutas) to nine different regions to spread the Buddha's teachings. The most famous missions were:

  • To Sri Lanka (Tambapanni): Led by Ashoka's own son Mahinda (a monk) and his daughter Sanghamitta (a nun). According to the Mahavamsa, Mahinda converted King Devanampiya Tissa of Sri Lanka, and Sanghamitta brought a cutting of the original Bodhi tree from Bodhgaya, which was planted at Anuradhapura � the Sri Maha Bodhi, which still stands today, the oldest historically documented tree in the world. This mission led to the establishment of Theravada Buddhism as the dominant religion of Sri Lanka.
  • To Suvarnabhumi ('The Golden Land'): Led by the monks Sona and Uttara, this mission targeted Southeast Asia � modern Myanmar (Burma), Thailand, and possibly the Malay peninsula. The tradition of Theravada Buddhism in Myanmar traces its origins to this mission.
  • To the Hellenistic World: Ashoka sent emissaries to the courts of the five Hellenistic kings named in the 13th Edict � Antiochus II (Syria), Ptolemy II (Egypt), Antigonus (Macedonia), Magas (Cyrene), and Alexander (Epirus). While there is no independent Greek record of these missions, the names are so precisely given in the edict that their historicity is widely accepted by scholars. These emissaries may have planted the seeds of Buddhist influence in the Mediterranean world, though there is no evidence of mass conversion.

Ashoka's Public Works and Administration of Welfare

Ashoka's edicts record a remarkable programme of public welfare that has few parallels in the ancient world. The 2nd Major Rock Edict states: 'Everywhere in the dominions of the Beloved of the Gods... arrangements have been made for medical treatment for humans and animals. Medicinal herbs, whether useful to humans or useful to animals, have been imported and planted wherever they did not grow. Similarly, roots and fruits have been imported and planted wherever they did not grow. Wells have been dug and trees planted along the roads for the enjoyment of humans and animals.' Ashoka's hospitals for animals are among the earliest known in world history � predating similar institutions in Europe by over 1,500 years. The rest-houses (sabhas) built at half-kos intervals along all major roads provided shelter for travellers. The Dhamma-mahamattas were appointed specifically from the 13th year of Ashoka's reign (c. 256 BCE) and were a new class of officers distinct from the regular administrative hierarchy. They were tasked with promoting Dhamma among all sects, supervising charitable works, looking after the welfare of prisoners (the 5th Pillar Edict instructs that prisoners condemned to death should be given three days' reprieve so their families could appeal), and ensuring justice for the poor and the marginalised. A special category of Dhamma-mahamattas � the itthijhaka mahamattas � were appointed specifically to look after the welfare of women in the royal household and across the empire.

Ashoka's Attitude towards the Forest Tribes (Atavika Rajyas)

The 13th Major Rock Edict mentions the forest peoples (atavika rajyas) who lived within and along the borders of the Mauryan Empire. Ashoka's attitude towards them is revealing: he addresses them in a tone of paternalistic benevolence mixed with firm authority. 'Even the forest people, who live in the dominions of the Beloved of the Gods, are entreated and reasoned with by him. They are told that they should be ashamed of their wrongdoings and should not be killed.' He clearly had not fully extended his principle of ahimsa to the tribal peoples � he warns them that if they resist, the king possesses the power to punish them. This dual strategy � persuasion backed by force � is characteristic of Ashoka's pragmatic approach to governance.

Mauryan Administration

The Arthashastra provides the blueprint for Mauryan governance, though whether it reflects the actual administration of Chandragupta or is a theoretical ideal remains debated. The empire was divided into several provinces: the metropolitan province (with Pataliputra as capital), and four provincial capitals � Taxila (northwest, controlling the passes into Central Asia and Afghanistan), Ujjain (west, controlling the Malwa region and trade routes to the Arabian Sea), Suvarnagiri (south, controlling the gold mines of Karnataka and the Deccan), and Tosali (east, controlling Kalinga/Odisha). These provinces were ruled by kumaras (royal princes) who held the title of viceroy (uparaja or kumara-amatya). The king was the head of state, aided by a council of ministers (mantri-parishad) comprising the chief minister (mukhya-mantrin), commander-in-chief (senapati), chief priest (purohita), chief judge (pradvivaka), treasurer (sannidhata), collector-general (samaharta), and the chief superintendent of the royal harem (antahpura-adhyaksha).

Central Administration � The 28 Superintendents (Adhyakshas): The Arthashastra describes 28 superintendents in charge of various departments of the state. The most important included: sita-adhyaksha (superintendent of crown agriculture), akara-adhyaksha (mines and metallurgy), kupyadhyaksha (forests and forest produce), govyadhyaksha (cattle and animal husbandry), panya-adhyaksha (commerce and trade), shulka-adhyaksha (tolls and customs), pautava-adhyaksha (weights and measures), sutra-adhyaksha (spinning and weaving), suradhyaksha (liquor and excise), navadhyaksha (shipping and ports), lakshanadhyaksha (precious metals and the mint), mudradhyaksha (passports and seals), nagaraka (superintendent of the city), vivitadhyaksha (pasture lands), sulkadhyaksha (tolls), ganikadhyaksha (courtesans and entertainment), devatadhyaksha (temples and religious establishments), gajasthana-adhyaksha (elephant forests and stables), ashva-adhyaksha (horses and cavalry stables), rathadhyaksha (chariots), padadhyaksha (infantry), lohadhyaksha (iron mines and metalworking), lavana-adhyaksha (salt production), dhanyadhyaksha (granaries and food storage), vanikpatha-adhyaksha (trade routes), bhadrashreni-adhyaksha (coastal trade), koshtagarika (treasury), and durgapala (fortifications). Each adhyaksha ran a department staffed by subordinate clerks, accountants, and inspectors, forming the most elaborate bureaucratic apparatus of the ancient world outside China.

Urban Administration � The 6 Boards of Pataliputra: Megasthenes' Indica (through the fragments preserved by Strabo and Arrian) describes the municipal administration of Pataliputra, managed by a commission of 30 members divided into 6 boards of 5 members each: (1) Board of Industries and Arts � overseeing manufacturing, crafts, and artisans; (2) Board of Trade and Commerce � regulating weights, measures, prices, markets, and foreign merchants; (3) Board of Public Records � maintaining birth and death registrations; (4) Board of Inspections and Licensing � inspecting manufactured goods, issuing licenses, and regulating the sale of goods by measure and weight; (5) Board of Public Welfare � supervising foreigners, managing inns and hostels, and registering births and deaths; (6) Board of Religious Affairs � regulating temples, festivals, and religious endowments. This sophisticated urban administration was praised by Megasthenes as more efficient than similar institutions in Greek cities.

Village and District Administration: Below the provinces, the administrative hierarchy descended through layers of territorial units. The gramika (village headman) was the lowest official, responsible for each individual village. Above him, the gopa was the village accountant responsible for 5-10 villages � he maintained records of land ownership, livestock, tax payments, and the movement of people. The sthanika was the district officer in charge of a sthana (a district comprising several hundred villages). The samaharta (collector-general) was the chief revenue officer of the entire empire, while pradesikas were provincial revenue officers and rajukas were survey and assessment officers who measured land and assessed revenue. Land revenue, the principal source of state income, was 1/4th to 1/6th of the produce (bhaga) � the state's share varied depending on soil quality, irrigation, and type of crop.

The Tax System: The Mauryan state levied a comprehensive range of taxes. Bhaga was the land-revenue share (1/4 to 1/6 of produce). Bali was a voluntary offering that became a compulsory tax. Kara was a general tax on non-agricultural households. Sulka was the customs and toll duty on goods entering or leaving markets. Pindakara was a lump-sum tax on villages. Hiranya was a tax paid in gold or precious metals. Pranaya was a special levy imposed during emergencies (the Arthashastra authorises the king to demand 1/4 to 1/3 of agricultural produce in emergencies). Tax exemptions were granted to Brahmins engaged in Vedic studies, ascetics, and persons afflicted by disease or calamity.

The Spy Network (Gudhapurushas): The Arthashastra devotes an entire book (Book 2, 'The Activities of Superintendents') and significant parts of Book 1 to the state's espionage apparatus. Spies (gudhapurushas, meaning 'secret persons') were classified into several types: kapatika (the fraudulent disciple � a student who pretends to be virtuous while gathering intelligence), udvasthita (the apostate monk � a renunciate who spies for the state), grihapaitaka (the householder-spy), vaidehaka (the merchant-spy), tapasa (the ascetic-spy), tikshna (the assassin-poisoner, used for elimination), rasada (the poisoner, who administers poison to enemies), and bhikshuki (female spy disguised as a mendicant nun). Spies operated in pairs or groups, unknown to each other, reporting directly to the king or the chief minister. The spy network was a central pillar of Mauryan authoritarian control.

Military Organization: The Mauryan military was legendary for its size and sophistication. According to Pliny (citing Megasthenes), the army comprised 600,000 infantry, 30,000 cavalry, 9,000 elephants, and 8,000 chariots � the largest standing army of the post-Alexandrian world. The Arthashastra describes a military administration divided into 6 departments: infantry (padadhyaksha), cavalry (ashvadhyaksha), elephants (gajadhyaksha or hastiadhyaksha), chariots (rathadhyaksha), navy (navadhyaksha), and commissariat and transport (vivitadhyaksha) � a structure remarkably similar to the modern Indian Army's organization of arms and services. The superintendent of elephants (gajadhyaksha) was particularly important: elephants were the 'tank' of ancient warfare, breaking enemy infantry lines. The Arthashastra describes the ideal elephant in detail � minimum age, height, temper, and the proper method of capture (in designated elephant forests administered by the state). The army was paid in cash (not land grants, as in later feudal systems), ensuring loyalty to the emperor rather than to local commanders.

Mauryan Economy

Agriculture was the backbone of the Mauryan economy. The state owned vast crown lands (sita) farmed by labourers, sharecroppers, and slaves under the supervision of the superintendent of agriculture (sita-adhyaksha). The Arthashastra prescribes that the state should directly manage the best lands, using wage labourers (karmakaras) and slaves (dasa-bhritakas), while private farmers held inheritable rights over their land and paid the state share of produce. The sita lands produced grains (rice, wheat, barley, millet), oilseeds (sesame, mustard), sugarcane, cotton, and lentils. Crop rotation, manuring, and irrigation were all systematically described in the Arthashastra. The state advanced agricultural loans (karshaka-riddhi) to farmers during sowing season.

Irrigation: The Mauryan state invested heavily in irrigation infrastructure. The most famous example is the Sudarshana Lake in Junagadh, Gujarat, built by the Mauryan governor of Saurashtra, Pushyagupta (a Vaishya by caste, according to the Junagadh inscription), using local labour. The artificial lake, created by building a dam across the Suvarnarekha river, irrigated thousands of acres. It was later repaired after a severe flood by the Shaka ruler Rudradaman I in 150 CE (recorded in the Junagadh Rock Inscription of Rudradaman � the first long Sanskrit inscription) and again repaired under the Gupta emperor Skandagupta in the 5th century CE. The Arthashastra prescribes that the state should build dams, tanks, and canals, and should exempt from taxes for five years any farmer who builds his own irrigation work.

Trade and Commerce: The Mauryan period saw a dramatic expansion of both internal and external trade. Internal trade moved along several arterial routes: the Uttarapatha (the northern route) connecting Pataliputra to Taxila and the northwest; the Dakshinapatha (the southern route) connecting Pataliputra to the Deccan via Ujjain and Pratishthana; and the eastern routes connecting to Kalinga and the Bay of Bengal ports. External trade flourished with three major regions: the Hellenistic world (through the Indus delta and the Persian Gulf, as well as overland through Bactria), Southeast Asia (Suvarnabhumi � 'The Golden Land' � probably modern Myanmar, Thailand, and the Malay peninsula), and Sri Lanka (Tambapanni). Major exports from India included: spices (especially pepper from the Malabar coast), pearls (from the Gulf of Mannar), precious gemstones (diamonds from central India, beryl from Coimbatore), cotton textiles (famous as 'muslin' from Bengal and the south), ivory, sandalwood, teak, tortoise shell, peacocks, monkeys, and above all, elephants � the elephant trade was a state monopoly, and the Mauryan king controlled the capture and sale of elephants to foreign purchasers. Major imports into India included: horses (from Central Asia and Arabia � India never had enough horses for its large cavalry), gold and silver bullion (from the Roman world and Central Asia), glassware (from Alexandria and Syria), wine, fine cloth, and luxury items for the royal court.

State Regulation of Trade: The state, through the superintendent of commerce (panyadhyaksha), tightly regulated trade. The Arthashastra prescribes that: prices should be fixed by the state with a 5% profit margin for local goods and 10% for imported goods (covering transport costs and risk); hoarding and cornering of markets was punishable; adulteration of goods was a serious offence; the state maintained public granaries to stabilise grain prices during shortages; goods were weighed and measured at government-stamped weights and measures; customs duties (sulka) were collected at entry points to cities and towns. The superintendent of customs inspected all goods and levied a duty that varied from 1/10th to 1/20th of the value of goods.

Coinage: The Mauryan state issued the first empire-wide coinage system in Indian history. The standard silver coin was the pana (or karshapana), a punch-marked coin weighing approximately 3.4 grams. These coins were not minted in the modern sense (with a die) but were made by cutting a silver sheet into rough square or rectangular pieces and then stamping them with individual punches (the 'punch-marked' technique). The symbols punched included: the sun (a circle with rays), the peacock, the mountain (three hills), the elephant, the tree-in-railing (a sacred tree), the fish, the conch, the swastika, the 'triskelis' (three running legs), and various geometric patterns. The state mint was under the lakshanadhyaksha (superintendent of the mint), and the coins bore the royal mark. Copper coins (masaka and karshapana of copper) were used for smaller transactions. The Arthashastra also mentions gold coins (suvarna) but these were ceremonial rather than everyday currency � no gold Mauryan coins have been found archaeologically.

Northern Black Polished Ware (NBPW): The Mauryan period is closely associated with Northern Black Polished Ware (NBPW) � a high-quality, wheel-made pottery with a distinctive glossy black or steel-grey surface, produced in the Gangetic basin and the central Deccan. The surface was created by applying a fine slip of iron-rich clay and firing it in a reducing kiln at high temperature, producing a mirror-like finish. NBPW is the archaeological marker of the Mauryan period � its presence at a site is considered diagnostic of Mauryan occupation. It was used by the elite for dining and ceremonial purposes and was widely traded across the Mauryan empire. The finest examples come from the Mauryan capital Pataliputra (modern Kumrahar, Patna).

Industries and Guilds (Shrenis): The Mauryan economy had a vibrant manufacturing sector. Textiles were the largest industry � silk from Varanasi and cotton from Bengal were famous across the ancient world. Metallurgy was highly advanced: the Mauryan smiths produced high-quality iron (for tools, weapons, and the famous Ashokan pillars), gold and silver (for coinage and ornaments), copper, lead, tin, and bronze. Ivory carving (from the abundant Indian elephants) and gem-cutting (diamonds, rubies, sapphires, beryl) were specialized crafts. The shrenis (guilds) were powerful corporate organizations of artisans and merchants. The Arthashastra recognizes the shrenis as autonomous entities, but the state supervised them through the superintendent of trade. The shrenis functioned as trade unions, banks (they accepted deposits and lent money at interest), and even as local militia forces � they had their own military units that could be called upon by the state. Guilds of weavers, metalworkers, ivory carvers, potters, and oil-pressers are mentioned in Mauryan and post-Mauryan sources.

Prices and Wages (from the Arthashastra): The Arthashastra provides specific prices and wages that give us insight into the Mauryan economy. A labourer (karmakara) earned 1 pana per month plus food; a skilled artisan earned 1.25 panas; a carpenter or blacksmith earned 1.5 panas; a physician earned 2-4 panas; a teacher or priest received 4-8 panas. Agricultural land was valued at 100 panas per 'kulyavapa' (a measure of land sufficient to produce one kulya of grain). A milch cow cost 10-12 panas; a horse cost 200-1,000 panas (depending on quality); a war elephant cost 12,000-36,000 panas. Fines for various offences were also specified � for example, cutting a tree was fined 10-25 panas; damaging a city wall was fined 100-500 panas. These figures indicate a highly monetised economy where state and private transactions were regularly conducted in coin.

Mauryan Art and Architecture

The Mauryan period saw the first monumental stone architecture and sculpture in India. The art of the Mauryan period can be broadly divided into two streams: court art (royal patronage � pillars, palaces, stupas, caves) and popular art (folk traditions � yaksha and yakshini statues, terracotta figurines, folk motifs).

Ashokan Pillars: The most spectacular surviving monuments of the Mauryan period are the monolithic sandstone pillars erected by Ashoka across his empire. These pillars were quarried at Chunar (near Varanasi, Uttar Pradesh) from fine-grained, hard, buff-coloured sandstone, and transported over distances of hundreds of kilometres � a pillar at Lauriya-Nandangarh (Bihar) weighs about 50 tons and was transported over 400 km from Chunar. Each pillar is a single piece of stone (monolithic), 12-15 metres in height, weighing 40-50 tons. The surface was polished to a mirror-like finish using a technique now known as the 'Mauryan polish' � a high-gloss finish achieved by applying a fine emulsion of sand and polishing powder and then rubbing with cloth or leather over a long period. This technique was lost after the Mauryan period and was never fully replicated. The pillar consists of three parts: the shaft (a tapering cylinder, often inscribed with Ashoka's edicts), the capital (a bell-shaped lotus or inverted lotus), and the crowning animal (the most famous being the four lions at Sarnath). Surviving pillars include those at Sarnath (lions � the most famous), Rampurva (bull), Sankissa (elephant), Lauriya-Araraj (lion), Lauriya-Nandangarh (lion), Allahabad/Kaushambi (lion, now at Allahabad Fort), Delhi-Topra (lion, now in Delhi), Delhi-Meerut (lion, now in Delhi), and Nandangarh (lion). The Lion Capital at Lauriya-Nandangarh is particularly well-preserved. The pillars are not merely decorative � they are functional monuments designed to display Ashoka's edicts publicly. The pillar shaft was erected first, then the capital was lowered onto it and fixed with an iron dowel.

The Sarnath Lion Capital � A Detailed Study: The Sarnath Lion Capital, now in the Sarnath Museum, is the most celebrated example of Mauryan sculpture and was adopted as the National Emblem of India in 1950. It originally crowned the Ashokan pillar at Sarnath, marking the site where the Buddha delivered his first sermon (the Dharmachakra Pravartana Sutta � 'Turning of the Wheel of Dharma'). The capital is carved from a single block of Chunar sandstone, highly polished. It consists of four parts from bottom to top: (1) a bell-shaped lotus (the base, representing the cosmic waters from which creation emerges � a common Indian and ancient Near Eastern symbol); (2) a circular abacus adorned with four animals in relief � an elephant (representing the East), a horse (South), a bull (West), and a lion (North) � separated by four smaller dharma-chakras (wheels of law), each with 24 spokes; (3) four lions seated back-to-back on the abacus, their muscles tensed, their mouths open in a roar � representing the Buddha's proclamation of the law to the four quarters of the universe; (4) a dharma-chakra originally crowned the entire capital (only fragments of this wheel survive, and the chakra is now kept separately at the Sarnath Museum). The four lions symbolise Ashoka's sovereignty, the Buddha's universal message, and the spread of Dhamma in all directions. The 24-spoke wheel appears on the Indian national flag. The carving is extraordinarily detailed � the lions' manes are rendered in flame-like curls, their claws are sharply defined, and the muscles of their haunches are tensed as if about to spring.

Stupas: According to Sri Lankan Buddhist tradition (Mahavamsa), Ashoka built 84,000 stupas across his empire to house the relics of the Buddha, which he obtained by opening seven of the original eight relic stupas built by the clans after the Buddha's cremation. While the number 84,000 is symbolic (it represents the number of dharma skandhas or categories of Buddha's teachings), many Mauryan-period stupas have been archaeologically identified. The most famous is the Great Stupa at Sanchi (Madhya Pradesh), which was originally a simple brick hemisphere (anda, 'egg') built by Ashoka, about half the size of the present structure. The original Mauryan brick stupa was later enlarged and faced with stone during the Shunga period (c. 2nd-1st century BCE), and the four elaborate gateways (toranas) were added during the Satavahana period (c. 1st century BCE-1st century CE). Excavations at Sanchi have revealed Ashokan-period bricks 40 cm long � a distinctive Mauryan size. Other important Ashokan-period stupas include those at Bharhut (Madhya Pradesh, expanded by the Sungas), Dhauli (Odisha, associated with the Kalinga War), Lauriya-Nandangarh (Bihar), and Bairat (Rajasthan). The stupa, originally a simple burial mound, became the primary monument of Buddhist worship, symbolising the Buddha's Mahaparinirvana (final passing away).

Barabar Caves: The Barabar Caves (Barabar Hills, near Gaya, Bihar) are the earliest surviving rock-cut caves in India and mark the beginning of India's long and distinguished tradition of rock-cut architecture. Ashoka dedicated three caves at Barabar to the Ajivika sect (perhaps in honour of his father Bindusara's patronage of the sect). These are: (1) the Lomas Rishi Cave � the most famous, with a beautifully carved entrance imitating a wooden thatched hut with a chaitya-arch (gavaksha or chandrashala) doorway, which became the prototype for all later Indian cave architecture; (2) the Sudama Cave � a circular chamber with a rectangular antechamber, both with highly polished interiors; (3) the Karna Chaupar Cave � a rectangular hall with polished interior. The interiors of all three caves were given the same mirror-like 'Mauryan polish' as the pillars � the walls gleam as if made of black glass. An inscription in the Lomas Rishi Cave records: 'By Devanampriya Priyadarshi, in the 12th year of his reign, this cave was given to the Ajivikas.' Two further caves in the nearby Nagarjuni Hills were donated by Ashoka's grandson Dasharatha.

The Palace of Pataliputra: The Mauryan royal palace at Pataliputra (modern Kumrahar, near Patna) was described by Megasthenes (through fragments in Strabo, Arrian, and Aelian) as surpassing the Persian palaces of Susa and Ecbatana in magnificence. The palace was built primarily of wood (the Mauryas had an abundant supply of timber from the Himalayan foothills), but the excavated remains at Kumrahar (unearthed by the Archaeological Survey of India in the 1920s-1950s) reveal a massive hypostyle hall with 80 sandstone pillars (each about 30 feet tall) arranged in a grid pattern of 8 rows of 10 pillars. The pillars were also given the Mauryan polish, and the hall was probably the audience chamber of the Mauryan emperors. The palace complex extended over several acres, surrounded by a wooden palisade with 570 towers and 64 gates (as described by Megasthenes). The timber used was deodar (Cedrus deodara) and sal (Shorea robusta), both highly durable and insect-resistant.

Yaksha and Yakshini Statues: The tradition of monumental stone sculpture in India begins not with court art but with the yaksha (male nature spirit) and yakshini (female nature spirit) statues that were worshipped by the common people. These represent the 'popular art' stream as distinct from the state-sponsored court art. The most important examples are: the Parkham Yaksha (from Parkham, near Mathura, c. 3rd-2nd century BCE � a massive, 2.6-metre-high sandstone figure of a yaksha standing with feet apart, heavy jewellery, a dhoti, and a fierce expression, now in the Mathura Museum); the Didarganj Yakshini (from Didarganj, near Patna, c. 3rd century BCE � a beautifully polished sandstone statue of a yakshini holding a fly-whisk (chauri), with an elaborately carved headdress, heavy earrings, a transparent muslin saree, and a sensuous, confident posture, now in the Patna Museum; it received the Mauryan polish, showing that popular art also adopted the courtly technique); the Manibhadra Yaksha (from Parkham, with an inscription mentioning its installation by a guild of merchants); and numerous smaller yakshini statues from various sites. The Didarganj Yakshini is one of the finest examples of Mauryan-period sculpture � the translucency of the muslin drapery and the soft contours of the body are rendered with extraordinary skill. These yaksha/yakshini figures established the iconographic conventions (frontal posture, heavy ornamentation, stylised anatomy, lotus pedestals) that were later adopted for the first human images of the Buddha in the Mathura school.

Court Art vs Popular Art: The Mauryan period exhibits a fascinating distinction between court art (imperial, polished, formal, official � the pillars, capitals, palace, caves) and popular art (folk, regional, unpolished, religious � the yakshas, terracotta figurines, folk reliefs). The court art is characterised by: the use of Chunar sandstone, the Mauryan polish, highly formalised and symmetrical compositions, and a clear Achaemenid Persian influence (the bell-shaped lotus capital, the use of the abacus, the lion motif). The popular art is characterised by: coarser stone (often local sandstone or spotted red sandstone from Mathura), absence of polish, more naturalistic and vigorous forms, and strong indigenous folk traditions. The interaction between these two streams � the Persian-influenced court art and the indigenous popular tradition � produced the classical Indian sculptural idiom that would fully flower during the Gupta period.

Decline of the Mauryan Empire (c. 232-185 BCE)

The Mauryan Empire declined rapidly after Ashoka's death in 232 BCE, collapsing entirely within 47 years. Historians have proposed multiple interconnected causes:

  • Weak successors after Ashoka: Ashoka's sons and grandsons were weak rulers incapable of managing the vast empire. Kunala (Ashoka's son by his first wife Durdhara) was blinded by a rival queen, Tishyarakshita, according to the Ashokavadana � this episode, whether historical or legendary, symbolises the internal court intrigues that weakened the dynasty. Samprati (Kunala's son, who became a Jain and was a patron of Jainism), Dasharatha (Ashoka's grandson, who maintained Ajivika patronage and dedicated the Nagarjuni caves), Salisuka, Devavarman, Shatadhanvan, and finally Brihadratha (the last Mauryan king, who ruled only a fraction of the original empire) � none of these successors possessed Ashoka's administrative ability, military acumen, or the personal authority to command the loyalty of the provincial governors and generals.
  • Division of the empire: After Ashoka's death, the empire was probably divided into western and eastern halves � possibly under Samprati (western half, based at Ujjain) and Dasharatha (eastern half, based at Pataliputra), or under Kunala's descendants. The provinces, especially Taxila in the northwest, increasingly asserted their independence. The provincial governors (kumaras) who had been royal princes began to act as independent rulers.
  • Financial crisis: The Mauryan state had enormous expenditures: the world's largest standing army (600,000+ troops to maintain, feed, and equip), a massive bureaucracy of superintendents, clerks, and officers, the Dhamma-mahamattas (an entirely new cadre of officers added by Ashoka), the expensive public works programme (hospitals, wells, roads, rest houses � all requiring ongoing maintenance), and the lavish royal court. The policy of Dhamma, with its emphasis on non-violence and welfare, may have reduced the efficiency of tax collection � the state's coercive apparatus was deliberately softened. The gold mines of Suvarnagiri may have been depleted, and the silver mines of the northwest may have become harder to access as central control weakened.
  • Brahmanical reaction: Ashoka's pro-Buddhist policies � his extensive patronage of the Buddhist Sangha (the building of 84,000 stupas, land grants to monasteries, the Third Buddhist Council, state-funded missions), his appointment of Dhamma-mahamattas who supervised all sects (undermining Brahmanical authority), and his ban on animal sacrifices (a direct attack on Brahmanical ritual) � deeply alienated the Brahmin elite, who had been the traditional ideological supporters of kingship in India. The abandonment of the realist Arthashastra in favour of the idealist Dhamma was seen by Brahmin intellectuals as a fundamental error. Pushyamitra Shunga, the last Mauryan commander-in-chief (senapati) and a Brahmin himself, assassinated the last Mauryan king Brihadratha (c. 185 BCE) during a military parade, and established the Shunga dynasty with a deliberate return to Vedic orthodoxy � he performed the Ashvamedha (horse sacrifice), restored the importance of Brahmins, and promoted Sanskrit as the courtly language.
  • Invasions and provincial revolts: The northwest frontier, which had been stable under the strong rule of Chandragupta, Bindusara, and Ashoka, came under increasing pressure from Indo-Greek (Bactrian Greek) incursions under Demetrius and later Menander (c. 190-165 BCE), as well as Parthian and Scythian/Shaka movements into the Indus valley. The Mauryan army, which had been largely ceremonial under Ashoka's policy of non-violence, may have lost its fighting edge. The Kalinga province (now not Ashoka's new conquest to be remorseful about, but a conquered land) may have reasserted its independence soon after Ashoka's death. The Yavana-raja (Greek king) Tulamaya (Ptolemy?) is named in the Hathigumpha inscription of Kharavela of Kalinga as being defeated by Kalinga � suggesting that Kalinga freed itself and even expanded at Mauryan expense.
  • Over-centralisation and over-extension: The empire of 5 million square kilometres was too large to be administered effectively from Pataliputra without modern communications. The provincial capitals (Taxila, Ujjain, Suvarnagiri, Tosali) were separated from the imperial capital by hundreds of kilometres of difficult terrain. The system required a strong and active emperor at the centre to coordinate the vast bureaucracy and keep provincial governors in check � the later Mauryas could not provide this. The provincial kumaras and pradesikas became increasingly autonomous, and the over-extended empire fragmented along natural geographical and political fault lines.
Historiographical Debate: Ashoka's Dhamma
Scholars remain deeply divided on the nature and purpose of Ashoka's Dhamma. Romila Thapar (in her seminal work Ashoka and the Decline of the Mauryas, 1961, revised 1997) argues that Dhamma was not a religious policy at all but a political ideology of social integration � an attempt to create a unified imperial consciousness among the diverse peoples, castes, and sects of the Mauryan Empire by promoting a set of universally acceptable ethical values. Thapar points out that Dhamma deliberately avoids core Buddhist doctrines (the Four Noble Truths, the Eightfold Path, nirvana) and never mentions the Buddha as an authority. According to Thapar, the Dhamma was Ashoka's solution to the problem of ruling a multicultural, multi-religious empire � it was the ideological superstructure of the Mauryan state. G. C. Pande (in his essay 'Ashoka: His Dhamma and His Purpose') argues, by contrast, that Dhamma was deeply influenced by Ashoka's personal Buddhist faith � the Bairat Edict, which recommends specific Buddhist texts, the Third Buddhist Council, the missions, and Ashoka's identification of himself as an upasaka all demonstrate a personal commitment to Buddhism. Pande sees Dhamma as a 'Buddhist-inspired universal ethic' that was deliberately non-sectarian for political reasons. B. M. Barua viewed Dhamma as Ashoka's personal moral philosophy, while N. A. Nikam and R. McKeon (editors of The Edicts of Ashoka) saw it as a 'religion of piety' rooted in Buddhist compassion. The debate is significant for UPSC because it touches on a crucial historical question: is Ashoka's Dhamma evidence of the political use of religion (or religion's influence on politics), or is it an example of a purely secular, ethical universalism � something remarkably modern for a king of the 3rd century BCE? The fact that Ashoka did not make Buddhism the state religion, did not persecute non-Buddhists (unlike Constantine's adoption of Christianity in the Roman Empire, which led to persecution of pagans), and continued to patronise Brahmins and Ajivikas alongside Buddhists, strongly supports Thapar's interpretation. However, Ashoka's paternalistic attitude towards the Sangha � his Schism Edict (Sarnath) ordering the expulsion of dissident monks, his attempt to bureaucratise the Sangha, his recommending of specific texts � shows that his relationship with Buddhism went far beyond personal piety. Perhaps the most balanced view is that Dhamma was a Buddhist-inspired imperial ideology � a creative synthesis of Buddhist ethics, Mauryan political necessity, and Ashoka's personal response to the horrors of the Kalinga War.

• 321 BCE � Chandragupta Maurya founds the Mauryan Empire; captures Pataliputra from Dhana Nanda
• 305 BCE � Treaty with Seleucus Nicator; Arachosia, Gedrosia, Paropamisadae, Aria ceded to Chandragupta; diplomatic marriage alliance; Megasthenes arrives at Pataliputra
• 297-272 BCE � Bindusara's reign; expansion into Deccan; Deimachus arrives from Antiochus I
• 269 BCE � Ashoka's coronation (4 years after accession, following a succession war)
• 261 BCE � Kalinga War; Ashoka converts to Buddhism and adopts Dhamma policy (8th year of reign)
• c. 256 BCE � Appointment of Dhamma-mahamattas (13th year of reign)
• c. 250 BCE � Third Buddhist Council at Pataliputra under Moggaliputta Tissa; Buddhist missions to Sri Lanka (Mahinda, Sanghamitta), Suvarnabhumi, and Hellenistic kingdoms
• 232 BCE � Ashoka's death at age 72 (approximately); rapid decline of the empire begins
• 185 BCE � Brihadratha (last Mauryan king) assassinated by Pushyamitra Shunga; Mauryan Empire ends; Shunga dynasty begins
• 1837 CE � James Prinsep deciphers Brahmi script on Ashokan edicts, unlocking the history of the Mauryas
