7. Post-Mauryan Period (185 BCE-320 CE)

The Post-Mauryan period (also called the 'Middle Period' or 'Age of Small Kingdoms') saw the fragmentation of the Mauryan Empire into multiple successor states, the revival of Brahmanical religion and Vedic sacrifices, and extensive foreign contact through invasions and trade. This was the period of the Indo-Greeks, Shakas (Scythians), Parthians, Kushans (Yuezhi), Hunas (Hephthalites), and indigenous dynasties like the Sungas, Kanvas, Satavahanas, Vakatakas, and Ikshvakus. It was also a period of great commercial and cultural expansion — Indian merchants and Buddhist monks traversed the Silk Route, establishing contacts with the Roman Empire, Central Asia, and Southeast Asia. The Periplus of the Erythraean Sea (c. 60 CE, an anonymous Greek text describing ports and trade routes on the Red Sea, East Africa, and the Indian coast) provides invaluable evidence of India's maritime trade. The epoch also witnessed the rise of Mahayana Buddhism, the composition of the earliest Buddhist art in human form, and the flourishing of the Sangam age in the far south — making this one of the most dynamic and transformative periods in Indian history.

The Shunga Dynasty (185-73 BCE)

Pushyamitra Shunga, the Mauryan commander-in-chief, assassinated the last Mauryan king Brihadratha at a military review and founded the Shunga dynasty. The Puranas and the play Malavikagnimitram by Kalidasa mention him. He revived Vedic orthodoxy — performed two Ashvamedha (horse) sacrifices (the Shunga horse sacrifice is mentioned in the Ayodhya inscription of Dhanadeva), restored the importance of Brahmins, and promoted Sanskrit as the courtly language. The great grammarian Patanjali flourished during his reign — Patanjali's Mahabhashya (Great Commentary) on Panini's Ashtadhyayi is the foundational text of classical Sanskrit grammar and was composed around 150 BCE. Patanjali mentions the Shunga horse sacrifice and gives us invaluable linguistic evidence of spoken Sanskrit in the post-Mauryan period. There is debate about Pushyamitra's persecution of Buddhists (the Divyavadana, a Buddhist text, claims he offered 100 gold dinaras per Buddhist monk head and destroyed monasteries), but most historians consider this polemical exaggeration — the Sanchi and Bharhut stupas were expanded and richly ornamented during Shunga rule, suggesting Buddhist patronage continued. His capital was Pataliputra (later shifted to Vidisha in modern Madhya Pradesh). He fought against the Indo-Greeks under Demetrius in the northwest, defending the Yamuna valley.

His son Agnimitra (the hero of Kalidasa's Malavikagnimitram) ruled from Vidisha. The Shunga realm stretched from the Punjab to the Bay of Bengal and from the Himalayan foothills to the Narmada. However, the later Shungas lost territory to the Satavahanas in the south and the Indo-Greeks in the north-west. The last Shunga king Devabhuti was killed by his minister Vasudeva Kanva in 73 BCE.

Shunga Contributions to Art and Architecture

The Shunga period was a golden age of Indian sculpture and architecture. The Bharhut Stupa (in present-day Madhya Pradesh, c. 150-100 BCE) and the Sanchi Stupa (the Great Stupa at Sanchi, substantially enlarged during the Shunga period from the original Mauryan brick structure) represent the earliest surviving school of Indian stone sculpture. The Bharhut reliefs are particularly important for UPSC and art history:

  • Bharhut Stupa: Its railings and gateways (toranas) are covered with narrative panels depicting the Jataka tales (birth stories of the Buddha). The Buddha is not shown in human form — he is represented symbolically by a footprint (buddhapada), a throne (vajrasana), a bodhi tree, a stupa, a wheel (dharmachakra), or an umbrella. This aniconic tradition was standard before the Gandhara and Mathura schools created the first human images of the Buddha in the 1st-2nd centuries CE. The Bharhut medallions also show yakshas, yakshinis, lotus motifs, and scenes from daily life — they are our best visual source for post-Mauryan material culture (clothing, jewellery, architecture, musical instruments).
  • Sanchi Stupa: The Great Stupa (Stupa 1) was originally a brick structure built by Ashoka. The Shungas enlarged it to twice its original size, faced it with sandstone, and built the first stone railing. The four magnificent toranas (gateways) were added later (c. 1st century BCE-1st century CE, during the Satavahana period). The Shunga-period railings at Sanchi show similar aniconic conventions to Bharhut, with elaborate carvings of lotus scrolls, animals (elephants, horses, lions), and Buddhist symbols.
  • The Shungas also built the Heliodorus Pillar at Vidisha (c. 113 BCE) — erected by Heliodorus, the Greek ambassador of the Indo-Greek king Antialcidas, who converted to Vaishnavism. The pillar, crowned with a Garuda figure, bears an inscription in Prakrit stating: 'This Garuda-standard was erected by Heliodorus, a devotee of Vasudeva (Vishnu-Krishna).' This is the earliest known evidence of the Bhagavata (Vaishnava) tradition in India and a powerful testament to the cultural assimilation of Greeks into Indian religious life.

The Kanva Dynasty (73-28 BCE)

Vasudeva Kanva founded a short-lived dynasty of four rulers. They ruled from Pataliputra over a much-reduced territory (Magadha and parts of UP). They followed Brahmanical religion. The Kanvas are historically insignificant — their main contribution was to mark the end of Magadha's political dominance, which now shifted westward to the Deccan (Satavahanas) and Ujjain (Western Kshatrapas). They were overthrown by the Satavahanas under Simuka or Gautamiputra Satakarni (c. 28 BCE).

The Indo-Greeks (Bactrian Greeks, c. 200-10 BCE)

After the collapse of the Mauryan Empire, Greek rulers from the Greco-Bactrian kingdom (established when the Greek satrap Diodotus declared independence from the Seleucid Empire c. 256 BCE) invaded India. Demetrius I (c. 190-180 BCE) invaded the Indus valley and Punjab, establishing an Indo-Greek kingdom at Taxila. Menander I (Milinda) (c. 165-145 BCE) was the greatest Indo-Greek ruler — famous for his dialogue with the Buddhist monk Nagasena (the Milindapanha — Questions of King Milinda, a classic of Buddhist literature). Menander's capital was at Sakala (Sialkot, modern Pakistan). He converted to Buddhism and is the only Greek king remembered in Indian texts. The Milindapanha is structured as a series of philosophical questions and answers: it discusses the soul (anatta), karma, rebirth, and the nature of nirvana, using vivid similes (the chariot simile for the non-self is the most famous). Indo-Greek rulers issued the earliest dated coins with bilingual legends (Greek on one side, Kharosthi on the other) and introduced the portrait coin — the first ruler portraits on Indian coinage. Their rule was confined to the Kabul valley, Punjab, and parts of eastern Afghanistan. They were eventually replaced by the Shakas and Yuezhi/Kushans. The Indo-Greeks contributed to the development of Gandhara art — the fusion of Hellenistic and Indian artistic styles that produced the first anthropomorphic images of the Buddha in human form.

More than 40 Indo-Greek kings are known from their coins. Other notable rulers include Antialcidas (c. 115-95 BCE), whose ambassador Heliodorus erected the Garuda pillar at Vidisha, and Eucratides (c. 170-145 BCE), who issued massive silver coins (the largest silver coins of the ancient world, weighing up to 42 grams). The Indo-Greeks also introduced Greek military engineering (siege towers, fortified cities) and astronomical knowledge (Greek astrology and the five-planet system were transmitted to India, influencing the Yavanajataka — a Sanskrit text on horoscopy translated from Greek in the 2nd century CE).

The Indo-Parthians (Parthians, c. 19-45 CE)

The Indo-Parthians (also called the Pahlavas in Indian sources) succeeded the Indo-Greeks in parts of the north-west. Their most famous ruler was Gondophares (c. 19-45 CE), whose name survives in the Takht-i-Bahi inscription (c. 46 CE) — one of the earliest dated inscriptions in the region. Gondophares ruled an extensive kingdom covering Arachosia (southern Afghanistan), Sindh, and the Punjab. The Indo-Parthians were Iranian-speaking rulers who continued the Hellenistic administrative traditions of the Indo-Greeks and issued bilingual coins (Greek and Kharosthi) with the king's portrait.

The Indo-Parthians are particularly famous for the Thomas legend. According to the Christian Acts of Thomas (a 3rd-century CE Syriac text), the apostle St. Thomas was sold as a slave to a merchant of King Gondophares and arrived in India to preach Christianity. It is claimed that Thomas built a palace for Gondophares in heaven by distributing the king's wealth to the poor — the king, initially angry, was converted when his brother Gad (or Gath) died and returned to life, describing the heavenly palace. The Thomas tradition links the apostle's mission to the court of Gondophares and later to the Coromandel Coast (Mylapore, Chennai), where he is said to have been martyred. The Indo-Parthians were eventually absorbed by the Kushans under Kujula Kadphises.

The Shakas (Scythians, c. 90 BCE-250 CE)

The Shakas were nomadic tribes from Central Asia (the Sacae or Scythians) who displaced the Indo-Greeks in the northwest and established several ruling houses: the Indo-Scythian rulers of Taxila (Maues, c. 85-60 BCE), the Western Kshatrapas (Satraps) of Ujjain (Nahapana, Chashtana, Rudradaman I — the most famous), and the Kardamaka family. Rudradaman I (c. 130-150 CE) of the Kardamaka family issued the famous Junagadh Rock Inscription (c. 150 CE) — the first long inscription in classical Sanskrit (all earlier royal inscriptions were in Prakrit), which describes his repair of the Sudarshana lake (originally built by the Mauryas) during a severe storm. The inscription was composed by his chief minister and is a landmark in Indian epigraphy: it demonstrates that by 150 CE, Sanskrit had replaced Prakrit as the language of royal eulogies. Rudradaman was a great patron of Sanskrit learning and culture. He is described in the inscription as 'the lord of the whole of western India' who 'by his own prowess, defeated the Yaudheyas' (a republican clan of the Punjab).

The Western Kshatrapas ruled western India (Saurashtra, Malwa, Gujarat, Konkan, and parts of Rajasthan) until c. 410 CE, when they were conquered by the Gupta emperor Chandragupta II (Vikramaditya). They minted extensive silver coinage (drachmas with the king's bust on the obverse and a chaitya/stupa on the reverse, with Brahmi or Kharosthi legends) which shows they had access to rich silver mines and controlled the lucrative trade routes to the Roman Empire. The Shaka era (beginning 78 CE, adopted by the Indian government as the official national calendar in 1957) is named after these rulers. Nahapana (fl. 78 CE), the most powerful early Western Kshatrapa, is known from the Nasik inscriptions of his son-in-law Ushavadata, which record generous donations to Buddhist monasteries — including one inscription that mentions a donation of 300,000 cows and the establishment of free ferries across the rivers of the region. Nahapana's silver coins are found widely and were later overstruck by the Satavahana king Gautamiputra Satakarni after his victory over the Shakas.

The Kushans (c. 30-375 CE)

The Kushans were a clan of the Yuezhi confederation — nomadic people from the Gansu region of western China, driven west by the Xiongnu (Huns) in the 2nd century BCE. They conquered the Greco-Bactrian kingdom, then the Indo-Greek territories, and established a vast empire stretching from Central Asia (Bactria/Afghanistan) to the Gangetic plain. Their empire connected the Silk Route — controlling both the overland routes through Central Asia and the sea routes through the Indus delta.

Kushan Rulers and Chronology

  • Kujula Kadphises (c. 30-80 CE): First Kushan ruler, unified the Yuezhi tribes, conquered the Kabul valley, Kashmir, and Parthian territories. Issued coins imitating Roman aurei. Called himself 'Great King, King of Kings, Son of God, the Great Saviour' in the Rabatak inscription.
  • Vima Kadphises (c. 80-100 CE): Expanded into the Gangetic plain; introduced the first Indian gold coinage (modelled on Roman aurei); issued coins showing Shiva with a bull (Nandi) with the legend 'Siva' in Greek letters — the earliest representation of a Hindu deity on a coin.
  • Kanishka (c. 127-150 CE): The greatest Kushan ruler. His empire extended from Mathura to Central Asia, with two capitals — Purushapura (Peshawar) in summer and Mathura in winter. He convened the Fourth Buddhist Council in Kashmir (Kundalavana) under Vasumitra and Asvaghosha. He built the tallest stupa in the ancient world at Peshawar (the 'Kanishka Stupa', estimated at 150-180 metres tall). He patronised the Gandhara and Mathura schools of art. The earliest known representation of the Buddha in human form appears on his coins.
  • Huvishka (c. 150-190 CE): Continued patronage of Buddhism; his coins show a wide pantheon of Greek, Persian, Indian, and Zoroastrian deities — reflecting the Kushan role as cultural crossroads.
  • Vasudeva I (c. 190-230 CE): Last of the great Kushan rulers; later Kushans were weak and lost territory to the Sassanians (the Kushano-Sassanian period followed) and the rising Gupta empire.

The Rabatak Inscription

The Rabatak Inscription (found at Rabatak, Afghanistan, in 1993) is one of the most important epigraphic discoveries of the 20th century. Written in Bactrian language using Greek script, it records that Kanishka ordered the erection of a sanctuary and the installation of deities to legitimise his rule. It traces the Kushan genealogy from Kujula Kadphises through Vima Takto and Vima Kadphises to Kanishka, establishing: (1) the lineage connecting the great Kushans, (2) Kanishka's conquests extending 'from the Kashi region (Varanasi) to Saketa (Ayodhya)' — proving his control over the central Gangetic plain, (3) the earliest use of the title 'Devaputra' (Son of God / Son of Heaven) by a Kushan ruler, a borrowing from Chinese imperial ideology, and (4) the syncretic religious policy of the Kushans, with the inscription naming a set of deities including the Iranian goddess Nana, the Greek god Helios, and the Indian god Shiva (Oesho in Bactrian). The inscription revolutionised the chronology of the early Kushan period.

Kushan Administration and Coinage

The Kushans administered their vast empire through a system of satraps (kshatrapas) and great satraps (maha-kshatrapas), a Persian-derived title that the Shakas also used. Provinces were governed by these satraps who reported to the king. The Kushans were remarkably adaptive — they used Greek (in Bactria), Kharosthi (in Gandhara), and Brahmi (in India) scripts on their coins and inscriptions, and their administration absorbed local traditions wherever they ruled. The Kushan coinage system was the most sophisticated in ancient India:

  • Gold coinage: The Kushans were the first Indian dynasty to issue gold coins on a large scale. Kanishka's gold dinaras (weighing about 7.9 grams, matching the Roman aureus) are among the most beautiful ancient coins. Over 30 different deities appear on Kanishka's gold coins — Greek (Helios, Selene, Hephaistos), Iranian (Mithra, Verethragna, Ardoksho, Nana, Atar, Mao), and Indian (Buddha as 'Boddo', Shiva as 'Oesho'). The gold for these coins came from Roman trade (Pliny's complaint about 100 million sesterces flowing to India is directly connected to Kushan control of the trade routes) and possibly from Siberian and Central Asian gold mines.
  • Copper coinage: Vast quantities of copper coins (tetradrachms and smaller denominations) were issued for daily transactions, found in hoards across the Gangetic plain and the north-west. These provide the primary archaeological evidence for the monetisation of the economy in the Kushan period.
  • The Kushan monetary system established the standard that the Guptas later adopted — the Gupta gold dinara is a direct continuation of the Kushan tradition.

Gandhara vs Mathura Schools of Art

The Kushan period witnessed the simultaneous flourishing of two distinct schools of Buddhist sculpture — the Gandhara School (in the region of Peshawar, Taxila, and the Kabul valley, corresponding to modern Pakistan and eastern Afghanistan) and the Mathura School (in and around Mathura in the central Gangetic plain). Both schools produced the first human images of the Buddha in the 1st-2nd centuries CE, breaking with the earlier aniconic tradition. A detailed comparison:

FeatureGandhara SchoolMathura School
MaterialGrey schist (blue-grey or greenish-grey stone) — fine-grained, allows detailed carving; later also stucco (lime plaster) for mass productionRed sandstone with white spots (from the Sikri quarries near Mathura) — warm, flesh-coloured stone
InfluencesHellenistic (Greek), Roman, Persian (Achaemenid) — shows strong Classical realismIndigenous Indian (yaksha/yakshini traditions, folk art) — draws on the earlier Mauryan/Shunga sculptural tradition
Buddha's HairWavy, curly hair tied in a topknot (ushnisha) — resembling Greek Apollo/HeraclesSmooth, tightly curled spirals (snail-shell curls) covering the head; the ushnisha is a distinct knob on top
Facial FeaturesGreek nose (straight, sharp), deep-set eyes with half-closed eyelids, thin lips, moustache (early Buddhas)Indian face (round, full cheeks, broad nose), open or half-closed eyes, thick lips; moustache rare
RobesHeavy, voluminous monastic robe (sanghati) with deep, wavy folds like a Roman toga; the body is hidden beneath the draperyThin, transparent robe clinging to the body, leaving the right shoulder bare (as described in the vinaya rules); the body is visible through the cloth
Halo (Prabhamandala)Plain, undecorated disc behind the headElaborately carved halo with scalloped edges, prominent rim, and floral or geometric decorations
Mudras (Gestures)Limited variety — predominantly abhaya-mudra (fearlessness, right hand raised, palm outward)Multiple mudras — abhaya, dhyana (meditation), dharmachakra-pravartana (teaching, with hands at chest level)
Human AnatomyNaturalistic, realistic proportions, modelled on Graeco-Roman statuary; muscles and bones clearly definedSymbolic, idealised proportions — broad shoulders, narrow waist, powerful physique representing the 'mahapurusha' (great man) ideal of Indian iconometry
Narrative ReliefsHighly detailed continuous narrative panels (like a comic strip) depicting Jataka scenes and events from the Buddha's life, with Hellenistic treatment of perspective (buildings shown in profile, figures in three-quarter view)Simpler, more schematic narrative panels; emphasis on iconic standing or seated Buddhas rather than extensive storytelling
Other FiguresBodhisattvas (e.g., the famous Bodhisattva with a Wreath or the Fasting Buddha from Lahore Museum) shown as princely figures with heavy jewellery, moustaches, and Central Asian features; yakshas, yakshinis, and Greek-inspired atlantes (supporting figures)Bodhisattvas (e.g., the Mathura Bodhisattva statues) shown as Indian princes with dhotis, turbans, and elaborate jewellery; yakshas (like the monumental Manibhadra yaksha from Parkham, c. 200 BCE) are the precursors of Mathura Buddha figures
PatronsKushan royalty, Bactrian merchants (traders on the Silk Route), Buddhist monasteries of the Sarvastivadin schoolKushan royalty (Kanishka's Mathura workshop), local merchant guilds, Jain and Buddhist monastic communities
Key SitesHadda (near Jalalabad, Afghanistan), Takht-i-Bahi, Sahri Bahlol, Jamalgarhi, Loriyan Tangai, Taxila (Sirkap, Dharmarajika stupa)Mathura (Katra, Jamalpur, Kankali Tila), Sarnath (where the Gupta period developed the Mathura style further)
LegacySpread Buddhism to Central Asia and China (the Serindian art of Xinjiang, the cave temples of Dunhuang, and the Buddhist art of China, Korea, and Japan all derive ultimately from Gandhara)Influenced Gupta classical sculpture, which then influenced the Buddhist art of Southeast Asia (Myanmar, Thailand, Cambodia, Indonesia)

The Fasting Buddha from the Gandhara School (Lahore Museum) is one of the most powerful sculptures in Buddhist art — showing Siddhartha's extreme asceticism with visible ribs, sunken belly, and emaciated physique, yet with a serene expression. The Mathura Kapardin Buddha (c. 2nd century CE, Mathura Museum) shows the Buddha with a shaven head (originally covered with snail-shell curls), a simple halo, and a transparent robe, standing in the typical abhaya-mudra. These two schools established the iconographic conventions for all later Buddhist art across Asia. The Gandhara influence is visible in the Buddhas of Bamiyan (Afghanistan, destroyed by the Taliban in 2001) and the Serindian (Central Asian) art of the Tarim Basin. The Mathura school, through its Gupta-era refinement at Sarnath, produced the iconic 'Sarnath Buddha' (c. 5th century CE) with its transparent robe and meditative expression — the single most influential model for Buddhist sculpture in East and Southeast Asia.

The Satavahanas (c. 200 BCE-250 CE)

The Satavahanas were the most important indigenous dynasty of the Deccan and south India after the Mauryas. They ruled from Pratishthana (Paithan, Maharashtra) over the Deccan plateau between the Narmada and Krishna rivers. They are also called the Andhras (in the Puranas). The Puranas list 30 kings over 460 years.

Key Rulers and Political History

  • Simuka (c. 200-180 BCE): Founder of the dynasty; overthrew the last Kanva ruler; established Satavahana power in the western Deccan.
  • Gautamiputra Satakarni (c. 106-130 CE): The greatest Satavahana ruler. His achievements are recorded in the Nasik Prashasti inscription composed by his mother Gautami Balashri. The inscription boasts that he 'destroyed the Shakas, Yavanas (Indo-Greeks), and Pahlavas (Parthians); re-established the glory of the Satavahanas; stopped the contamination of the four varnas (caste system).' He defeated the Western Kshatrapa king Nahapana and recovered Malwa and Kathiawar. He assumed the title 'Rajanya-Rajaraja' (King of Kings of the Kshatriyas). The Nasik inscription describes his kingdom as extending from the Krishna river in the south to the Sahyadri mountains and the Narmada in the north.
  • Vasishthiputra Pulumavi (c. 130-160 CE): His capital was Amravati on the Krishna river — the site of the famous Amravati Stupa, one of the largest and most beautiful stupas of ancient India (its sculpted marble panels are now in the British Museum, Chennai Museum, and Amravati Museum).
  • Yajna Sri Satakarni (c. 165-195 CE): The last strong Satavahana ruler. He reclaimed some territory from the Shakas and is the only Satavahana king to issue coins with ship motifs on both sides — evidence of his naval power and maritime trade connections. His coins are the most numerous and widely distributed of all Satavahana coinage.

Satavahana Administration and Society

The Satavahanas maintained a decentralised administrative system based on the Mauryan model but adapted for the Deccan. The empire was divided into aharas (districts, a term derived from the Sanskrit 'ahara' — meaning food or revenue collection), each administered by an amatya (minister/officer) or maharathi (feudal lord). The gramika (village headman) was the lowest-level official. The army consisted of four branches: elephants (the strongest arm of the Deccan armies), chariots, cavalry, and infantry.

Satavahana society was characterised by caste fluidity in the early period, though the Nasik Prashasti of Gautamiputra Satakarni boasts that he 'restored the four-varna system.' The king's matronymic (Gautamiputra = 'Son of Gautami', Vasishthiputra = 'Son of Vasishthi') is unusual — they used the mother's name and gotra (clan) in their titles, which may reflect the persistence of matrilineal elements in Deccan society (a legacy of the earlier megalithic cultures) or simply the importance of the mother's lineage for legitimacy. Women enjoyed relatively high status: we have records of women making donations to Buddhist monasteries independently (the queen of Gautamiputra, his mother Gautami Balashri, and numerous lay female donors at Nashik, Karle, and Kanheri). The king maintained a council of ministers (mantri-parishad) but inscriptions show he held ultimate authority over land grants, tax exemptions, and military campaigns.

Satavahana Culture: The Gatha Saptashati and Cave Architecture

The Satavahanas were great patrons of Prakrit literature. The Gatha Saptashati (Sattasai in Prakrit — 'Seven Hundred Verses') is a collection of 700 love poems attributed to King Hala (c. 20-35 CE). It is the most important surviving work of Maharashtri Prakrit literature. The verses cover the entire range of human emotion — love in separation, the joy of union, the pangs of jealousy, the beauty of nature, and village life — all in a remarkably concise, lyrical style. The poems give us vivid insights into the social and cultural life of the Deccan in the early centuries CE: the roles of women, the caste system, agricultural practices (rice cultivation, irrigation, cattle rearing), and the beautiful landscape of the Western Ghats. Many verses are dialogues between lovers, and the collection is a precursor to the later Sanskrit kavya tradition.

All Satavahana inscriptions are in Prakrit (using Brahmi script), unlike the later Sanskrit inscriptions of the Guptas. The Satavahanas were patrons of both Brahmanism (they performed Vedic sacrifices — the Ashvamedha and the Rajasuya) and Buddhism (they donated extensively to Buddhist monasteries). This dual patronage is best seen in their magnificent cave architecture:

  • Nashik Caves (Pandavleni): A group of 24 Buddhist caves carved into the Trirashmi hills near Nashik, Maharashtra. The most important is Cave 3 (the Gautamiputra Vihara), a large monastery with a beautiful fa�ade and an inscription recording a donation by Gautamiputra Satakarni's mother. Cave 10 is the chaitya (prayer hall) with a stupa. The inscriptions here provide the most detailed evidence of land grants to Buddhist monks — they record grants of entire villages to the Buddhist sangha, with tax exemptions and legal rights.
  • Karle Caves: The Great Chaitya Cave (Cave 8) at Karle is the largest and most perfectly preserved chaitya hall in India (c. 40 metres long, 15 metres wide, 14 metres high). Its massive horseshoe-shaped window (chaitya-arch) admits light; the hall contains 37 columns with ornate pot-and-foliage (ghata-pallava) capitals, and a large stupa at the apsidal end. The fa�ade has a spectacular gallery with carved wooden ribs and a sun window. The cave was donated by a wealthy merchant named Bhutapala from Vaijayanti and by the Yavana (Greek) devotee Dhamadhaya — showing that donors included both Indian merchants and Hellenised foreigners. The Karle chaitya is the culmination of the rock-cut architecture that began at the Barabar Caves (Mauryan period).
  • Kanheri Caves: A complex of over 100 caves in the Basalt hills of the Sanjay Gandhi National Park, Mumbai. Kanheri was a major Buddhist monastic university and port monastery from the 1st century BCE to the 10th century CE. The largest cave (Cave 3) is a huge chaitya with 44 columns. The Kanheri inscriptions (over 50) are important for the history of the Konkan coast and the maritime trade. Kanheri was closely connected to the international Buddhist network — monks from Sri Lanka, Kashmir, and Central Asia studied there.

Satavahana Economy: Roman Trade

The Satavahanas controlled the most important ports on the west coast of India — Barygaza (Bharuch, Gujarat), Kalyan (near Mumbai), and the access routes to the Deccan plateau. The Periplus of the Erythraean Sea (c. 60 CE) gives us a detailed picture of this trade. The discovery of the monsoon winds by Hippalus (c. 50 CE) allowed Roman ships to sail directly across the Indian Ocean from the Red Sea to the Malabar Coast in 40 days. The Deccan exported: pepper (from the Malabar Coast), pearls (from the Gulf of Mannar), ivory, precious stones (diamonds from Golconda, onyx, carnelian), cotton cloth (the finest muslins of the Deccan), indigo, and spices. The imports included: Roman gold and silver coins (which were melted down for jewellery — vast hoards of Roman aurei and denarii have been found in the Deccan, including the famous Coimbatore hoard). The Satavahanas issued lead coins (their most distinctive coinage — lead was abundant in the Deccan) with ship, elephant, and Ujjain-symbol motifs. The Satavahana seafaring and shipbuilding tradition is evident on their coins — the ship motif on Yajna Sri Satakarni's coins is one of the earliest representations of an Indian sailing vessel. The Satavahanas also controlled the east coast ports (Masulipatnam, Kalingapatnam) for trade with Southeast Asia.

The Vakatakas (c. 250-500 CE)

The Vakatakas were the successors of the Satavahanas in the Deccan. They ruled from c. 250 to 500 CE over the region of the modern Vidarbha (Maharashtra) and parts of Madhya Pradesh. The Puranas describe them as the successors of the Satavahanas (or 'Andhras') and the contemporaries of the Guptas. The Vakatakas were a Brahmanical dynasty — they performed Vedic sacrifices and were devout followers of the Brahmanical religion, but they also patronised Buddhist and Jain institutions. They are important for three reasons:

  • Vakataka-Gupta Alliance: The Vakataka king Rudrasena I married a Gupta princess (the daughter of Chandragupta II). This alliance gave the Guptas influence in the Deccan and linked the two greatest dynasties of the 4th-5th centuries. The Vakatakas adopted Gupta administrative practices and cultural norms, spreading the 'Golden Age' culture of the Guptas to the Deccan.
  • Harishena (c. 475-500 CE): The greatest Vakataka ruler. Under him, the Vakataka kingdom reached its largest extent, covering most of the Deccan. He is famous for patronising the Ajanta Caves — Caves 16 and 17 were excavated and painted during his reign. Varahadeva, a minister of Harishena, donated Cave 16. The Ajanta paintings of this period (the so-called 'Vakataka phase') are the finest surviving examples of ancient Indian mural painting. They include the famous 'Flying Ascetic' (Cave 17), 'The Dying Princess' (Cave 16), and the magnificent scenes from the Jataka tales.
  • Ajanta Caves (Vakataka Contribution): While the earlier Ajanta caves (Caves 9, 10, 12) belong to the Satavahana period (c. 1st century BCE-1st century CE), the later caves (Caves 1, 2, 16, 17, 19, 26) were built under the Vakatakas. The Ajanta paintings are the greatest surviving corpus of ancient Indian painting. They depict the Jataka stories (the previous lives of the Buddha) and scenes from the life of the Buddha, with extraordinary naturalism, modelling in colour, and emotional depth. The use of perspective (figures in three-quarter profile, overlapping, shading), the depiction of nature (trees, flowers, animals), the costumes and jewellery of the Gupta-Vakataka period, and the facial expressions of the figures (especially the compassion on the Bodhisattva's face) make Ajanta one of the supreme achievements of world art.

The Vakataka kingdom collapsed after Harishena's death (c. 500 CE), probably due to attacks from the rising Kalachuri and Vishnukundin dynasties. The Vakatakas are a crucial link between the Satavahanas and the later Chalukyas of Badami.

The Ikshvakus of Nagarjunakonda (c. 225-340 CE)

The Ikshvakus of Nagarjunakonda (in Guntur district, Andhra Pradesh) were the successors of the Satavahanas in the eastern Deccan (the Krishna-Godavari delta region). They claimed descent from the legendary Ikshvaku of the Ramayana — the first king of the Solar dynasty. They ruled from their capital Vijayapuri (Nagarjunakonda) on the Krishna river. The site of Nagarjunakonda (named after the Buddhist philosopher Nagarjuna, who lived in the 2nd-3rd centuries CE) was extensively excavated in the 1950s and 1960s before the area was submerged by the Nagarjuna Sagar Dam — many structures were dismantled and reconstructed on a nearby island.

The Ikshvaku period was a great age of Buddhist monastic construction. Nagarjunakonda had more than 30 Buddhist monasteries (viharas), several stupas, chaitya halls, and pillared halls, making it one of the largest Buddhist monastic complexes in India. The site also contains the remains of an Ikshvaku palace, an apsidal temple, and a theatre/amphitheatre — unique for an Indian archaeological site. The Ikshvaku queens were particularly active donors to Buddhism — inscriptions record the building of stupas and monasteries by royal women. The most famous ruler was Vasishthiputra Santamula I (or Siri Santamula), who performed the Ashvamedha sacrifice (shown on a Nagarjunakonda panel relief). The Ikshvakus were overthrown by the Pallavas of Kanchipuram in the mid-4th century CE.

The Sangam Age (c. 300 BCE-300 CE): Cheras, Cholas, and Pandyas

The far south of India (present-day Tamil Nadu, Kerala, and parts of Andhra and Karnataka) was ruled by three powerful dynasties during the Sangam period: the Cheras (in the Kerala region and the Tamil Kongu country), the Cholas (in the Kaveri delta region around Uraiyur/Thanjavur), and the Pandyas (in the far south, around Madurai). The period is called the 'Sangam Age' after the Sangam (academy) of poets and scholars that flourished under the patronage of these kings, particularly the Pandyas. According to Tamil tradition, three successive Sangams (academies) were held at Madurai over thousands of years — the Third Sangam (c. 300 BCE-300 CE) produced the corpus of poetry that survives today as the Sangam literature.

Sangam Literature

Sangam literature is the earliest surviving body of secular poetry in India — unlike the religious texts of the Vedas, Upanishads, and Buddhist scriptures, the Sangam poems are love poems (akam) and war poems (puram) written by over 470 poets (including 30 women poets). The corpus consists of 2,381 poems organised into eight anthologies (Ettuthokai) and ten long poems (Pattupattu). The most important works include:

  • Tolkappiyam: The earliest Tamil grammar text (c. 2nd-1st century BCE), which categorises Tamil phonology, morphology, syntax, and poetics (the famous akam/puram and tinai/landscape classifications). It is a linguistic masterpiece that demonstrates Tamil was already a developed classical language by the 1st century BCE.
  • Purananuru: An anthology of 400 puram poems (exterior/war poems) that describe the heroism, generosity, and valour of kings and warriors. It provides vivid descriptions of Chola, Chera, and Pandya kings — their battles, their patronage of poets, their palaces, and their daily life.
  • Akananuru and Kuruntokai: Love poems that use the landscape as a metaphor for the five phases of love (tinai) — mountains (union), forests (waiting), riverside (impatience), seashore (separation), wasteland (pining).
  • Silappadikaram (The Jewel Anklet, c. 5th-6th century CE) and Manimekalai: Post-Sangam Tamil epics. Silappadikaram, by the Jain prince Ilango Adigal, tells the story of Kovalan, his wife Kannagi (who becomes a goddess of chastity and destroys the city of Madurai with her curse), and the courtesan Madhavi. Manimekalai, by Sattanar (a Buddhist poet), continues the story of Kovalan and Madhavi's daughter.
  • Thirukkural (by Thiruvalluvar, c. 3rd-5th century CE): 1,330 couplets on virtue (aram), wealth (porul), and love/kama (inbam) — one of the greatest works of Indian ethical literature, translated into all major Indian and world languages. It is a landmark text of the Sangam tradition, with its focus on secular ethics, social justice, and human values.

The Three Kingdoms

Cheras: The Cheras ruled the present-day Kerala region and the mountainous Kongu country. Their capital was Vanji (Karur, Tamil Nadu) on the Periyar river and Muziris (Kodungallur, Kerala), the most important port for Roman trade. The greatest Chera ruler of the Sangam period was Senguttuvan (the Red Chera, c. 2nd century CE), who is celebrated in the Silappadikaram — he invaded north India, crossed the Ganges, and brought back a stone from the Himalayas to carve the image of Kannagi as the goddess Pattini. The Cheras controlled the spice trade (pepper, cardamom, cinnamon) that made Kerala famous in the ancient world. Roman ships called at Muziris in large numbers — Strabo (c. 20 CE) records that 120 Roman ships sailed annually from the Red Sea to Muziris. The Cheras issued coins with bow-and-arrow motifs and the distinctive Chera royal emblem.

Cholas: The Cholas ruled the fertile Kaveri delta from their capital Uraiyur (Tiruchirappalli) and the port city of Kaveripattinam (Puhar). The Sangam Cholas (distinct from the later medieval Cholas) are celebrated in Sangam poetry for their conquests and patronage of poets. Notable rulers: Karikala Chola (c. 120-150 CE) — the greatest of the Sangam Cholas. He is credited with building the Grand Anicut (Kallanai) across the Kaveri river, the oldest irrigation dam in the world still in use, a massive stone dam 330 metres long and 18 metres high. Karikala defeated the Cheras and Pandyas at the battle of Venni and established Chola hegemony. He was a patron of trade — Kaveripattinam was a major port for trade with Sri Lanka and Southeast Asia. The Chola emblem was the tiger, and they issued copper coins with the tiger symbol.

Pandyas: The Pandyas ruled from Madurai on the Vaigai river, the oldest continuously inhabited city in the Indian subcontinent (over 2,500 years). The Pandyas are mentioned in Greek sources (Megasthenes in the 4th century BCE refers to the 'Pandian kingdom' as the southernmost kingdom of India, under the rule of a queen, Pandaia). They were the greatest patrons of the Sangam academy — the poets flocked to Madurai for royal patronage. The greatest Pandya ruler was Neduncheliyan (c. 2nd century CE), who is the hero of the Silappadikaram (he wrongly executes Kovalan for the theft of the jewel anklet, precipitating Kannagi's curse). The Pandyas controlled the pearl fisheries of the Gulf of Mannar — their pearls were famous in the Roman world (Pliny mentions them). The Pandya emblem was the fish. They conducted extensive maritime trade with the Roman Empire and later with China (Pandya embassies to the Chinese emperor are recorded in Chinese sources).

The Kalabhra Interregnum (c. 250-600 CE)

The Kalabhra interregnum is one of the most mysterious periods of Tamil history. Between the decline of the Sangam states and the rise of the later Pallavas, Pandyas, and Cholas (c. 6th-7th centuries CE), the Kalabhras (Kalappalar in Tamil) conquered the entire Tamil region. They are described in Buddhist and Jain texts as 'evil rulers' who patronised Buddhism and Jainism at the expense of Brahmanism. They overthrew the Chera, Chola, and Pandya kings and disrupted the traditional order. The Kalabhras are mentioned in a few inscriptions (the Velvikudi copper plates of the Pandya king Nedunchezhian III, c. 770 CE, describes how the Pandya kingdom was 'swallowed by the Kalabhras') and in Buddhist chronicles. The Kalabhra period (c. 250-600 CE) saw the dominance of Jainism and Buddhism in Tamil society — the Jain and Buddhist monasteries of Tamil Nadu (the caves at Sittannavasal, the hill of Samanar Malai) date from this period. The Kalabhras were eventually overthrown by the Pallava king Simhavishnu (c. 575-600 CE) in the north and the Pandya king Kadungon (c. 590-620 CE) in the south, who re-established Brahmanical rule. The Kalabhra interregnum is important because it explains the gap in Tamil literary history between the Sangam corpus and the later Bhakti literature of the Nayanars and Alvars.

The Hunas (Hephthalites / White Huns, c. 450-560 CE)

The Hunas (also called Hephthalites or White Huns) were a Central Asian nomadic confederation who invaded India in the 5th-6th centuries CE. They should not be confused with the Huns of Europe (the Huns of Attila were a separate group). The Hephthalites are first mentioned in Chinese sources as the 'Ye-da' tribe, who controlled Bactria and defeated the Persian Sassanian emperor Peroz I (c. 484 CE). They invaded India through the Khyber Pass and established a kingdom in Gandhara, Punjab, and parts of Malwa and central India. The two most important Huna rulers in India were Toramana and his son Mihirakula.

  • Toramana (c. 490-515 CE): The first Huna ruler to establish a kingdom in India. He conquered Gandhara, the Punjab, and parts of Rajasthan and Malwa. His name appears in the Gwalior inscriptions (reused by Mihirakula). He issued coins modelled on the Gupta gold dinara, showing his head on the obverse with a crescent moon and the legend 'Sri Toramana Deva.' He is described in the Jain text Kuvalayamala as a powerful king ruling from Peshawar.
  • Mihirakula (c. 515-550 CE): The most famous (and notorious) Huna ruler. His name means 'Sun Clan' and his title was 'Shahi.' The Chinese pilgrim Sung Yun (c. 520 CE) visited his court and described him as 'the destroyer of many Buddhist monasteries and stupas, a persecutor of Buddhism, and a man of terrifying cruelty.' The Rajatarangini (Kalhana's history of Kashmir) describes Mihirakula's cruelty in vivid detail — he is said to have slaughtered hundreds of thousands of people and delighted in the massacre of elephants. The Gwalior Stone Inscription of Mihirakula (c. 530 CE) records the construction of a sun temple and refers to him as 'the glorious Mihirakula Shaluvanda' — it is the only inscription issued by a Huna king in India. Mihirakula was defeated by the Gupta king Yashodharman of Malwa (the Mandasor Pillar Inscription of Yashodharman, c. 532 CE, boasts: 'He forced the Huna king Mihirakula to bow at his feet') and by the Kashmiri king Meghavahana. Mihirakula's defeat marked the end of systematic Huna power in India, though Huna chiefdoms survived in the Punjab and western Himalayas until the 7th century.

The impact of the Huna invasions was devastating: they disrupted the Gupta Empire (contributing to its collapse), destroyed Buddhist monasteries in Gandhara and the Punjab, and broke up the trade networks of the Silk Route. However, the Hunas were gradually absorbed into Indian society — some Huna clans became Rajput clans (the Gurjaras, the Huna kshatriyas of Gujarat), and the Gurjara-Pratihara dynasty claimed Huna descent. The Huna invasions also stimulated the development of the land-grant (feudal) system — as central authority collapsed, local lords fortified themselves and the economy became more localised.

Cultural Developments: The Rise of Mahayana Buddhism and Hindu Traditions

The post-Mauryan period witnessed transformative developments in Indian religion and culture.

Rise of Mahayana Buddhism

The Mahayana (Great Vehicle) tradition of Buddhism emerged in the 1st century BCE-1st century CE, in part through the doctrinal developments of the Sarvastivadin school under the patronage of the Kushans and the Satavahanas. Key features of Mahayana that crystallised in this period:

  • The Bodhisattva ideal: In contrast to the earlier Theravada/Hinayana ideal of the arhat (one who attains nirvana for himself), Mahayana emphasised the Bodhisattva — one who postpones his own nirvana to help all beings achieve enlightenment. The Bodhisattva is the compassionate hero who takes the 'Bodhisattva vow' to save all sentient beings.
  • The deification of the Buddha: The historical Buddha (Sakyamuni) was increasingly seen not just as a human teacher but as a transcendent, cosmic being — one of many Buddhas in infinite time and space. The doctrine of the Three Bodies (Trikaya) of the Buddha was developed: the Dharmakaya (the ultimate reality / Buddha-nature), the Sambhogakaya (the celestial Buddha body visible to Bodhisattvas), and the Nirmanakaya (the physical body manifested on earth).
  • New texts and philosophical schools: The Prajnaparamita Sutras (Perfection of Wisdom texts, c. 1st century BCE-5th century CE) introduced the concept of emptiness (shunyata), the Lotus Sutra (Saddharma Pundarika) taught the universal Buddhahood of all beings, and the Vimalakirti Sutra celebrated the lay Bodhisattva ideal. The philosopher Nagarjuna (c. 150-250 CE, patronised by the Satavahanas at Sriparvata/Nagarjunakonda) founded the Madhyamaka (Middle Way) school, one of the most sophisticated philosophical systems in world thought.
  • Buddha images: Mahayana Buddhism required images of the Buddha and Bodhisattvas for worship (bhakti) — this directly stimulated the creation of the first anthropomorphic Buddha images in Gandhara and Mathura during the Kushan period.
  • Spread to Central Asia and China: Mahayana Buddhism spread from India to Central Asia through Gandhara and Kashmir, and from there to China. The Kushan monk Lokakshema (c. 147-189 CE) was the first translator of Mahayana sutras (the Prajnaparamita, the Lotus Sutra, the Vimalakirti Sutra) into Chinese. This transmission established the textual foundations of Chinese, Korean, and Japanese Buddhism.

Bhagavata (Vaishnava) and Shaiva Traditions

The post-Mauryan period saw the emergence of theistic devotional (bhakti) traditions as organised religions. The Bhagavata (Vaishnava) tradition centred on the worship of Vasudeva-Krishna as the Supreme Being. The earliest evidence of the Bhagavata tradition is the Heliodorus Pillar (c. 113 BCE) at Vidisha, where a Greek ambassador declares himself a devotee of 'Vasudeva, the God of Gods.' The Bhagavad Gita (incorporated into the Mahabharata in the early centuries CE) became the foundational text of the Vaishnava tradition, teaching devotion (bhakti) to Krishna as the path to liberation. The Narayaniya section of the Mahabharata (c. 3rd-4th century CE) further developed Vaishnava theology. The Gupta kings, who adopted the title 'Paramabhagavata' (Supreme Devotee of the Bhagavata), were the first imperial patrons of Vaishnavism.

The Shaiva tradition (worship of Shiva) also developed its theology and iconography in this period. The earliest representation of Shiva in anthropomorphic form is on the coins of Vima Kadphises (c. 80-100 CE), which show Shiva holding a trident (trishula) and standing with the bull Nandi. The Shiva Linga was worshipped as the central symbol of the god, as seen in the Gudimallam linga (c. 2nd century BCE, Andhra Pradesh) — the earliest surviving Shiva linga, carved with a standing figure of Shiva in high relief. The Pashupata sect (one of the earliest Shaiva ascetic orders) was founded by Lakulisha in the 2nd century CE. The Kushan king Vasudeva I (c. 190-230 CE) was named after Vasudeva-Krishna, indicating the popularity of both Vaishnava and Shaiva traditions among the foreign dynasties.

Stupa Architecture

Stupa building reached its zenith in the post-Mauryan period. The Stupa (from the Sanskrit 'to heap') evolved from a simple funerary mound into an elaborate cosmological structure representing the Buddha's parinirvana (his passage into final nirvana). The key elements of a classical stupa are: the anda (the dome, representing the cosmic mountain Meru), the harmika (the square railing on top, representing the heaven of the 33 gods), the yashti (the central spire/umbrella, representing the axis mundi), the medhi (the base terrace), the sopana (the stairway), the toranas (gateways at the four cardinal points, richly carved with scenes from the Buddha's life and Jataka tales), and the vedika (the railing around the stupa, with carved medallions and panels). Major stupas of the post-Mauryan period:

  • Sanchi Stupa (Great Stupa, Stupa 1): Built by Ashoka, expanded by the Shungas, and its four toranas added during the Satavahana period (c. 1st century BCE-1st century CE). The most perfectly preserved stupa in India, with the finest torana carvings (the eastern gateway showing the seven incarnations of the Buddha, the western gateway with the 'Great Departure' of Siddhartha).
  • Bharhut Stupa: Now largely dismantled (its railings and gateways are in the Indian Museum, Kolkata and the Allahabad Museum), but its sculptures are the oldest surviving narrative Buddhist art (c. 150-100 BCE). Each medallion on the railing is labelled with a short inscription identifying the Jataka scene depicted.
  • Amravati Stupa (Mahachaitya): In the Krishna valley, Andhra Pradesh, built and enlarged from the 2nd century BCE to the 3rd century CE, patronised by the Satavahanas and the Ikshvakus. It was the largest stupa in south India (approximately 50 metres in diameter, 30 metres high) and was covered with exquisitely carved white marble panels. Its distinctive feature is the elaborate decoration of the dome itself (not just the railings and toranas), with figures of the Buddha and the worship of the Bodhi tree. The Amravati style is notable for the emphasis on yantra-like geometric patterns and the depiction of the Buddha in human form (influenced by the Mathura school). The Amravati sculptures are now divided between the British Museum (London), the Chennai Government Museum, and the Amravati Museum.
  • Nagarjunakonda Stupas: The Ikshvaku period stupas at Nagarjunakonda (c. 3rd century CE) are the latest in the classical stupa tradition, with elaborate sculpted panels showing scenes from the Buddha's life and the worship of the Buddha's relics.

Foreign Invasions and Indian Responses: Assimilation

The period saw the influx of Central Asian peoples (Shakas, Kushans, Hunas) into India. They were gradually assimilated into Indian society — the Shakas adopted Indian religion (especially Buddhism), patronised Indian culture, and became part of the Kshatriya caste system through the Puranic genealogies. The term 'Shaka' eventually became a generic term for barbarian/foreigner in Indian literature, but the Shakas of Ujjain (Western Kshatrapas) were fully integrated into the cultural mainstream. By the 4th century CE, the Shakas were regarded as a respectable kshatriya clan, and the Gupta king Chandragupta II's daughter Prabhavatigupta married a Shaka prince. The Kushans were particularly important for their role in spreading Buddhism to Central Asia and China through the Silk Route — the Kushan monk Lokakshema (c. 147-189 CE) was the first translator of Mahayana Buddhist texts into Chinese. The Kushan royal dynastic title 'Devaputra' ('Son of God') was borrowed from the Chinese imperial title (Tianzi, 'Son of Heaven') and later adopted by Gupta rulers. The Huna clans were absorbed into the Rajput system — the Gurjaras, the Huna kshatriyas of Gujarat, and the early medieval Rajput clans of Rajasthan (the Guhilas, the Paramaras) claimed descent from the Hunas or were influenced by Huna traditions.

The fusion of cultures is best seen in the art and coinage of the period. The Gandhara and Mathura schools of sculpture were the direct result of the meeting of Hellenistic, Central Asian, and Indian traditions under Kushan patronage. The Kushan coinage, with its fusion of Greek, Roman, Iranian, and Indian iconography, represents the most cosmopolitan numismatic tradition in the ancient world. The Shaka coinage, with its Roman-inspired portrait bust and Indian architectural motifs, shows the same synthesis. This period established the pattern that later Indian dynasties would follow: foreign invaders were culturally Indianised within a few generations and became indistinguishable from indigenous rulers.

The Periplus of the Erythraean Sea — A Detailed Analysis

The Periplus of the Erythraean Sea (Periplus Maris Erythraei, c. 60-70 CE) is an anonymous Greek text describing the ports and trade routes of the Red Sea, the Horn of Africa, and the Indian coast. 'Erythraean Sea' refers to the entire western Indian Ocean (the Red Sea, the Persian Gulf, and the Arabian Sea). It is the single most important document for understanding India's maritime trade with the Roman world. For UPSC, the Periplus is a key source for the economic history of the Satavahana and Western Kshatrapa periods.

The Periplus describes the following Indian regions and ports in sequence:

  • Barbarikon: The port at the mouth of the Indus (near modern Karachi). Exports: Indian cotton cloth, indigo, lapis lazuli, silk (from China via the Silk Route). Imports: Roman glass, silverware, wine, gold coins.
  • Barygaza (Bharuch, Gujarat): The most important port on the west coast, the terminus for the inland trade route from the Gangetic plain via Ujjain. Controlled by the Western Kshatrapa king Nahapana. Exports: cotton cloth (of many grades, including the finest muslins), agate and carnelian (from the mines of Gujarat), ivory (from Deccan elephants), spices (nard from the Himalayas), and spikenard. Imports: Roman glass vessels, fine wines (Italian and Laodicean), gold and silver coins, bronze statues, copper, tin, lead, coral, topaz, thin clothing, and antimony. The Periplus notes that Barygaza had an 'orbit' or fixed schedule of trade — ships arrived with the monsoon winds in July and departed in November.
  • Muziris (Kodungallur, Kerala): The richest port on the Malabar Coast, in the Chera kingdom. The Periplus describes it as 'a port of great importance, richly furnished with ships from Arabia and Greece.' Exports: pepper (the most important commodity — the Periplus distinguishes between 'long pepper' and 'black pepper'), betel, true cinnamon (cassia), ginger, cardamom, pearls, ivory, fine silk (from China), diamonds, sapphires, and tortoise shell. The Muziris Papyrus (a 2nd-century CE Greek papyrus found in Vienna) is a contract for a shipment from Muziris to the Red Sea worth 9 million sesterces, listing the cargo of pepper, ivory, and fabrics — it confirms the Periplus's description and the enormous value of the trade.
  • Nelcynda (near Quilon, Kerala): Another pepper port, south of Muziris, under Pandya control.
  • Comari (Kanyakumari): 'The cape of the pearl fishery' — the Periplus notes the pearl banks of the Gulf of Mannar.
  • Poduca and Sopatma (near modern Pondicherry and Madras/Chennai): Ports on the east coast, the former identified as Arikamedu.
  • Masalia (the Andhra coast): Ports trading in muslin and other cotton textiles.
  • Ganges delta: The Periplus mentions the great river Ganges and a port called 'Gange' (probably Tamralipti, modern Tamluk, West Bengal), from which Indian goods were exported to Southeast Asia (Chryse Chersonesos — the Malay Peninsula).

The Periplus also describes the pearl trade (the Gulf of Mannar was the world's premier pearl fishery until the 19th century), the trade in tortoise shell (from the Lakshadweep and Maldives islands), and the export of teak (from the forests of the Deccan and Kerala). It mentions that Indian ships (the 'Kolandiophonta' — large Indian ocean-going vessels) sailed directly to the Red Sea and to Southeast Asia, and that Indian merchants lived in Roman Egypt (at the port of Berenike, on the Red Sea coast, where Indian pottery has been excavated).

Trade with the Roman Empire

The post-Mauryan period witnessed unprecedented maritime trade with the Roman Empire. The discovery of the monsoon winds by Hippalus (c. 50 CE) allowed ships to sail directly across the Indian Ocean from the Red Sea to the Malabar Coast in 40 days. Major Roman trading settlements existed at Muziris (modern Kodungallur, Kerala — described in the Periplus as 'richly furnished with ships from Arabia and Greece'), Arikamedu (Pondicherry — a Roman factory/trading station excavated by Sir Mortimer Wheeler, with Roman amphorae and Arretine ware), and Mangalore. Pliny the Elder (Natural History, c. 77 CE) complained that Roman trade with India cost the empire 100 million sesterces annually — '50 million sesterces of our money goes to India every year.' Roman gold coins (aurei and denarii) have been found in vast hoards in south India (the Kodungallur hoard, the Coimbatore hoard — with thousands of coins). Indian exports to Rome: pepper (40 ships annually from Muziris to the Red Sea according to Strabo), cinnamon, cardamom, ginger, sandalwood, ivory, pearls (from the Gulf of Mannar), precious stones (diamonds from Golconda, rubies from Myanmar), silk (from China via India), cotton textiles (from Bengal and Gujarat), and wild animals (tigers, elephants, monkeys for Roman games). Roman imports to India: gold and silver coins (which were melted down for jewellery), glassware (Roman glass has been found at Arikamedu, Nevasa, and Taxila), wine (amphorae at Arikamedu), coral, topaz, and slaves (though slavery was less central to the Indian economy). This trade declined after the 3rd century CE due to the Crisis of the Third Century in Rome, Sassanian Persian control of overland and sea routes, and the shift of trade to the Axumite kingdom (Ethiopia) and the Persian Gulf. The decline of the Roman trade was a major factor in the economic downturn of the post-Gupta period and the rise of the self-sufficient village economy in the early medieval period.

Kushan-Mathura and Gandhara Influence on Later Indian Art

The artistic traditions of the Kushan period had a profound and lasting impact on Indian and Asian art. The Mathura School directly developed into the Gupta classical style (c. 4th-6th centuries CE), which is considered the apex of Indian sculptural art. The famous Sarnath Buddha (c. 5th century CE) — with its transparent robe, serene expression, and elaborate halo — is a direct evolution from the Mathura Kapardin Buddha of the 2nd century CE. The Sarnath school's refinement of the Mathura style became the canonical model for Buddhist sculpture across Asia: the Buddhas of Ajanta (cave paintings), the bronze Buddhas of Sri Lanka (the Anuradhapura period), the Buddhas of Myanmar (the Pyu and Bagan periods), the Thai Buddhas (the Dvaravati period), the Cambodian Buddhas (the pre-Angkorian and Angkorian periods), and the Indonesian Buddhas (the Borobudur style) all trace their iconographic conventions to the Mathura-Sarnath tradition.

The Gandhara School exerted an equally powerful influence on the art of Central and East Asia. Gandhara Buddhist art spread through the Silk Route to the Tarim Basin (Serindia — the sites of Miran, Kizil, Dunhuang, and Turfan), where the Gandhara style fused with Chinese and Iranian elements. The Bamiyan Buddhas (two colossal standing Buddhas, 55m and 38m tall, carved into the sandstone cliffs of Bamian, Afghanistan, between the 4th and 6th centuries CE) were the most monumental products of the Gandhara tradition — their drapery, the folds of their robes, and the gentle expressions of their faces were directly derived from Gandhara prototypes. The art of Dunhuang (Mogao Caves, China, 4th-14th centuries CE), Longmen (the Fengxian Temple Buddha, 7th century CE), and Yungang (the 5th-century CE cave temples of Shanxi, China) all show the influence of Gandhara in the handling of robes, the treatment of the face, and the iconography of Bodhisattvas. The Gandhara halo (the plain disc behind the head) and the Gandhara ushnisha (the topknot of hair) became standard features of Chinese and Japanese Buddha images. Even the Korea and Japan (the Asuka period and Nara period Buddha sculptures) ultimately depend on the iconographic vocabulary first established by the Gandhara and Mathura schools. In this sense, the post-Mauryan period laid the visual foundations for Buddhist art throughout Asia.

    |--- Kalabhra Interregnum (250-600 CE)
UPSC Key Insight: Post-Mauryan Period
This period is crucial for understanding the transformation of Indian civilisation: (1) The shift from Prakrit to Sanskrit as the language of royal inscriptions (Junagadh inscription of Rudradaman, 150 CE), (2) The first human images of the Buddha (Kushan period, Gandhara and Mathura schools), (3) The rise of Mahayana Buddhism and its spread to Central Asia and China, (4) The emergence of Vaishnava and Shaiva devotional traditions (Bhagavata religion), (5) The intensification of maritime trade with the Roman Empire (Periplus, Pliny, Muziris), (6) The development of rock-cut architecture (Karle, Nashik, Kanheri, Ajanta), (7) The flourishing of Tamil Sangam literature as the first Indian secular poetic tradition, (8) The pattern of foreign invasion and cultural assimilation (Shakas → Kshatriyas, Kushans → feudal lords, Hunas → Rajputs).

8. Gupta Empire (320-550 CE)

The Gupta Empire is universally regarded as the 'Golden Age' of ancient India — a period of classical refinement in art, literature, science, and philosophy that defined the norms of Indian civilisation for centuries. The Gupta period (particularly under Chandragupta I, Samudragupta, and Chandragupta II) saw the emergence of the classical iconography of Hindu gods, the codification of law (the Dharmashastras), the perfection of Sanskrit literature, the evolution of the Nagara temple style, and the mathematical and astronomical knowledge (the zero, the decimal system, Aryabhata's heliocentrism) that would later spread to the Arab world and Europe via the Islamic Golden Age. The Gupta Empire ruled over most of northern India and parts of the Deccan — though not as vast as the Mauryan Empire in territorial extent, its economic and cultural influence extended far beyond its political boundaries through maritime trade with Southeast Asia (Suvarnabhumi), the Byzantine Empire, China, and the Persian Gulf. The Gupta period is also foundational for the study of Indian feudalism, the development of the bhakti tradition, and the classical synthesis of Hindu, Buddhist, and Jain cultural elements under imperial patronage.

The Gupta dynasty was founded by Srigupta (c. 240-280 CE), who ruled a small principality in Magadha (the region around Pataliputra, modern Patna, Bihar). The Guptas were originally vaishya (though some inscriptions claim Kshatriya or Brahmin status), and they rose from obscurity to become the paramount power of northern India within three generations. The Gupta Era (319-320 CE), traditionally associated with the accession of Chandragupta I, is one of the most important dating systems in Indian history — it was used for centuries across India and Southeast Asia. The empire reached its territorial peak under Chandragupta II Vikramaditya (c. 375-415 CE), whose conquest of the Western Kshatrapas gave the Guptas access to the Arabian Sea ports and the lucrative western trade. The decline began after Skandagupta's costly defence against the Huna (Hephthalite) invasions (c. 455-467 CE), and by 550 CE the empire had fragmented into a network of successor kingdoms (the Pushyabhutis of Thanesar, the Maukharis of Kannauj, the Later Guptas of Magadha, the Maitrakas of Valabhi).

Gupta Chronology and Major Rulers

RulerReignKey Achievements
Sriguptac. 240-280 CEFounder of the Gupta dynasty; ruler of a small principality in Magadha (Pataliputra area); held the title 'Maharaja'; the Allahabad Pillar Inscription traces the dynasty back to him as 'Maharaja Srigupta'
Ghatotkachac. 280-319 CESon of Srigupta; expanded the kingdom towards Prayag (Allahabad) and Kaushambi; retained the title 'Maharaja'; the Puranas mention the Guptas as ruling over Prayag and Saketa (Ayodhya)
Chandragupta I319-335 CEThe first great Gupta ruler; married Kumaradevi, a Lichchhavi princess of Vaishali — the alliance gave him control over Pataliputra, Vaishali, and the Gangetic plain. He assumed 'Maharajadhiraja' (Great King of Kings). The Gupta Era (319-320 CE) dates from his accession. His joint gold coins with Kumaradevi celebrate the Lichchhavi alliance.
Samudragupta335-375 CEThe 'Indian Napoleon' (V. A. Smith) — never lost a battle. Knowledge of his reign comes from the Prayaga Prashasti (Allahabad Pillar Inscription) composed by his court poet Harishena. The inscription lists three campaign categories: (1) Aryavarta — defeated and exterminated 9 kings (Ganga-Yamuna doab); (2) Dakshinapatha — policy of dharmavijaya, capturing and releasing 12 southern kings (as far as Kanchipuram); (3) Forest Kings — atavika rajas subdued as tributaries. His coins depict him playing the veena (Lyrist type — unique in Indian coinage) and performing the Ashvamedha (first Gupta to revive this Vedic ritual).
Ramaguptac. 375 CEA shadowy figure from Devichandraguptam (by Vishakhadatta); said to have surrendered his queen Dhruvadevi to the Shaka king, but was saved and later killed by his brother Chandragupta II. His historicity is debated (D. C. Sircar argues he is fictional; others cite his coins and a Durjanpur inscription).
Chandragupta II (Vikramaditya)375-415 CEThe greatest Gupta ruler, who brought the empire to its territorial and cultural zenith. His most important conquest was that of the Western Kshatrapas (Shakas of Ujjain, Malwa, Gujarat, Saurashtra) in c. 409 CE, giving the Guptas access to Arabian Sea ports (Bharuch, Cambay) and the Indo-Roman trade. He assumed the title 'Vikramaditya' (Sun of Valour). His court was the legendary Navaratna (Nine Gems): Kalidasa (poet/playwright), Amarasimha (lexicographer — Amarakosha), Dhanvantari (physician), Varahamihira (astronomer), Vararuchi (grammarian), Shanku (architect), Kshapanaka (astrologer), Vetalabhatta (magician), and Ghatakarapara (sculptor). The Chinese pilgrim Faxian visited India (399-414 CE), recording a peaceful kingdom with no land tax for monks, no capital punishment, and a flourishing Buddhist culture. His gold coinage has the widest variety — 8 types including Archer, Couch, Lion-slayer, Horseman, and Standard. His capital was at Pataliputra, but Ujjain became the cultural heart of the empire.
Kumaragupta I415-455 CEHis 40-year reign was mostly peaceful. His greatest legacy is the founding of Nalanda University (the Mahavihara), which became the ancient world's foremost centre of Buddhist learning, housing 10,000 monks and students with a 9-storey library. His coins show him performing the Ashvamedha and as a lion-slayer. His reign was disturbed by the Pushyamitra rebellion and the first Huna incursions, repelled by his son Skandagupta. He assumed the titles Mahendraditya and Shakraditya.
Skandagupta455-467 CEThe last great Gupta ruler. The Huna (Hephthalite) invasions began in earnest during his reign. The Junagadh Rock Inscription records his repair of the Sudarshana Lake and describes the Hunas 'falling on the earth like a terrible torrent of poisonous water.' His Bhitari Pillar Inscription celebrates his victory over the Hunas. He repelled the invasion but the empire was financially exhausted — gold coinage debased from 80%+ to below 60% gold content. After his death, the empire collapsed under renewed Huna invasions (Toramana, Mihirakula), feudal rebellions, and the rise of the Pushyabhutis, Maukharis, and Maitrakas.
Later Guptas467-550 CEA succession of weak rulers: Purugupta, Kumaragupta II, Budhagupta, Narasimhagupta, Kumaragupta III, Vishnugupta. The Hunas under Toramana (c. 493-515 CE) and Mihirakula (c. 515-540 CE) overran Gwalior, Malwa, and Saurashtra. Mihirakula was described by Chinese pilgrim Song Yun as 'a king of terrifying cruelty, the destroyer of many Buddhist monasteries.' He was finally defeated by Yashodharman of Malwa (c. 530 CE, Mandasor Pillar Inscription). By 550 CE, the empire had collapsed into independent kingdoms.

Gupta Administration

The Gupta administrative system was significantly more decentralised than the Mauryan system — power was shared with feudatories, local chiefs, and the newly emerging class of landed intermediaries. The king bore the titles Maharajadhiraja (Great King of Kings), Paramabhattaraka (Supreme Lord), and Parmeshvara (Supreme God). The empire was a flexible hierarchy of directly administered provinces and subordinate allied kingdoms.

The empire was divided into Bhuktis (provinces) governed by Uparikas, then Vishayas (districts) administered by Vishayapatis. The district council (adhikarana) included the nagarashreshthin (merchant guild head), sarthavaha (artisans' representative), prathama-kayastha (chief scribe), and pustapala (records officer). Villages (gramas) were administered by Gramikas and village councils. The Damodarpur Copper Plates (Bengal), Baigram Plate, and Nalanda Plate provide detailed evidence of this system — recording land sales, donations, tax exemptions, and the council's role in approving transactions.

The most significant administrative innovation was the land grants to Brahmins (brahmadeya/agrahara grants). The state granted tax-free, often uncultivated, land to Brahmins for clearing and settling, recorded on copper-plate inscriptions (tamra-shasana). The grants exempted donated land from royal taxes (bhaga, bhoga, kara, uparikara, forced labour) and transferred revenue rights to the donee. Consequences: (1) expansion of agriculture into forested areas; (2) creation of a powerful Brahmin landlord class; (3) weakening of central authority as grant areas became autonomous; (4) foundation of early Indian feudalism (a debated thesis).

Revenue system: Land revenue was the principal income, assessed at 1/6th of produce (shadbhaga). Additional taxes: bhaga (king's share), bhoga (periodic offerings in kind), kara (forced labour/tax), uparikara (extra levy), hiranya (gold tax), sulka (customs/tolls). Merchant guilds (shrenis) paid trade taxes and functioned as quasi-banking institutions.

Military: The army comprised elephants, cavalry, chariots, and infantry under a senapati. The Guptas maintained a navy, forts (durgas), a royal bodyguard (pratihara), and a network of spies. The sandhivigrahaka (minister for peace and war) was a key officer. Feudatory kings increasingly supplied troops, weakening imperial military effectiveness over time.

Gupta Economy

The Gupta period witnessed a flourishing of trade, crafts, and agriculture underpinned by royal taxation, temple-controlled redistribution, merchant guild activity, and overseas trade extending from the Red Sea to the Malay Archipelago.

Agriculture benefited from iron ploughshares, well irrigation (including step-wells), rice expansion in the Ganga valley, and forest clearance through brahmadeya grants. Major crops: rice (Bengal, Bihar, east UP), wheat and barley (Punjab, west UP), cotton (Bengal, Gujarat — Indian cotton was famous in Rome), sugarcane (UP, Bihar), indigo (Bihar), pepper and spices (Kerala, Deccan — exported to the West), sandalwood, camphor, aloe-wood (exported to SE Asia). The Guptas promoted sericulture (silk) in Bengal and Assam to reduce dependence on Chinese silk imports.

Inland trade was facilitated by the Uttarapatha (Northern Route: Taxila-Mathura-Pataliputra-Tamralipti) and the Dakshinapatha (Southern Route: Pataliputra-Vidisha-Ujjain-Pratishthana to the Deccan). Important commercial hubs (nigamas): Mathura (textiles, jewellery), Varanasi (silk, perfumes, ivory), Pataliputra (grain, cloth, metalware), Ujjain (western commercial capital), Vidisha (Buddhist/Jain trade centre). The guilds (shrenis) regulated prices, maintained quality, trained apprentices, and served as banks accepting deposits.

Overseas trade reached unprecedented levels after the Kshatrapa conquest gave the Guptas control over the west-coast ports: Tamralipti (Bengal — described by Faxian as 'a great emporium on the seacoast'), Bharuch (Barygaza), Kalyan, Cambay, Sopara. The Indian Ocean trade network linked India with: (1) Byzantium — Indian exports: pepper, precious stones, cotton muslins, silk, indigo, ivory, sandalwood; imports: gold (aurei, solidi), silver, wine, glassware, coral, horses; (2) Southeast Asia (Suvarnabhumi — Myanmar, Thailand, Java, Sumatra, Bali) — Indian merchants established settlements, trading spices, sandalwood, camphor, aromatic woods, and brought Indian scripts, Hindu-Buddhist ideas, and administrative concepts that shaped Funan, Champa, Srivijaya, and Kediri; (3) China and Central Asia — the Silk Route brought Chinese silk, and Faxian's travels show the flow of Buddhist manuscripts and pilgrims from India to China.

Coinage: The Guptas produced the most beautiful gold coinage in Indian history. The Dinara (standard gold coin, 7.8-8.4 g, derived from Roman denarius aureus) was issued in 8 major types: (1) Standard Type (king standing, goddess Lakshmi on reverse); (2) Archer Type (king holding a bow — Chandragupta II's most common); (3) Couch Type (king seated on a couch); (4) Lion-slayer Type (king hunting a lion); (5) Horseman Type (king on horseback); (6) Elephant-rider Type (king on elephant); (7) Ashvamedha Type (Kumaragupta's sacrificial horse with yupa pillar); (8) Lyrist Type (Samudragupta playing the veena). After Skandagupta's Huna wars, the coinage was debased — gold content fell from over 80% to under 60%, weight from 7.8g to ~6.5g. Silver coins (Rupaka) were minted for western India after the Kshatrapa conquest, modelled on Kshatrapa designs (king's bust obverse, temple/garuda reverse). Copper coins are rare, suggesting most small transactions used barter or cowrie shells.

Gupta Culture — The Golden Age

The Gupta period is the classical age of Indian culture — the canonical forms of Sanskrit literature, Hindu iconography, temple architecture, and Indian science were definitively established. This efflorescence was driven by imperial patronage (the Guptas were generous patrons of the arts), trade wealth (which funded monumental architecture and supported artists and scholars), and the intellectual ferment of a cosmopolitan society at the intersection of Vedic, Buddhist, Jain, Greek, and Persian traditions.

Literature

Kalidasa (c. 400-450 CE) is the greatest Sanskrit poet and playwright, often called the 'Shakespeare of India' (Goethe, influenced by Shakuntala, preferred Kalidasa for his 'graceful blending of the natural and the ideal'). He is associated with Chandragupta II's court (one of the Navaratna). His surviving works display a mastery of language, profound emotional insight, and a vivid love of nature that is almost Romantic in its sensibility. Three plays: Abhijnanashakuntalam (The Recognition of Shakuntala — the most celebrated Sanskrit play, telling the story of King Dushyanta's love for Shakuntala, their separation through a curse, and their eventual reunion; Goethe wrote of it: 'Wouldst thou the young and the old, the beautiful and the year combine...'), Vikramorvasiyam (Urvashi Won by Valour — the story of the love between the mortal King Pururavas and the celestial nymph Urvashi), and Malavikagnimitram (Malavika and Agnimitra — a palace intrigue comedy). Two long poems (mahakavyas): Raghuvamsha (The Dynasty of Raghu, 19 cantos tracing the Solar Dynasty from Dilipa through Rama to later kings) and Kumarasambhava (The Birth of Kumara — the story of Shiva and Parvati's marriage and the birth of Kartikeya/Skanda). Lyric poem: Meghaduta (The Cloud Messenger — one of the most exquisite poems in world literature, in which a banished Yaksha sends a love message to his wife through a passing cloud, describing the Indian landscape with extraordinary geographical and emotional precision).

Shudraka's Mrichchhakatika (The Little Clay Cart, c. 4th-5th cent. CE) is a unique Sanskrit play for its social realism — it is not a court drama but a bustling urban comedy of intrigue involving a Brahmin merchant, a beautiful courtesan, a gambling den, a political rebellion, and a murder trial, set in the city of Ujjain. It is the only classical Sanskrit play with a tragic ending averted only at the last moment. Vishakhadatta's Mudrarakshasa (The Signet Ring of Rakshasa, c. 5th cent. CE) is a sophisticated political drama of diplomacy and espionage set in the Mauryan period, featuring Chanakya's cunning intrigues to win over the Nanda minister. Vishakhadatta also wrote Devichandraguptam (about Chandragupta II's defeat of Ramagupta). Bharavi's Kiratarjuniya (c. 6th cent. CE) is an epic poem of 18 cantos on Arjuna's penance and his encounter with Shiva disguised as a Kirata hunter — celebrated for its verbal complexity, dense metaphors, and philosophical depth. Dandin's Dashakumaracharita (Tales of the Ten Princes, c. 6th-7th cent. CE) is a witty prose romance about the adventures of ten princes, providing vivid descriptions of contemporary urban life, courtesans, and bandits. He also wrote the Kavyadarsha (a work on poetics). Vatsyayana's Kama Sutra (c. 3rd-4th cent. CE) is the most famous Indian treatise on erotica, but it is far more than that — it is a comprehensive work on the art of living, covering the 64 arts (chatuh-shashti-kala), courtship, marriage, courtesans, and the philosophy of pleasure (kama) as a legitimate goal of life (alongside dharma and artha). Panchatantra (c. 3rd-4th cent. CE, attributed to Vishnusharma) is a collection of animal fables with moral lessons, framed as a series of stories within stories ('frame narrative'), written to teach three dull princes the art of statecraft. Via the Persian translation (Anwar-i-Suhaili, 6th cent.) and the Arabic translation (Kalila wa Dimna by Ibn al-Muqaffa', 8th cent.), it became one of the most translated books in world history, influencing La Fontaine, the Grimm Brothers, and Aesop. Hitopadesha (c. 5th-6th cent. CE) is a similar collection of fables with a more explicitly didactic focus on worldly wisdom.

The Gupta period saw the codification of the Dharmashastras. The Manusmriti reached its final form; the Yajnavalkya Smriti (4th-5th cent.) and Narada Smriti (5th-6th cent.) show a sophisticated legal system with detailed property, inheritance, contract, and criminal law. The Puranas (Vishnu, Vayu, Matsya Puranas) were compiled or extensively revised, establishing the classical Puranic framework of cosmology, genealogy, and mythology.

Science and Mathematics

The Gupta period was a golden age of Indian science, producing discoveries that fundamentally shaped mathematics and astronomy in the Islamic world and Europe.

Aryabhata (born 476 CE at Pataliputra/Ashmaka) wrote the Aryabhatiya (499 CE, 121 verses), a revolutionary text. His achievements: (1) calculated pi (π) as 3.1416 (62,832/20,000), remarkably accurate for the 5th century; (2) gave the true explanation for solar and lunar eclipses — the moon and earth cast shadows, contradicting the Puranic myth of Rahu 'swallowing' the sun and moon; (3) stated that the earth rotates on its axis ('the sphere of the earth, being round, rotates daily') — a heliocentric insight not accepted in Europe until Copernicus; (4) calculated the solar year as 365.358 days (modern value: 365.2422, error ~2 hrs 46 min); (5) pioneered an alphabetical notation for numbers (consonants = digits, vowels = powers of ten).

Varahamihira (c. 505-587 CE, of Ujjain) wrote the Brihat Samhita (encyclopaedia of astronomy, astrology, geography, architecture, gemology, agriculture, botany, and meteorology) and the Pancha Siddhantika (comparative summary of five astronomical systems: Surya, Romaka/Greek, Paulisha/Paul of Alexandria, Vasishtha, Paitamaha — showing cross-fertilisation of Greek and Indian astronomy). He contributed to trigonometry (sine table) and noted that ants and plants have sex and that the moon shines by reflected sunlight.

Sushruta Samhita (compiled c. 1st cent. BCE, revised in Gupta period) is the foundational text of Indian surgery, describing 300 surgical procedures, 121 surgical instruments (scalpels, lancets, needles, catheters, forceps), and operations for rhinoplasty (nose reconstruction — the first recorded plastic surgery in world history, using a cheek/forehead flap, later transmitted to Europe via Arab physicians), cataract removal (couching), Caesarean sections, bladder stone removal, and intestinal suturing.
