The text emphasises anatomy through dissection (students observed decomposition of a body placed in a basket in a river). Charaka Samhita (c. 3rd cent. CE, revised in Gupta period) is the foundational Ayurvedic text, 120 chapters covering 8 branches of medicine, emphasising the tridosha theory (vata-pitta-kapha, the three humours whose balance determines health), diagnosis through pulse/urine/stool examination, and the physician's ethical obligations.

Mathematics: The Gupta period saw the maturation of the decimal place-value system with the concept of zero as both a placeholder (dot, later a circle) and a number. The Bakhshali Manuscript (c. 3rd-4th cent. CE) contains the earliest surviving zero symbol. The Brahmi numerals evolved into Gupta numerals, then into Nagari (Devanagari) numerals. These were transmitted to the Arab world via al-Khwarizmi's 'On the Calculation with Hindu Numerals' (c. 820 CE), and from there to Europe as 'Arabic numerals' — one of the most important intellectual transmissions in world history. The Gupta period also saw advances in algebra (quadratic equations), trigonometry (sine, cosine functions), and combinatorics (permutations and combinations, known to Jain mathematicians). Brahmagupta (b. 598 CE) later systematised this knowledge in the Brahmasphutasiddhanta (628 CE).

Art and Architecture

The Gupta period defined the classical canon of Indian art — the norms of proportion, posture, ornamentation, and expression that guided Indian sculpture, painting, and architecture for centuries. The Gupta style is characterised by balance, serenity, refinement, and spiritual idealism, in contrast to the earlier Mauryan and Kushan styles.

Ajanta Caves (Aurangabad, Maharashtra): The Gupta period produced the finest surviving paintings of ancient India at Ajanta. While the earlier caves (9, 10, 12) belong to the Satavahana period (1st cent. BCE-2nd cent. CE), the Gupta-Vakataka phase (Caves 16, 17, 19, 1, 2, c. 4th-6th cent. CE) produced the masterpieces. The frescoes (more accurately, tempera paintings with mineral pigments on a ground of clay, cowdung, lime, and vegetable fibre) depict the Jataka tales (the previous lives of the Buddha) with extraordinary narrative power. Key masterpieces: (1) the 'Dying Princess' (Cave 16 — Siddhartha's sister-in-law Yashodhara mourning his departure, showing exquisite modelling of the female form and subtle depiction of grief); (2) the 'Flying Ascetic' (Cave 17); (3) the 'Bodhisattva Padmapani' (Cave 1 — the most famous Indian painting, a full-length portrait of a Bodhisattva with a lotus, serene expression, elaborate jewellery, and complex composition); (4) the 'Simhala Avadana' (the story of Simhala's journey to Sri Lanka, Cave 17). The Ajanta paintings are remarkable for their naturalistic modelling (using graded shading to suggest three-dimensional volume), their perspective (objects further away are shown higher on the picture plane), their expressive faces and gestures, and their rich decorative detail (textiles, jewellery, architecture).

Mathura School (Mathura, UP, c. 1st-6th cent. CE): Sculptures in red sandstone, robust and energetic. In the Gupta period, the style became more refined. The Kapardin Buddha (seated Mathura Buddha c. 5th cent., Mathura Museum) shows the Buddha with a shaven head (kapardin), padmasana posture, and a large elaborate halo (prabhamandala) with concentric floral and geometric bands. Standing Buddhas show the Gupta halo, the transparent sanghati robe, and the graceful tribhanga posture.

Sarnath School (Sarnath, Varanasi, c. 5th-6th cent. CE): The highest refinement of Gupta Buddhist art, using Chunar sandstone (fine-grained beige-cream). The Sarnath Buddha (c. 5th cent., Sarnath Museum) is the quintessential Gupta Buddha — seated in Dharmachakra-mudra (teaching gesture), serene face with half-closed eyes, subtle smile, transparent robe, seated on a throne with a dharma-chakra flanked by deer (symbolising the First Sermon at Sarnath). The Sarnath style influenced all later Buddhist art in Asia (Tibet, Nepal, China, Korea, Japan). The Sarnath Lion Capital (Ashoka's pillar capital, c. 250 BCE) was part of the Gupta-period monastic complex and was adopted as the National Emblem of India in 1950.

Udayagiri Caves (Vidisha, MP, c. 400 CE): 20 rock-cut caves patronised by Chandragupta II. Cave 5 contains the colossal Varaha Avatar panel — Vishnu as a boar rescuing the earth goddess Prithvi from the cosmic ocean, with Shesha, sages, and gods looking on — a masterpiece of Gupta narrative relief sculpture.

Temple Architecture: The Emergence of the Nagara Style

The Gupta period witnessed the birth of the classical Indian Hindu temple — the transition from rock-cut caves (still dominant in the Gupta period, as at Udayagiri and Ajanta) to structural temples built of brick and stone. Three main temple styles crystallised in the post-Gupta centuries, but their foundations were laid in the Gupta period: the Nagara (northern, curvilinear shikhara), the Dravida (southern, pyramidal vimana), and the Vesara (hybrid, Deccan).

The Nagara style evolved from simple flat-roofed shrines to the curvilinear shikhara. Key Gupta-period surviving temples:

  • Dashavatara Temple, Deogarh (near Jhansi, UP, c. 500 CE) — dedicated to Vishnu's 10 avatars, the earliest surviving Nagara structural temple with a square sanctum, pillared mandapa, and the beginnings of a shikhara tower. Famous for exquisite panel reliefs: Anantashayin (Vishnu sleeping on Shesha), Gajendra-moksha (Vishnu rescuing the elephant king), Nara-Narayana (twin sages), Narasimha (man-lion killing Hiranyakashipu). The doorway is carved with river goddesses Ganga and Yamuna, foliage scrolls, and dwarapalakas.
  • Bhitargaon Brick Temple (near Kanpur, UP, c. 400-450 CE) — the earliest surviving structural brick temple in India, with a high curvilinear shikhara (the earliest known true Nagara shikhara in brick). Originally covered with terracotta Ramayana/Mahabharata panels (now in Lucknow Museum, British Museum).
  • Temple 17 at Sanchi (c. 400-450 CE) — a simple flat-roofed, four-pillared temple representing the earlier 'flat-roof' phase. The Kankali Devi Temple at Tigawa, Vishnu Temple at Eran, and Shiva Temple at Bhumra are other important Gupta-period temples showing the evolution of the temple form.

The Gupta period also saw the development of the Brahminical cave temple (Udayagiri Caves, Mandapeshwar Cave, Jogeshwari Cave). The great stupa at Sanchi, though Mauryan, was extensively renovated during the Gupta period, which added new shrine structures around the stupa.

Decline of the Gupta Empire

The Gupta Empire declined due to a combination of internal and external factors that led to its fragmentation by 550 CE — a pattern of foreign invasions, feudal disintegration, and economic contraction that would recur in later Indian empires.

1. Huna Invasions — The White Huns (Hephthalites), a Central Asian confederation, raided the northwest frontier from c. 450 CE. Skandagupta repelled the first wave, but later invasions under Toramana (c. 493-515 CE) and Mihirakula (c. 515-540 CE) devastated the Punjab, Rajasthan, Malwa, and Gujarat. Mihirakula was described by Chinese pilgrim Song Yun (520 CE) as 'a man of terrifying cruelty, the destroyer of many Buddhist monasteries and stupas, a persecutor of Buddhism.' The Gwalior Inscription of Mihirakula shows he ruled as far east as Gwalior. He was finally defeated by Yashodharman of Malwa (c. 530 CE, Mandasor Inscription), but the damage to the Gupta economy and military was irreversible.

2. Feudal disintegration — the rise of the samantas — The brahmadeya land-grant system progressively weakened central authority. Recipients of grants (Brahmins, temples, military officers) became hereditary landlords with independent administrative, judicial, and fiscal powers. These samanta lords stopped paying tribute and ceased supplying troops. After Skandagupta's death, Gupta governors declared independence: the Pushyabhutis (Thanesar), Maukharis (Kannauj), Later Guptas (Magadha), Maitrakas (Valabhi), Vardhanas (Thanesar), and Kalachuris (Mahishmati) all emerged as independent kingdoms.

3. Loss of the western trade — The Gupta economy depended on western ports (Bharuch, Cambay, Kalyan) for Indian Ocean trade with Byzantium and SE Asia. Huna invasions disrupted this trade, and the loss of ports to the Maitrakas and Kalachuris cut off the empire's main revenue source. The debasement of Gupta gold coinage (from 80%+ gold to under 60%) is a visible index of this decline.

4. Succession disputes and weak rulers — After Skandagupta's death in 467 CE, the empire was ruled by a series of weak and short-lived kings (Purugupta, Kumaragupta II, Budhagupta, Narasimhagupta, Kumaragupta III, Vishnugupta). The empire split between different branches of the Gupta family (the eastern/Patna branch and the western/Malwa branch), with each controlling only a fraction of the original territory. The Later Guptas of Magadha survived until the 7th century but as minor regional powers, not imperial rulers.

5. Rise of regional powers — The power vacuum was filled by a new generation of regional kingdoms: the Pushyabhutis of Thanesar (who, under Harshavardhana, would reunite much of northern India by 612 CE), the Maukharis of Kannauj (who controlled the strategic Kannauj-Prayag region), the Later Guptas of Magadha (who continued the Gupta lineage in a reduced territory around Pataliputra), the Maitrakas of Valabhi (who controlled Saurashtra and Gujarat), the Vakatakas of the Deccan (contemporaries and allies of the Guptas, who outlasted them), and the Hunas themselves (who settled in the Punjab and Rajasthan, assimilating into Indian society as the Gurjara and Rajput clans). By 550 CE, the Gupta Empire had ceased to exist as a political entity. The post-Gupta period (c. 550-750 CE) is known as the Early Medieval period, characterised by political fragmentation, the dominance of Brahmanical feudalism, the decline of long-distance trade, and the emergence of new regional identities that would shape the medieval history of India.

Debate: Was the Gupta Period Really a 'Golden Age'?
The 'Golden Age' label has been critically re-examined by modern historians (Romila Thapar, R. S. Sharma, D. N. Jha, Irfan Habib) who argue that the characterisation relies on a selective reading of evidence and an uncritical acceptance of courtly panegyrics (prashastis).

Arguments against:
1) Decline of political participation — The Gupta period saw the final extinction of the gana-sanghas (republics: Yaudheyas, Lichchhavis, Malavas) that had survived since pre-Mauryan times. Participatory democracy was replaced by absolute monarchy and divine kingship ideology (the king as Narayana/Vishnu).
2) Feudal exploitation — The brahmadeya system created parasitic landlords extracting maximum surplus from peasants (R. S. Sharma's 'Indian Feudalism' thesis: serfdom, forced labour/vishti, decline of trade, localisation of the economy).
3) Decline of women's status — The Dharmashastras of this period (Manusmriti) were deeply patriarchal: child marriage (girls before puberty), prohibition of Vedic study for women, denial of independent property rights (stridhana limited), perpetual subordination to father/husband/son. The practice of sati is first attested in this period (Eran inscription, c. 510 CE).
4) Caste rigidity — The varna-jati hierarchy solidified into rigid social stratification with Brahmins at the apex and Chandalas as 'untouchables' forced to live outside city gates (Faxian's account). Proliferation of jatis documented in legal texts.
5) Uneven scientific progress — Most advances were in astronomy/mathematics (linked to astrological/ritual needs), not in medicine or technology for improving common living standards. The rust-resistant Iron Pillar of Delhi (c. 400 CE) is an isolated achievement with no evidence of widespread metallurgical application.

Arguments for retaining the label:
1) Unprecedented cultural refinement — The Gupta period established classical norms of Indian art, literature, dance, and temple architecture that guided Indian civilisation for a millennium. Kalidasa, the Sarnath Buddha, Ajanta murals, and Deogarh temple are extraordinary aesthetic achievements.
2) Major scientific discoveries — Aryabhata's heliocentrism, calculation of pi, eclipse explanation, Sushruta's plastic surgery, and the zero-decimal-place-value system are fundamental contributions to world civilisation.
3) Prosperity and trade — High-quality gold coinage, Faxian's account of a peaceful kingdom, extensive overseas trade, and flourishing guilds indicate genuine prosperity for merchants and the courtly elite.
4) Nalanda University — The founding of the world's foremost centre of learning (scholars from across Asia) represents an unparalleled institutional achievement in higher education, influencing early Islamic madrasas.
5) Comparative perspective — By comparison with the post-Gupta 'early medieval crisis' (550-750 CE) of fragmentation, economic decline, and cultural stagnation, the Gupta period appears as a genuine golden interlude of stability and creativity.
  GUPTA EMPIRE: TERRITORIAL EXPANSION & DECLINE

  EARLY GUPTAS (c. 240-319 CE)
    Srigupta / Ghatotkacha
    Small principality: Magadha (Pataliputra region)
    Titles: 'Maharaja' only

  CHANDRAGUPTA I (319-335 CE)
    Magadha + Lichchhavi territory (Vaishali, east UP)
    Title: 'Maharajadhiraja'
    Gupta Era begins (319 CE)

  SAMUDRAGUPTA (335-375 CE)
    Aryavarta: Annexed 9 kingdoms (Ganga-Yamuna doab)
    Dakshinapatha: Captured & released 12 kings (to Kanchi)
    Forest kings (atavika rajas) subdued
    Direct rule: Brahmaputra to Yamuna-Krishna line
    Tributary: Whole of N. India + Deccan to Kanchi

  CHANDRAGUPTA II VIKRAMADITYA (375-415 CE)
    Added: Western Kshatrapas (Gujarat, Saurashtra, Malwa)
    Empire at peak: From Indus R. to Bengal delta,
      from Himalayas to Narmada R.
    Ports: Bharuch, Cambay, Kalyan, Tamralipti

  KUMARAGUPTA I (415-455 CE)
    Nalanda University founded
    Pushyamitra rebellion; first Huna incursions
    Territory maintained

  SKANDAGUPTA (455-467 CE)
    Huna wars exhaust the treasury
    Junagadh inscription: repairs Sudarshana lake
    After his death: rapid decline

  LATER GUPTAS (467-550 CE)
    Purugupta → Budhagupta → Narasimhagupta → Vishnugupta
    Huna invasions (Toramana, Mihirakula)
    Feudal disintegration (samantas become independent)
    Loss of western trade ports
    By 550 CE: Empire fragmented

  CAPITAL: Pataliputra (administrative), Ujjain (cultural)
  COINAGE: Gold dinara (7.8-8.4g) — 8 types: Standard, Archer,
    Couch, Lion-slayer, Horseman, Elephant-rider, Ashvamedha, Lyrist
  SILVER: Rupaka (post-Kshatrapa conquest, western India)
  SCRIPT: Gupta Brahmi → Proto-Nagari → Nagari/Devanagari
  LANGUAGE: Sanskrit (court, literature, inscriptions),
    Prakrit (popular speech, everyday transactions)
  RELIGION: State patronage of all three — Vaishnavism (paramount),
    Shaivism, Buddhism, Jainism
  KEY SOURCES: Allahabad Pillar Prashasti (Harishena),
    Junagadh Inscription, Damodarpur Plates,
    Faxian's travelogue, coin hoards (Bayana, etc.)

9. Early Medieval India & Harshavardhana (550-750 CE)

The Early Medieval period (c. 550–750 CE) in North India was characterised by political fragmentation, the dominance of Brahmanical feudalism, the decline of long-distance trade, and the emergence of new regional identities. After the collapse of the Guptas, no single empire controlled northern India. Instead, several regional kingdoms competed for supremacy, most notably Harshavardhana of Thanesar/Kannauj (c. 606–647 CE), the last great north Indian ruler before the Islamic invasions. This period also saw the rise of powerful kingdoms in the Deccan (the Chalukyas of Badami, the Pallavas of Kanchipuram, and the Rashtrakutas of Manyakheta) and South India (the Cholas, Cheras, and Pandyas). The period is documented by three major sources: the Chinese pilgrim Xuanzang (Hiuen Tsang) who visited India 629–645 CE and wrote detailed accounts of political, religious, and social conditions; Bana Bhatta's Harshacharita (the biography of Harsha); and numerous copper-plate inscriptions of the various dynasties.

Periodisation Note
The Early Medieval period is conventionally dated from the Gupta decline (c. 550 CE) to the rise of the Delhi Sultanate (c. 1206 CE). However, for UPSC, the period 550–750 CE is treated separately as the 'Post-Gupta & Harshavardhana' phase, marked by Harsha's brief unification of the north, the flourishing of the Deccan kingdoms, the beginnings of the Tripartite Struggle, and the crystallisation of Indian feudalism.

Harshavardhana (606–647 CE)

The Pushyabhuti Dynasty — Origins and Rise

The Pushyabhutis of Thanesar (modern Kurukshetra district, Haryana) emerged as a regional power after the Gupta decline. The dynasty's origins are obscure — the Harshacharita by Bana Bhatta traces their lineage to the sun god (Surya) and describes them as 'of the Vrishni race' (linking them to the Yadavas of the Mahabharata). They originally ruled as feudatories of the Guptas from their capital at Sthanvishvara (modern Thanesar). The family deity was Surya. The early Pushyabhuti rulers were minor chieftains controlling the region around Thanesar and Sirhind, but their strategic location on the Grand Trunk Route connecting the Gangetic plain to the Indus valley gave them outsized importance.

Prabhakaravardhana (c. 580–605 CE) was the first significant ruler, earning the title Maharajadhiraja. According to Bana's Harshacharita, he fought the Hunas (Hephthalite remnants), the Gaudas of Bengal (under King Shashanka), and the Malavas (Later Guptas). He extended his control over eastern Punjab, the Doab, and parts of Rajasthan. His expansion brought him into conflict with the Maukhari kingdom of Kannauj, resolved through the marriage of his daughter Rajyashri to the Maukhari king Grahavarman, creating a powerful alliance.

The Maukhari Dynasty

The Maukharis controlled the region around Kannauj, Magadha, and the central Gangetic plain, with their capital at Kannauj (Kanyakubja). Their name derives from the Sanskrit mukhara ('face' or 'mouth'). Originally Gupta feudatories, early rulers Harivarman (c. 510–530 CE) established independence, and Ishanavarman (c. 530–550 CE) defeated the Later Guptas of Malwa. The most important was Grahavarman (c. 600–605 CE), who married Rajyashri. The Maukharis were patrons of Brahmanical religion, issued distinctive coinage (bull and horseman motif), and made Kannauj the most politically significant city in northern India. The kingdom was absorbed into Harsha's empire after Grahavarman's death.

Rajyavardhana's Murder and Harsha's Rise

On Prabhakaravardhana's death (c. 605 CE), his elder son Rajyavardhana succeeded to the throne of Thanesar. His reign was tragically brief. The Later Gupta king Devagupta of Malwa, a descendant of the Gupta line, formed a coalition with the Gauda king Shashanka of Bengal (who had himself been a Maukhari feudatory before declaring independence). Together, they invaded the Maukhari kingdom. Grahavarman was captured and executed, and Rajyashri was thrown into prison at Kannauj. Rajyavardhana marched south with a small cavalry force to avenge his brother-in-law. He defeated Devagupta in battle but was treacherously killed in his camp by Shashanka's agent or by Devagupta's soldiers (sources differ: Bana's Harshacharita is deliberately vague, while Xuanzang records that he was 'murdered by the king of Gauda'). The Harshacharita describes the scene: 'The king was sleeping in his camp, and in the darkness of the night, the Gaudas attacked him.'

At age 16, Harshavardhana became king of Thanesar (c. 606 CE). His first act was to rescue his sister Rajyashri, who had escaped from prison and fled into the Vindhya forests intending to immolate herself. Harsha found her at a Buddhist monastery where she had taken shelter; she later became a Buddhist nun. Harsha united the Pushyabhuti and Maukhari kingdoms and established his capital at Kannauj (Kanyakubja) on the Ganga — the most strategically located city in the Gangetic plain, controlling trade routes from the Deccan to the Himalayas and from Bengal to the Punjab.

The Six-Year Campaign (606–612 CE)

Harsha's first six years were consumed by a relentless campaign to avenge his brother. He first marched against Devagupta of Malwa, defeating and killing him. He then pursued Shashanka of Gauda, the king most directly responsible for Rajyavardhana's murder. Shashanka had established the first independent kingdom in Bengal, with his capital at Karnasuvarna (modern Murshidabad), and was known for his persecution of Buddhists — reportedly cutting down the Bodhi tree at Bodh Gaya and damaging the Nalanda monastery. Harsha drove Shashanka out of Magadha, though Shashanka retained parts of Bengal and died in c. 625 CE before Harsha could deliver a final blow. Harsha also subdued the Sulikas, Madhava Gupta of Magadha, the Gandhara region, and various Vindhya tribes. By 612 CE, he was the paramount power in northern India, ruling from the Punjab to Bengal and from the Himalayas to the Narmada.

The Kannauj Assembly (643 CE)

The most famous event of Harsha's reign was the great Buddhist Council at Kannauj in 643 CE, organised in honour of the Chinese pilgrim Xuanzang. It was a magnificent imperial spectacle attended by 18 tributary kings (from Kashmir, Sindh, Odisha, Nepal), 3,000 Buddhist monks (Mahayana and Hinayana), 1,000 scholars from Hindu and Jain traditions, and thousands of lay spectators. A temporary structure was built at the confluence of the Ganga and the Kali Nadi, decorated with flags and precious cloth. A golden Buddha image was installed, and Harsha distributed gifts (gold, silver, fine muslin, jewels) for 23 days. The central event was a debate in which Xuanzang expounded Mahayana doctrine; he refuted all challengers, and the Mahayana was declared victorious. After the assembly, the kings sent a copper-plate memorial to the Chinese emperor Taizong expressing desire for friendly relations.

The Prayag Assembly — Mahamoksha Parishad

Even more remarkable was Harsha's Prayag Assembly at Prayag (modern Prayagraj), held every five years as a Mahamoksha Parishad ('Great Assembly for Liberation') at the confluence of the Ganga, Yamuna, and the mythical Saraswati. Xuanzang witnessed the sixth assembly in 644 CE. For 75 days, Harsha gave away all his accumulated wealth — elephants, horses, chariots, gold, silver, gems, and precious cloth — to the poor, orphans, and religious mendicants. On the final day, he removed the royal necklace (the heirloom of his dynasty) and handed it to the crowd. When his ministers protested, he replied that he was 'only giving away perishable treasures to gain the imperishable treasure of religious merit.' At the end, he borrowed his sister's worn-out clothes to wear. The Prayag Assembly is considered a prototype of the later Kumbha Mela. These assemblies demonstrate Harsha's fusion of imperial power with Buddhist ideology — the chakravartin who conquers not by war but by generosity.

Harsha's Administration

Harsha's empire at its peak covered most of northern India: Punjab, Uttar Pradesh, Bihar, Bengal (Magadha and parts of Gauda), Odisha (Kalinga), Malwa, Gujarat, Saurashtra, and Rajasthan. The main exception was the Deccan: in c. 620 CE, he attempted to cross the Narmada River but was repulsed by the Chalukya king Pulakeshin II of Badami. The Aihole inscription of Pulakeshin II boasts of defeating Harsha, describing how 'Harsha, whose elephants were frightened by the raging ocean of his army, lost his spirits, and his troops were scattered like the darkness of the night at sunrise.' This was Harsha's only major military defeat and it established the Narmada as the traditional boundary between North and South India for centuries.

Harsha's administration was based on the Gupta model but was more decentralised, with greater power delegated to local governors and feudatories. According to Xuanzang, he maintained a large standing army: 60,000 war elephants, 100,000 cavalry, and 50,000 infantry. The army included a specialised elephant corps, camel cavalry (for desert warfare in Rajasthan), and a naval flotilla on the Ganga. The empire was divided into bhuktis (provinces, administered by uparikas), vishayas (districts, under vishayapatis), and gramas (villages, under gramikas or village headmen). Land grants to Brahmins (brahmadeya) and Buddhist monasteries (vihara grants) continued under a structured feudal hierarchy. The term samanta (feudatory lord) became standardised — subordinate kings who accepted Harsha's suzerainty were integrated into the imperial system through conquest and alliance, paying tribute and providing military service while managing their own internal affairs. These included the Maukharis of Magadha, the Guhilas of Mewar, the Chahamanas of Sakambhari, the Maitrakas of Vallabhi (Saurashtra), and the king of Kashmir.

Revenue system: The king's share of the agricultural produce was 1/6th of the gross output (the traditional 'shadbhaaga'), collected in cash or kind. Additional taxes included the bhaga (king's share), bhoga (periodic offerings by peasants), kara (an additional tax on land), hiranya (a tax paid in gold), and baluta (forced labour for public works). Harsha was known for his mild taxation — Xuanzang remarks that 'the people were prosperous and contented, and taxes were light.' The emperor conducted regular tours of inspection and personally heard the grievances of his subjects. A secret police system (the charas or spies) kept the emperor informed of conditions throughout the empire.

Harsha as a Patron of Learning and Culture

Harsha was himself a poet and playwright. Three plays survive under his name: Ratnavali (a romantic comedy about a princess of Ceylon, set in a harem, revolving around a magical garland), Nagananda ('The Joy of the Serpents' — based on Prince Jimutavahana, a Bodhisattva who sacrifices himself to save the Naga king, blending Buddhist and Hindu themes), and Priyadarshika (a romantic comedy with a veiled princess and a love potion). Written in the nataka style, main characters speak Sanskrit while females and lower-class characters speak Prakrit. Ratnavali was translated into German by Goethe, who praised it.

Harsha was a generous patron of Nalanda University, endowing 100 villages for its maintenance. Xuanzang studied at Nalanda for five years and described its magnificence: eight halls, 300 apartments, 10,000 monks and students from across Asia (China, Korea, Japan, Tibet, Central Asia, Sri Lanka, and Southeast Asia), and a nine-storey library (the Ratnasagara, Ratnaranjaka, and Ratnodadhi). The curriculum included Mahayana and Hinayana Buddhism, the Vedas, logic (Hetuvidya), grammar (Shabdavidya), medicine (Chikitsavidya), and Brahmanical shastras. Harsha also built stupas and viharas at Kushinara, Kosambi, and Shravasti, and erected an 80-foot bronze Buddha at Kannauj.

Bana Bhatta and the Harshacharita

The Harshacharita ('The Life of Harsha') by Bana Bhatta is the most important literary source for Harsha's reign and a masterpiece of Sanskrit prose (gadya-kavya). Bana, a Brahmin scholar, served as Harsha's court poet (astana-kavi). The Harshacharita covers Harsha's life from his early years through the rescue of Rajyashri and his early campaigns, but breaks off abruptly. Bana's other great work is the Kadambari, a romantic prose romance. The Harshacharita describes Thanesar as a bustling city and contains vital information on social conditions, including the status of Brahmins, the royal harem, and the practice of sati. Bana died before completing the work; it was finished by his son Bhushanabhatta.

Wang Xuance's Mission and the Chinese Intervention

In 646 CE, a Chinese diplomatic mission led by Wang Xuance arrived at Harsha's court, sent by the Tang emperor Taizong to establish formal relations. Wang was well received at Kannauj. When he returned in 647 CE for a second visit, Harsha was dead and the empire in chaos. According to Chinese sources (the Jiu Tangshu and Xin Tangshu), Harsha had been murdered by a usurper named Arjuna (Aluonu), who attacked Wang's party. Wang escaped to Tibet and mobilised 7,000 Tibetan cavalry (from King Songtsen Gampo) and 1,200 Nepalese soldiers (from the Licchavi king). Wang defeated Arjuna on the banks of the Ganga, captured him alive, and took him to the Chinese capital Chang'an, where the emperor held a triumphal procession. This is the only known instance of a Chinese military intervention in India and underscores the sudden collapse of Harsha's empire.

Harsha's Religious Policy — The Syncretic Emperor

Harsha's religious policy exemplifies the syncretic attitude of early medieval North Indian kings. His seals describe him as a devotee of Shiva ('Parama-maheshvara'), a worshipper of the Sun (Surya), and a follower of the Buddha. The Harshacharita confirms he worshipped Surya on Sunday, Shiva on Monday, and the Buddha on Wednesday. His royal seals show three figures: Lakshmi (Vaishnava), Nandi (Shiva's bull), and Garuda (Vishnu's bird). He built Brahmanical temples (Shiva at Thanesar, Sun at Multan), endowed Buddhist monasteries, erected an 80-foot bronze Buddha at Kannauj, hosted a Buddhist council, and patronised Jain scholars. Despite the Kannauj assembly declaring Mahayana victorious, there was no Buddhist-Shaivite conflict — monasteries and temples coexisted peacefully, and Harsha's grants show a balanced approach. His sister Rajyashri became a Buddhist nun. This was the typical early medieval pattern: personal faith was eclectic, but state patronage was distributed evenly to maintain social harmony. The decline of Buddhism cannot be attributed to royal persecution but to the gradual absorption of Buddhist institutions into the Brahmanical temple economy and the rise of the Bhakti movement.

Major Kingdoms of the Deccan and South (550–750 CE)

Chalukyas of Badami (Vatapi) (c. 540–757 CE)

The Chalukyas of Badami were the dominant power in the Deccan for over two centuries and the first great indigenous kingdom of the Karnataka region. They established a distinctive style of administration, architecture, and culture that influenced the entire Deccan and South India. The dynasty was founded by Pulakeshin I (c. 540–566 CE), a feudatory of the Kadambas of Banavasi. He captured the hill fort of Badami (Vatapi) in the Bijapur district of Karnataka — a spectacular sandstone gorge fortress surrounded by red sandstone cliffs, naturally defensible and strategically located. He performed the Ashvamedha sacrifice (the horse sacrifice) to declare his independence and assumed the title 'Maharajadhiraja'. His son Kirtivarman I (c. 566–597 CE) expanded the kingdom by defeating the Kadambas (absorbing their territory), the Gangas of Mysore, and the Mauryas of Konkan. He constructed the earliest cave temples at Badami. The dynasty reached its zenith under Pulakeshin II (c. 610–642 CE), the greatest Deccan king of his time.

Pulakeshin II is one of the most celebrated rulers of ancient India. The Aihole Inscription (c. 634 CE), composed by his court poet Ravikirti in classical Sanskrit, is the primary source for his reign. It is the earliest dated Sanskrit inscription in the Deccan and is a masterpiece of prashasti (panegyric) literature. Ravikirti begins by invoking the Jain tirthankaras (he was a Jain) and then describes Pulakeshin's conquests in epic style. The inscription records that Pulakeshin II: defeated the Kadambas of Banavasi (in North Karnataka); conquered the Gangas of Mysore; subdued the Alupas of South Canara (the coastal chiefs); defeated the Latas, Malavas, and Gurjaras (of Gujarat, Malwa, and Rajasthan); and most famously, repulsed Harshavardhana on the Narmada River (c. 620 CE). The inscription boasts that Harsha's 'war-elephants were frightened by the raging ocean of Pulakeshin's army.' Pulakeshin II also defeated the Pallava king Mahendravarman I and annexed parts of the Pallava kingdom. He sent a prestigious diplomatic embassy to the Sassanian Persian king Khusrau II (Parvez, c. 590–628 CE), recorded in the Persian historical chronicle Tarikh-i-Tabari. The embassy included gifts of Indian textiles, spices, and precious stones, and Pulakeshin's portrait was painted in Khusrau's palace at Ctesiphon. Pulakeshin II was defeated and killed in 642 CE by the Pallava king Narasimhavarman I (Mamalla), who captured Badami and assumed the title 'Vatapikonda' (Conqueror of Vatapi).

Vikramaditya I (c. 654–681 CE), the son of Pulakeshin II, restored Chalukya fortunes after the Pallava occupation. He reconquered Badami and fought a series of wars against the Pallavas, eventually capturing Kanchipuram. His successor Vikramaditya II (c. 733–746 CE) conquered Kanchipuram three times and, unlike earlier conquerors, famously did not loot the temples — an act recorded approvingly in Pallava and Chalukya inscriptions alike. He donated gold and gifts to the Rajasimheshwara (Kailasanatha) Temple at Kanchipuram. The dynasty ended with Kirtivarman II (c. 746–757 CE), who was overthrown by his feudatory Dantidurga, the founder of the Rashtrakuta dynasty. The Chalukyas of Badami thus gave way to the Rashtrakutas, who would become the next Deccan superpower.

Chalukya Architecture: The Chalukya period was a golden age of temple architecture, marking the transition from rock-cut to structural temples. The three great centres are Badami, Aihole, and Pattadakal in Karnataka. At Badami, four cave temples (c. 550–700 CE) were excavated: Cave 1 (Shiva with the famous 18-armed Nataraja); Cave 2 (Vaishnava with a Trivikrama panel); Cave 3 (dated 578 CE, the largest, with a colossal Varaha panel and the earliest surviving Indian wall paintings after Ajanta); Cave 4 (Jain). At Aihole, over 70 temples survive, earning it the title 'museum of Indian temple architecture.' The Lad Khan Temple (c. 450 CE) is the oldest, with a low-roofed pillared hall. The unique Durga Temple has an apsidal back wall resembling a Buddhist chaitya-griha; its name derives from 'durgada' (fort), not the goddess. At Pattadakal (UNESCO World Heritage Site), 10 temples represent the climax of Chalukya architecture. The Virupaksha Temple (c. 740 CE), built by Queen Lokamahadevi to commemorate Vikramaditya II's victory over Kanchipuram, was inspired by the Kailasanatha Temple and later became the model for the Rashtrakuta Kailasa at Ellora.

Pallavas of Kanchipuram (c. 275–897 CE)

The Pallavas of Kanchipuram were the dominant power in Tamil Nadu from the 4th to the 9th centuries CE and laid the foundation for the classical Dravidian (Dravida) style of temple architecture. Their origins are obscure — the once-popular theory that they were Parthian feudatories of the Satavahanas has been discarded; current scholarship sees them as indigenous Tondaiyar chieftains (the 'Tondaimandalam' lords) who rose to power after the decline of the Satavahanas. The early Pallavas (c. 275–575 CE) were a minor dynasty issuing Prakrit and Sanskrit copper-plate grants. The dynasty's greatness began with Simhavishnu (c. 575–600 CE), who ended the Kalabhra interregnum, conquered the Chola and Pandya kingdoms, and established Kanchipuram as a magnificent capital.

Mahendravarman I (c. 600–630 CE) is one of the most colourful figures in Indian history. He was a poet, playwright, musician, painter, and architect. He wrote the Sanskrit farce Mattavilasa Prahasana ('The Sport of Drunkards'), which satirises the hypocrisies of a drunken Shaiva ascetic (a Kapalika) and his mistress, and their interactions with a Buddhist monk. The play is a rare example of Sanskrit comedy and is an invaluable source for the social and religious life of the period (it shows that Kapalika ascetics, Buddhist monks, and orthodox Brahmins coexisted with considerable tension). Mahendravarman was originally a Jain but converted to Shaivism under the influence of the Saiva saint Appar (Tirunavukkarasar). His conversion is recorded in the Mandagapattu inscription, which famously declares that he built a temple 'without using brick, timber, metal, or mortar.' This marked the beginning of rock-cut cave temples in South India — temples carved from living rock. His cave temples at Mandagapattu, Mamandur, Dalavanur, and Mahendravadi are the earliest rock-cut structures in Tamil Nadu. He also patronised Dandin, the court poet and author of the Dashakumaracharita ('Tales of the Ten Princes'), a classic of Sanskrit prose literature (a picaresque romance following the adventures of ten princes separated after the death of the king).

Narasimhavarman I (c. 630–668 CE), known as Mamalla ('the Great Wrestler'), was the greatest Pallava king. He avenged his father's defeat by decisively defeating Pulakeshin II at the Battle of Manimangala (c. 642 CE), capturing the Chalukya capital Vatapi (Badami), and assuming the title 'Vatapikonda'. He also sent two naval expeditions to Sri Lanka (c. 668 CE) to restore his ally Manavarman. His greatest legacy is the monuments at Mamallapuram (Mahabalipuram), a UNESCO World Heritage Site named in his honour. The Pancha Rathas ('Five Chariots') are five monolithic temples carved from single boulders, showing the evolution of Dravidian architecture. The Descent of the Ganges (also called Arjuna's Penance) is the world's largest bas-relief — an open-air rock carving measuring 30m by 13m, depicting hundreds of figures including Shiva, sages, celestial dancers, Nagas, and life-sized elephants. The Shore Temple (c. 700–728 CE under Narasimhavarman II) is a structural granite temple on the seashore with three shrines and a pyramidal vimana — the earliest important structural temple in South India, having survived 1,300 years of sea erosion.

Rajasimha / Narasimhavarman II (c. 700–728 CE) built the Kailasanatha Temple at Kanchipuram, the finest example of early Pallava architecture and the largest Pallava temple. The Kailasanatha Temple is notable for: its 58 small shrines lining the inner walls of the courtyard (each containing a linga); the exquisite sandstone sculptures of Somaskanda (Shiva with Parvati and their son Skanda/Kartikeya) in multiple panels; the continuous friezes of mythical animals (yali), lions, and makaras (crocodile-like creatures) that form the architectural decoration; and the original paintings on the inner walls (the earliest known Pallava paintings, faint but visible, depicting Somaskanda). The temple established a standard for Dravidian architecture that Chola builders would later refine into the magnificent Brihadishwara Temple at Thanjavur.

Pallava Art and Architecture: The Pallavas pioneered Dravidian temple architecture through three phases: the rock-cut cave phase (Mahendravarman I), the monolithic ratha phase (Narasimhavarman I), and the structural temple phase (Rajasimha). This evolution — from simple cave to complex multi-shrine temple — established the template for all later South Indian architecture: the pyramidal vimana, pillared mandapa, gopuram gateway, and sculptural decoration. The Pallavas patronised Sanskrit literature; the court poet Dandin (author of the Dashakumaracharita and Kavyadarsha) is the most famous Pallava literary figure. The Pallava period also saw the rise of the Bhakti movement — the 12 Alvars (Vaishnava) and 63 Nayanars (Shaiva) composed their devotional hymns (the Divya Prabandham and Tevaram) during this period, rejecting caste distinctions in favour of direct devotion. The Pallava kingdom was annexed by the Chola king Aditya I in 897 CE.

UPSC Key Insight: Pallava-Chalukya Rivalry
The Pallava-Chalukya conflict (c. 610–757 CE) was the defining political rivalry of the Deccan in the early medieval period. It was driven by competition for control of the fertile Krishna-Tungabhadra Doab (the Raichur Doab) and access to the west coast ports (the Konkan coast, through which South Indian trade with the Arab world and the Roman Empire flowed). The rivalry saw alternating victories: Pulakeshin II (Chalukya) defeated Mahendravarman I (Pallava); Narasimhavarman I (Pallava) sacked Badami; Vikramaditya I (Chalukya) recaptured Kanchipuram; Vikramaditya II (Chalukya) conquered Kanchipuram a third time. The conflict exhausted both kingdoms and contributed to their eventual decline — the Pallavas fell to the Cholas, and the Chalukyas fell to the Rashtrakutas. The rivalry also inadvertently spurred architectural innovation: the competitive building of temples at Aihole, Badami, Pattadakal, Mahabalipuram, and Kanchipuram produced some of the finest monuments of Indian architecture.

Economic and Social Changes in the Early Medieval Period

The Feudalism Thesis and Land Grant System

The Early Medieval period (c. 550–750 CE) saw profound economic and social transformations that shaped Indian society for the next millennium. The most significant change was the deepening of the land-grant system — Brahmins (brahmadeya/agrahara), temples (devadeya), and Buddhist monasteries (vihara) were granted tax-free land in perpetuity (as recorded in thousands of copper-plate inscriptions across India). This practice, which had begun in the Satavahana period and accelerated under the Guptas, became a fundamental restructuring of the economy in the post-Gupta period. According to the Indian Feudalism thesis (pioneered by R.S. Sharma and D.D. Kosambi), these land grants created a class of landed intermediaries (the Brahmana donees and later the samanta lords) who extracted surplus from the peasants and remitted a share to the king. The grants usually transferred not only land but also the right to collect taxes, administer justice, and mobilise labour — effectively creating miniature states within the state. The key consequences were:

  • Political fragmentation — as the landed intermediaries became hereditary and autonomous, no single empire could control the vast network of local lords. The Bhakti saint Appar's remark that 'kings come and go like autumn clouds' captures this political fluidity. The number of land grants increased exponentially: while Gupta inscriptions number in the dozens, post-Gupta copper-plates number in the hundreds, each documenting the transfer of rights to a new class of local power-holders.
  • Decline of urban centres and trade — the land grants reduced the circulation of money (peasants paid in kind rather than cash). The great urban centres of the Gupta period — Pataliputra, Ujjain, Mathura, Varanasi, Vaishali — declined in population and commercial importance. Xuanzang, visiting India (629–645 CE), noted that many once-great cities like Pataliputra, Vaisali, and Shravasti were in ruins or reduced to villages. He observed that 'most of the cities are deserted or in ruins.'
  • Decline of long-distance trade — the great guilds (shrenis) that had dominated the Mauryan and Gupta economies lost their power. The Roman trade had ended by the 5th century. The maritime trade routes shifted from the western Indian ports (Bharuch, Kalyan, Muziris) to the Malabar coast (Quilon/Kollam) under the control of Arab and Persian merchants. Indian merchants played a smaller role in the Indian Ocean trade, which revived only with the Cholas in the 9th–10th centuries.
  • Rise of the temple economy — temples became the largest landowners, the most important employers, and the central redistributive institutions of the period. They received land, cash, grain, and livestock as endowments, which they used to support priests, musicians, dancers, cooks, gardeners, and artisans. Temple centres (Puri, Bhubaneswar, Kanchipuram, Srirangam, Madurai) became the nuclei of new urban settlements — temple towns that replaced the old commercial cities.
  • Self-sufficient village economy — the village became a closed economic unit, producing its own food, crafts, and services with minimal external trade. The jajmani system (a traditional system of reciprocal service obligations between castes in a village) began to crystallise, where each caste performed its traditional occupation in exchange for grain payments. This economic localism is one of the defining features of the Indian medieval economy.

Jati Proliferation and Caste Hierarchy

The period saw the proliferation of jatis (sub-castes) from the traditional four varnas into hundreds of occupation-based endogamous groups. The Smriti literature of the period (the Narada Smriti, the Brihaspati Smriti, the Katyayana Smriti, the Parashara Smriti) codified increasingly rigid rules of caste interaction — rules about food (who could accept cooked food from whom), marriage (hypergamy was permitted in limited cases, but hypogamy was strictly forbidden), touch (the concept of untouchability became more elaborate, with distinctions between 'touchable' Shudras, 'untouchable' Chandalas, and 'unseeable' groups), and occupation (each jati was assigned a traditional calling, and deviation was punished by excommunication). The position of Brahmins was strengthened enormously: they were the sole custodians of sacred knowledge, the exclusive performers of public rituals, and the legal arbiters (the king was expected to rule according to the Dharmashastras, which the Brahmins interpreted). The Brahmins also received the bulk of land grants, making them economically dominant as well as ritually supreme. This period also saw the hardening of the varna-jati hierarchy in South India, where the four-fold varna system had been weaker in the Sangam period. The Pallava and Chalukya inscriptions show the gradual adoption of Brahmanical norms in South Indian society, including the granting of brahmadeya villages to Brahmins from the north.

Women's Status in the Early Medieval Period

The status of women declined sharply in the early medieval period. The liberal traditions of the Vedic and Mauryan periods gave way to increasingly restrictive norms:

  • Sati (sahagamana) — widow immolation became more common among Kshatriya and upper-caste families. The Harshacharita mentions a queen performing sati; the Eran inscription (c. 510 CE) is the earliest epigraphic record, describing a queen ascending the pyre.
  • Child marriage — the Dharmashastras prescribed marriage before puberty, with the ideal age for a bride at 7–9 years, curtailing women's education and autonomy. The veiling (khilvata) of upper-class women began in North India during this period.
  • Restrictions on widow remarriage — upper-caste widows were expected to live a life of austerity (shaving the head, white clothes, sleeping on the ground, one meal a day) as penance for their husband's death.
  • Stridhana (women's property) — while the concept existed, women's right to control it was limited. The Mitakshara school gave women limited rights; the Dayabhaga school (Bengal) was more liberal.
  • Decline in female education — the Vedic initiation (upanayana) of girls was discontinued, and women were denied Sanskrit learning, though Bhakti worship provided some avenues of religious expression.

The Rise of the Rajputs

The early medieval period saw the rise of the Rajputs ('son of a king') as a distinct social category — a martial community claiming Kshatriya status and ruling over much of Rajasthan, Gujarat, central India, and parts of the Gangetic plain. The Rajputs emerged from the assimilation of foreign groups (the Hunas, the Gurjaras, the Shakas, and the Kushans) into the Brahmanical caste hierarchy. By performing Vedic sacrifices (especially the Hiranya-garbha) patronised by Brahmins, these groups were 'purified' and admitted into the Kshatriya varna as Rajputs. The Rajput clans claimed descent from either the solar (Suryavanshi) or lunar (Chandravanshi) dynasties of the Puranas, or from the 'fire-born' (Agnikula) clans of Mount Abu (the Paramaras, Pratiharas, Chahamanas, and Solankis). The Agnikula legend (first recorded in the 10th-century text Paramara-charita of Padmagupta) tells how four Rajput clans emerged from a sacrificial fire pit on Mount Abu created by the sage Vashishtha to defend Brahmanical order against foreign invaders. The Rajputs established numerous kingdoms: the Guhilas of Mewar (the oldest Rajput dynasty, claiming descent from the sun), the Chahamanas of Sakambhari (the Chauhans of Ajmer), the Paramaras of Malwa, the Chalukyas (Solankis) of Gujarat, the Kachchhapaghatas of Gwalior, and the Tomaras of Delhi. The Rajputs were characterised by a shared martial ethos, a code of honour (the rajputana values of bravery, loyalty, and protection of women), and a feudal relationship with their Brahmin priests who legitimised their rule. The Rajput kingdoms fought among themselves and against the invading Islamic armies from the 8th century onwards, producing a rich tradition of heroic poetry (the Prithviraj Raso of Chand Bardai is the most famous). The rise of the Rajputs was accompanied by the decline of Buddhism — the Buddhist monasteries, dependent on royal patronage and land grants, were absorbed into the expanding Brahmanical temple economy.

The Bhakti Movement — Beginnings under the Pallavas

The most significant religious development of the early medieval period was the emergence of the Bhakti movement in Tamil Nadu between the 6th and 9th centuries CE — a devotional movement centred on personal love and surrender to God, expressed through ecstatic poetry and temple worship. It marked a radical departure from Vedic sacrifice and Upanishadic abstraction. In Tamil Nadu, it was divided into the Shaiva Bhakti of the 63 Nayanars and the Vaishnava Bhakti of the 12 Alvars.

The Nayanars included Appar (Tirunavukkarasar), a Jain convert whose Tevaram hymns express passionate devotion in simple Tamil; Sambandar, a child saint who debated Jains at the Pallava court and converted Mahendravarman I; Sundarar, known for addressing Shiva intimately as 'the madman of the burning ground'; and Manikkavacakar (c. 9th century), author of the Tiruvacakam, considered the masterpiece of Tamil Bhakti literature. The Nayanars came from diverse backgrounds — Brahmins, kings, untouchables, women, and tribals.

The Alvars included Nammalvar, the greatest of the Alvars, a Shudra who composed the Tiruvaymoli ('The Sacred Word of 1,000 Verses'); Andal, the only female Alvar, who wrote the Tiruppavai (30 verses of longing for union with Krishna); Periyalvar, who composed hymns in the voice of Yashoda; and Tirumalisai Alvar, a contemporary of Mahendravarman I. Their hymns were collected as the Nalayira Divya Prabandham ('The Divine Collection of 4,000 Verses'), considered the Tamil Veda. The Alvars emphasised prapatti (total surrender to God) and rejected caste as a barrier to salvation.

The Bhakti movement under the Pallavas laid the foundation for later developments: the Lingayat movement in Karnataka, Ramanujacharya's Vishishtadvaita, the Shaiva Siddhanta system, and the North Indian Bhakti movement of Kabir, Mirabai, Tulsidas, and Surdas. It also contributed to the decline of Buddhism in South India — its emotional appeal and vernacular accessibility offered an attractive alternative to the monastic, Sanskritic Buddhist tradition.

The Tripartite Struggle — A Brief Introduction

The period after Harsha (from c. 750 CE onwards) saw the emergence of a three-way conflict for the control of Kannauj, the ancient capital of Harsha's empire and the symbolic seat of imperial power in North India. This Tripartite Struggle (c. 750–950 CE) was fought between the Palas of Bengal (under Gopala, Dharmapala, and Devapala), the Gurjara-Pratiharas of Rajasthan (under Nagabhata I, Vatsaraja, and Bhoja I), and the Rashtrakutas of Manyakheta (under Dhruva, Govinda III, and Krishna III). The struggle lasted for nearly two centuries, with Kannauj changing hands multiple times. The Pratiharas finally secured Kannauj under Bhoja I (c. 836–885 CE) and held it for a century, but the wars exhausted all three kingdoms and left them vulnerable to external invasions. The Tripartite Struggle is a key theme for later sections (Section 10 and Section 11), but it is important to note here that its origins lie in the power vacuum left by Harsha's death and the competition among the three successor powers that emerged in the early medieval period.

Key Inscriptions for UPSC
• Aihole Inscription (634 CE) — Pulakeshin II Chalukya, composed by Ravikirti in Sanskrit; records his conquests, including the defeat of Harsha on the Narmada
• Mandagapattu Inscription — Mahendravarman I Pallava; records the first rock-cut temple built 'without brick, timber, metal, or mortar'
• Prayaga Prashasti (Allahabad Pillar Inscription) — Samudragupta, composed by Harishena
• Junagadh Rock Inscription — Skandagupta (repair of Sudarshana lake), also Rudradaman I (150 CE, first Sanskrit prashasti)
• Gwalior Inscription — Mihirakula Huna ruler
• Nasik Prashasti — Gautamiputra Satakarni, composed by his mother
• Mandasor Inscription — Yashodharman (victory over Mihirakula, 528 CE)
• Rummindei Pillar Inscription — Ashoka's visit to Lumbini
• Madhuban Copper-Plate — Harshavardhana (recording land grants)
• Kailasanatha Temple Inscription — Narasimhavarman II Pallava (Rajasimha), Kanchipuram
• Pattadakal Inscription — Queen Lokamahadevi, commemorating the Virupaksha Temple
Summary for UPSC Revision
Harshavardhana (606–647 CE): Pushyabhuti dynasty; capital at Kannauj; defeated by Pulakeshin II on the Narmada; patron of Nalanda; Kannauj Assembly (643) and Prayag Assembly (every 5 years); three plays (Ratnavali, Nagananda, Priyadarshika); syncretic religion (Shaiva & Buddhist); death led to Chinese intervention by Wang Xuance.
Chalukyas of Badami (540–757): Founder Pulakeshin I; greatest ruler Pulakeshin II (Aihole inscription, defeat of Harsha, embassy to Khusrau II); architecture at Badami, Aihole, Pattadakal (UNESCO); overthrown by Rashtrakuta Dantidurga.
Pallavas of Kanchipuram (275–897): Mahendravarman I (rock-cut caves, Mattavilasa Prahasana); Narasimhavarman I Mamalla (defeat of Pulakeshin II, title Vatapikonda); monuments at Mahabalipuram (Pancha Rathas, Arjuna's Penance, Shore Temple); Rajasimha (Kailasanatha Temple); Bhakti movement patronage (Alvars & Nayanars); annexed by Chola Aditya I (897 CE).
Social changes: Feudalism (land grants → political fragmentation); decline of trade; rise of temple economy; jati proliferation; decline of women's status; beginning of Bhakti movement.

10. South Indian Kingdoms (750-1300 CE)

The period from 750 to 1300 CE saw the emergence of South India as a major centre of political power, temple architecture, maritime trade, and Tamil/Bhakti culture. The most important dynasties were the Rashtrakutas (Deccan), the Chola Empire (Tamil Nadu/Sri Lanka/Southeast Asia), the Chalukyas of Kalyani (Western Chalukya), the Kakatiyas (Warangal), the Hoysalas (Halebidu/Belur), and the Yadavas of Devagiri. The Chola Empire under Rajaraja I and Rajendra I was the most powerful — one of the greatest naval and commercial powers in Indian history, with control over Sri Lanka, the Maldives, and parts of the Malay Archipelago. This period also saw the flourishing of the Bhakti movement (Alvars and Nayanars), the spread of Srivaishnavism and Shaiva Siddhanta, and the codification of temple rituals and Agamic texts. The South Indian temple became the central institution of economic, social, and cultural life — landowner, employer, bank, school, hospital, and art gallery rolled into one.

The Pallavas (c. 275-897 CE) — The Founders of Dravidian Architecture

The Pallavas of Kanchipuram were the dominant power in Tamil Nadu from the 4th to the 9th centuries CE, and they laid the foundation for the classical Dravidian style of temple architecture. Although their origins are obscure (some theories suggest they were Parthian feudatories of the Satavahanas, others argue they were indigenous Tondaiyar chieftains), they emerged as a major force under Simhavishnu (c. 575-600 CE), who ended the Kalabhra interregnum and conquered the Chola and Pandya kingdoms. The greatest Pallava ruler was Mahendravarman I (600-630 CE), a poet, playwright, and architect. He wrote the Sanskrit play Mattavilasa Prahasana (a farce about a drunken Shaiva ascetic) and was a patron of the Jain monk Dandin (author of the Dashakumaracharita or 'Tales of the Ten Princes', a classic Sanskrit prose work). Mahendravarman converted from Jainism to Shaivism under the influence of the Saiva saint Appar (Tirunavukkarasar). He introduced rock-cut cave temples (mandapa style) carved out of living rock, beginning the Dravidian architectural tradition. His cave temples at Mandagapattu, Mamandur, and Dalavanur are among the earliest rock-cut structures in South India. The Mandagapattu inscription famously declares that the temple was built 'without using brick, timber, metal, or mortar' — i.e., entirely cut from rock.

Narasimhavarman I (630-668 CE), known as Mamalla ('the Great Wrestler'), was the greatest Pallava king. He defeated the Chalukya king Pulakeshin II at the Battle of Manimangala (c. 642 CE), avenging his father's earlier defeat, and captured the Chalukya capital of Vatapi (Badami). He assumed the title Vatapikonda (Conqueror of Vatapi). He also sent two naval expeditions to Sri Lanka (668 CE) to restore his ally Manavarman to the throne. Narasimhavarman's greatest legacy is the magnificent Mamallapuram (Mahabalipuram) monuments, a UNESCO World Heritage Site. This includes the Pancha Rathas (Five Chariots) — five monolithic rock-cut temples shaped like chariots, each carved from a single boulder, named after the Pandava brothers and Draupadi. The Descent of the Ganges (also called Arjuna's Penance) is the world's largest bas-relief (27 metres long, 9 metres high), carved on two massive boulders, depicting the descent of the sacred Ganges River from the heavens, with hundreds of figures including gods, sages, animals, and Nagas. The Shore Temple (built c. 700-728 CE under Narasimhavarman II, Rajasimha) stands on the seashore at Mamallapuram — a structural (not rock-cut) temple with three shrines, the earliest important structural temple in South India, built of granite blocks, with a pyramidal vimana and a gopuram. The later Pallava king Narasimhavarman II (Rajasimha) (700-728 CE) built the Kailasanatha Temple at Kanchipuram, the finest example of early Pallava architecture, with 58 small shrines around the main sanctum and exquisite sculptures of Somaskanda (Shiva with Parvati and Skanda). The Pallava kingdom was annexed by the Chola king Aditya I in 897 CE. The Pallava legacy is immense: they invented the Dravidian temple style (rock-cut to structural), patronised Sanskrit and Tamil literature, and established Kanchipuram as a great centre of Hindu and Buddhist learning.

The Rashtrakutas (c. 753-982 CE) — The Deccan Superpower

The Rashtrakutas of Manyakheta (Malkhed, in present-day Karnataka) were one of the most powerful empires in Indian history, controlling the entire Deccan plateau and parts of northern and southern India at their peak. They were of Kannada origin (the term 'Rashtrakuta' means 'chief of a district/rashtra') and ruled from their capital at Manyakheta (Gulbarga district, Karnataka). Their rise coincided with the decline of the Chalukyas of Badami. The empire was founded by Dantidurga (c. 753-756 CE), a Rashtrakuta feudatory of the Chalukyas, who performed a ritual (the Hiranya-garbha or 'golden womb' ceremony) to declare his independence and conquered the Chalukya king Kirtivarman II. He also defeated the Pallavas and the Gurjara-Pratiharas, laying the foundation for the Rashtrakuta supremacy.

Krishna I (756-774 CE), the uncle of Dantidurga, was a great builder and conqueror. He defeated the Western Ganga dynasty and the Eastern Chalukyas of Vengi. His most famous achievement is the Kailasa Temple at Ellora (c. 756-773 CE) — the single greatest rock-cut monument in the world. The Kailasa Temple (Cave 16 at Ellora) is a massive monolithic structure carved from top to bottom out of a single basalt cliff, covering twice the area of the Parthenon in Athens. It is dedicated to Lord Shiva and represents Mount Kailasa, the abode of Shiva. The temple complex has a Nandi mandapa, a main shrine with a 4-metre-high linga, a gopuram, and a courtyard lined with sculpted panels depicting scenes from the Ramayana, Mahabharata, and Puranas. It is estimated that 400,000 tons of rock were removed over 18 years to create this masterpiece. The Rashtrakuta period also produced the Elephanta Caves (near Mumbai, on Gharapuri Island), a collection of cave temples dedicated to Shiva. The most famous sculpture at Elephanta is the Trimurti (the three-headed Shiva as Maheshamurti), a 6-metre-tall bust depicting Shiva as the Creator (Brahma aspect, right face), the Preserver (Vishnu aspect, left face), and the Destroyer (Rudra aspect, central face) — one of the supreme achievements of Indian art.

Dhruva (780-793 CE) expanded the empire by defeating the Ganga kingdom of Talakad and the Vengi Chalukyas. He also defeated the Pratihara king Vatsaraja and the Pala king Dharmapala in the Gangetic plain, raising the Rashtrakuta flag on the banks of the Yamuna. His son Govinda III (793-814 CE) was the greatest military conqueror of the dynasty. He defeated the Pala king Dharmapala and the Pratihara king Nagabhata II, reasserting Rashtrakuta dominance over northern India. His empire extended from the Narmada to the Kaveri, and from the Arabian Sea to the Bay of Bengal. The Rashtrakutas under Govinda III became the paramount power in India, a status they would maintain for nearly a century.

Amoghavarsha I (814-878 CE) was the greatest Rashtrakuta ruler, reigning for 64 years — one of the longest reigns in Indian history. Unlike his warlike predecessors, Amoghavarsha was a scholar, poet, and patron of the arts. He is the author of the Kavirajamarga (c. 850 CE), the earliest extant work on poetics in the Kannada language. The Kavirajamarga ('The Royal Path of Poets') is a treatise on the principles of Kannada poetry and grammar, and it provides invaluable insights into the Kannada language and literature of the 9th century. Amoghavarsha was a devout Jain and patronised the great Jain philosopher Jinasena, under whose guidance he built the Jain basadi (temple) complex at Shravanabelagola (though the famous Gommateshwara statue was built later by the Ganga minister Chamundaraya). Amoghavarsha also patronised Jinasena's pupil Gunabhadra, who wrote the Adipurana, a Jain version of the life of the first Jain tirthankara Rishabha. He established the city of Manyakheta (Malkhed) as a magnificent capital. Despite his peaceful inclinations, he successfully defended his kingdom against invasions from the Gangas, the Vengi Chalukyas, and the Pratiharas.

Krishna III (939-967 CE) was the last great Rashtrakuta ruler. He fought the Chola king Parantaka I and defeated him decisively at the Battle of Takkolam (949 CE) in present-day Tamil Nadu. The Chola crown prince Rajaditya was killed on the battlefield (struck by an arrow while seated atop his war elephant). Krishna III annexed the northern part of the Chola kingdom (Tondaimandalam, including Kanchipuram) and assumed the titles 'Kannara' and 'Trikalingadhipati' (Lord of the three Kalingas). He also defeated the Paramara king of Malwa and the Kalachuris of Tripuri. After Krishna's death, the Rashtrakuta Empire declined rapidly due to the resurgence of the Western Chalukyas under Tailapa II. The last Rashtrakuta king, Karka II, was defeated by Tailapa II in 982 CE, ending the dynasty's 230-year rule.

Rashtrakuta Administration and Culture: The Rashtrakuta Empire was divided into provinces (rashtras) under governors (rashtrapatis), and further into vishayas (districts) and gramas (villages). The Rashtrakutas were patrons of a remarkable range of religions: they were generally Shaiva (Krishna I, Govinda III, Krishna III were Shaivas) but were exceptionally tolerant of Jains, Buddhists, and Vaishnavas. Amoghavarsha, as noted, was a Jain. The Rashtrakuta period saw the flourishing of Kannada literature (Pampa, Ponna, and Ranna — the 'three gems of Kannada literature' — all wrote during this period), Sanskrit literature (the philosopher Shankara, the mathematician Mahaviracharya who wrote the Ganita Sara Samgraha), and architecture (the Kailasa Temple and Elephanta Caves are the supreme examples). The Rashtrakutas were also active in trade with the Arab world, especially in horses, spices, and textiles. The Arab traveller Sulaiman al-Tajir (851 CE, in his Silsilat al-Tawarikh) described the Rashtrakuta king Amoghavarsha as 'one of the four great kings of the world' — the others being the Caliph of Baghdad, the Chinese Emperor, and the Byzantine Emperor. This reflects the immense prestige and power of the Rashtrakuta Empire on the global stage.

The Tripartite Struggle: The Rashtrakutas were a key participant in the Tripartite Struggle (c. 750-950 CE) for the control of Kannauj (the ancient capital of Harsha's empire, which controlled the Gangetic plain and was the symbolic seat of imperial power in North India). The three contenders were the Rashtrakutas (under Dhruva, Govinda III, and others), the Gurjara-Pratiharas of Rajasthan (under Nagabhata I, Vatsaraja, and Bhoja I), and the Palas of Bengal (under Gopala, Dharmapala, and Devapala). The struggle lasted for nearly two centuries, with no single power winning permanently. Kannauj changed hands multiple times. The Pratiharas finally secured Kannauj under Bhoja I (c. 836-885 CE) and held it for a century, but the wars exhausted all three kingdoms and left them vulnerable to external invasions (Mahmud of Ghazni for the Pratiharas, the Ghurid invasions for the Palas, and the Western Chalukya resurgence for the Rashtrakutas).

The Chalukyas of Kalyani / Western Chalukyas (c. 973-1189 CE)

The Western Chalukyas (also called the Chalukyas of Kalyani or the Later Chalukyas) were a Kannada dynasty that revived the power of the earlier Chalukyas of Badami. They ruled from their capital at Kalyani (modern Basavakalyan, Bidar district, Karnataka) and controlled much of the Deccan plateau for over two centuries. The dynasty was founded by Tailapa II (973-997 CE), a feudatory of the Rashtrakutas who defeated the last Rashtrakuta king Karka II and established the new Chalukya dynasty. Tailapa II also defeated the Paramara king Munja and the Chola king Rajaraja I's initial campaigns. He assumed the title Ahavamalla (One who invites battle).

The greatest Western Chalukya ruler was Vikramaditya VI (1076-1126 CE), who reigned for 50 years — one of the longest and most celebrated reigns in Deccan history. He ascended the throne after deposing his elder brother Someshvara II in a civil war. Vikramaditya VI fought a series of wars against the Chola emperor Kulottunga I for control of the Vengi kingdom and the Raichur Doab, but neither side gained lasting supremacy. He assumed the title Permadideva and Tribhuvana Malla (Lord of the Three Worlds). He began the Chalukya-Vikrama era (also called the Vikrama era, starting from 1076 CE), a new calendar era used in inscriptions for two centuries. Vikramaditya VI was a great patron of learning and the arts. His court poet was the Kashmiri Brahmin Bilhana, who wrote the Vikramankadeva-charita ('The Life of Vikramaditya'), a Sanskrit epic poem in 18 cantos celebrating the king's life and accomplishments (it is the most important source for the early Western Chalukya history). Another great scholar in his court was Vijnaneshwara, who wrote the Mitakshara, the most authoritative commentary on the Yajnavalkya Smriti and the foundational text of Hindu inheritance law across most of India (it was adopted as the standard by the British colonial courts for Anglo-Hindu law). The Mitakshara belongs to the 'Dayabhaga-Mitakshara' dichotomy that still governs Hindu succession in India.

Western Chalukya Architecture: The Western Chalukya period is famous for a distinctive architectural style (the Chalukya or Gadag style), which was a transitional style between the early Dravida style of the Badami Chalukyas and the later Hoysala style. The style is characterised by the use of soapstone (chloritic schist) as the primary building material, richly carved stepped wells, star-shaped platforms, and intricately sculpted pillars and ceilings. The main centres of Western Chalukya architecture are Lakkundi (the ancient capital), Gadag, Huligere (Lakshmeshwar), and Dambal. The Mahadeva Temple at Ittagi (built c. 1112 CE by the commander Mahadeva, a general of Vikramaditya VI) is considered the finest example of the Western Chalukya style. The temple has a staggering 68 inscriptions on its walls and is built in a 14-pointed star-shaped plan with elaborate sculptures of Vishnu, Shiva, Brahma, and various deities. The Kashivishvanatha Temple at Lakkundi (built in 1006 CE) is a masterpiece of the early Western Chalukya style, with exquisite lathe-turned pillars and a stepped well (the Lakkundi stepped well is one of the finest in India). The Hoysaleswara Temple at Halebidu, though built under the Hoysalas, shows the mature influence of the Western Chalukya style. The Western Chalukya period also saw the construction of several Jain basadis (temples). The dynasty declined in the late 12th century due to the rise of their feudatories — the Hoysalas (under Vishnuvardhana), the Kakatiyas (under Prola II), and the Yadavas of Devagiri. The last Chalukya king, Someshvara IV, was defeated by the Hoysala king Veera Ballala II and the Yadava king Bhillama V in 1189 CE.

The Kakatiyas (c. 1163-1323 CE) — The Lords of Warangal

The Kakatiyas were a Telugu dynasty that ruled the Warangal region (in present-day Telangana) from their capital at Orugallu (Warangal). They began as feudatories of the Western Chalukyas and later became the dominant power in the Telugu-speaking region. The dynasty was founded by Prola II (1110-1158 CE), who established Kakatiya independence. However, the greatest Kakatiya ruler was Ganapati Deva (1199-1262 CE), who ruled for 63 years and expanded the kingdom to its greatest extent. He conquered the coastal Andhra region (the Vengi kingdom), the Kalinga region (parts of Odisha), and the adjoining areas. He assumed the title Andhra Desadheeshwara (Lord of the Andhra Country). Ganapati Deva established an efficient administration based on the nayamkara system (a land-grant system for military service). He built the Thousand Pillar Temple at Warangal (a magnificent Shiva temple with 1000 intricately carved pillars, though many were destroyed in later invasions).

Rudrama Devi (1262-1289 CE) is one of the most remarkable figures in Indian history — one of the very few women to rule a kingdom in her own right in medieval India. She was the daughter of Ganapati Deva, who had no sons and raised her as a prince. She was trained in military arts, archery, and statecraft. Rudrama Devi ruled for 27 years, dressed as a king, led her armies in battle, and is depicted in contemporary sculptures with a beard and moustache (the kingly ideal of the time). She successfully repelled invasions from the Seuna Yadavas, the Hoysalas, and the Pandyas. She continued building the magnificent Warangal Fort, especially the famous gateways (the four massive stone toranas or ornamental gateways, carved from single granite blocks, which are now the symbol of Telangana). She also patronised literature and the arts: the Sanskrit poet Vidyanatha's Prataparudriya was written in her court. She appointed her grandson Prataparudra II (1289-1323 CE) as her successor. Prataparudra II was the last and most celebrated Kakatiya ruler. He defended the kingdom against the first invasion from the Delhi Sultanate under Alauddin Khalji's general Malik Kafur (1310 CE) by paying a huge tribute. However, the second invasion under the Tughlaq ruler Muhammad bin Tughlaq (1323 CE) was successful. Prataparudra II was captured and died on the way to Delhi (he is said to have drowned himself in the Narmada River). The Kakatiya kingdom was annexed to the Delhi Sultanate, and their treasuries — the famous 'Koh-i-Noor' diamond (discovered in the Kollur mines, part of the Kakatiya dominion) and the 'Great Mughal' diamond — were taken to Delhi. The Kakatiya period saw the flourishing of Telugu literature: Tikkana (1220-1300 CE), the second poet of the Andhra Mahabharatamu, translated the Mahabharata into Telugu under the patronage of a Kakatiya noble, and Ketu wrote the Dasha-skandha. The Kakatiya architectural style is distinctive — the use of granite, the unique torana gateways, and the star-shaped platforms influenced the later Vijayanagara style.

The Hoysalas (c. 1026-1343 CE) — The Temple Builders of Karnataka

The Hoysalas were a Kannada dynasty that ruled most of modern Karnataka from their capitals at Belur and Halebidu (Dwarasamudra). They began as feudatories of the Western Chalukyas of Kalyani and later became independent and built one of the most distinctive architectural traditions in Indian history. The founder of the dynasty was Nripa Kama II (1026-1047 CE), but the first significant Hoysala ruler was Vishnuvardhana (1108-1152 CE), originally called Bittiga. Under the influence of the Vaishnava saint Ramanujacharya, he converted from Jainism to Srivaishnavism and changed his name from Bittiga to Vishnuvardhana. He defeated the Chola king Kulottunga I at the Battle of Talakad (1117 CE), conquering the Gangavadi region (southern Karnataka), and captured the Chola town of Nidugal. He fought the Western Chalukyas, winning independence for the Hoysala kingdom. He also patronised the Kalachuri rebellion against the Chalukyas. Vishnuvardhana's greatest legacy is the Chennakesava Temple at Belur (built 1117 CE), one of the most beautiful temples in India. The temple is built on a star-shaped platform and is made of chloritic schist (soapstone), which allows for extraordinarily intricate carving. The temple has 48 pillars, each carved differently, with exquisite sculptures of Madanikas (celestial dancers) in sensuous poses, bracket figures of women playing musical instruments, and detailed friezes of elephants, horses, and scenes from the Ramayana and Mahabharata. The temple's gopuram (gateway) is a later addition of the Vijayanagara period.

Veera Ballala II (1173-1220 CE) was the greatest Hoysala military ruler. He defeated the Western Chalukya king Someshvara IV and the Yadava king Bhillama V, establishing Hoysala supremacy over southern Karnataka. He took the title 'Chalukya-rajya-pratishtacharya' (the restorer of the Chalukya kingdom) and 'Dakshina-chakravarti' (Emperor of the South). He also captured the Pandya territories in Tamil Nadu. His capital at Dwarasamudra (Halebidu) became a magnificent city described by contemporary travellers as one of the finest in India. The most important work of Hoysala architecture, the Hoysaleswara Temple at Halebidu, was built during his reign (completed c. 1120 CE). The temple is dedicated to Lord Shiva and is one of the largest and most ornate temples in Karnataka. It is built on a star-shaped platform (a characteristic Hoysala feature), with two shrines (one for Hoysaleswara, one for Shantaleswara, named after the king and queen). The temple walls are covered with an astonishing 1,000-2,000 friezes of elephants, lions, horses, swans, and scenes from the Ramayana, Mahabharata, and Bhagavata Purana — every inch of the temple surface is carved with intricate detail. The Hoysaleswara Temple was never completed (the vimana is missing) because it was sacked during the Delhi Sultanate invasions. The Kedareshwara Temple at Halebidu and the Ishvara Temple at Arasikere are other fine examples of the Hoysala style.

Veera Ballala III (1291-1343 CE) was the last great Hoysala ruler and one of the few South Indian kings who offered prolonged resistance to the Delhi Sultanate. When Alauddin Khalji's general Malik Kafur invaded Dwarasamudra in 1311 CE, Veera Ballala III surrendered and agreed to pay an annual tribute. After Alauddin's death, he stopped paying and reasserted Hoysala independence. When Muhammad bin Tughlaq invaded in 1327 CE, Veera Ballala III again submitted but was treated with respect. He later joined the rebellion of the Deccan chiefs and was captured and killed at the Battle of Madurai (1343 CE) while trying to reclaim the Pandya territories. His son Veera Ballala IV was the last nominal Hoysala ruler, but the kingdom had already fragmented. The Hoysala legacy includes the distinctive Hoysala architectural style characterised by: (1) the use of chloritic schist (soapstone) as the primary building material, which enables extraordinarily fine carving; (2) the star-shaped or stellate platform (jagati); (3) the use of lathe-turned pillars; (4) the elaborate and intricately carved vimana (tower) with multiple miniature decorative towers; (5) the absence of large gopurams (gateways) found in the later Vijayanagara and Tamil styles; and (6) the profusion of sculptures — every surface is covered with figures of gods, goddesses, celestial dancers, animals, and scenes from Hindu epics. The Hoysala style directly influenced the later Vijayanagara temple architecture.

The Yadavas of Devagiri (c. 1187-1317 CE)

The Yadavas of Devagiri (also called the Seuna Yadavas) were a Marathi-Kannada dynasty that ruled the northern Deccan from their capital at Devagiri (modern Daulatabad, Maharashtra). They began as feudatories of the Western Chalukyas and later emerged as an independent kingdom. The dynasty was founded by Bhillama V (1187-1191 CE), who defeated the Western Chalukya king Someshvara IV and his Hoysala allies, capturing the strategic forts of Devagiri and Sinnar.

The greatest Yadava ruler was Singhana (1210-1247 CE), who expanded the kingdom to its greatest territorial extent, covering much of modern Maharashtra, northern Karnataka, and parts of Madhya Pradesh. He defeated the Paramaras of Malwa (capturing the fort of Mandu), the Kakatiyas, the Hoysalas, and the Vaghelas of Gujarat. Singhana was a great patron of learning and the arts. He established a library and patronised scholars in Sanskrit, Marathi, and Kannada. The most famous scholar in his court was Hemadri (also called Hemadpant), the chief minister and a polymath who wrote the Chaturvarga Chintamani, an encyclopedic digest of Hindu rituals, and the Vratakhanda (a manual of religious observances). Hemadri developed the Hemadpanti architectural style (a style of temple architecture characterised by the use of black stone or basalt, with intricately carved pillars, ceilings, and a distinctive shikhara). The Gondeshwar Temple at Sinnar (built by Hemadri's family) and the Patnadevi Temple at Patan are fine examples of the Hemadpanti style. Singhana also patronised the Kannada poet Kavichakravarti and the Marathi saint Dnyaneshwar (Gyaneshwar), who wrote the Dnyaneshwari, a commentary on the Bhagavad Gita in Marathi (1290 CE), and is considered the father of Marathi literature.

Ramachandra (1271-1309/1311 CE), also known as Ramadeva, was the last great Yadava ruler. He expanded the kingdom, defeating the Hoysala king Veera Ballala III and the Kakatiya king Prataparudra II. However, his reign is most famous for his confrontation with the Delhi Sultanate. In 1296 CE, Alauddin Khalji, then the governor of Kara (near Allahabad), invaded Devagiri on his own initiative. Alauddin marched 800 km through the Vindhya forests with a select force, surprised the Yadava army, and captured Devagiri.
