Ramachandra was forced to pay a massive ransom (including 600 maunds of gold, 7 maunds of pearls, and 1,000 pounds of silver) and agreed to become a vassal of Delhi. When Alauddin became Sultan in 1296 CE, Ramachandra stopped paying tribute. In 1305-1307 CE, Alauddin sent his general Malik Kafur to Devagiri again. This time, Ramachandra was defeated, captured, and brought to Delhi. Alauddin treated him with honour, reinstated him as a vassal, and gave him the title 'Ray-i-Rayan' (King of Kings). Ramachandra served Alauddin loyally thereafter. He died in 1311 CE. His son Shankara Deva (1311-1313 CE) rebelled against Delhi but was defeated by Malik Kafur. The next ruler, Harapala Deva (1313-1317 CE), was Ramachandra's son-in-law. He also rebelled and was captured by the Delhi general Qutbuddin Mubarak Shah. Harapala was flayed alive (skinned) for his rebellion. The Yadava kingdom was annexed to the Delhi Sultanate after 1317 CE. The Yadavas are important in Indian history for patronising early Marathi literature, for the development of the Hemadpanti architectural style (which influenced the later Deccan Sultanate architecture), and for being the first major Deccan kingdom to encounter the expansionist Delhi Sultanate — an event that foreshadowed the Deccan wars of the Tughlaq and Vijayanagara periods.

The Pandyas (c. 590-1344 CE) — The Masters of Madurai

The Pandyas of Madurai were one of the three ancient Tamil kingdoms (Chera, Chola, Pandya) with a continuous history from the Sangam period (c. 300 BCE) to the 14th century CE. They revived their power in the 13th century CE under a series of able rulers who challenged the Chola supremacy. Their capital was Madurai, the oldest continuously inhabited city in the Indian subcontinent, a great centre of Tamil culture and the site of the legendary Sangam (the ancient Tamil literary academy). The most important Pandya ruler of this period was Jatavarman Sundara Pandya I (1251-1268 CE), who revived the Pandya kingdom and made it the dominant power in the Tamil country. He defeated the Chola king Rajendra III (who had ruled for only a year after a succession dispute), sacked Thanjavur, and captured the Chola regalia. He also defeated the Hoysala king Someshvara and the Chera king of Kerala. Sundara Pandya I assumed the title 'Trisamudradhipati' (Lord of the Three Seas) and expanded the kingdom to include most of Tamil Nadu and parts of Kerala and Andhra. He patronised the construction of the Meenakshi Temple at Madurai (the outer walls and gopurams were built later by the Vijayanagara Nayaks, but the core shrine was built by the Pandyas).

Maravarman Kulasekhara I (1268-1309 CE) was the greatest Pandya ruler. He defeated the Cholas decisively (the last Chola king Rajendra III was crushed in 1279, ending the Chola Empire). He also defeated the Hoysalas and the Cheras. The Madurai Meenakshi Temple reached its structural maturity under the Pandyas, though the iconic gopurams (gateway towers) were built later. The Pandya kingdom was the wealthiest and most powerful in South India at the end of the 13th century. In 1310 CE, Alauddin Khalji's general Malik Kafur invaded the Pandya kingdom. At this time, the kingdom was divided between two brothers: Jatavarman Vikrama Pandya (who ruled from Madurai) and Jatavarman Sundara Pandya III (who ruled from Kanchipuram). The fratricidal war weakened the kingdom, and Malik Kafur conquered both parts (1310-1311 CE). The temples of Madurai were plundered, and the Tamil country was subjected to Delhi's raids for the first time. A short-lived Madurai Sultanate (1335-1378 CE) was established by the Delhi governor Jalaluddin Ahsan Shah after the Tughlaq empire's collapse. The Pandya kingdom was finally extinguished by this Sultanate, and the region later passed under the control of the Vijayanagara Empire in the late 14th century.

The Pandya contribution to Tamil culture is immense. The Meenakshi Temple at Madurai is one of the most important temples in India (the goddess Meenakshi is an avatar of Parvati, and the temple is a masterpiece of Dravidian architecture). The Pandyas were patrons of Tamil literature, and the later Sangam works (especially the Silappadikaram and Manimekalai) were preserved and transmitted during their period. The Pandyas also built the Koodal Azhagar Temple at Madurai and the Tenkasi Nellaiappar Temple. They maintained extensive trade connections with the Roman Empire, Sri Lanka, and Southeast Asia. The port of Korkai (the pearl-fishing centre) and Kulam (Quilon) were important ports under the Pandyas.

The Chola Empire (c. 850-1279 CE)

Origins and Early Chola Kings

The Chola Kingdom of the Sangam period (c. 300 BCE-300 CE) had declined by the post-Sangam Kalabhra interregnum (c. 300-600 CE). The Cholas were reduced to small chieftains under the Pallava and Pandya overlords. The Chola resurgence began with Vijayalaya Chola (c. 850-871 CE), a feudatory of the Pallavas, who captured the city of Thanjavur from the Mutharaiyar chieftains and established the Chola capital there. His son Aditya I (871-907 CE) defeated the Pallava king Aparajitavarman and annexed the entire Pallava kingdom (Tondaimandalam). He also conquered the Kongu country (western Tamil Nadu) and parts of the Chera kingdom. Parantaka I (907-955 CE) conquered the Pandyas (he assumed the title Maduraiyum Elamum Konda — the one who conquered Madurai and Sri Lanka, though his Sri Lankan invasion failed), and fought the Rashtrakuta king Krishna III (who defeated him at the Battle of Takkolam, 949 CE). Parantaka I is famous for the Uttaramerur Inscriptions (c. 920 CE), the most detailed record of local self-government in the ancient world. The Chola kingdom declined for a generation after the Rashtrakuta defeat, and it was revived by Sundara Chola (Parantaka II) (957-973 CE) and his brilliant general Kannara Devan.

Rajaraja I (985-1014 CE)

Rajaraja I, born Arulmozhivarman, was the greatest Chola ruler and one of the most accomplished monarchs in Indian history. He ascended the throne at age 35 and immediately launched a series of military campaigns that transformed the Chola kingdom into an empire. He defeated the Cheras (the Chera Perumal kings of Kerala) and destroyed the Chera navy at the port of Kandalur Salai, capturing the Chera capital of Muziris (the greatest port of the ancient Kerala coast, the centre of the Indo-Roman spice trade). He conquered the Pandyas of Madurai, ending their independence (the Pandya king was killed in battle). He annexed the northern part of Sri Lanka (the Rajarata kingdom of Anuradhapura), which he renamed Mummudi Chola Mandalam, and built a new capital at Polonnaruwa (which became the Chola administrative centre in Sri Lanka). He campaigned against the Western Chalukyas of Kalyani (King Satyashraya) for control of the Raichur Doab, capturing the fort of Rattamandali and parts of the Tungabhadra region. He also sent a naval expedition to the Maldives (the 'Sea of a Thousand Islands' as the inscriptions call it). Rajaraja's greatest cultural achievement was the Brihadishwara Temple at Thanjavur (built 1003-1010 CE). The temple was designed by the architect Kunjara Mallan Raja Raja Perunthachchan. It is the largest and tallest Shiva temple in India: the vimana (main tower) rises to 66 metres (216 feet) in 13 storeys, crowned by a single 80-ton granite dome (the shikara), which was dragged up a 6.4-kilometre ramp from the quarry at Tirumalaipuram. The sanctum houses the largest Shiva linga in existence (3.6 metres high, made of two stones). The temple is built entirely of granite (the first such large-scale granite construction in India). The Brihadishwara Temple is one of the Great Living Chola Temples, a UNESCO World Heritage Site. It is notable for the 108 karanas (dance postures) carved on its gopuram, the massive Nandi (bull) monolith in the courtyard (5.9 metres long, 3.2 metres high, weighing 25 tons), and the Chola frescoes (murals of Shiva and Parvati, the Chola royal family) on the temple walls, which are the oldest surviving Indian frescoes.

Rajendra I (1014-1044 CE)

Rajendra I, the son of Rajaraja, extended the Chola Empire to its greatest territorial extent. He is the only Indian ruler to have led a successful military campaign to both the Ganges River and the Malay Archipelago. His northern campaign (c. 1020-1023 CE) marched through Odisha (Kalinga), defeated the Somavamsi king, crossed the Godavari, and defeated the Pala king Mahipala I of Bengal at the Battle of the Ganges. The Chola army reached the Ganges River and brought back the holy Ganges water in gold pots to consecrate his new capital. Rajendra assumed the title Gangaikonda Chola (the Chola who conquered the Ganges) and built a magnificent new capital at Gangaikonda Cholapuram (near Ariyalur, Tamil Nadu, about 100 km from Thanjavur). The capital's main temple, the Gangaikonda Cholapuram Temple, is the second great Shiva temple built by the Cholas: the vimana is 55 metres tall (only slightly shorter than the Thanjavur temple), with exquisite Chola bronzes and the largest courtyard of any Chola temple. Rajendra's most remarkable achievement was the naval expedition to Srivijaya in 1025 CE. Srivijaya was a powerful Buddhist thalassocracy based in Sumatra (Indonesia) that controlled the strategic Strait of Malacca (the main trade route between China and India). Rajendra's navy attacked and captured the Srivijayan capital of Kadaram (Kedah, in modern Malaysia), and conquered the kingdoms of Srivijaya (Sumatra), Pannai (Sumatra), Malaiyur (Malay Peninsula), and Tambralinga (southern Thailand, the Ligor region). The expedition broke Srivijaya's monopoly on the China trade and established direct Chola-Chinese commercial relations. A Chola diplomatic mission was sent to the Song Chinese court in 1077 CE (under Rajendra's grandson, Kulottunga I), and the Chinese emperor reciprocated with gifts. The Chola navy also continued to dominate the Palk Strait and the Bay of Bengal.

Later Cholas and the Decline

The later Chola kings after Rajendra I maintained the empire but gradually lost their northern territories. Kulottunga I (1070-1120 CE) was the grandson of Rajendra I through his daughter and was also the Eastern Chalukya king of Vengi (the Godavari delta region). He united the Chola and Vengi kingdoms, making him the ruler of the entire Tamil-Telugu coast. He ruled for 50 years, one of the longest reigns in Indian history. He sent the embassy to China mentioned above. After Kulottunga I, the Chola empire declined due to the rise of the Pandyas (under Jatavarman Sundara Pandya I, 1251-1268 CE, who defeated the Chola king Rajendra III and sacked Thanjavur), and the Hoysalas (who invaded from the west). The Chola dynasty ended in 1279 CE when the last Chola king, Rajendra III, was defeated by the Pandya king Maravarman Kulasekhara I. The Chola legacy is immense: they established the model of the temple as the centre of social and economic life (the Brihadishwara and Gangaikonda Cholapuram temples are UNESCO World Heritage Sites); they pioneered the system of village self-government (the Uttaramerur inscriptions are held up as a model of indigenous democracy); they created the finest school of bronze sculpture in world history (the Chola bronzes of Shiva Nataraja, Ardhanarishwara, Parvati, Vishnu, and others, cast using the lost-wax process, with their dynamic poses, perfect proportions, and sublime expressions); and they established the model of the maritime empire that projected Indian culture across South Asia and Southeast Asia.

Chola Administration: The Uttaramerur System

The Chola Empire had the most detailed and well-documented system of local self-government in the ancient world. The empire was divided into mandalams (provinces, initially 8-9), valanadus (districts), nadus (sub-districts of 100-500 villages), and urs (individual villages). The heart of the system was the village assembly, which existed in two forms: the ur (the general assembly of all adult male villagers, common in ordinary villages) and the sabha or mahajana (a more specialised assembly of Brahmins who had received land grants, found in brahmadeya or agrahara villages). The most famous description comes from the Uttaramerur Inscriptions (c. 920 CE) of Parantaka I, found on the walls of the Uttara Merur Vaikuntha Perumal Temple (in present-day Kanchipuram district, Tamil Nadu). According to this inscription, members of the sabha were elected by lottery (kudavolai, the pot-ticket system): candidates' names were written on palm-leaf tickets, placed in a pot, and a young boy (who did not know the names) drew the tickets. The qualifications for a candidate were strict: ownership of at least one-quarter veli (about 2 acres) of land, minimum age 35, knowledge of the Vedas, residence in the village, and no criminal record. Disqualifications: anyone who had been a committee member for the past 3 years, anyone whose close relatives (father, brother, son, son-in-law) had been a committee member in the past 3 years, anyone who had failed to pay taxes, and anyone who had committed incest or any of the five great sins (Brahma-hatya, etc.). The elected sabha had 12 committees (variyams), each serving for 360 days before fresh elections: the garden committee, the tank (irrigation) committee, the annual committee (general administration), the gold committee (managing temple funds), the five-fold committee (justice and arbitration), and others. The Uttaramerur system is often cited as an example of the democratic traditions of ancient India, though it should be noted that it was limited to the Brahmin landowning elite — it was not universal suffrage. The Chola central administration was headed by the king (absolute authority but bound by dharma and the advice of his council), assisted by the yuvaraja (crown prince), the pradhani (chief minister), the senapati (commander-in-chief), the madhyastha (chief justice), and the purohita (royal priest). The Chola army consisted of infantry, cavalry (though horses had to be imported from Arabia), war elephants, and a formidable navy.

Chola Economy, Trade, and Culture

The Chola economy was based on highly developed agriculture, extensive irrigation, and a flourishing overseas trade. The Kaveri River delta was the rice bowl of South India, irrigated by an elaborate system of canals and tanks (the Grand Anicut or Kallanai on the Kaveri River, built by the Sangam Chola king Karikala c. 150 CE, is still in use and is the oldest functioning water regulation structure in the world). Chola merchants traded extensively with Song China (Chinese records mention Chola embassies in 1015, 1033, 1077 CE), Southeast Asia (Srivijaya, the Khmer Empire, Champa, Burma), Sri Lanka, Arabia, and East Africa. Exports: pepper (the most valuable Indian export in the medieval world), cardamom, ginger, cinnamon, pearls, diamonds (from the Kollur mines), textiles (fine cotton from Madurai and Tirunelveli), sandalwood, ivory, spices, and aromatic woods. Imports: horses (from Arabia and Persia through the ports of Calicut and Quilon), Chinese silk and porcelain, aromatics (frankincense, myrrh), and precious stones. Trade was conducted through powerful merchant guilds: the Ainoorruvar ('Five Hundred Lords of Ayyavole') and the Manigramam guilds had branches across South India and Southeast Asia, with their own laws, courts, and armies. The guild members were called nanadesi (merchants of many countries) and svadesi (local merchants). The guilds built temples and monasteries in the ports of Burma, Sumatra, and China (a Tamil inscription from 1088 CE at Pagan, Burma, records a Tamil merchant guild donation). Chola culture flourished in every sphere: Tamil literature (the Ramavataram or Kamba Ramayanam by Kamban; the Periyapuranam by Sekkizhar, a hagiography of the 63 Nayanar saints; the Tevaram and Tiruvasagam hymns of the Saiva saints; the Nalayira Divya Prabandham of the Vaishnava Alvars); bronze sculpture (Chola bronzes — the finest in the world, cast by the lost-wax process, with the Nataraja or Dancing Shiva being the most famous); music and dance (Bharatanatyam was codified into its classical form during this period as the dance of the devadasis in the Chola temples).

The Bhakti Movement in South India

The Bhakti movement was the most important religious and cultural development in medieval India. It emerged in Tamil Nadu between the 6th and 9th centuries CE as a powerful devotional movement centred on personal love and surrender to God, and it spread across India over the next 600 years. The Bhakti movement in South India is divided into two main streams: the Shaiva Bhakti of the 63 Nayanars and the Vaishnava Bhakti of the 12 Alvars.

The Nayanars and the Shaiva Bhakti Movement

The 63 Nayanars (Nayanmars) were the Shaiva saint-poets of Tamil Nadu who lived between the 6th and 10th centuries CE. They came from every walk of life — kings (Cheraman Perumal), Brahmins (Sundarar), untouchables (Nandanar), hunters (Kannappa Nayanar), weavers, potters, and prostitutes. The most important Nayanars are the 'Three Great Saints' (the Muttalvar, the 'First Three'): Sambandar (also called Tirugnana Sambandar, c. 600-630 CE), a child prodigy who is said to have been fed by the goddess Parvati as a baby; Appar (Tirunavukkarasar, c. 580-660 CE), a former Jain convert to Shaivism who composed 306 hymns; and Sundarar (Sundaramurthi Nayanar, c. 8th century CE), a prince who gave up his kingdom to become a devotee of Shiva. The hymns of the three saints, along with the works of Manikkavasagar (c. 9th century CE, author of the Tiruvasagam), were compiled into the Tevaram (a collection of 796 hymns in 24 volumes), the canonical text of Tamil Shaivism. The Tevaram hymns were sung in Shiva temples across the Tamil country, a practice that continues to this day. The Tiruvasagam (the 'Sacred Utterance') by Manikkavasagar is considered the pinnacle of Tamil devotional poetry — with its intense emotional longing, its lyrical beauty, and its profound philosophical depth. The 63 Nayanars were canonised later in the Periyapuranam (c. 1130 CE) by Sekkizhar, a court poet of the Chola king Kulottunga II. The Periyapuranam is a hagiographical epic that recounts the lives and miracles of each of the 63 Nayanars, and it remains one of the most important works of Tamil literature.

The Alvars and the Vaishnava Bhakti Movement

The 12 Alvars (also spelled Azhwars, meaning 'those who are immersed in the love of God') were the Vaishnava saint-poets who lived between the 6th and 9th centuries CE. Like the Nayanars, the Alvars came from diverse social backgrounds: a king (Kulasekhara Alvar), a Brahmin (Tirumalisai Alvar), a chieftain (Periyalvar), a woman saint (Andal, the only female Alvar), and even an untouchable (Tiruppan Alvar). The most important Alvars are Nammalvar (the most prolific and influential, c. 800-830 CE, author of the Tiruviruttam and Tiruvaymoli, 1102 verses), Andal (the only female Alvar, c. 9th century CE, author of the beautiful Tiruppavai — 30 verses in praise of Krishna, sung by young girls during the month of Margazhi), and Tirumangai Alvar (c. 9th century CE, who composed the most verses among the Alvars, 936 hymns). The hymns of the 12 Alvars were compiled into the Nalayira Divya Prabandham (the 'Four Thousand Divine Hymns'), the canonical text of Tamil Vaishnavism. The Divya Prabandham is considered the Tamil Veda (Dravida Veda) by Srivaishnavas and is recited in Vishnu temples alongside the Sanskrit Vedas. The Alvars worshipped Lord Vishnu and his avatars (especially Krishna as the cowherd boy and Rama), and their poetry expresses intense personal love (bhakti) for God. The Srivaishnava tradition, which systematised the Alvar hymns and philosophy, was codified by the Acharyas (preceptors) Nathamuni (c. 10th century CE), Yamunacharya (c. 10th-11th century CE), and most importantly Ramanuja (1017-1137 CE).

The Great Acharyas: Shankara, Ramanuja, Madhva, and Basavanna

Shankara Bhagavatpada (c. 788-820 CE), born in Kaladi (Kerala), was the greatest philosopher of the Advaita Vedanta school. He established the doctrine of Advaita (Non-Dualism), teaching that Brahman (the ultimate reality) is one without a second, and that the world is an illusion (maya) superimposed on Brahman through ignorance (avidya). Liberation (moksha) is achieved through knowledge (jnana) of the identity of Atman (the individual self) with Brahman. Shankara wrote commentaries (bhashyas) on the Brahma Sutras, the Upanishads, and the Bhagavad Gita, which became the foundational texts of Advaita Vedanta. He also composed devotional hymns (the Bhaja Govindam, the Shivananda Lahari, and the Saundarya Lahari). Shankara established four Mathas (monastic centres) in the four corners of India — Joshimath (north), Dwarka (west), Puri (east), and Sringeri (south) — to preserve and propagate his teachings. Each Matha is headed by a Shankaracharya, a tradition that continues to this day. Shankara's Advaita had a profound influence on Indian philosophy and religion, and it was a powerful response to the rising influence of Buddhism and Jainism. He lived at the height of the Pallava and Rashtrakuta periods and is said to have debated with the Mandana Mishra (a prominent Mimamsaka philosopher in the Rashtrakuta court).

Ramanuja (1017-1137 CE), born in Sriperumbudur (Tamil Nadu), was the greatest philosopher of the Vishishtadvaita (Qualified Non-Dualism) school. He taught that Brahman (Narayana/Vishnu) is a personal God with attributes, that the individual soul (Atman) and the world (Prakriti) are real but dependent on Brahman, and that liberation is achieved through devotion (bhakti) and surrender (prapatti) to God, not mere knowledge. Ramanuja wrote the Sri Bhashya (a commentary on the Brahma Sutras), the Vedanta Sara, and the Vedanta Deepa. He travelled across India, debating and converting people to Srivaishnavism. He is said to have converted the Hoysala king Vishnuvardhana from Jainism to Srivaishnavism, and he reformed the administration of the Tirupati and Srirangam temples. Ramanuja's influence on the Bhakti movement was immense: he made temple worship accessible to all castes, including untouchables (though within the framework of varna-ashrama-dharma), and his emphasis on bhakti and prapatti (surrender) became the theological foundation of the Sri Vaishnava tradition and influenced later Bhakti saints across India (including the Alvars' poetry and the later North Indian Bhakti movement). The Ramanuja tradition (Sri Vaishnavism) continues to be one of the most important Hindu communities in South India, centred at the great temple of Srirangam.

Madhvacharya (c. 1238-1317 CE), born near Udupi (Karnataka), was the founder of the Dvaita (Dualism) school of Vedanta. He taught that there is an eternal and absolute difference (bheda) between God (Vishnu/Narayana), the individual soul, and the material world — five fundamental differences: (1) between God and the soul, (2) between God and matter, (3) between soul and matter, (4) between one soul and another, (5) between one material object and another. Liberation is achieved through devotion (bhakti) and the grace of God, but the soul remains eternally distinct from God. Madhva wrote commentaries on the Brahma Sutras (the Anu Vyakhyana), the Upanishads, and the Bhagavad Gita. He established the Udupi Krishna Matha in Karnataka, which remains a major centre of the Dvaita tradition. The Dvaita school was a direct challenge to Shankara's Advaita and represented the most theistic and dualistic interpretation of Vedanta.

Basavanna (1105-1167 CE) was a philosopher, statesman, and poet who founded the Lingayatism (also called Virashaiva) movement in Karnataka. He was born in Ingaleswaran, Bijapur district, and served as the chief minister of the Kalachuri king Bijjala II (who had overthrown the Western Chalukyas). Basavanna's teachings were radically egalitarian: he rejected the caste system, the authority of the Vedas, temple worship, and the supremacy of Brahmins. He taught that every human being is equal and can attain liberation through devotion to Shiva (manifested as the Ishtalinga — a small linga worn on the body). He composed simple, direct poems in Kannada called Vachanas (sayings, literally 'that which is said'), which are among the finest works of Kannada literature. The Vachanas use everyday language, metaphors from village life, and express a deeply personal, passionate devotion to Shiva ('Kudala Sangama Deva'). The movement attracted thousands of followers from all castes, including the poet-saint Allama Prabhu (the most intellectually profound of the Vachana poets) and Akkamahadevi (a female poet who renounced worldly life and walked naked, covered only by her long hair, singing her love for Lord Mallikarjuna). The Lingayats are a major religious community in Karnataka to this day.

Temple Architecture: The Dravida Style

The Dravidian style of temple architecture evolved in South India over a thousand years, passing through several distinct phases. The essential features of the Dravida style are: (1) the vimana (the main tower over the sanctum) is pyramidal in shape, rising in distinct storeys (talas), each with a row of miniature decorative roofs (haramika), and crowned by a shikhara (a dome-shaped finial) and a stupi/kalasha (the pinnacle pot); (2) the temple is enclosed by massive walls (prakara) with tall gateways called gopurams (which became increasingly large and elaborate in the later period); (3) the temple complex includes a Nandi mandapa (hall for the bull, in Shiva temples), a mukha mandapa (entrance hall), a maha mandapa (main hall), and an ardha mandapa (intermediate hall); (4) the temple tank (pushkarini) for ritual bathing; and (5) the use of granite and sandstone as the primary building materials.

The evolution of the Dravida style can be traced through four major phases. (1) Pallava Phase (600-900 CE): The earliest phase, beginning with the rock-cut cave temples of Mahendravarman I (the mandapa style), followed by the monolithic rathas of Mamallapuram (Pancha Rathas, the Descent of the Ganges) under Narasimhavarman I, and culminating in the structural stone temples of Rajasimha (the Shore Temple and the Kailasanatha Temple at Kanchipuram). The Pallava style established the basic vocabulary of the Dravida style. (2) Chola Phase (850-1250 CE): The Chola period saw the Dravida style reach its classical maturity. The Brihadishwara Temple at Thanjavur (1003-1010 CE) and the Gangaikonda Cholapuram Temple (c. 1035 CE) are the supreme achievements of the Chola style. The Chola vimana is the tallest and most imposing in the Dravida tradition. The Chola style is characterised by its massive scale, its geometric precision, and its restrained ornamentation (the focus is on the grandeur of the vimana rather than on elaborate surface carving). (3) Western Chalukya and Hoysala Phase (1000-1300 CE): The Western Chalukya style (Gadag style) and the Hoysala style developed as variations of the Dravida tradition. The Western Chalukya style is characterised by its use of soapstone, its star-shaped platforms, and its exquisite lathe-turned pillars (Mahadeva Temple at Ittagi, Kashivishvanatha Temple at Lakkundi). The Hoysala style (Chennakesava Temple at Belur, Hoysaleswara Temple at Halebidu) took the soapstone carving tradition to its ultimate refinement — every surface is covered with intricate sculpture, the towers are shorter and more compact than the Chola vimana, and the use of the star-shaped platform is universal. (4) Vijayanagara Phase (1336-1646 CE): The Vijayanagara style is an elaboration of the Chola and Hoysala traditions, distinguished by its massive gopurams (gateway towers), the use of colonnaded mandapas (thousand-pillared halls), and the introduction of the kalyana mandapa (marriage hall) and the pushkari (stepped tank). The Vitthala Temple at Hampi (with its stone chariot and musical pillars) and the Hazara Rama Temple are the finest examples of the Vijayanagara style. The Vijayanagara style continued to flourish under the Nayakas of Madurai and Thanjavur, who built the magnificent gopurams of the Meenakshi Temple (the tallest is 52 metres) and the Ranganathaswamy Temple at Srirangam (with 21 massive gopurams).

Maritime Trade and Merchant Guilds

South India participated in a vast network of maritime trade that connected the Indian Ocean world from China to East Africa. The period from 750 to 1300 CE was a golden age for South Indian trade, driven by the demand for Indian spices (especially pepper, cardamom, ginger, and cinnamon), textiles (fine cotton from Madurai and Tirunelveli), pearls (from the Gulf of Mannar, between India and Sri Lanka), diamonds (from the Kollur mines in the Kakatiya kingdom), sandalwood (from the forests of Mysore), and ivory (from the Kerala coast). The major ports of South India were Kaveripoompattinam (the ancient Chola port at the mouth of the Kaveri), Nagapattinam (the main Chola international port, which had a famous Buddhist monastery built by the Srivijaya king), Mahabalipuram (the Pallava port), Korkai (the Pandya pearl-fishing centre), Quilon/Kollam (the Chera port, founded by the Chera Perumal king Maruvan Kotha in 825 CE), and Calicut/Kozhikode (which emerged later as the centre of the spice trade with the Arab world).

The most distinctive institution of South Indian trade was the powerful merchant guild. The two most famous guilds were the Ainoorruvar (the 'Five Hundred Lords of Ayyavole') and the Manigramam. The Ainoorruvar guild originated in Ayyavole (Aihole, the early Chalukya capital in Karnataka) and had branches across South India, Sri Lanka, and Southeast Asia. They had their own army, their own legal code, and their own court system. Their inscriptions describe them as 'sword-bearing, bow-wielding, horse-riding merchants' who conducted trade on land and sea. The Manigramam guild (mentioned in early medieval Tamil inscriptions) was another major trading corporation that operated across the Indian Ocean. The guilds issued detailed regulatory rules for their members, including quality control, pricing, and dispute resolution. They were also major patrons of temple construction and temple charities (they financed temples in the ports where they operated). The guilds included both nanadesi (foreign merchants, who travelled to distant lands) and svadesi (local merchants). A famous Tamil inscription from 1088 CE at Pagan (Burma, modern Myanmar) records the donation of a temple by the Manigramam guild — a remarkable testament to the extent of South Indian trade networks. Another inscription from Sumatra records the trading activities of the 'Five Hundred Lords of Ayyavole'.

South Indian trade with Song China was particularly important. The Chola emperor Rajendra I sent embassies to the Song court in 1015, 1033, and 1077 CE (the last under Kulottunga I). Chinese records mention that the Chola embassy of 1077 CE brought gifts of corals, pearls, and coloured glass, and the Chinese emperor reciprocated with silk and porcelain. A large Tamil merchant community lived in the Chinese port cities of Quanzhou and Guangzhou. In Quanzhou, a bilingual Tamil-Chinese inscription (1281 CE) records the construction of a Shiva temple by Tamil merchants, indicating the presence of a substantial Tamil trading diaspora in China. South Indian trade with the Arab world and East Africa was equally extensive. The Arab merchant Sulaiman al-Tajir (9th century) and the Moroccan traveller Ibn Battuta (14th century) both describe the bustling ports of Kerala and the wealth of the Indian merchant communities. The Bahmani and Vijayanagara periods inherited and expanded this trade network, which would later attract the Portuguese, Dutch, and English to the Indian Ocean.

Literature in South Indian Languages

The period from 750 to 1300 CE was a golden age for literature in the four major Dravidian languages: Tamil, Kannada, Telugu, and Malayalam.

Tamil Literature: Tamil literature reached its classical maturity during the Chola period. The greatest Tamil literary works of this period include: the Ramavataram (the Tamil Ramayana) by Kamban (c. 1180 CE, the greatest Tamil epic after the Sangam works — a magnificent re-telling of Valmiki's Ramayana with distinctively Tamil sensibilities, rich in simile and metaphor); the Periyapuranam by Sekkizhar (c. 1130 CE, the canonical hagiography of the 63 Nayanars, commissioned by the Chola king Kulottunga II); the Tevaram (the 796 hymns of Sambandar, Appar, and Sundarar, compiled into 24 volumes); the Tiruvasagam by Manikkavasagar (the most beautiful and profound Tamil devotional poetry); the Nalayira Divya Prabandham (the Four Thousand Hymns of the 12 Alvars, compiled by Nathamuni in the 10th century CE); and the Kalingattuparani (a poem celebrating the Chola victory over Kalinga by Jayamkondar). The Chola period also saw the composition of commentaries on the ancient Sangam works (especially the Tolkappiyam, the earliest Tamil grammar, and the Sangam anthologies), which preserved these ancient texts for posterity.

Kannada Literature: The Rashtrakuta and Western Chalukya periods were the golden age of Kannada literature. The 'Three Gems of Kannada Literature' (Pampa, Ponna, and Ranna) wrote under the patronage of the Rashtrakuta and Chalukya kings. Adikavi Pampa (c. 902-975 CE) is the greatest Kannada poet. He wrote the Adipurana (a Jain epic about the life of the first Jain tirthankara, Rishabha) and the Vikramarjuna Vijaya (a Kannada version of the Mahabharata, with the hero Arjuna identified with his patron, the Chalukya king Arikesari II). Pampa's style is classical, dignified, and majestic. Ponna (c. 950 CE) wrote the Shantipurana (about the 16th Jain tirthankara), and was patronised by Krishna III. Ranna (c. 993 CE) wrote the Gadayuddham (the 'Battle of Clubs', describing the final Mahabharata war between Bhima and Duryodhana, with Bhima identified with the Chalukya king Satyashraya Irivabedanga) and the Ajithanatha Purana (about the second Jain tirthankara). Other important Kannada works include the Kavirajamarga by the Rashtrakuta king Amoghavarsha (the earliest Kannada poetic treatise), the Vaddaradhane (a collection of Jain stories) by Shivakotyacharya, the Jnaneshwari (a commentary on the Bhagavad Gita in Marathi/Kannada influence) and the Vachanas of the Lingayat saints (Basavanna, Allama Prabhu, Akkamahadevi) which are masterpieces of Kannada devotional poetry.

Telugu Literature: Telugu literature began with the Andhra Mahabharatamu, a Telugu translation of the Mahabharata by the poet Nannaya Bhattaraka (c. 1022-1063 CE), who wrote under the patronage of the Eastern Chalukya king Rajaraja Narendra (the half-brother of the Chola emperor Rajendra I). Nannaya's work is the first extended literary work in Telugu and established the literary form of the Telugu language. The work was completed by later poets Tikkana (13th century CE, under Kakatiya patronage) and Yerrapragada (14th century CE), but the three together are known as the 'Trinity of Telugu Literature' (Kavitraya). Other notable Telugu works of this period include the Dasha-skandha by Ketu and the Prataparudriya by Vidyanatha (a Sanskrit work on poetics written in the Kakatiya court).

Malayalam Literature: Malayalam emerged as a distinct language from Tamil during the Chera Perumal period (9th-12th centuries CE). The earliest literary work in Malayalam is the Ramacharitam (c. 12th-13th century CE), attributed to the poet Cheramaan Perumal (or possibly written by a later poet). The work is written in a mixture of Malayalam and Tamil (an early form of Manipravalam, the 'blend of gem and coral' — the literary style that combined Sanskrit and Malayalam). The Keralolpatti (a legendary history of Kerala) and the Kokasandesam (a message poem) are also important early Malayalam works. The Bhakti movement in Kerala produced devotional songs in Malayalam, including the works of the poet Tunchathu Ramanujan Ezhuthachan (though he lived in the 16th century, he is considered the father of modern Malayalam literature). The early period also saw the development of the Kathakali dance-drama and the Sopana Sangeetham (temple music) traditions, which drew on the Bhakti poetry of the region.

  KEY DATES IN SOUTH INDIAN KINGDOMS
  
  PALLAVAS:       275 - 897 CE  (Mahendravarman I 600-630, Mamallapuram c. 640)
  RASHTRAKUTAS:   753 - 982 CE  (Kailasa Temple c. 760, Amoghavarsha 814-878, Takkolam 949)
  WESTERN CHALUKYAS:  973-1189  (Vikramaditya VI 1076-1126, Ittagi Temple 1112)
  CHOLAS:         850 - 1279 CE  (Rajaraja I 985-1014, Rajendra I 1014-44, Srivijaya 1025)
  KAKATIYAS:      1163 - 1323 CE (Rudrama Devi 1262-89, Prataparudra II 1289-1323)
  HOYSALAS:       1026 - 1343 CE (Vishnuvardhana 1108-52, Belur 1117, Halebidu 1120)
  YADAVAS:        1187 - 1317 CE (Singhana 1210-47, Alauddin invades 1296)
  PANDYAS:        590 - 1344 CE  (Jatavarman Sundara I 1251-68, Maravarman Kula 1268-1309)
  
  BHAKTI SAINTS:  Shankara c.788-820, Ramanuja 1017-1137, Basavanna 1105-67, Madhva 1238-1317

11. Delhi Sultanate (1206-1526 CE)

The Delhi Sultanate was the first major Islamic state in India, established after the Ghurid invasions of the late 12th century. It comprised five successive dynasties: the Slave (Mamluk) Dynasty (1206-1290), the Khalji Dynasty (1290-1320), the Tughlaq Dynasty (1320-1414), the Sayyid Dynasty (1414-1451), and the Lodi Dynasty (1451-1526). The Sultanate period is a crucial era of Indo-Islamic synthesis � in administration (the iqta system), architecture (Qutb Minar, Alai Darwaza), culture (Persian as court language, emergence of Urdu, arrival of Sufi saints), and the economy (cash-based revenue, slave soldiers). At its peak under Alauddin Khalji (r. 1296-1316) and Muhammad bin Tughlaq (r. 1324-1351), the Sultanate controlled most of the Indian subcontinent, though the Deccan and South India became independent from the 1340s onwards with the rise of the Bahmani Sultanate and the Vijayanagara Empire.

Ghurid Invasions and the Background

The Delhi Sultanate was made possible by the invasions of Muhammad of Ghor (also called Muhammad Ghori, r. 1173-1206), a Turkic ruler of the Ghurid dynasty in central Afghanistan. The Ghurids originally ruled the region of Ghur (modern-day central Afghanistan, between Herat and Ghazni), a mountainous and backward area that had been overshadowed by the more powerful Ghaznavid Empire. The Ghurid dynasty rose under Ala-ud-din Husain (r. 1149-1161), known as Jahansoz (the World Burner), who sacked Ghazni and ended Ghaznavid power in the region. His nephews Ghiyas-ud-din Muhammad (r. 1163-1203) and Muizz-ud-din Muhammad (the future Muhammad Ghori, r. 1173-1206) partitioned the Ghurid domains � Ghiyas ruled from Firuzkuh in the west, Muizz from Ghazni in the east, giving him a base for Indian expeditions. Unlike Mahmud of Ghazni (who raided and retreated, driven primarily by plunder), Ghori aimed at territorial conquest and empire-building. He invaded India five times between 1175 and 1206.

Ghori's first attempt � the conquest of Multan (1175) � was successful; he was defeated by the Solanki ruler Bhima II at Kasahrada (Mount Abu, 1178); he conquered the Punjab (1186-1190) by annexing the Ghaznavid kingdom of Lahore. His decisive confrontation came with the Rajput confederacy led by Prithviraj III Chauhan of Ajmer and Delhi, the most powerful Rajput king of the time. The First Battle of Tarain (1191) was fought near the town of Taraori in present-day Haryana. Prithviraj's army � comprising Rajput cavalry and infantry from multiple allied kingdoms � met Ghori's Turkic cavalry archers. The Rajputs charged fiercely and broke the Ghurid centre. Ghori was personally captured but was allowed to retreat (some accounts claim Prithviraj spared him out of chivalry; others say Ghori escaped in the confusion). Ghori returned to Ghazni humiliated but determined to avenge his defeat.

The Second Battle of Tarain (1192) was one of the most decisive battles in Indian history. Ghori spent a year preparing: he assembled an army of 120,000 men, including 40,000 mounted archers, organised his troops into five divisions, and drilled them in new tactics. He also sent a false embassy to Prithviraj offering peace, which convinced the Rajputs that Ghori would not attack that year. When Ghori marched on Tarain in 1192, Prithviraj was caught off guard. Ghori's tactics were revolutionary for Indian warfare: he used the mounted archer (the Turkic horse-archer who could shoot while riding, a tactic the Rajputs never mastered), the feigned retreat (the standard Central Asian steppe tactic of pretending to flee, drawing the enemy out of formation, then turning and shooting), and the flanking manoeuvre (detaching a reserve division to attack the Rajput flanks and rear). Prithviraj's army was annihilated; he was captured and later executed. Ghori then conquered Ajmer, Delhi, and much of the Gangetic plain (Badaun, Kannauj, Varanasi) through his generals Qutb-ud-din Aibak and Muhammad bin Bakhtiyar Khalji. Bakhtiyar Khalji sacked the great Buddhist university of Nalanda (1202) and reached Bengal, establishing a pattern of destruction of Buddhist monasteries. Ghori was assassinated in 1206 on the Indus River, probably by a group of Khokhar tribesmen. His Indian territories passed to his slave-general Qutb-ud-din Aibak, who declared independence and founded the Delhi Sultanate.

Slave (Mamluk) Dynasty (1206-1290)

Qutb-ud-din Aibak (1206-1210) � a slave of Muhammad Ghori who was appointed viceroy of the Indian territories after Ghori's death. He founded the Slave (Mamluk) Dynasty and the Delhi Sultanate. Aibak was a capable general but ruled for only 4 years. He constructed the Quwwat-ul-Islam Mosque at the Qutb complex in Delhi (the earliest surviving mosque in India, built with materials from 27 demolished Jain and Hindu temples, demonstrating the reuse of indigenous craftsmanship in Islamic architecture). He began the construction of the Qutb Minar (a 72.5m tall, 5-storey minaret, the tallest brick minaret in the world, symbolising the might of Islam in India, completed by Iltutmish and later Firuz Shah Tughlaq). Aibak was known as Lakh Baksh (giver of lakhs) for his generosity. He died falling from a horse while playing polo (chaugan).

Iltutmish (1211-1236) � the real founder of the Delhi Sultanate as a consolidated state. He was Aibak's son-in-law, also a slave of Turkish origin (purchased by Qutb-ud-din). Iltutmish faced multiple challenges from rival claimants and rebellious governors, but overcame them all. He achieved several lasting accomplishments: he consolidated the Sultanate by suppressing the revolts of the Rajput chiefs (Ranthambore, Jalor, Gwalior) and bringing the entire Gangetic plain under Delhi's control; he neutralised the Mongol threat by refusing to give refuge to Jalaluddin Mangburni, the Khwarazm prince fleeing Genghis Khan (Genghis Khan's armies reached the Indus in 1221 but did not attack Delhi because Iltutmish did not provoke him, wisely adopting a policy of diplomatic caution); he standardised the coinage by introducing the silver tanka (178 grains, the basis of the modern rupee) and the copper jital; he organised the ruling elite into the Chalisa (The Forty � Turkan-i-Chihalgani), a group of 40 Turkish slave commanders who served as his military and administrative cadre, ensuring that power remained concentrated in Turkish hands; he completed the Qutb Minar (plus the Iltutmish Tomb, the first Islamic tomb in India with a squinch arch); and he sought formal recognition from the Abbasid Caliph in Baghdad (1229), legitimising his rule in Islamic terms as a sovereign Sultan. His administrative innovations � the iqta system, the organisation of the nobility into a hierarchy of ranks, the centralisation of revenue collection � became the foundation of the Sultanate's administration.

His daughter Razia Sultana (1236-1240) became the first and only female ruler of the Delhi Sultanate. Iltutmish had nominated her as his successor over his sons, judging her to be more capable than them. She was indeed an able ruler who fought in battle, dressed in male attire, and refused to be addressed as 'Sultana' (she insisted on 'Sultan'). She faced intense opposition from the Turkish nobility (the Chalisa) who could not accept a woman ruling over them. Her reign was a constant struggle: she was deposed once by the nobles, reinstated briefly, and finally deposed again and killed near Kurukshetra. Her fatal political error was appointing Yakut, an Ethiopian (Habshi) slave, as her horse commander (Amir-i-Akhur), which the Turkish nobility saw as a violation of their monopoly over high office. After Razia, the Sultanate went through a period of weak rulers (Bahram Shah, Masud Shah, Nasiruddin Mahmud) who were puppets of the Turkish nobility.

Ghiyasuddin Balban (1266-1287) restored the Sultanate's prestige after two decades of weak rulers. Balban was originally a Turkish slave (of the Ilbari tribe) purchased by Iltutmish, and rose through the ranks as a member of the Chalisa. He served as regent for the weak Sultan Nasiruddin Mahmud (1246-1266) and married his daughter to him, effectively ruling as de facto Sultan before formally ascending the throne. Balban's theory of kingship was harsh and uncompromising: he believed that the Sultan was God's representative on earth (the shadow of God � Zill-i-Ilahi) and must be feared, not loved. He adopted the policy of blood and iron � a combination of ruthless military suppression and iron-fisted centralisation. He crushed the rebellious Rajput chiefs of Mewat, Katehar, and the Doab, re-establishing Delhi's authority over the Gangetic plain. He suppressed the Mongol threat on the north-west frontier by organising an effective defence system: he posted his son Muhammad (the martyr, killed fighting the Mongols in 1286) at Multan and another son Bughra Khan at Samana, built a chain of frontier forts, and adopted a forward-defence policy that kept the Mongols at bay. He also destroyed the power of the Chalisa (The Forty), the group of Turkish nobles who had dominated the sultanate since Iltutmish, by systematically eliminating them through executions, poisonings, and imprisonment, thereby centralising power in his own hands. He introduced elaborate court ceremonial (the Persian sijda � full prostration before the Sultan, and paibos � kissing the Sultan's feet) to enhance his prestige and awe the nobility, though this was considered un-Islamic by the orthodox. He styled himself as Zill-i-Ilahi (Shadow of God on Earth) and Naib-i-Khudai (Deputy of God). He executed 40+ rebels and opponents, including Malik Baqbaq (the governor of Badaun) and several other powerful nobles. He installed a network of spies (munhiyan) to monitor the nobility and the provinces. He built the Balban's Tomb in Delhi (the first true Islamic arch in India, in the Mehrauli archaeological park). His death in 1287 was followed by a succession struggle between his grandson Kaiqubad and the Khalji nobles, leading to the end of the Mamluk dynasty.

Khalji Dynasty (1290-1320)

Jalaluddin Khalji (1290-1296) � founded the Khalji dynasty after a coup against the last Mamluk sultan. The Khaljis were Afghan Turks, not pure Turks, which broke the Turkish exclusivity of the ruling class. This was a social and political revolution � for the first time, non-Turks (Afghans, converts, Indian Muslims) could aspire to high office. Jalaluddin was old, gentle, and merciful (he pardoned rebels, refused to execute his ambitious nephew Alauddin). His nephew and son-in-law Alauddin Khalji (1296-1316) murdered him and became the most powerful ruler of the Delhi Sultanate.

Alauddin Khalji's reign (1296-1316) marked the territorial and administrative zenith of the Delhi Sultanate. He was the first Sultan to conquer the Deccan and South India. He sent his general Malik Kafur (a converted Hindu eunuch slave captured at Khambhat, also called Hazar Dinari for his purchase price of 1000 dinars, and later titled Malik Naib � the Vice-Regent) on two spectacular campaigns: the first (1310) to Devagiri (Yadava), Warangal (Kakatiya), Dwarasamudra (Hoysala), and the Pandya kingdom (Madurai); the second (1311) deeper into the Pandya kingdom, reaching Rameswaram, the southernmost point in India. Malik Kafur plundered the temples and palaces of the Deccan, exacting huge tributes in gold, elephants, and treasures that enriched the Delhi treasury. However, Alauddin did not establish direct administrative control over the Deccan � the conquered kings were left as vassals paying annual tribute (which they stopped paying after his death). Alauddin's empire extended from the Indus to Rameswaram, the largest territory controlled by any Delhi Sultan.

Alauddin's administrative and economic reforms are the most famous in medieval Indian history. His Market Control Policy (the most remarkable economic experiment of the period) fixed the prices of all essential goods: grain (the most important), cloth, sugar, ghee, spices, vegetables, cattle, and even horses, slaves, and weapons. The system was administered by a Shahna-i-Mandi (market superintendent) who reported directly to the Sultan. Prices were maintained through an elaborate infrastructure: state granaries (sarai-i-adl) at Delhi stored grain for distribution during shortages; traders were licensed and registered (the mandi system); a market intelligence network of barids (secret spies) reported on prices and supplies daily; and severe punishments were enforced for hoarding, false weighing, or profiteering � merchants who cheated were executed, flogged, or had their bones broken. The fixed low prices allowed Alauddin to pay his soldiers low salaries (234 tankas per year for a cavalryman, whereas a horse cost 100 tankas and a full set of equipment another 100), enabling him to maintain a large standing army of 475,000 cavalry without burdening the treasury. The system worked as long as Alauddin was alive but collapsed immediately after his death because it depended entirely on his personal enforcement.

The Revenue Reforms established the zabt system (tax assessed per unit area of measured land, not as a share of the crop). All land in the Delhi region was surveyed and measured using a standardised unit. The land tax was fixed at 50% of gross produce (kharaj � also called kharaj-i-jizya because non-Muslims paid it) � the highest rate in Sultanate history. He also introduced the daag (branding of horses with the royal insignia) and chehra (descriptive roll of each soldier, including his appearance, family, and village) systems to prevent corruption in the army � soldiers could no longer bring inferior horses or impersonate dead or absent soldiers. He built the magnificent Alai Darwaza (1311, the southern gate of the Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque complex), the first Indian building to employ true Islamic arches and domes (using voussoirs and keystones rather than the corbelled Indian arch). He also began the construction of the Alai Minar (a planned tower twice the height of the Qutb Minar, abandoned after his death, only the first storey stands as a massive stump in the Qutb complex).

Alauddin faced the most serious Mongol threat of any Delhi Sultan. Between 1299 and 1308, the Chagatai Mongols under Qutlugh Khwaja and Tarmashirin invaded India multiple times. In 1299, Zafar Khan defeated the Mongols near Kili but was killed (Alauddin may have intentionally failed to reinforce him). In 1303, the Mongols besieged Delhi itself for two months � Alauddin camped at Siri, a new fort he built, and the Mongols retreated only when Qutlugh Khwaja died. Alauddin adopted a scorched-earth policy in Punjab, destroyed pastures and wells, and built the fort of Siri as outer defence. The Mongol menace was contained by 1308 when his general Malik Tughlaq crushed an invasion near the Indus.

Tughlaq Dynasty (1320-1414)

Ghiyasuddin Tughlaq (1320-1325) � founded the Tughlaq dynasty after a military coup against the last Khalji ruler, a child sultan named Qutbuddin Mubarak Shah who had been murdered by his favourite, Khusrau Khan (a Hindu convert who briefly seized the throne). Ghiyasuddin was a capable ruler of Turco-Indian origin (his mother was a Jat woman from Punjab). He built the massive Tughlaqabad Fort in Delhi (a vast fortification with sloping stone walls 10-15m thick and 13 gates, designed to be impregnable against Mongol siege tactics). He restored order after the Khalji collapse, recovered the lost territories of the Deccan, and suppressed rebellions in Bengal and other provinces. He died when a wooden pavilion collapsed on him during a victory parade in Kara (probably assassinated by his son Muhammad, who arranged the accident).

Muhammad bin Tughlaq (1325-1351) � the most contradictory and controversial ruler of the Delhi Sultanate. He was extraordinarily intelligent: a master of logic, philosophy, astronomy, mathematics, Persian, Arabic, and Sanskrit literature, a calligrapher, a poet, and a scholar who corresponded with the leading intellectuals of the Islamic world. He patronised scholars from across the Islamic world, including the famous traveller Ibn Battuta (who served as the Chief Qazi of Delhi for 8 years and left a detailed account of his reign). But his policies were disastrous, and his reign is a case study in brilliant ideas implemented without any understanding of practical realities. His five famous failures:

(1) Transfer of Capital to Daulatabad (1327): Muhammad bin Tughlaq decided to move the capital from Delhi to Devagiri in the Deccan (renamed Daulatabad, in present-day Maharashtra, 1100 km southwest of Delhi). His motives were strategic: he wanted to control the rebellious Deccan region more closely, and his empire now extended deep into South India. The entire population of Delhi � men, women, children, the elderly, the sick � was forced to march across India. The journey took 40 days. Thousands died of exhaustion, thirst, disease, snake bites, and animal attacks. After two years, the Sultan realised that Daulatabad was uninhabitable for a large population � it had inadequate water supply, was too hot, and was too far from the northern frontier to defend against the Mongols. He ordered everyone to march back to Delhi. The disastrous transfer destroyed Delhi's economy and social fabric, depopulated the city, and made the Sultan widely hated.

(2) Token Currency (1329): He introduced copper and brass tokens to replace silver tankas, theoretically backed by the royal treasury. The idea was sound (it anticipated modern fiat currency by 500 years), but the execution was fatally flawed. The treasury did not control the minting of tokens, and the tokens were easy to forge � people began minting them in their homes using household copper. The value of the currency collapsed, trade ground to a halt, and the state's revenues were destroyed because taxes were paid in worthless tokens. The Sultan was forced to redeem all tokens for silver at enormous cost, emptying the treasury. He also invited foreign experts to teach him how to mint better coins, but the damage was done.

(3) Taxation in the Doab: He increased the land tax in the fertile Ganga-Yamuna Doab from 10% to 50% during a severe famine. His intention was to raise revenue for his ambitious projects, but the timing was catastrophic. The peasants could not pay, rebelled, and many starved to death. The Sultan's punitive expeditions burned villages, destroyed crops, and further worsened the famine. This was the most cruel and short-sighted of his policies, and it permanently alienated the peasantry of the Doab � the traditional heartland of the Sultanate.

(4) The Khurasan Expedition: He planned a massive campaign to conquer Khurasan (north-eastern Iran), Afghanistan, and Iraq, raising a huge army of 370,000 soldiers and paying them a full year's salary in advance. The plan was grandiose and impractical. The army was assembled, paid, and then the expedition was abandoned for reasons that are unclear (Ibn Battuta says it was because the Sultan's astrologers predicted disaster). The army was disbanded without ever marching, and the entire cost (millions of tankas) was wasted, further draining the treasury.

(5) The Qarachil Expedition: He sent an army of 100,000 soldiers into the Kumaon Hills (the Himalayas) to conquer the Chinese border region (Qarachil may refer to modern-day Uttarakhand or possibly further into Tibet). The army was commanded by Prince Muhammad (a cousin of the Sultan). The expedition was destroyed by the terrain (mountainous, forested, with no roads), disease (malaria, dysentery), and the climate (heavy monsoon rains). Most of the 100,000 soldiers died. The survivors retreated in disorder. This was the final disaster that broke the Sultan's military power and prestige.

After these failures, the Sultanate disintegrated: Bengal declared independence under Fakhruddin Mubarak Shah (1338), Madurai became independent under Jalaluddin Ahsan Shah (1334), the Deccan rebelled under Zafar Khan (founding the Bahmani Sultanate in 1347), and Vijayanagara was founded by Harihara and Bukka (1336). By his death in 1351, Muhammad bin Tughlaq ruled only the Delhi region and parts of the Doab. Ibn Battuta described him as a brilliant scholar generous to a fault but too harsh and unpredictable.

Firuz Shah Tughlaq (1351-1388) � restored stability by reversing most of his predecessor's policies. He reduced taxes, restored the iqta system (assignment of land revenue to nobles in lieu of salaries), patronised the Ulama (Islamic scholars), and persecuted Shia Muslims and non-Muslims (re-imposing strict jizya, demolishing Hindu temples). He was the first Sultan to impose a separate house tax on Brahmins. He built extensively and his public works were the most extensive of any Delhi Sultan: (a) Canals: he built five major canals � the most important being the 150-mile Yamuna-Hisar canal (restored from an ancient pre-Islamic channel), the Sirsa-Hisar canal, and the Yamuna-Firuzabad canal � which irrigated large tracts of land in Punjab and Haryana; (b) Hospitals: he established the Dar-ul-Shafa (free hospitals) in Delhi, the first state-run hospitals in India, with free medicines and food for the poor; (c) New Cities: he founded the city of Jaunpur (named after his cousin Jauna Khan, later a major centre of Islamic learning and the Sharqi kingdom), Fatehabad, Hissar, and Firuzabad (now the Firuz Shah Kotla area of Delhi); (d) he built 40+ mosques, 30+ colleges (madrasas), 100+ caravanserais, 50+ public baths, 200+ wells, and several pleasure gardens. He established a marriage fund (Diwan-i-Khairat) for poor girls who could not afford dowries. He also transliterated ancient Sanskrit texts (Lat � Ashokan pillars) from Topra (Haryana) and Meerut to Delhi, erecting them atop his palace at the Firuz Shah Kotla complex (today the most famous Ashokan pillar stands there). He employed 180,000 slaves according to his autobiography, the Futuhat-i-Firuz Shahi � including artisans, craftsmen, and slaves for domestic service. However, his reign marked the beginning of the Sultanate's terminal decline: he made no effort to recover Bengal or the Deccan, his army became weak and ill-disciplined, and his religious persecution alienated the Hindu population. After his death in 1388, the Tughlaq dynasty collapsed in a succession war among his sons and grandsons, with four sultans in six years (1388-1394).

The Timurid Invasion (1398): The Turco-Mongol emperor Amir Timur (Tamerlane) of Samarkand invaded India in 1398. He had already conquered Persia, Mesopotamia, the Caucasus, Russia, and Anatolia. His stated reason was that the Delhi Sultans were too tolerant of Hindus, but the real motive was plunder. The Tughlaq sultan was the weak Nasiruddin Mahmud (a grandson of Firuz Shah). Timur crossed the Indus in September 1398 and reached Delhi in December. Before the battle, he ordered the massacre of 100,000 Hindu prisoners captured from Punjab towns. The Battle of Delhi (17 December 1398) was a rout � Nasiruddin Mahmud fled. Timur sacked Delhi for three days: houses, temples, mosques, and palaces were burned; the surviving population was enslaved; skilled artisans were deported to Samarkand; and the accumulated treasures � gold, silver, jewels, the Koh-i-Noor diamond, and thousands of elephants � were carted away. Another 50,000-100,000 were executed after the main massacre. Delhi was reduced to a ghost town � "not a bird moved its wings" for months. Timur appointed Khizr Khan as his governor in Punjab and returned to Central Asia. The Delhi Sultanate never recovered: Delhi was depopulated for decades, the economy of the Gangetic plain was ruined, and central authority collapsed.

Sayyid Dynasty (1414-1451)

The Sayyid dynasty consisted of four rulers who claimed descent from the Prophet Muhammad (sayyids). They were essentially Timurid vassals and puppets of the Afghan nobles, with no real authority beyond a 30 km radius of Delhi. Khizr Khan (1414-1421) founded the dynasty by taking control of Delhi after Timur's departure. He acknowledged Timur's grandson Shah Rukh of Herat as his overlord, struck coins in Shah Rukh's name, and sent him annual tribute. The Sayyids were constantly fighting the Sharqi kingdom of Jaunpur (which had declared independence under the Sharqi dynasty, ruling from Jaunpur to Bihar with a glittering court), the Rajput chiefs of Mewar (Rana Kumbha expanded Mewar's territory at the expense of Delhi), and the Afghan rebels in the Doab. Mubarak Shah (1421-1434) was the most capable Sayyid ruler, repelling a Sharqi invasion of Delhi and temporarily restoring some authority. Muhammad Shah (1434-1445) was weak and lost control to the Afghan nobles. The last ruler, Alam Shah (1445-1451), voluntarily abdicated and retired to Badaun, handing over Delhi to Bahlul Lodi. The Sayyid period is important mainly for the further decline of Delhi's central authority � by this time, dozens of independent kingdoms had emerged in all regions: Bengal, Jaunpur, Malwa, Gujarat, Khandesh, Kashmir, the Rajput states (Mewar under Rana Kumbha), the Bahmani Sultanate, and Vijayanagara.

Lodi Dynasty (1451-1526)

The Lodi dynasty was the first Afghan dynasty of the Delhi Sultanate, and the only one to restore some of the Sultanate's former authority. The Lodis were Afghan chiefs from the Lodi tribe, who revived the Afghan tradition of rule by a council of chiefs (oligarchy) rather than absolute monarchy. Bahlul Lodi (1451-1489) was a capable Afghan chief who expanded the kingdom to include Gwalior, Jaunpur (he defeated the Sharqi sultan Husayn Shah after a long war), and Bihar. His policy was to treat the Afghan chiefs as equals � he held court without the elaborate ceremonial of the earlier Turkish sultans, consulted the Afghan nobility on all major decisions, and distributed conquered territories among Afghan chiefs rather than consolidating them under the crown. This Afghan state-building style � decentralised, based on clan loyalty and consultation � was the opposite of the centralised Turkish model of Balban and Alauddin.

Sikandar Lodi (1489-1517) was the greatest Lodi ruler. He founded the city of Agra (1504), shifting the capital from Delhi to Agra to better control the rebellious Doab region and the Rajput states. He also established a new city at Dholpur. He patronised learning: his court poet Mian Bheekh wrote in Persian, and Sikandar commissioned the first Persian work on Indian medicine (Tibb-i-Sikandari). He had Sanskrit works translated into Persian, including the Rajatarangini (the history of Kashmir). He imposed strict Sunni orthodoxy: re-imposing jizya on Hindus, demolishing temples at Mathura and elsewhere, and forbidding Hindus from performing their religious rites publicly (bathing in the Ganga at Mathura was banned). He established a strong administration with a well-regulated revenue system based on Sher Shah's later model (measurement of land, classification by fertility, fixed revenue rates), and the market at Agra became a major centre of trade connecting India with Central Asia. He also built the Baradari (a pleasure pavilion) and several mosques in Agra.

Ibrahim Lodi (1517-1526) was the last Lodi ruler. His arrogance and high-handedness alienated the Afghan nobility � he treated the proud Afghan chiefs as subordinates, punished them publicly, executed some (including the governor of Gwalior), and tried to centralise power in the Turkish-Absolute style that was anathema to the Afghan tradition. The Afghan nobles repeatedly rebelled, and several turned traitor. His uncle, Alam Khan Lodi, and the powerful governor of Punjab, Daulat Khan Lodi, both invited Babur, the Mughal ruler of Kabul, to invade India and overthrow Ibrahim. At the First Battle of Panipat (21 April 1526), Babur's army of 12,000-15,000 men defeated Ibrahim Lodi's 100,000-strong army and 1,000 war elephants. Babur's victory was due to his superior tactics: the tulughma (flanking technique using cavalry archers to attack the enemy's flanks and rear) and his pioneering use of gunpowder cannons and matchlocks (the first large-scale battlefield use of artillery in India, organised by his Ottoman master gunner Ustad Ali Quli). The Lodi army was trapped in a killing zone between Babur's defensive line of carts (araba) chained together and his flanking cavalry. Ibrahim Lodi was killed on the battlefield, his body found among thousands of Afghan dead. The Delhi Sultanate ended, and the Mughal Empire began.

Architecture of the Delhi Sultanate

The Delhi Sultans developed a distinctive Indo-Islamic style, combining Islamic elements (arches, domes, minarets, calligraphy) with Indian materials (red sandstone, marble) and craftsmanship. Key features introduced: the true arch (voussoir arch with keystone, replacing the Indian corbelled arch), the dome, and the minaret. The major monuments are:

(1) The Qutb Complex in Mehrauli, Delhi � the most important group of Sultanate buildings. The Quwwat-ul-Islam Mosque (the first mosque in India, built by Qutb-ud-din Aibak, 1193-1198) was constructed using materials from 27 demolished Jain and Hindu temples � the colonnades are made of reused temple pillars, each carved with Hindu motifs over which plaster was applied to obscure the figures. The mosque originally had a prayer hall on the west and cloisters on three sides. The Qutb Minar (72.5m tall, 5-storey minaret, the tallest brick minaret in the world) was begun by Aibak (only the first storey), completed by Iltutmish (three more storeys), and repaired by Firuz Shah Tughlaq (the fifth storey, after lightning destroyed the top in 1368). The minaret is decorated with bands of Quranic inscriptions in Arabic and intricate geometrical patterns. The Alai Darwaza (built by Alauddin Khalji, 1311) is the southern gate of the mosque and the first Indian building to employ a true Islamic arch with voussoirs and keystones, as well as a true dome. The Iron Pillar of Chandragupta II Vikramaditya (4th century CE, 7.2m tall, 6 tonnes) stands in the courtyard � it was brought to the Qutb complex by Iltutmish as a war trophy and is famous for being rust-free for 1,600 years. Also in the complex are the Iltutmish Tomb (the first Islamic tomb in India, with a squinch arch and a red sandstone cenotaph, but no dome � possibly never completed or collapsed) and the unfinished Alai Minar (Alauddin's planned giant minaret, only the first massive storey stands).

(2) Tughlaqabad Fort (built by Ghiyasuddin Tughlaq, 1321-1325) � a vast fortress in Delhi, designed to be impregnable against Mongol siege warfare. The walls are massive sloping stone structures (10-15m thick at the base), with 13 gates, a moat, underground passages, and a palace complex. The Ghiyasuddin Tughlaq Tomb (built 1325) within the fort is a transitional architectural form � a small square structure with sloping walls and a marble dome, set in a walled enclosure, combining fortress-like severity with decorative marble. The tomb is notable for being the first Delhi Sultanate tomb to fully integrate a dome with Indian building techniques.

(3) Firuz Shah Kotla (built by Firuz Shah Tughlaq, 1354) � the palace-fortress complex of Firuz Shah in Delhi, built on the banks of the Yamuna. The complex includes the Ashoka Pillar (brought from Topra, Haryana, 13m tall, dating to 3rd century BCE, erected on the palace roof), the Jami Masjid (the congregational mosque), a baoli (stepwell), and underground chambers. The architectural style of the Firuz Shah period shows a shift from the grandiosity of the earlier Khalji and Tughlaq buildings to more functional, utilitarian structures with less ornamentation.

(4) Hauz Khas (built by Firuz Shah Tughlaq, 1352) � a large water tank (reservoir) originally 700m by 600m, built to provide water to the city of Delhi. Around it, Firuz Shah built a magnificent madrasa (Islamic college) with two-storey buildings, arcades, and several small tombs. The Hauz Khas complex also includes the Firuz Shah Tomb (a small domed structure) and later Lodi-period additions. The madrasa at Hauz Khas was one of the largest and most prestigious educational institutions in the Islamic world, attracting scholars from across Asia.

(5) Lodi Gardens (built during the Lodi period, 15th-16th centuries) � the finest surviving group of Lodi tombs in Delhi. The Bara Gumbad (Big Dome, 1494) and the Shisha Gumbad (Glazed Dome) are the most prominent. The Lodi style is characterised by the octagonal tomb plan with a verandah, influenced by the earlier Sayyid tombs. The tombs of Muhammad Shah Sayyid (r. 1434-1445, the first octagonal tomb with a verandah in Delhi) and Sikandar Lodi (1517, the largest and finest Lodi tomb, built of grey sandstone with a single dome) are key examples. The Lodi gardens style � with its careful placement of tombs within a landscaped garden (a charbagh-like enclosure) � directly influenced the later Mughal garden tomb tradition that culminated in the Taj Mahal.

Other notable monuments: the Balban's Tomb (the first true arch in India, at Mehrauli), the Jamali Kamali Mosque and Tomb (Lodi period), Khirki Masjid (Tughlaq period), Moth ki Masjid (Lodi period), and Purana Qila (begun by the Lodis, completed by Sher Shah Suri).

Sufi Movements during the Sultanate Period

The Delhi Sultanate period saw the great flowering of the Sufi movement in India. Sufism � Islamic mysticism focused on personal experience of God through love and devotion rather than legalistic observance � resonated with the existing Bhakti tradition of Hinduism. The two main Sufi orders (silsilas) were the Chishti and the Suhrawardi orders.

The Chishti Order was founded in India by Moinuddin Chishti (1141-1236), who settled in Ajmer around 1192, shortly after the Ghurid conquest. His dargah (shrine) at Ajmer became the most important Sufi shrine in India � a centre of pilgrimage for both Muslims and Hindus, visited by rulers from Akbar to Queen Victoria. The great Chishti lineage continued through his disciples: Qutbuddin Bakhtiyar Kaki (d. 1235) of Delhi (who settled in Delhi and attracted the patronage of Iltutmish, though he refused royal gifts), Fariduddin Ganjshakar (1173-1265) of Pakpattan (modern Pakistan, known as Baba Farid, a great poet whose verses are included in the Guru Granth Sahib, the Sikh scripture), Nizamuddin Auliya (1238-1325) of Delhi (the most influential Sufi saint of the Sultanate period, known as Mahbub-i-Ilahi � the Beloved of God, who lived a life of extreme simplicity and poverty, held daily assemblies of rich and poor, Hindu and Muslim alike, and whose dargah at Nizamuddin West in Delhi is one of the most important Sufi shrines in the world), and Nasiruddin Chiragh Dehlavi (1274-1356, the last of the great Chishti saints of Delhi, known as Chiragh-i-Delhi � the Lamp of Delhi).

The Chishti saints lived by strict principles: they lived simple lives, accepted voluntary gifts (futuh) but never accepted service or salary from the state (which distinguished them from the Ulama), held free communal kitchens (langar) where all visitors were fed regardless of caste or religion, sang qawwali (devotional music, which Nizamuddin Auliya particularly encouraged as a means of spiritual ecstasy), and held assemblies (sama) where Hindus and Muslims participated together. The Chishtis believed in sulh-i-kul (peace with all) and actively reached out to lower-caste Hindus, winning converts through their piety and social service rather than through state patronage. They opposed the orthodox Ulama's insistence on Islamic legalism and their emphasis on the superiority of Muslims.

The Suhrawardi Order was founded in India by Bahauddin Zakariya (1182-1262) of Multan. Unlike the Chishtis, the Suhrawardis were more worldly and connected with the state � they accepted state patronage, held high offices (Bahauddin Zakariya was given the title Sheikh-ul-Islam and was a close advisor to Iltutmish), and enjoyed luxurious lifestyles. They were active in Multan, Sindh, and Punjab. The Suhrawardi order also influenced the Deccan through the Bahmani Sultanate.

The great Bhakti-Sufi synthesis produced the devotional poetry of Kabir (1398-1448, a Varanasi weaver who condemned both Hinduism and Islam as empty ritualism), Namdev (1270-1350, whose hymns are included in the Guru Granth Sahib), Ramananda (the first Bhakti saint to use vernacular Hindi), and the Alvars and Nayanars of South India. The Sufi and Bhakti movements created a common cultural space of dargah worship (Hindus offered chadar and prasad at Sufi shrines), shared music (bhajans and qawwalis), and the growth of vernacular languages (Hindavi, Urdu, Braj, Awadhi, Marathi) as media of religious expression.

The court poet Amir Khusrau (1253-1325), a disciple of Nizamuddin Auliya, was one of the greatest cultural figures of the Sultanate. He wrote in Persian and Hindavi, and is credited with: (a) the invention of the sitar (from Persian sehtar) and the tabla (by splitting the mridangam); (b) the development of the qawwali form; (c) the invention of the khayal vocal style; and (d) the use of Urdu/Hindavi as a literary medium. His famous couplet � "If there is a paradise on earth, it is here, it is here, it is here" � is still sung at qawwali performances across South Asia.

  DELHI SULTANATE DYNASTIES

  MAMLUK (1206-1290)
    Qutb-ud-din Aibak → Iltutmish → Razia (1236-40) → Balban (1266-87)
    
  KHALJI (1290-1320)
    Alauddin Khalji (1296-1316) � Market reforms, Deccan conquests
    
  TUGHLAQ (1320-1414)
    Ghiyasuddin → Muhammad bin Tughlaq → Firuz Shah → Timur invades (1398)
    
  SAYYID (1414-1451)
  LODI (1451-1526)
    Bahlul → Sikandar → Ibrahim (killed at 1st Panipat, 1526)

12. Mughal Empire (1526-1707)

The Mughal Empire was the most powerful, wealthy, and culturally refined empire in Indian history � often called India's 'Second Classical Age' after the Guptas. The Mughals were of Turco-Mongol origin (descended from Timur on the father's side and Genghis Khan on the mother's side), but they rapidly became Indianised and cosmopolitan. The empire at its peak (under Akbar, Jahangir, Shah Jahan, and Aurangzeb) covered almost the entire Indian subcontinent � from Kabul in the west to Bengal in the east, and from Kashmir in the north to the Kaveri in the south � with a population of 100-150 million people and a GDP that, according to economic historian Angus Maddison, was 24.4% of world GDP in 1700 (larger than all of Europe combined). The Mughals created a distinctive architectural legacy (Taj Mahal, Red Fort, Agra Fort, Fatehpur Sikri, Humayun's Tomb, the Shalimar Gardens) and a system of administration and culture that shaped India's early modern history. The dynasty produced six great emperors � Babur, Humayun, Akbar, Jahangir, Shah Jahan, and Aurangzeb � whose combined reigns spanned 181 years of continuous rule.

Babur (1526-1530)

Zahir-ud-din Muhammad Babur was born on 14 February 1483 in Ferghana (modern Uzbekistan), a small principality in Central Asia. He was descended from Timur on his father's side and Genghis Khan on his mother's side � a lineage of which he was intensely proud both in his life and in his memoirs. He inherited the throne of Ferghana at age 12, but his reign was immediately challenged by his uncles and the powerful Uzbek confederation led by Shaibani Khan. He lost Ferghana twice and wandered for years as a homeless prince � an experience vividly recorded in his memoirs, which describe his poverty, his loneliness, and his longing for his ancestral lands � before finally capturing Kabul in 1504, establishing a base for himself in Afghanistan. For over two decades he ruled Kabul, dreaming of reclaiming his Central Asian homeland, but eventually turned his ambitions eastward towards India at the invitation of Daulat Khan Lodi (the rebel governor of Punjab) and Rana Sangha of Mewar (who wanted Babur to overthrow Ibrahim Lodi but expected him to leave).

At the First Battle of Panipat (21 April 1526), Babur's army of 12,000 men faced Ibrahim Lodi's massive force of 100,000 soldiers and 1,000 war elephants. Babur's victory was a textbook demonstration of the gunpowder revolution in warfare � he used Ottoman-style cannons (tufangchi � matchlockmen) and field artillery (zarbzan) placed behind a defensive line of carts (araba) chained together, and deployed the tulughma (flanking) tactic of Central Asian warfare. The Lodi army was annihilated and Ibrahim Lodi was killed on the battlefield. Babur occupied Delhi and Agra and proclaimed himself Emperor of Hindustan. The Rajput confederation under Rana Sangha of Mewar challenged Babur at the Battle of Khanwa (17 March 1527) near Fatehpur Sikri. Rana Sangha's army included 120 Rajput chiefs and 80,000 horsemen. Babur used the same defensive cart-and-cannon tactic, and the Rajput charge was shattered by artillery fire. This battle broke Rajput resistance for a generation and established Mughal supremacy in northern India. Babur then defeated the Afghan confederation at the Battle of Ghaghra (1529), securing Bihar and Bengal. He died on 26 December 1530 at age 47 at his garden palace in Agra.

Babur's most remarkable legacy is the Baburnama (Memoirs of Babur), written in Chagatai Turkish in a simple, frank, and elegant style. It is one of the great autobiographies of world literature � he describes his daily life, his love of wine (he was a heavy drinker but also lamented his addiction), the flora and fauna of India (he was astonished by the Indian rhinoceros, the mango, and the coconut palm � noting that the coconut 'is the nut of the Hindus, and every part of it is useful'), the battles and intrigues, the landscapes, and his deep attachment to the homeland of Ferghana. The book was later translated into Persian by his grandson Akbar's courtier Abdur Rahim Khan-i-Khanan. Babur was a passionate lover of gardens � he built the Bagh-e-Babur in Kabul (his chosen burial place, where his remains were transferred after his death) and the Rambagh Garden in Agra (the first Mughal garden in India). His love of gardens established the Mughal garden tradition (the charbagh � four-part garden divided by water channels) that would culminate in the Taj Mahal. Babur's military innovations � artillery, combined arms tactics, and the tulughma maneuver � gave the Mughals a decisive technological edge over Indian armies that still fought in the traditional manner of massed cavalry charges.

Humayun (1530-1540, 1555-1556)

Nasir-ud-din Muhammad Humayun inherited the empire at age 22 but faced an impossible situation.
