He had three ambitious brothers � Kamran (who controlled Kabul and Kandahar), Askari, and Hindal � and the formidable Afghan chief Sher Shah Suri in the east. Humayun was an able scholar, astrologer, and mathematician, but he was indecisive and a poor general. His first act � dividing the empire among his brothers (giving Kabul to Kamran, Sambhal to Askari, and Mewat to Hindal) � was a fatal concession that fragmented Mughal resources. Sher Shah defeated him at the Battle of Chausa (1539) on the Ganges near Buxar � a surprise attack at dawn that routed the Mughal army. Humayun escaped by swimming his horse across the river and was saved by a water-carrier (Nizam, whom he later crowned king for one day). The Battle of Kannauj (also called the Battle of Bilgram, 1540) was the final disaster � Sher Shah defeated Humayun decisively, and the Mughal emperor fled the field. The Sur dynasty began.

Humayun's exile (1540-1555) is one of the most remarkable refugee journeys in history. He wandered through Rajasthan, barely escaping capture by Sher Shah's agents. He was accompanied by his devoted wife Hamida Banu Begum (whom he had married in 1541 after a prolonged courtship � she initially refused him), his loyal servant Jauhar Aftabachi (whose memoirs Tazkirat-ul-Waqiat record the exile), and a small band of followers. Their son Akbar was born in 1542 at Umarkot (Sindh) while they were taking shelter with the local Hindu chief Rana Prasad. Humayun finally reached the court of the Safavid Shah Tahmasp I of Persia in 1544. Tahmasp gave him 14,000 cavalry on condition that Humayun convert to Shia Islam and conquer Kandahar for the Safavids (which Humayun did, taking Kandahar in 1545). The Safavid exile profoundly influenced Mughal culture � Humayun brought Persian painters (Mir Musavvir, Mir Sayyid Ali, Dust Muhammad) to his court, who would later found the Mughal painting tradition under Akbar. He recaptured Delhi in July 1555 after Sher Shah's successors proved weak, but his second reign lasted only six months. In January 1556, Humayun fell down the stairs of his library (Sher Mandal, built by Sher Shah at the Purana Qila, Delhi) while hurrying to the evening prayer, his arms full of books. He died three days later at age 48. His devoted wife Hamida Banu Begum (Akbar's mother) supervised the construction of the magnificent Humayun's Tomb in Delhi (completed 1572, designed by the Persian architect Mirak Mirza Ghiyas, a UNESCO World Heritage Site) � the first great Mughal garden tomb, built of red sandstone with white marble highlights, set in a charbagh garden, and the direct architectural precursor of the Taj Mahal.

Sher Shah Suri (1540-1545)

Although not a Mughal, Sher Shah is included here because his administrative reforms were adopted by Akbar and formed the foundation of Mughal administration. Born Farid Khan in Sasaram (Bihar), he served under the Afghan ruler of Bihar, then rebelled and eventually defeated Humayun to establish the Sur Empire (1540-1555). Despite ruling only 5 years, Sher Shah was arguably the greatest administrative reformer of medieval India. His Land Revenue Reforms introduced a systematic measurement of all cultivable land using a standardised measuring rod (the Sikandari gaz, about 32 inches). Land was classified into three categories based on fertility � good (avval), middling (doyam), and bad (siyam) � and the state's share was fixed at 1/3rd of the produce, payable in cash or kind. He issued detailed revenue rolls (pattas) to peasants and gave them receipts (qabuliyats) after payment, ensuring they could not be arbitrarily taxed. This system became the direct blueprint for Akbar's Todar Mal bandobast. He built the Grand Trunk Road (Sadak-e-Azam) from Sonargaon (Bengal) to Peshawar (Afghan frontier) with 1,700 sarais (rest houses) at every kos (approximately 2 miles), each with a well, a mosque, separate accommodation for Hindus and Muslims, and fruit trees planted along both sides for shade � this network later became the arteries of the Mughal Empire. He issued the silver rupiya (178 grains, 11.5g of pure silver), which became the standard Mughal currency and the ancestor of the modern Indian rupee, alongside gold mohurs and copper dams. He divided the empire into 47 sarkars (districts) with civil (shiqqdar) and military (faujdar) officers, and introduced the muhtasib (superintendent of public morals). He built the Purana Qila (Old Fort) in Delhi and the Sher Mandal within it. He died in 1545 in a gunpowder explosion during the siege of Kalinjar Fort. His magnificent tomb at Sasaram, Bihar, built in the middle of an artificial lake � a 5-storey octagonal structure with a massive dome blending Afghan and Hindu styles � is one of the most beautiful tombs in India.

The Sur Interregnum (1540-1555)

After Sher Shah's death, his son Islam Shah Suri (r. 1545-1554) succeeded him. Islam Shah was an able ruler who continued his father's administrative system, completed the Purana Qila, suppressed rebellions from Afghan nobles and the Mughal prince Kamran, and maintained a stable empire. However, after his death in 1554, the Sur dynasty collapsed rapidly in a civil war among his successors. The Sur empire lasted only 15 years, and its decline created the opening for Humayun's return. The last Sur ruler, Adil Shah Suri, was defeated by Humayun at Sirhind in 1555. The Hindu general Hemu (Hem Chandra, a minister who became the de facto ruler of Delhi for a brief period) captured Delhi in 1556 but was defeated by Akbar's army at the Second Battle of Panipat. The Sur interregnum, though brief, was profoundly important � Sher Shah's administrative innovations became the foundation of the Mughal system, and Humayun's Persian exile shaped Akbar's cultural and religious outlook through the Safavid influence on Mughal art and administration.

Akbar the Great (1556-1605)

Jalal-ud-din Muhammad Akbar is the greatest ruler of the Mughal dynasty � arguably the greatest ruler in Indian history, who transformed a fragile, newly-restored kingdom into the most powerful empire in the world. He ascended the throne at age 13 (after Humayun's death) in Kalanaur (Punjab), with his guardian Bairam Khan as regent. Bairam Khan defeated Hemu at the Second Battle of Panipat (5 November 1556) � Hemu was struck in the eye by an arrow when his army was winning, captured, and executed. Akbar dismissed Bairam Khan in 1560 (after Bairam became increasingly overbearing) but treated him with remarkable magnanimity � Bairam was given the choice of serving anywhere or going on pilgrimage to Mecca. He chose Mecca but was assassinated en route.

Military Conquests: Akbar's reign is divided into three expansion phases. Phase I (1560-1580): Central India and Rajputana � Malwa (1562, defeating Baz Bahadur), Chittor (1568, after a 6-month siege, all 30,000 defenders massacred), Ranthambhor and Kalinjar (1569), defeat of Rana Pratap Singh at Haldighati (1576, though Rana Pratap escaped and continued resistance until 1597). Phase II (1572-1585): Gujarat (1572-1573, Akbar personally marched to Ahmadabad in 9 days, covering 400 km in 3 days � the conquest gave access to India's richest ports of Surat, Cambay, and Ahmedabad); Bengal annexed at the Battle of Rajmahal (1576, Daud Khan Karrani defeated and executed). Phase III (1585-1605): Kashmir (1586, annexed peacefully), Sindh (1591), Kandahar (1595, handed over peacefully by the Persians), and the Deccan campaigns against Ahmadnagar (captured 1600, though Malik Ambar continued resistance). At Akbar's death, the empire covered 4 million sq km � from Kabul to the Godavari, Kashmir to the Narmada � the largest in Indian history up to that time.

Administrative Reforms: Akbar's government is documented in Abul Fazl's Ain-i-Akbari (the third volume of the Akbarnama). The empire was divided into subas (provinces, initially 12, later 15), each governed by a subahdar and a diwan who reported separately to the emperor � a check-and-balance system. Below were sarkars (districts), parganas (sub-districts), and villages. The mansabdari system (1572-73) was the core of Mughal administration � every officer held a mansab with zat (personal rank, 10 to 7,000, determining salary) and sawar (cavalry rank, indicating horsemen maintained). By 1595 there were 2,002 mansabdars, including both Hindus and Muslims. The system allowed the emperor to transfer, demote, or dismiss any officer at will. Officers were paid via jagirs (revenue assignments), which were transferable and non-hereditary. The land revenue system (Todar Mal's bandobast) involved a comprehensive land survey using the Ilahi Gaz (33 inches), classifying land into four categories � polaj (annually cultivated), parauti (fallow 1-2 years), chachar (fallow 3-4 years), and banjar (uncultivable waste) � and fixing the state share at 1/3rd of average produce over 10-19 years (the dahsala system), payable in cash. The system was highly efficient � by 1605, annual revenue was 3.6 crore rupees (£17.5 million). Alternative systems like kankut (crop estimation by visual inspection) and naskh (crop sharing) continued in areas where the zabt system was impractical.

Religious Policy � Din-i-Ilahi and Sulh-i-Kul: Akbar's religious policy is his most distinctive legacy. He was initially orthodox but became increasingly influenced by the Sufi saint Shaikh Mubarak and his sons Abul Fazl and Faizi; the Jain monk Hira Vijaya Suri (who convinced Akbar to ban animal slaughter on certain days); the Portuguese Jesuit missionaries Rodolfo Acquaviva, Antonio Monserrate, and Francisco Henriques (invited to the court in 1580); the Zoroastrian scholar Dastur Meherji Rana (who taught Akbar about sun and fire worship); and Hindu philosophers. In 1575, he built the Ibadat Khana (House of Worship) at Fatehpur Sikri for interfaith debates, initially limited to Sunni scholars but gradually opened to all religions. In 1579, he issued the Mahzar (Infallibility Decree), drafted by Shaikh Mubarak, declaring himself the supreme religious authority in Islamic law. In 1582, he introduced the Din-i-Ilahi (Divine Faith) � a syncretic order combining elements of Islam (monotheism, circumcision), Hinduism (transmigration of souls, sun reverence, vegetarianism), Zoroastrianism (fire worship, the solar Ilahi calendar of 1584), Jainism (non-violence), and Christianity (veneration of the gospel but rejection of the Trinity). The initiation involved offering life, property, honour, and faith to the emperor. The Din-i-Ilahi had only 19 followers (Birbal, Abul Fazl, Faizi, Raja Man Singh, Bhagwan Das) and died with Akbar, but it symbolised his complete independence from Islamic orthodoxy. Far more important was Sulh-i-Kul (Universal Peace) � abolition of jizya (1579), banning forced conversions, permitting Hindu temples, celebrating Hindu festivals at court (Diwali, Holi), and translating Hindu texts into Persian (the Mahabharata as the Razmnama, the Ramayana, the Atharva Veda, the Lilavati, and the Panchatantra). The Rajput policy was the most visible expression � Akbar married Mariam-uz-Zamani (Jodha Bai of Amber, daughter of Raja Bharmal Kachhwaha, mother of Jahangir) and appointed Rajput nobles to the highest positions: Raja Man Singh (governor of Kabul and Bengal), Raja Bhagwan Das (diwan), Todar Mal (revenue minister), and Birbal (wazir and closest confidant). This policy brilliantly secured Rajput loyalty for 150 years.

The Navratnas and Court of Akbar: Akbar's court had nine 'gems': Abul Fazl (grand vizier, author of the Akbarnama, assassinated in 1602 by Prince Salim at the instigation of Bir Singh Bundela); Faizi (poet laureate, completed the Persian translation of the Mahabharata); Mian Tansen (legendary musician, founder of the Gwalior gharana of Hindustani classical music, who died in 1589 � his tomb in Gwalior is visited by musicians); Birbal (Mahesh Das, the court wit and confidant, killed in 1586 during a campaign against the Yusufzais in the Khyber Pass � Akbar was devastated by his death); Raja Todar Mal (revenue minister who reformed the land revenue); Raja Man Singh (the Kachhwaha general and governor of Bengal); Abdur Rahim Khan-i-Khanan (poet and general, son of Bairam Khan, who translated Baburnama into Persian and wrote beautiful Hindi poetry under the pen name 'Rahim'); and two scholar-courtiers. Though Akbar was illiterate (according to Abul Fazl, he 'had not been taught to read or write'), he had books read to him daily and memorised the entire Shahnama and Masnavi � he loved debating philosophy, religion, and history.

Architectural Legacy: Akbar's greatest building is Fatehpur Sikri (1571-1585), the City of Victory, a UNESCO World Heritage Site built in red sandstone blending Hindu and Islamic elements. Key structures: the Buland Darwaza (53m high gateway, the largest in the world, built to commemorate the conquest of Gujarat); the Jama Masjid; the Dargah of Salim Chishti (white marble shrine with exquisite jali screens); the Panch Mahal (5-storey palace, each floor smaller than the one below); the Diwan-i-Khas (Hall of Private Audience with a central pillar supporting a throne platform with four radiating bridges � symbolising the emperor at the centre of the universe); the Ibadat Khana; the Ankh Michauli (Treasury); and the Pachisi Court. The city was abandoned in 1585 due to water shortage. Other buildings: the Agra Fort (1565-1573, red sandstone, later modified by Shah Jahan with marble additions); the Lahore Fort (rebuilt 1575); and Humayun's Tomb (completed 1572, supervised by Akbar and his mother). Akbar's own mausoleum at Sikandra (completed 1613 by Jahangir) is a unique open-air tomb with five tiers of red sandstone and marble, topped by a marble cenotaph exposed to the sky.

Jahangir (1605-1627)

Nur-ud-din Muhammad Jahangir � 'Conqueror of the World.' He ascended the throne in 1605 after crushing a rebellion by his son Khusrau (who was supported by Sikh Guru Arjan Dev � Jahangir executed Guru Arjan Dev in 1606, the first major Mughal-Sikh conflict). Jahangir was addicted to wine and opium (he drank 20 cups of wine daily) but was a keen naturalist and patron of the arts. His reign is famous for his chain of justice (a gold chain with 60 bells stretching from the Shah Burj of the Agra Fort to a pillar on the Yamuna bank � anyone could ring it and directly petition the emperor). He married Nur Jahan (Mihr-un-Nissa) in 1611 � the most powerful woman in Mughal history. She issued coins in her own name (the only Mughal empress to do so), had her name read in the khutba (Friday sermon, the traditional symbol of sovereignty), commanded a regiment of female bodyguards, and effectively ruled the empire for 15 years. Her father Itimad-ud-Daula (Mirza Ghiyas Beg) became wazir, her brother Asaf Khan became vakil, and Asaf Khan's daughter Mumtaz Mahal married Prince Khurram (Shah Jahan). The Irani clique dominated the court. The Mewar Rajputs (Rana Amar Singh) finally submitted in 1615. The British East India Company obtained permission to trade at Surat (1613) through the embassy of Sir Thomas Roe (1615-1619), whose journal provides the best eyewitness account of the Mughal court � he famously described Jahangir as 'the most glorious, most magnificent, most wise, most just, most learned, most religious, most merciful, most charitable.' Prince Khurram (Shah Jahan) rebelled in 1622, fearing Nur Jahan's preference for Shahryar, but was defeated. Jahangir died in 1627 while being carried towards Kashmir, and was buried at Shahdara Bagh, Lahore.

Cultural Contributions: Jahangir's reign was the golden age of Mughal miniature painting. His court artists � Ustad Mansur (the greatest natural history painter � his paintings of the dodo, zebra, and Siberian crane are world-famous), Abul Hasan (portraitist of great psychological depth), Govardhan (master of colour), Bishandas (portrait specialist), and Daulat (calligrapher-artist) � produced works characterised by brilliant colour, precise naturalism, and psychological depth. Jahangir famously could identify any artist's work by examining a single eye. His memoirs, the Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri, describe court ceremonies, the natural world (he classified animals and birds and recorded the mating habits of cranes), rebellions, and his own personality with remarkable candour. The Itimad-ud-Daula's Tomb at Agra (1622-1628, built by Nur Jahan) is a masterpiece of Mughal architecture � the first structure built entirely of white marble and the first to use pietra dura (marble inlay with semi-precious stones such as jasper, jade, carnelian, and lapis lazuli) on a large scale, a technique perfected in the Taj Mahal.

Shah Jahan (1628-1658)

Shahab-ud-din Muhammad Shah Jahan � 'King of the World.' He ascended the throne in 1628 after killing all rivals � his brother Shahryar, his nephews (Khusrau's sons), and the rebel noble Khan Jahan Lodi. This bloody accession set a dangerous precedent of fratricidal succession. His reign is the Golden Age of Mughal architecture, but it was also a period of enormous expenditure on monuments and military campaigns that emptied the treasury accumulated by Akbar.

War of Succession (1657-1658): When Shah Jahan fell ill in 1657, his four sons � Dara Shikoh (the eldest, a brilliant scholar of comparative religion who translated the Upanishads into Persian and wrote Majmu-ul-Bahrain � the Mingling of the Two Oceans, seeking to synthesise Islam and Vedanta); Shah Shuja (governor of Bengal); Murad Bakhsh (governor of Gujarat); and Aurangzeb (governor of the Deccan) � fought the Mughal War of Succession. Dara was a philosopher, not a general; Aurangzeb was a brilliant soldier. Aurangzeb defeated Dara at Samugarh (1658) and Deorai (1659), executed him for heresy (charging him with apostasy), and imprisoned Shah Jahan in Agra Fort for 8 years, where he could see the Taj Mahal across the Yamuna. Shah Jahan died in 1666 and was buried beside Mumtaz Mahal.

Architectural Achievements: The Taj Mahal (1632-1653) at Agra � built for his wife Mumtaz Mahal (Arjumand Banu Begum), who died in 1631 giving birth to their 14th child. Designed by Ustad Ahmad Lahauri, involving 20,000 artisans from India, Persia, Central Asia, and Europe (including the Italian jeweller Geronimo Veroneo, who may have advised on pietra dura). The Taj Mahal is a perfect octagonal building of white Makrana marble with a Persian double dome (44m outer), flanked by 4 minarets tilted slightly outward (so they would fall away from the tomb in an earthquake). The marble is inlaid with 28 types of semi-precious stones � jasper (Punjab), jade (China), lapis lazuli (Afghanistan), turquoise (Tibet), coral (Arabia), carnelian (Baghdad), onyx (Turkey), amethyst (Persia) � in floral arabesque pietra dura. The interior octagonal chamber has an exquisite marble jali screen. The tomb sits in a 42-acre charbagh garden with four water channels representing the four rivers of Paradise. The complex took 22 years and cost 40 lakh rupees. The Red Fort (1639-1648) in Shahjahanabad (Old Delhi) � 2.4 km perimeter, walls 33m high, containing the Diwan-i-Am (Hall of Public Audience with the marble jharokha throne), the Diwan-i-Khas (Hall of Private Audience with the famous inscription by Amir Khusrau � 'Agar Firdaus bar-rue zamin ast, hamin ast-o hamin ast-o hamin ast' � If there is Paradise on earth, it is this, it is this, it is this), the Rang Mahal (harem), the Khas Mahal (private apartments), the Naubat Khana (drum house), and the Hammam (royal baths). The Jama Masjid (Delhi, 1650-1656) � the largest mosque in India, capacity 25,000, built on a 10m-high plinth. The Shalimar Gardens of Lahore (1641-1642) � three-terraced charbagh with 410 marble fountains. The Moti Masjid (Pearl Mosque, Agra Fort, 1648-1654). The Peacock Throne (Takht-e-Taus, built 1632-1639, costing 1 crore rupees) � 12ft x 8ft x 14ft high of solid gold (1,150 kg), studded with 108 rubies (including the Timur Ruby), 116 emeralds (60 carats each), and the Koh-i-Noor diamond (186 carats, then the largest known diamond). The throne was looted by Nadir Shah of Persia in 1739, dismantled, and its jewels sold.

Military Campaigns: Shah Jahan annexed Ahmadnagar (1633), forced Bijapur and Golconda to accept Mughal suzerainty, and campaigned in Balkh and Badakhshan (1646-1647), a costly failure. Kandahar was lost to the Safavids in 1649. The Mughal army peaked at 200,000 cavalry, 4,000 war elephants, and a massive artillery train. The court ceremonial became increasingly ornate and rigid, distancing the emperor from ordinary subjects � in contrast to Akbar's accessibility. The expenditure on architecture and war emptied the treasury, and the Mughal economy, though still the world's largest manufacturer (25% of global industrial output), consumed its surplus on the court rather than productive investment.

Aurangzeb (1658-1707)

Muhi-ud-din Muhammad Aurangzeb (Alamgir � 'World-Seizer') ruled for 49 years, the longest Mughal reign. His reign was the territorial peak � 4.5 million sq km stretching from Kashmir to Madurai and Kabul to Chittagong � but also the beginning of terminal decline. A devout Sunni Muslim, he reversed Akbar's Sulh-i-Kul and attempted to enforce sharia throughout the empire. In 1669, he issued a general order for the destruction of Hindu temples and schools. Major temples destroyed include the Kashi Vishwanath Temple at Varanasi (1669, the Gyanvapi Mosque built on the site), the Keshav Dev Temple at Mathura (1670, built by Raja Man Singh � the Jami Masjid built on the site), and the Somnath Temple (again, after its rebuilding following Mahmud of Ghazni's destruction). In 1679, he reimposed jizya (abolished by Akbar in 1579) � this raised about 40 lakh rupees annually but caused immense resentment. Hindu mansabdars fell from 24% (1658) to 6% (1679), though they rose to 13% in his later reign when he needed Maratha and Rajput allies. He executed Guru Tegh Bahadur in 1675 at Delhi � the execution led to the militarisation of the Sikh community under Guru Gobind Singh, who founded the Khalsa in 1699 and fought the Battle of Chamkaur (1704). He banned Hindu festivals at court, restricted temple construction, and prohibited Hindus from riding palanquins or thoroughbred horses. Revisionist historians (M. Athar Ali, S. Moosvi) argue temple destruction was primarily political (punishing rebellion by destroying the chief's symbol of authority) and jizya was reimposed due to fiscal crisis, but the cumulative effect of Aurangzeb's policies was to destroy the Mughal-Rajput alliance that had been the bedrock of Akbar's success.

The Deccan Wars: Aurangzeb annexed the Shia sultanate of Bijapur (1686, after an 18-month siege) and Golconda (1687, through the treachery of an Afghan officer, Abdullah Pani, who left a gate open). The diamond mines of Golconda (which produced the Koh-i-Noor, the Hope Diamond, and the Orlov) brought wealth but extended Mughal commitments deep into the Deccan. The Maratha War was the longest and most destructive conflict. After Shivaji's death (1680), Aurangzeb captured his son Sambhaji in 1689 at Sangameshwar and executed him after 40 days of torture (eyes gouged out, tongue cut off, limbs amputated, then beheaded). But Sambhaji's brother Rajaram (1689-1700) escaped to Gingee (Tamil Nadu) and continued the war. After Rajaram's death, his widow Tarabai led a relentless guerrilla campaign that exhausted the Mughal army. Aurangzeb moved his court to the Deccan in 1682 and never returned to the north, spending his last 26 years fighting an unwinnable war. The cost was staggering � 250,000 men, 10 crore rupees annually (two-thirds of total revenue), and the complete exhaustion of Akbar's accumulated reserves. The Rajput war (1679-1681) was another costly failure � Aurangzeb's own son Prince Akbar rebelled and allied with the Rajputs before fleeing to Persia. By Aurangzeb's death in 1707, the empire was bankrupt, the army was exhausted, and the Marathas were poised to take over northern India.

He died on 3 March 1707 at Ahmednagar at age 88, having outlived his sons, queens, and generals. His tomb at Khuldabad (near Aurangabad) is extraordinarily modest � a simple open-air grave of earth, level with the ground, without marble or ornament, in accordance with his will. He earned his living in his last years by copying the Quran and selling 29 handwritten copies. The contrast between this humble grave and the magnificent mausoleums of his ancestors (Humayun's Tomb, Akbar's Tomb, the Taj Mahal) is the most powerful symbol of the collapse of Mughal power.

The Later Mughals (1707-1857)

Aurangzeb's death in 1707 triggered a war of succession among his three surviving sons. Bahadur Shah I (r. 1707-1712, aged 63) abolished jizya, restored tolerance, and tried to conciliate Rajputs and Marathas, but his reign was too short to reverse decline. After him, 10 emperors reigned in 36 years (1712-1748), most as puppets of factional nobles. Jahandar Shah (1712-1713) was a puppet of Zulfiqar Khan. Farrukhsiyar (1713-1719) was placed on the throne by the Sayyid Brothers (Abdullah Khan and Husain Ali Khan, the 'Kingmakers') and later murdered by them. Muhammad Shah 'Rangila' (1719-1748) was a pleasure-loving emperor who patronised art and music but presided over disaster � the invasion of Nadir Shah of Persia (1739), who sacked Delhi, massacred 30,000 inhabitants, and looted the Peacock Throne, the Koh-i-Noor diamond, and treasure worth 700 million rupees. This invasion was the death blow to Mughal prestige. Ahmad Shah Abdali (Durrani), Nadir Shah's Afghan general, invaded repeatedly (1748-1767), annexing Punjab and Kashmir and defeating the Marathas at the Third Battle of Panipat (1761). Shah Alam II (r. 1759-1806) was a British pensioner � the Battle of Buxar (1764) and the Treaty of Allahabad (1765) gave the British diwani rights over Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa. The last emperor, Bahadur Shah Zafar II (r. 1837-1857), was the symbolic leader of the Revolt of 1857 and was exiled to Rangoon, where he died in 1862, ending 331 years of Mughal rule. His death marks the final extinction of the dynasty that had ruled India for over three centuries.

Mughal Administration in Depth

The Mansabdari System: The core of Mughal administration. Every imperial officer held a mansab with two numerical components: zat (personal rank determining salary, status, and precedence � from 10 to 7,000 for princes) and sawar (cavalry rank indicating the number of horsemen to maintain). The system required periodic dagh (branding of horses) and chehra (descriptive rolls of horsemen) to prevent fraud. A zat of 1,000 paid 17,000 rupees/month; zat of 5,000 paid 100,000 rupees/month. Since cash was often short, officers were paid via jagirs (revenue assignments of specific territories), which were transferable and non-hereditary. The system was universal � both Hindus and Muslims held mansabs � and created a unified service elite. However, as mansabdars proliferated from 2,002 under Akbar to 8,000 under Aurangzeb, the demand for jagirs exceeded supply, creating the jagirdari crisis � officers received jagirs producing far less revenue than their rank entitled them to, leading to corruption, peasant exploitation, and military breakdown. Provincial Administration: The empire had 15-21 subas (provinces), each with a subahdar (governor) and diwan (revenue chief) reporting separately to the emperor. Below were sarkars (districts), parganas (sub-districts with shiqdar, munsif, qanungo, potdar, qazi, and kotwal), and villages (with muqaddam and patwari). Coinage: The Mughal monetary system was stable and sophisticated � the gold mohur (169 grains, 16-20 rupees), the silver rupiya (178 grains, the standard, 96% pure), and the copper dam (323 grains, 40 dams = 1 rupee). The mints were open to all, and the coins' exceptional purity made them internationally accepted. There were 40 mints under Akbar, expanding to over 100 by Aurangzeb's reign.

Mughal Economy

The Mughal economy was the largest in the world, producing about 25% of global manufacturing output. Agriculture employed 85% of the population. New world crops were introduced � maize, tobacco, potato, chilli, and groundnut alongside the traditional cash crop indigo ('blue gold'), which was exported to Europe in enormous quantities. Textiles were the largest industry � the finest cotton textiles in the world: muslins from Bengal (so fine that a 20-yard piece could be packed into a matchbox, according to contemporary accounts), chintz from the Coromandel Coast, calico from Calicut, and shawls from Kashmir. Bengal's textile industry alone was larger than all of Europe's combined in the 17th century. Other major exports: spices (pepper, cardamom, ginger), sugar, opium (for Southeast Asia and medicinal use in Europe), saltpetre (for gunpowder), and precious stones (diamonds from Golconda's Kollur mines � the source of the Koh-i-Noor, Hope Diamond, Orlov, and Regent Diamond; pearls from the Gulf of Mannar). The Indian Ocean trade was dominated by sophisticated Indian merchant communities � the Gujarati Banias, the Parsis of Surat, the Chettis of Tamil Nadu, and the Marwaris of Rajasthan. The European presence expanded: the Portuguese (Goa since 1510, controlling the pepper trade), the English East India Company (factories at Surat 1613, Madras 1639, Bombay 1661, Calcutta 1691), the Dutch VOC (Surat, Chinsurah, Pulicat), and the French (Pondicherry 1674). Until the early 18th century, Europeans were minor players � they paid in silver and gold (which the Mughals needed for their coinage) and were tolerated as sources of customs revenue (2.5% on imports and exports). The agrarian economy was monetised (taxes paid in cash, not kind), which encouraged trade, market growth, and the development of sophisticated banking and credit instruments (the hundi � a bill of exchange used by Indian merchants).

Mughal Culture

Mughal Painting: The Mughal school of miniature painting originated with Persian artists (Mir Sayyid Ali, Abdus Samad) brought by Humayun from the Safavid court. Under Akbar (1556-1605), the imperial studio (kitabkhana) produced the magnificent Hamzanama (1,400 large-format paintings, 1557-1572) � the greatest illustrated manuscript of the Mughal era. The studio employed over 100 painters, both Persian masters and Hindu Indian artists, who created a distinctive Mughal synthesis: the Persian love of fine line and controlled composition combined with the Indian traditions of vibrant colour and the depiction of movement. Under Jahangir (1605-1627), painting reached its peak � Ustad Mansur (natural history master � his dodo, zebra, and Siberian crane paintings are world-famous), Abul Hasan (portraitist), Govardhan (colourist), and Daulat produced works of unsurpassed naturalism and psychological depth. Under Shah Jahan (1628-1658), painting became more formal � the Padshahnama (the official court chronicle illustrated with formal durbar scenes) and the Shah Jahan Album feature delicate floral borders and precise architectural settings. Under Aurangzeb (1658-1707), imperial patronage sharply declined (he disapproved of image-making as un-Islamic), and artists migrated to the provincial courts of Rajasthan (founding the Rajput schools of painting), the Deccan, and to the Sikh hill states (influencing the later Kangra and Pahari schools). Mughal Literature: Persian was the official court and administrative language. Major works include Abul Fazl's Akbarnama and Ain-i-Akbari (the most detailed administrative manual of any pre-modern Indian empire, describing every aspect of Akbar's empire � provinces, revenues, military, law, fauna, flora, Hindu philosophy, and social customs � with encyclopedic precision); Badauni's Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh (a hostile critique of Akbar's religious policies written secretly by an orthodox Sunni scholar); Faizi's Nal-Daman (Persian retelling of Nala-Damayanti); Jahangir's Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri; and the Padshahnama of Abdul Hamid Lahori. Hindu texts were systematically translated: the Mahabharata as the Razmnama (supervised by Abul Fazl and Naqib Khan, 1582-1586), the Ramayana, the Atharva Veda, the Lilavati (mathematics text), the Panchatantra, and the Upanishads (translated by Dara Shikoh as the Sirr-e-Akbar � the Great Secret). Hindi literature flourished under Mughal rule � Tulsidas composed the Ramcharitmanas (1574) during Akbar's reign; the Bhakti poets Surdas and Mirabai and the Sant poet Kabir all flourished. Music: Mian Tansen (c. 1500-1589) was the most famous musician in Indian history � he was Akbar's court musician, credited with transforming the dhrupad style, inventing Raga Darbari Kanada, and founding the Gwalior gharana. His tomb in Gwalior is a place of pilgrimage for musicians. Jahangir and Shah Jahan continued musical patronage � Shah Jahan's court musicians included Jagannath and the dhrupad singer Lal Khan. Aurangzeb banned court music as a puritan, but his sister Roshanara Begum and son Prince Muazzam patronised it privately. The Mughal period saw the full development of the dhrupad and khyal styles. Mughal Architecture: The key architectural innovations of the Mughals include the Persian double dome (an outer and inner dome with a void between, first used in Humayun's Tomb), the charbagh garden (the four-part paradise garden divided by water channels), pietra dura marble inlay (first used in Itimad-ud-Daula's Tomb, perfected in the Taj Mahal), jali (latticed marble screens), chhatris (pillared pavilions), and iwans (towering arched portals). The early Mughals (Babur, Humayun, Akbar) used red sandstone extensively; the later Mughals (from Jahangir onward) increasingly used white marble. Key monuments by emperor: Babur � mosques at Panipat and Sambhal, Ram Bagh (Agra), Bagh-e-Babur (Kabul); Humayun/Sher Shah � Purana Qila, Sher Mandal, Humayun's Tomb, Sasaram Tomb; Akbar � Fatehpur Sikri (Buland Darwaza, Jama Masjid, Salim Chishti's Dargah, Panch Mahal, Diwan-i-Khas), Agra Fort, Lahore Fort, Allahabad Fort, Tomb at Sikandra; Jahangir � Itimad-ud-Daula's Tomb (Agra), Jahangir's Tomb (Lahore); Shah Jahan � Taj Mahal (Agra), Red Fort (Delhi), Jama Masjid (Delhi), Shalimar Gardens (Lahore), Moti Masjid (Agra Fort), Peacock Throne; Aurangzeb � Badshahi Mosque (Lahore, 1673, capacity 60,000, second largest mosque of the Mughal era), Bibi ka Maqbara (Aurangabad, a poor copy of the Taj Mahal built for his wife), Moti Masjid (Red Fort, Delhi).

Historiographical Debate: Aurangzeb's 'Religious Bigotry'
Traditional historiography (Jadunath Sarkar's History of Aurangzeb, 1912-1924) portrays Aurangzeb as a bigoted Sunni Muslim whose persecution of Hindus, Sikhs, and Shias alienated non-Muslim subjects and caused the empire's decline. Revisionist historians (Satish Chandra, M. Athar Ali, Iqtidar Alam Khan, S. Moosvi) argue: 1) Aurangzeb appointed more Hindus (especially Marathas and Rajputs) in his later reign than before � Hindu mansabdars rose from 6% in 1679 to 13% in 1702. 2) Temple destruction was not a sweeping policy � many temples were destroyed only after rebellions (political motivation was primary; the temple was the symbol of royal authority, and destroying it was the traditional way of asserting conquest). 3) Jizya was reimposed not purely out of religious zeal but because the treasury was exhausted by Shah Jahan's extravagance and the Deccan wars � it raised about 40 lakh rupees annually. 4) Hindus held 33% of mansabdars in his last decade, and he even patronised Hindu temples when politically useful. The truth is complex: Aurangzeb was a devout Sunni who enforced sharia more strictly than any predecessor, but he was also a practical ruler who employed Hindus when needed. His lasting damage was political and strategic � he exhausted the treasury on the Deccan wars, destroyed the Mughal alliance with the Rajputs (the key to Akbar's success), overextended the empire, and left it too weak to resist the British, Marathas, and Afghans. The 'Aurangzeb debate' remains one of the most contested topics in Indian historiography, with implications for understanding the relationship between religion and state in pre-modern India.

Causes of Decline

The Mughal Empire declined rapidly after Aurangzeb's death in 1707 due to multiple interconnected causes. (1) The Jagirdari Crisis � as the number of mansabdars increased far beyond the available supply of fertile jagirs, officers were forced to take poor-quality land and extract maximum revenue from peasants, creating agrarian distress, corruption, and the breakdown of the military system. (2) The Deccan Wars � 26 years of continuous warfare (1682-1707) drained the treasury of an estimated 20-30 crore rupees (the empire's annual revenue was about 15 crore rupees), exhausted the army, and caused enormous loss of life. The Mughal army never recovered its mobility or morale after this war. (3) The Rise of the Marathas � after Aurangzeb's death, the Marathas under the Peshwas expanded rapidly, conquering Malwa, Gujarat, and Bundelkhand by the 1720s and extracting chauth from Mughal provinces. By 1750, the Marathas were the de facto rulers of northern India. (4) The Invasion of Nadir Shah (1739) � the Persian invasion was catastrophic: it demonstrated Mughal military inferiority, looted the imperial treasury (the Peacock Throne, the Koh-i-Noor, and 700 million rupees), and destroyed the political and psychological prestige of the empire. (5) Rajput and Sikh Rebellions � the Rajputs of Mewar and Marwar broke away and ceased to provide military service; the Sikhs under the Khalsa became a powerful military force in Punjab. (6) The British Conquest � the Battle of Plassey (1757) and the Battle of Buxar (1764) gave the British control of Bengal, the richest Mughal province. The Treaty of Allahabad (1765) gave the British the diwani of Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa, and they used Bengal's wealth to finance the conquest of the rest of India. (7) Structural Weaknesses � the empire was over-centralised (only a strong emperor could control the nobles), the succession system was based on fratricidal warfare (every emperor's death triggered a destructive civil war), the mansabdari system could not cope with the over-extended territory, and the empire had no institutional mechanism for continuity beyond the emperor's personal authority. The Mughal Empire did not fall but disintegrated � piece by piece, province by province, the regional governors (the Nizam of Hyderabad, the Nawab of Bengal, the Nawab of Awadh, the Rajput kingdoms, the Sikh confederacy, the Jat rulers of Bharatpur) declared independence. By 1765, the Mughal emperor was a British pensioner ruling only over the Red Fort, and by 1858, the last emperor was exiled to Rangoon. The greatest dynasty of Indian history had come to an end.

13. Vijayanagara & Bahmani Kingdoms (1336-1614 CE)

The Vijayanagara Empire (City of Victory) and the Bahmani Sultanate dominated the Deccan for over 200 years, locked in a continuous struggle for supremacy over the Raichur Doab � the fertile region between the Krishna and Tungabhadra rivers. The Vijayanagara Empire was the last great Hindu kingdom of medieval India, a wealthy, cosmopolitan empire that controlled the maritime trade of the southern Indian Ocean. Its capital, Vijayanagara (modern Hampi, Karnataka), was one of the largest and most magnificent cities of the medieval world, with a population estimated at 500,000 � the second-largest city in the world after Beijing at its peak. The Bahmani Sultanate was the first independent Islamic kingdom in the Deccan, established after a rebellion against Muhammad bin Tughlaq. The rivalry between Vijayanagara and Bahmani, and the eventual fragmentation of Bahmani into 5 successor sultanates (Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar, Bidar, Berar), shaped the history of the Deccan until the Mughal conquest.

Vijayanagara Empire (1336-1614)

Origins: Theories and Historical Context

The origins of the Vijayanagara Empire have been the subject of scholarly debate. The traditional account, recorded in the Sanskrit Vidyaranya Kalajnana and the Telugu Vijayanagara Rajyacharitramu, states that the brothers Harihara I and Bukka Raya I were captured by Muhammad bin Tughlaq in 1323 during his Kakatiya campaign, forcibly converted to Islam, and later sent back to the Deccan as governors. They reconverted to Hinduism under the influence of the sage Vidyaranya (Madhavacharya, the 12th Jagadguru of the Sringeri Sharada Peetham), and established an independent Hindu kingdom on the southern banks of the Tungabhadra River in 1336. Vidyaranya � a towering figure who wrote the Shankara Vijaya and commentaries on the Vedas � is believed to have been the spiritual force behind the empire's founding, and his brother Sayana authored classical commentaries on the Vedas (the Sayana Bhashya).

The eminent historian P. B. Desai challenged this narrative. Desai proposed that the Sangama brothers were Hoysala officers in the service of the Hoysala king Veera Ballala III. According to Desai, Harihara and Bukka were posted as commanders in the Kampili region, and after the Hoysala kingdom fell to the Delhi Sultanate, they carved out an independent kingdom from the ruins. This theory is supported by epigraphic evidence showing early Vijayanagara rulers using Hoysala titles and administrative structures.

The British civil servant and historian Robert Sewell, in his classic work A Forgotten Empire: Vijayanagar (1900), brought the empire to the modern world by translating the Portuguese chronicles of Domingo Paes and Fernao Nuniz. Sewell's work, based on these first-hand accounts and local traditions, portrayed Vijayanagara as a glorious Hindu kingdom tragically destroyed by the Deccan sultanates. Though Sewell's chronology has been revised by later scholarship, his narrative of the empire's rise and fall remains the foundational text for Vijayanagara studies.

After Kampili was crushed by Muhammad bin Tughlaq's forces (c. 1327), the brothers were taken to Delhi, converted to Islam, and later sent back as governors of the Deccan frontier of the Delhi Sultanate. Their task was to administer the newly-conquered territories and suppress local resistance. However, the revolt of the Deccan amirs � led by Alauddin Bahman Shah (Hasan Gangu) in 1347 � created a power vacuum. The Sangama brothers broke away, reconverted to Hinduism under Vidyaranya's influence, and established an independent kingdom on the southern banks of the Tungabhadra River in 1336. They expanded rapidly by conquering the remnants of the Hoysala, Kampili, and Kakatiya kingdoms, and by resisting the new Bahmani Sultanate's invasions.

Key Theory: P.B. Desai vs Traditional Account
The traditional account traces Vijayanagara's founding to the blessing of Vidyaranya and the reconversion of the Sangama brothers from Islam. P.B. Desai argued they were Hoysala officers, not Kakatiya ministers. Robert Sewell's 'A Forgotten Empire' (1900) popularised the empire using Portuguese chronicles. For UPSC, all three perspectives are important � the Vidyaranya legend, the Hoysala connection, and the Kampili rebel origin.

Sources: Sewell (1900), A Forgotten Empire; P.B. Desai (1960), Studies in Vijayanagara History.

Sangama Dynasty (1336-1485)

Harihara I (1336-1356) � the founder � ruled for 20 years, consolidating the kingdom from the Tungabhadra to the Kaveri. He and his brother Bukka Raya I (1356-1377) expanded into Tamil country, bringing the Tamil chieftains � the Sambuvarayars and Kadavarayars � under Vijayanagara suzerainty. Bukka I conquered the Reddi kingdoms of Kondavidu and the Kakatiya remnants in Telangana, patronised the Vaishnava philosopher Vedanta Desika, and repaired the Varadharaja Perumal Temple at Kanchipuram.

Vira Harihara II (Buka Raya II, 1377-1404) � the greatest Sangama ruler before Deva Raya II � extended the empire to the Krishna River and the Malabar Coast. He captured the port of Goa (c. 1378) from the Bahmanis, conquered the Kaveri delta and Kongu country, and patronised the Jain poet Madhava (Dharmanatham Puranam). His struggles with Bahmani rulers Muhammad Shah I and Mujahid Shah over the Raichur Doab were inconclusive.

Deva Raya II (1422-1446) � the most outstanding Sangama king, titled 'Gajabetekara' (Hunter of Elephants). He fought the Gajapati wars against the Gajapati kingdom of Odisha (Prataparudra Deva), capturing the Godavari delta forts of Kondavidu and Rajahmundry and pushing the frontier northward. His Kalyana campaign against the Bahmani fortress of Kalyana captured the Raichur Doab temporarily. His southern campaigns reached Kanyakumari (Cape Comorin) and Pamban Island (Rameswaram), bringing the entire southern peninsula under Vijayanagara authority.

Deva Raya II introduced a remarkable military innovation: he recruited Muslim cavalry (Mamluk archers) into his personal army and built a mosque within the capital. His court was renowned for its literary brilliance: the Telugu poet Srinatha (Kavi Sarvabhauma), the Sanskrit scholar Lolla Lakshmidhara (Kavya Mala), and the Persian noble Adil Khan who served as a military commander. Deva Raya II built the Hazarama Temple, commissioned the Kadalaleu irrigation works, refurbished the Virupaksha Temple, and supported the Kannada poet Chamarasa (Prabhulinga Leela). The Italian traveller Nicolo de Conti (c. 1420) noted the city's circumference of 60 miles.

Saluva and Tuluva Dynasties (1485-1570)

The Saluva dynasty was founded by Saluva Narasimha Deva Raya (1485-1491), a general who usurped the throne to prevent fragmentation. The Saluvas ruled for only 20 years. The Tuluva dynasty was founded by Tuluva Narasa Nayaka (1491-1503). His son Vira Narasimha Raya (1503-1509) formally established the dynasty. The most brilliant period of the empire was under his younger brother Krishnadeva Raya (1509-1529).

Vijayanagara Dynasty Timeline

Sangama (1336-1485) → Saluva (1485-1505) → Tuluva (1505-1570) → Aravidu (1570-1614)

Key Rulers:
Harihara I → Bukka I → Harihara II → Deva Raya II → Saluva Narasimha → Krishnadeva Raya → Achyuta Raya → Sadasiva Raya → Tirumala

Krishnadeva Raya (1509-1529): The Golden Age

Krishnadeva Raya � the greatest Vijayanagara emperor, often described as the 'most powerful Hindu ruler of India' since the 12th century. He came to the throne at age 24 and immediately launched military campaigns that made him the undisputed master of southern India.

Military Campaigns: His first major war was the 6-year war with Bijapur for the Raichur Doab. The Battle of Raichur (1520) was the decisive engagement � according to Domingo Paes, Krishnadeva Raya's army comprised 736,000 infantry, 32,600 cavalry, and 550 elephants (numbers certainly exaggerated). He defeated Ismail Adil Shah and captured Raichur. His Kalinga / Gajapati campaign (1512-1516) against Prataparudra Gajapati of Odisha captured the fortresses of Kondavidu (1515), Kondapalli, and Rajamahendri (Rajahmundry) � the Rajamahendri march was a brilliant operation involving siege warfare and forced river crossings. He married a Gajapati princess as a diplomatic gesture, and the Gajapati kingdom became a tributary ally. He defeated the rebellious Ummattur chiefs of Mysore, subdued the Portuguese at Goa (allowing them to trade at Bhatkal and Honavar in exchange for the horse trade from Hormuz), and received tribute from the Pandya and Chera kingdoms.

The Ashtadiggajas � Telugu Literary Renaissance: His court was adorned by eight celebrated Telugu poets � the Ashtadiggajas: Allasani Peddana (Manucharitramu), Nandi Thimmana (Parijatapaharanamu), Madayyagari Mallana (Rajasekharacharitramu), Dhurjati (Kalahasti Mahatyamu), Ayyalaraju Ramabhadrudu (Ramabhyudayamu), Pingali Surana (Raghavapandaviyamu), Ramaraja Bhushanudu (Vasucharitramu), and the legendary Tenali Ramakrishna (Panduranga Mahatyamu), the court jester immortalised in Telugu folklore.

Krishnadeva Raya himself was a distinguished poet. He wrote the Amuktamalyada ('The Giver of the Worn Garland') in Telugu � a 600-verse poem narrating the story of the Tamil Alvar saint Andal (Godadevi) who offered a garland to Lord Venkateswara at Tirupati before wearing it herself. The work is notable for its devotional fervour, poetic excellence, and administrative advice � the most famous section is the Suraksha Niti chapter, where he advises rulers on statecraft, diplomacy, and revenue administration. The opening lines declare: "A king should always rule with an eye towards dharma. A kingdom where the subjects live in fear is not a kingdom". He also wrote the Jambavati Kalyanam in Sanskrit.

Construction Works: At Hampi, he constructed the Vithala Temple (stone chariot, 56-pillared musical mandapa, monolithic gopuram), the Hazarama Temple (1000 sculpted Ramayana panels), the Lotus Mahal (Indo-Islamic pavilion), the Elephant Stables (11 domed chambers), the Queen's Bath (stepped bath with arched corridors), the Krishna Temple (commemorating his Gajapati victory), and the 9-storey eastern gopuram of the Virupaksha Temple (50m high, the tallest structure at Hampi). He also built the Hampi Bazaar � a 1km-long market street that once bustled with trade from Arabia, Persia, and Portugal.

Krishnadeva Raya: The Amuktamalyada
The 'Amuktamalyada' is celebrated for the Suraksha Niti chapter, which prescribes taxation policy: "A king should levy taxes like a bee collecting honey � just enough to sustain the hive, not so much that the flower withers." This principle of moderate, dharma-based taxation is frequently cited in UPSC essays on ancient Indian governance.

Battle of Talikota (Rakkasa-Tangadi) 1565

The Battle of Talikota (also called the Battle of Rakkasa-Tangadi, 23 January 1565) was the decisive battle that destroyed the Vijayanagara Empire.

Causes and Alliance of the Deccan Sultanates: By the 1540s, the empire under the regency of Aliya Rama Raya (Krishnadeva Raya's son-in-law and de facto ruler under the puppet emperor Sadasiva Raya) had become increasingly powerful. Rama Raya pursued a policy of playing the Deccan sultanates against each other � he allied with one against another, then switched sides, alienating all five. His arrogance became intolerable: he ordered the public humiliation of a Bijapur noble and killed a Bijapur ambassador. The five Deccan sultanates � Bijapur (Ali Adil Shah I), Golconda (Ibrahim Qutb Shah), Ahmadnagar (Hussain Nizam Shah I), Bidar (Ali Barid Shah I), and Berar (Burhan Imad Shah) � set aside their mutual rivalries and formed a united alliance, cemented by the marriage of Hussain Nizam Shah's son to Ali Adil Shah's daughter.

The Battle: The united Deccan army, numbering about 80,000 infantry, 30,000 cavalry, and powerful artillery, crossed the Krishna River. The Vijayanagara army � estimated by Portuguese accounts at 600,000 infantry, 45,000 cavalry, and 500 elephants � met them near the villages of Rakkasa and Tangadi. The battle lasted over five months (August 1564 to January 1565), with the actual decisive engagement on 23 January 1565. The Deccan artillery � trained by Turkish and Persian gunners � was superior. The Golconda cavalry feigned flight, drawing the Vijayanagara troops into a disordered pursuit. When the Vijayanagara lines broke formation, the Bijapur artillery and Ahmadnagar cavalry closed in. Rama Raya, fighting from a palanquin (too old to ride a horse), was captured and beheaded by Hussain Nizam Shah I. His head was mounted on a spear and displayed to the Vijayanagara forces, who immediately broke and fled. An estimated 100,000 soldiers were killed.

The Sack of Vijayanagara: For six months (January to July 1565), the Deccan armies systematically demolished the city � pulling down palaces, smashing temple sculptures, looting gold and jewels, burning the bazaars and great libraries of Sanskrit, Telugu, and Kannada manuscripts. The Mahanavami Dibba was defaced. The city that Domingo Paes had described as 'the best-provided city in the world' was reduced to ruins. The Portuguese chronicler Fernao Nuniz wrote: "They razed the city to the ground... nothing remained but the ruins of its greatness." The capital was moved to Penukonda, and Hampi was abandoned to the jungle, lying forgotten until its rediscovery by British antiquarians in the 19th century.

Strategic Mistakes of Rama Raya
1) He alienated Brahmin Muslim allies who served as mediators with the Deccan sultanates. 2) His arrogance � public humiliation and killing of Bijapuri nobles � united the sultanates against him. 3) His divide-and-rule policy worked only when sultanates were weak; once united, their combined force was overwhelming. 4) He failed to maintain adequate cavalry and artillery. 5) He fought from a palanquin rather than on horseback � his capture and beheading instantly demoralised his army.
Battle of Talikota (1565): Tactical Analysis

Phase 1 (Aug-Dec 1564): Deccan sultanates unite (Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar, Bidar, Berar). Armies cross Krishna River. Vijayanagara forces take defensive positions south of the river.
Phase 2 (Jan 1565): Initial Vijayanagara artillery barrage forces Deccan advance to stall. Golconda cavalry feigns retreat, drawing Vijayanagara troops into disordered pursuit.
Phase 3 (23 Jan 1565): Vijayanagara lines break formation. Bijapur artillery and Ahmadnagar cavalry encircle the exposed flank. Rama Raya is captured and beheaded.
Aftermath: Vijayanagara army collapses. 100,000 estimated casualties. Six-month sack of the city begins.

Key factor: The absence of a unified command structure in the Vijayanagara army, contrasted with the coordinated Deccan alliance under Hussain Nizam Shah's leadership.

Decline and the Aravidu Dynasty (1570-1614)

The Aravidu dynasty was founded by Tirumala Raya (Rama Raya's brother), who fled to Penukonda after Talikota and crowned himself emperor in 1570. The Aravidu kings ruled a much-reduced kingdom, constantly fighting the Deccan sultanates and rebellious Nayakas. Venkata II (1586-1614) � the greatest Aravidu king � temporarily revived the empire, recovered territories from the Nayakas (Kanchipuram, Tirupati), moved the capital to Chandragiri, and built the Venkateswara Temple. Under Ramadeva (1617-1632) and Venkata III (1632-1642), the empire shrank further. The Madurai Nayakas under Tirumala Nayaka and the Keladi Nayakas of Ikkeri became independent. The Mysore Wodeyar kingdom under Raja Wodeyar declared independence by 1610. The final extinction came under Sriranga III (1642-1646), who was defeated by Golconda and Bijapur armies, and the remaining territories were divided between the Deccan sultanates and the Nayaka kingdoms.

The Nayaka successor states � Madurai, Tanjore, Gingee, Keladi, and Mysore � continued Vijayanagara's administrative traditions, built magnificent temple complexes (Meenakshi Temple at Madurai, Brihadisvara temple at Tanjore), and patronised Carnatic music and Bharatnatyam dance. The legacy of Vijayanagara lives on in temple architecture, Telugu and Kannada literary traditions, Carnatic music, and the Nayaka administrative structure adopted by the British in the Madras Presidency.

UPSC Tip: Remembering the Four Dynasties
Mnemonic: Sangama → Saluva → Tuluva → Aravidu � "Some Sultans Try Arrogance". The Tuluva dynasty produced the greatest ruler (Krishnadeva Raya) and ended at Talikota (1565). The Aravidu dynasty presided over the decline and fragmentation.

Administration and the Nayaka System

Central Administration

The empire was divided into mandalams (provinces), nadus (districts), and gramas (villages). The king was the supreme executive, military, and judicial authority, aided by a council of ministers (mantri mandala) including the mahapradhana (prime minister), dandanayaka (commander-in-chief), srikarana (treasury in-charge), and sahadeva (judge advocate). The king's secretariat included the rayasam (scribes and record-keepers) who maintained detailed revenue and administrative records on palm-leaf manuscripts.

Provincial Administration: The Nayankara System

Land Revenue: Krishnadeva Raya conducted a comprehensive land classification survey: bhatta (irrigated wet land, taxed at 1/3 to 1/2 of produce), punja (dry land, 1/4), and bagayat (garden land, 1/5). The sist (king's share) was generally 1/6th of gross produce. Additional taxes included rajabhoga (court tax), rathantaka (professions tax), sulka (customs and tolls), and kattubadi (garden tax). The empire had market towns (pettas) and major ports � Bhatkal, Honavar, Mangalore, Goa, Kannur, Calicut, Nagapatam.

Land TypeDescriptionTax Rate
BhattaIrrigated wet land (rice, sugarcane)1/3 to 1/2 of produce
PunjaDry land (ragi, jowar, millets)1/4 of produce
BagayatGarden land (coconut, areca, pepper)1/5 of produce

Economy, Trade, and Commerce

Agriculture was the mainstay, with extensive tank irrigation. The empire built thousands of tanks (kere in Kannada) � the Kodiyar Dam, Mannargudi tank, Bengaluru Kere tanks (Kempambudhi Kere, Dharmambudhi Kere in present-day Bengaluru). Main crops: rice, sugarcane, cotton, millets, pulses, coconuts, betel leaf, areca nut, and spices (pepper, ginger, cardamom).

Trade with Portugal: The Portuguese arrival in 1498 transformed external trade. They operated through the cartaz system � a pass/licence for Indian ships. The empire imported horses from Hormuz (Persia) through Goa and Dabhol � 10,000-15,000 horses annually. Domingo Paes described the Hampi markets: "In the Royal Mahal, there are halls with ivory thrones, gold-decorated walls... The streets are full of merchants selling precious stones, pearls, silks, brocades, horses, elephants, and every kind of merchandise."

The textile industry was globally famous � calico (from Calicut), painted chintz, printed calico of the Coromandel Coast were exported to the Middle East, Africa, and Europe. Silk was produced in Kanchipuram. The spice trade (pepper, cardamom, ginger, cinnamon) was the most valuable commodity. The diamond mines of Kollur supplied the world's finest diamonds. The empire also produced Bidriware (metalwork from Bidar, inlaid with silver and brass) and Hanamakonda bronze casting. Exports: spices, textiles, rice, iron, sugar, indigo, camphor, sandalwood, precious stones. Imports: horses, copper, silk, velvet, porcelain, damask, dates, perfumes, cannon. The gold pagoda (Varaha, 3.4g) was the standard coin, alongside silver tara and copper jital coins.

Domingo Paes on Hampi Markets (c. 1520)
"The streets are full of merchants of every kind. The bazaar street is very beautiful � wide, with rows of houses on each side, and in it a great number of merchants who trade in silk, satin, velvet, and fine cloth. There is a saying among the Portuguese: 'Hampi has everything � if you cannot find it in Hampi, it does not exist in the world.'"

Society and Culture

Vijayanagara society was hierarchical: Brahmins at the top, followed by the Nayaka warrior class, traders and artisans, then peasants and labourers. Women held a complex position � royal women like Reyana (Telugu poetess) and Kempamahadevi (temple patron) wielded influence. The devadasi system (temple dancers) flourished, with women trained in classical dance and music, though the system later degenerated. Goddess cults � worship of Bhagavati-Durga, Pattini, and Yellamma � were prominent. Sati was practised among upper castes, though not encouraged by the state.

Notable WomenRoleSignificance
ReyanaTelugu poetessComposed devotional poetry; one of the few known female poets of the Vijayanagara period
KempamahadeviQueen of Bukka IPatronised temple construction; commissioned the Kempambudhi tank in Bengaluru
Nagala DeviQueen of Krishnadeva RayaTown of Nagalapura named after her; patronised the Krishna Temple at Hampi
Chand BibiRegent of AhmadnagarDefended Ahmadnagar against Akbar (1595-1600); symbol of Deccan resistance
Tukka DeviGajapati princessMarried Krishnadeva Raya as part of the Kalinga peace settlement

The empire patronised literature in four languages: Sanskrit (Vidyaranya's Shankara Vijaya, Sayana's Veda commentaries), Telugu (the Ashtadiggajas), Kannada (Chamarasa's Prabhulinga Leela, Kumaravyasa's Kannada Bharata), and Tamil (Vaishnava scholars of the Tenkalai tradition). Persian influence at court was notable � Persian merchants and soldiers served in the administration, and Indo-Islamic architectural elements (arches, domes, plasterwork) appear in the Lotus Mahal and Elephant Stables.

Hampi Architecture: UNESCO World Heritage Site

The city of Vijayanagara (now a UNESCO World Heritage Site as the Group of Monuments at Hampi) was one of the wonders of the medieval world. The architecture is a distinctive Vijayanagara style: massive gopurams, use of granite, intricate pillared mandapas, and Ramayana/Mahabharata scenes carved onto temple pillars.

Vithala Temple Complex

The most magnificent temple at Hampi, the Vithala Temple is dedicated to Vithala (a form of Vishnu/Krishna). Its 56-pillared mandapa (Ranga Mandapa) is a masterpiece � each pillar is carved from a single granite block, and when struck, they produce musical notes (hollow resonators carved into the granite produce the sounds of different ragas). The Stone Chariot � a monolithic shrine carved to resemble a chariot with rotating stone wheels � is the iconic symbol of Hampi, dedicated to Garuda (Vishnu's eagle mount). The temple also features a grand kalyana mandapa (marriage hall), a devastanam (inner sanctum), and a ruined gopuram.

Hazarama Rama Temple

The Hazarama Temple (Temple of a Thousand Ramas) was the royal private shrine. Its walls are covered with over 1000 sculpted panels depicting the entire Ramayana (from Rama's birth to his coronation) and scenes from the Mahabharata � the most complete sculptural narration of the Ramayana anywhere in India. The temple also shows the king with his queens, court scenes, and processions.

Virupaksha Temple

The main temple of the city, dedicated to Virupaksha (Shiva), the family deity of the Vijayanagara kings. Its 9-storey eastern gopuram (50m high, built by Krishnadeva Raya) is the tallest structure at Hampi. The temple complex has pillared halls, a sacred tank, and a shrine to the goddess Pampa (from whom 'Hampi' is derived). The temple is still in active worship � the oldest continuously functioning temple in India (over 1300 years).

Royal Centre Structures

Lotus Mahal � a two-storeyed Indo-Islamic palace pavilion with a pyramidal roof, arched corridors, and a central dome resembling a lotus bud, possibly the queen's leisure palace. The Elephant Stables � 11 adjoining domed chambers for royal elephants, with the central chamber being the largest and most ornate. The Mahanavami Dibba � a 22m-high tiered granite platform from which the king viewed Dasara festival displays with horses, elephants, dancers, and military parades. The Kings' Balance (Tulabhara) � where kings were weighed against gold, jewels, or grain for distribution to the poor. The Pushkarni � a circular stepped tank, 7m deep, built of green schist with beautifully carved tiers. The Queen's Bath � an ornate stepped bath with a central pool (15m x 4m) surrounded by arched corridors and balconies.

Engineering Marvels

The aqueduct system brought water from the Tungabhadra River through granite channels to the royal baths and stepped tanks, using natural gradients and underground conduits. The hanging bridge across the Tungabhadra gorge connected the city to the southern bank using sophisticated cantilevered construction. The underwater dam (submerged weir) raised the water level to feed the aqueduct system. The Underground Temple (Prasanna Virupaksha) remains partially submerged for most of the year due to groundwater level.

Hampi: Sacred & Royal Centres

Sacred Centre (along Tungabhadra River): Virupaksha Temple → Hampi Bazaar → Vithala Temple → Stone Chariot → Hazara Rama Temple

Royal Centre (south-east): Mahanavami Dibba → Lotus Mahal → Elephant Stables → Queen's Bath → Pushkarni → Kings' Balance → Underground Temple

Bahmani Sultanate (1347-1527)

Foundation and the Four Capitals

The Bahmani Sultanate was the first independent Islamic kingdom in the Deccan, founded by Alauddin Bahman Shah (Hasan Gangu / Zafar Khan), a general who rebelled against Muhammad bin Tughlaq and established an independent state at Gulbarga in 1347. According to legend, Hasan Gangu was a servant of a Brahmin astrologer named Gangu, hence the title 'Bahman Shah' (Brahmin-king). The sultanate lasted 180 years with 18 sultans ruling from four capitals: Gulbarga (1347-1424), Bidar (1424-1527), Daulatabad (secondary capital), and Firozabad (a new city built by Ahmad Shah Wali, now in ruins).

The Deccan plateau frontiers shaped Bahmani history. The sultanate stretched from the Godavari to the Tungabhadra, and from the Arabian Sea to the Bay of Bengal (at its peak under Mahmud Gawan). The kingdom was constantly at war with Vijayanagara over the Raichur Doab � the first war (1347-1367), the capture of Raichur (1367), the second war (1398-1420), and battles of 1420-1440. The sultanate was a centre of the Persianate tradition, with Persian as the official language.

Mahmud Gawan (1463-1482): The Persian Merchant Prime Minister

Mahmud Gawan (originally Khwaja Imaduddin Mahmud, a Persian merchant from Gilan, Iran) was the greatest minister of the Bahmani Sultanate. He entered the service of Sultan Muhammad Shah III Lashkari and rose to become Vakil-us-Sultanat (Prime Minister).

Reforms: He centralised the revenue system (uniform measurement and assessment, standardised land revenue rates, abolished illegal cesses), established a postal system, and divided the sultanate into four provinces (Gulbarga, Bidar, Daulatabad, Berar). His campaigns expanded the sultanate to its greatest extent � conquering Belgaum (1473), the Konkan coast (ports of Dabhol and Goa from Vijayanagara), and forcing the Vijayanagara king to pay tribute. He also campaigned against the Chera and Pandya kingdoms, the Gajapatis of Odisha, and Khandesh.

Cultural Legacy: His greatest achievement was the Madrasa of Mahmud Gawan at Bidar (built 1472) � a three-storeyed Persianate madrasa with a high entrance ivan, blue glazed tiles from Iran, a central courtyard, mosque, lecture halls, and a library of 3,000 manuscripts � the finest example of Islamic architecture in the Deccan. Gawan was also a scholar and poet in Arabic and Persian; his letters (Riyaz-ul-Insha) are a valuable historical source.

Execution: The Deccani faction forged a letter from Gawan to the Vijayanagara king proposing treachery. The aged Sultan Muhammad Shah III, in a fit of drunkenness, signed the execution order. Gawan was executed on 6 April 1481 at Firozabad � he recited the Quran as the sword fell. The sultan, realising his mistake, died within a year of remorse. Gawan's execution was the turning point � after his death, the sultanate fragmented into the five Deccan sultanates (1490-1527).

Deccani vs Foreigner Faction Conflict

The Bahmani Sultanate was characterised by a bitter conflict between the Deccanis (old Indian Muslims, mostly converted Hindus, speaking Dakhani Urdu or Marathi) and the Foreigners (Pardesis from Iran, Turkey, Arabia, and Central Asia, speaking Persian). The Deccanis resented the Foreigners' dominance at court; the Foreigners considered the Deccanis culturally inferior. The conflict turned violent in the 1470s and reached its peak with the execution of Mahmud Gawan (a Foreigner) in 1481. After Gawan's death, the Deccani faction purged the Foreigners, but the resulting instability destroyed the unity of the sultanate. The provincial governors � the Deccani nobles who ruled Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar, Bidar, and Berar � declared independence between 1490 and 1527. The Bahmani Sultanate was reduced to a 5 km radius around Bidar by the 1520s. The last sultan was deposed in 1527.

Deccan Culture Under the Bahmanis

The Bahmani period saw the flowering of a distinctive Deccan culture. Persian was the official language, but Dakhani Urdu emerged as a popular literary language � the earliest Deccani Urdu work, Kadam Rao Padam Rao, was written in 1435 under Bahmani patronage. The sultans patronised Persian poets and historians: Isami wrote the Futuh-us-Salatin, a history of the Delhi Sultanate and the early Bahmani period, in 1350. The Sufi saints � especially the Chishti and Qadiri orders � flourished in the Deccan under Bahmani patronage, and their khanqahs (hospices) became centres of syncretic culture. The Deccani style of architecture emerged during this period � a blend of Persian, Turkish, and local Deccani elements, featuring coloured glazed tiles, high entrance ivans, and intricate plasterwork, best seen in the Madrasa of Mahmud Gawan and the Bahmani tombs at Ashtur.

The Five Deccan Sultanates (1490-1687)

SultanateFounded ByCapitalKey Features
Bijapur (Adil Shahi)Yusuf Adil Khan (1490)BijapurGreatest ruler: Ibrahim Adil Shah II (1580-1627) � syncretic ruler who worshipped both Hindu and Islamic deities, patron of art and music, wrote Kitab-i-Nauras (treatise on music and ragas). Famous Gol Gumbaz (tomb of Muhammad Adil Shah � second largest dome in the world, 44m diameter, 51m high, with famous 'Whispering Gallery'), Ibrahim Rauza (inspiration for Taj Mahal), Malik-e-Maidan cannon (4m-long, 55-tonne bronze cannon, one of the largest in the world). Ibrahim II was called Jagat Guru by his Hindu subjects for his religious tolerance. Annexed by Aurangzeb 1686.
Golconda (Qutb Shahi)Quli Qutb Shah (1518)Golconda (Hyderabad)Greatest ruler: Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah (1580-1612) � founded Hyderabad (1591), built the Charminar (iconic four-minaret gateway, 56m high, with a mosque on the top floor), Mecca Masjid, Golconda Fort (famous acoustic system � handclap at Fateh Darwaza heard 1 km away at Balahisar palace). Golconda was the diamond capital of the world � Koh-i-Noor, Hope Diamond, Orlov, Darya-i-Noor, Regent Diamond from the Kollur mines. Centre of Persian poetry and Dakhani Urdu literature. Annexed by Aurangzeb 1687.
Ahmadnagar (Nizam Shahi)Malik Ahmad Nizam Shah I (1490)AhmadnagarGreatest ruler: Burhan Nizam Shah I (1508-1553). Famous for Chand Bibi (regent 1595-1600) who bravely defended against Akbar's Mughal invasions, and Malik Ambar (1548-1626) � Ethiopian slave who became effective ruler, reformed revenue, introduced guerrilla warfare against Mughals, founded Aurangabad. Annexed by Shah Jahan 1633.
Bidar (Barid Shahi)Amir Qasim Barid (1492)BidarSmallest sultanate. Known for Madrasa of Mahmud Gawan, Bidar Fort (water management system), Bahmani tombs at Ashtur, and Bidriware (zinc-copper metalwork inlaid with silver). Annexed by Bijapur 1619.
Berar (Imad Shahi)Fathullah Imad-ul-Mulk (1490)Achalpur / GawilgarhWeakest sultanate. Gawilgarh Fort (hill fort in Satpura range) was its main stronghold. Annexed by Ahmadnagar 1574.

The five Deccan sultanates, despite mutual rivalries, united when facing a common enemy � most famously against Vijayanagara at Talikota (1565). They developed a distinctive Deccan style of architecture, literature, and culture blending Persian, Turkish, Indian, and local Deccani elements. They patronised Dakhani Urdu literature, were centres of Persian poetry (the Shahnama and Khamsa of Nizami were illustrated in the Deccan courts), and developed the Deccan School of Painting (a fusion of Persian miniature painting with Hindu and Deccani elements). The sultanates were also famous for Bidriware metalwork, Golconda's painted chintz and cotton textiles, and Ibrahim Adil Shah II's contributions to music.

UPSC Tip: Five Deccan Sultanates Mnemonic
Remember them as B.A.G.B.B. � Bijapur (Adil Shahi), Ahmadnagar (Nizam Shahi), Golconda (Qutb Shahi), Bidar (Barid Shahi), Berar (Imad Shahi). Their alliance at Talikota (1565) destroyed Vijayanagara. They were later all annexed by the Mughals (1633-1687).

Significance: The Deccan Legacy

Vijayanagara and the Bahmani Sultanate represent two contrasting civilisations � Hindu and Islamic � that coexisted, competed, and influenced each other for over 200 years. The Vijayanagara legacy: Hampi architecture (UNESCO World Heritage Site), classical Telugu and Kannada literature, Carnatic music, the Nayaka administrative system adopted by the British, and Nayaka-period temple architecture (Meenakshi Temple at Madurai, Ranganathaswamy Temple at Srirangam, Brihadisvara Temple at Tanjore). The Bahmani legacy: Deccan Islamic architecture (Gol Gumbaz, Charminar, Madrasa of Mahmud Gawan, Ibrahim Rauza), Dakhani Urdu literature (the earliest Deccani Urdu works were written under the Bahmanis � Kadam Rao Padam Rao, 1435), the Deccan School of Painting (a distinctive blend of Persian, Mughal, and Hindu styles seen in the Nujum-ul-Ulum manuscript and the Bijapur and Golconda schools), the Persianate court culture of the Deccan, and the political framework for later Deccan kingdoms (Hyderabad under the Asaf Jahs, Mysore under Hyder Ali and Tipu Sultan).

The Battle of Talikota (1565) and the subsequent sack of Vijayanagara marked one of the great turning points of Indian history � the end of the last major Hindu kingdom and the opening of the Deccan to Mughal conquest, completed by Aurangzeb's annexation of Bijapur (1686) and Golconda (1687). The Deccan sultanates, despite falling to the Mughals, left an enduring cultural legacy in architecture, language, music, and painting that shaped the identity of the Deccan for centuries.

UPSC Theme: 'City of Victory' � Rise and Fall of Vijayanagara
A classic UPSC theme: rise through military expansion and maritime commerce, golden age under Krishnadeva Raya (cultural efflorescence, temple architecture, Telugu literary renaissance), fatal arrogance of Rama Raya, united Deccan sultanates, catastrophic defeat at Talikota, sack of the city, fragmentation into Nayaka kingdoms, final extinction by 1646. Key lessons: diplomacy over arrogance, interdependence of military power and economic prosperity, and cultural legacy that outlives political collapse.

14. Maratha Empire & Sikh Confederacy (1674-1818)

The Maratha Empire was the last major indigenous Indian empire before the British conquest. It emerged from guerrilla resistance to Mughal expansion in the Deccan under Shivaji Bhonsle (1630-1680) � who founded the Maratha kingdom, established a navy, and challenged Mughal and Bijapur authority. After his death, the Maratha state expanded under the Peshwas (Brahmin prime ministers of the Bhat family who became hereditary rulers) into a confederacy that controlled most of India by the mid-18th century. The defeat at the Third Battle of Panipat (1761) against Ahmad Shah Abdali broke their northward expansion, but they recovered under Madhavrao I and remained dominant until the British conquered them in the Anglo-Maratha Wars (1775-1818). In Punjab, the Sikh religion � founded by Guru Nanak (1469-1539) and militarised under Guru Gobind Singh (the Khalsa, 1699) � evolved into a confederacy of twelve Misls (1716-1801), unified by Maharaja Ranjit Singh (1780-1839) into a powerful Sikh Empire. The Maratha navy under the Angre family challenged the British, Portuguese, and Siddis for control of the Konkan coast.

Shivaji (1630-1680)

Shivaji Bhonsle was born at Shivneri Fort (near Pune) to Shahaji Bhonsle (a Maratha general in Bijapur and Ahmadnagar service) and Jijabai. He was trained by his mother and guardian Dadaji Kondadev. From age 16, he captured forts in the Maval region (Torna, 1645 � his first conquest). His conflict with Bijapur began in 1659 when the Adil Shah sent Afzal Khan to capture him. At Pratapgad Fort, Shivaji killed Afzal Khan with tiger claws (wagh-nakh) concealed in his hands, and the Marathas routed the Bijapuri force � this established him as the foremost Maratha leader. He fought the Mughals from 1660; Aurangzeb sent Jai Singh to defeat him. Shivaji signed the Treaty of Purandar (1665), surrendering 23 of 35 forts. He visited Aurangzeb's court at Agra (1666), was insulted and placed under house arrest, and famously escaped hidden in a fruit/sweets basket. He returned to the Deccan and launched a reconquest (1670-1674), recovering all his forts.

Coronation at Raigad (1674)

Shivaji was crowned Chhatrapati at Raigad Fort in 1674 in a grand ceremony lasting several days, conducted by the learned Brahmin Gaga Bhatta of Varanasi, who traced Shivaji's ancestry to the Sisodia Rajputs (solar line of Mewar) to establish kshatriya status � a necessary precondition for a Vedic coronation. The ceremony included the sacred thread ceremony (upanayana), abhisheka with water from seven sacred rivers (Ganga, Yamuna, Godavari, Krishna, Narmada, Sindhu, Kaveri), and Vedic chanting. Shivaji assumed the title Chhatrapati (Lord of the Umbrella, symbolising universal sovereignty), and established the Rajyabhisheka Shaka (Maratha Era). He also took the titles Haindava Dharmodharak (Protector of the Hindu Faith) and Kshatriya Kulavatamsa (Jewel of the Kshatriya Clan). The total expenditure exceeded 5 million rupees. The coronation transformed him from a rebel chieftain into a legitimate sovereign in the eyes of Indian and European powers.

Administration: The Ashta Pradhan

Shivaji established a Council of Eight Ministers (Ashta Pradhan), each with clearly defined duties, though ultimate authority rested with the Chhatrapati: (1) Peshwa (Prime Minister) � general administration; (2) Amatya/Majumdar (Finance Minister) � revenue, accounts, and auditing; (3) Waqenavis (Intelligence Minister) � espionage, intelligence, and foreign correspondence; (4) Sachiv (Secretary) � royal correspondence and court functioning; (5) Sumant (Foreign Minister) � diplomatic relations with foreign powers; (6) Dabir (Ceremonies Minister) � protocol and internal security; (7) Nyayadhish (Chief Justice) � judicial administration and Hindu law; (8) Panditrao (Ecclesiastical Minister) � religious endowments and charity. Unlike the Mughal mansabdari system, ministers were paid in cash salaries, not land grants (jagirs), ensuring loyalty and reducing feudal fragmentation. The system combined indigenous Maratha traditions with elements from the Bahmani and Vijayanagara models.

Military Organisation

Shivaji's military system was built on ganimi kava (guerrilla warfare). His core infantry � the Maval infantry � were lightly armed, highly mobile hillmen from the Western Ghats around Pune, expert in hill fighting, rapid marches (50-60 km per day), and sudden night attacks. Each carried a sword (talwar), shield (dhal), and spear (bhalta). His cavalry had two types: Shiledar (horsemen owning their own horses and equipment) and Bargir (state-provided horses and equipment), both trained for hit-and-run attacks, foraging, and cutting enemy supply lines � devastating against the slow-moving Mughal army. He controlled over 300 forts along the Sahyadri range, each garrisoned with water and ammunition, serving as bases for operations and refuges. Soldiers were paid in cash, not land grants, ensuring professionalism and loyalty. Strict discipline was enforced � plunder of civilians and violation of women were severely punished, and officers were expected to inspect their troops personally.

Naval Policy and the Angre Family

Shivaji built a navy of about 350-400 ships (gurabs and galivats) based at Suvarndurg, Vijaydurg, and Kolaba to protect the Konkan coast from the Siddis of Janjira, the Portuguese of Goa, and the British and Dutch. After his death, Kanhoji Angre (1669-1729) was appointed Maratha Admiral (Daria Sarang) in 1698 and built a powerful fleet that challenged European naval supremacy in the Arabian Sea for decades. The Angre family controlled the Konkan coast from Mumbai to Vijaydurg, attacking European ships and demanding convoy passes.
