The British and Portuguese made several unsuccessful attempts to destroy his fleet � notably the Battle of Suvarndurg (1724). The Angre dynasty ended in 1756 when the Peshwa captured Vijaydurg.

Religious Policy

Shivaji was a devout Hindu but practised religious tolerance. He instructed his officers not to desecrate mosques, harm Muslim women, or disturb Muslim practices. He employed Muslims in his army � including artillery expert Ibrahim Khan and naval commander Siddi Mistri. He patronised the Marathi language over Persian for official documents. This policy made him popular across religious divides. He died in 1680 at Raigad (possibly dysentery), followed by a succession crisis.

Shivaji introduced the chauth (25% revenue as protection money) and sardeshmukhi (10% additional overlord fee). The Shivaji icon was revived by B.G. Tilak for swaraj agitation and by V.D. Savarkar as a Hindu nationalist hero. His emphasis on guerrilla warfare, respect for women and religious places, fair treatment of peasants, naval policy, and constitutional monarchy mark him as a remarkable ruler.

The Peshwa Era (1713-1818)

After Shivaji's death, his elder son Sambhaji (Chhatrapati 1680-1689) � a brave but reckless ruler whose debauchery alienated his nobles � was captured by the Mughals at Sangameshwar through the treachery of his own Brahmin general. He was executed by Aurangzeb after 40 days of brutal torture (his eyes were gouged, tongue cut, limbs amputated, and then beheaded). The Mughals captured his wife and son Shahu. His younger brother Rajaram (1689-1700) continued the war from the fortress of Gingee in Tamil Nadu � the only time Maratha forces operated deep in the south. After Gingee fell to the Mughals (1698), Rajaram fled to Satara (the new Maratha capital). Rajaram's widow Tarabai acted as regent for her infant son Shivaji II (1700-1707) and led a determined guerrilla resistance that exhausted Aurangzeb's army through incessant hit-and-run attacks, reducing Mughal morale.

The Maratha Civil War: Shahu vs Tarabai (1707-1713)

After Aurangzeb's death (1707), the Mughals released Shahu (Sambhaji's son, held captive since 1689) as a deliberate policy to divide the Marathas. Shahu, then aged 25, claimed the Chhatrapati title, leading to a six-year civil war. The Maratha nobility split: Shahu's faction was supported by the Senapati Dabhade family, the Bhonsle of Berar, and a rising Brahmin family from the Konkan � the Bhat family (the future Peshwas). Tarabai's faction supported her son Shivaji II and controlled the traditional Maratha heartland around Satara. Tarabai was an able leader who nearly defeated Shahu initially, but Shahu's forces gradually gained the upper hand, aided by the diplomatic and organisational skills of Balaji Vishwanath Bhat, whom he appointed Peshwa in 1713. By 1714, Tarabai was defeated and sidelined, though the rivalry between the two lines simmered for decades. The civil war had two major consequences: it exhausted the Maratha state at a time when the Mughal Empire was collapsing, and it elevated the Peshwa from a mere prime minister to the de facto ruler, as Shahu increasingly relied on him for administration and military campaigns.

Balaji Vishwanath: The Diplomat (Peshwa 1713-1720)

Balaji Vishwanath (a Chitpavan Brahmin from the Konkan coast who had served as a minor revenue official at Daulatabad) quickly emerged as the real power behind Shahu. His diplomatic masterpiece was negotiating with the Mughals: the Treaty of 1719 between the Peshwa and Emperor Farrukhsiyar (mediated by the Sayyid Brothers, the kingmakers of the Mughal court) was a diplomatic masterstroke. The Mughals recognised Shahu as Chhatrapati, agreed to accept the Chauth and Sardeshmukhi from the six Deccan subahs of the Mughal Empire, and gave the Peshwa control of Malwa as a jagir. This treaty legitimised the Maratha claim to a share of Mughal revenues and effectively made the Marathas partners in the Mughal Empire rather than rebels. Balaji also settled the civil war by negotiating with rival factions, ensuring Shahu's undisputed authority across the Maratha domains. Under his successors, the Peshwas became hereditary rulers, while the Chhatrapati at Satara became a figurehead. The Peshwa was based at Pune, which became the effective capital.

Bajirao I (Peshwa 1720-1740): The Greatest Peshwa

Bajirao I, son of Balaji Vishwanath, was appointed Peshwa at age 20 by Chhatrapati Shahu. He is widely regarded as the greatest Maratha military commander. His strategic vision was encapsulated in his famous declaration: "Let us strike at the trunk of the withering tree (the Mughal Empire), and the branches will fall of themselves." This aggressive northern strategy replaced the Deccan-focused approach and transformed the Marathas into the premier Indian power.

Bajirao's Campaigns: (1) Malwa (1728) � expelled Mughal governor Girdhar Bahadur at the Battle of Amjhera. (2) Gujarat (1729-30) � defeated Sarbuland Khan; the Gaikwad family was placed as governors at Baroda. (3) Bundelkhand (1729) � rescued Bundela ruler Chhatrasal, receiving a huge territory (Sagar, Jhansi, Kalpi) as jagir and his daughter Mastani in marriage. The marriage with Mastani � born of a Muslim mother � was deeply controversial. The orthodox Pune Brahmins, led by his mother Radhabai and brother Chimaji Appa, never accepted her; Bajirao kept her in the separate Mastani Mahal, causing him great personal anguish. (4) Delhi (1737) � led 50,000 cavalry to the Mughal capital, defeating the garrison at the Battle of Delhi. Though he did not capture Delhi, the psychological impact demonstrated Mughal helplessness. (5) Bhopal (1737-38) � surrounded the Nizam's camp, cut supplies, and forced the Treaty of Doraha, confirming Maratha supremacy in the Deccan and ceding Malwa. He forced the Portuguese to pay tribute; Chimaji Appa captured Salsette and Bassein (1739). Bajirao died in 1740 at age 40, having transformed the Marathas into a pan-Indian empire in just 20 years.

Nanasaheb and the Zenith (Peshwa 1740-1761)

Bajirao's son Balaji Bajirao (Nanasaheb) continued expansion, bringing the empire to its greatest territorial extent: from Attock (Pakistan) to the Kaveri, and from the Arabian Sea to the Bay of Bengal. The Marathas collected Chauth from the entire Deccan, Gujarat, Malwa, Bundelkhand, and parts of Bengal (the Bargi raids by Maratha light cavalry devastated Bengal in 1741-48). However, expansion brought conflict with the Durrani Empire under Ahmad Shah Abdali, who invaded India repeatedly (1747-1769).

The Third Battle of Panipat (14 January 1761)

The causes of this devastating battle: the Maratha conquest of Punjab (1758), their expulsion of Abdali's son Timur Shah from Lahore, appeals by the Rohilla chief Najib-ud-Daulah and Shuja-ud-Daulah of Awadh to Abdali, and the weak Mughal emperor Shah Alam II's nominal allegiance to the Marathas. The Maratha army under Sadashivrao Bhau (Nanasaheb's cousin) marched from Pune with 50,000 cavalry, 20,000 infantry, 200 cannon, and 200,000 camp followers (including women, children, servants, and merchants), accompanied by the young heir Vishwasrao.

The Marathas made fatal strategic errors: they alienated the Jats of Bharatpur under Suraj Mal, while Rajputs, Rohillas, Shuja-ud-Daulah, and the Mughal nobility sided with Abdali. Abdali's cavalry cut Maratha supply lines; horses and pack animals died from starvation.

The battle began at dawn on 14 January 1761 and lasted most of the day. The starved but desperate Maratha army fought with extraordinary courage. Vishwasrao was killed early by a bullet to the head, causing a morale collapse. Sadashivrao Bhau dismounted from his elephant and fought on foot, killing hundreds before being overwhelmed. Malharrao Holkar's left wing fled early, exposing the centre to Afghan flank attacks. The final stage was a massacre: between 70,000 and 100,000 Maratha soldiers and camp followers were killed. The Yamuna riverbed near Panipat ran red with blood. The Afghan army also suffered heavily (20,000-30,000 dead). The entire Maratha nobility was decimated. Sadashivrao, his wife Parvatibai, and his young son were all killed.

Aftermath: Nanasaheb died of shock in June 1761. Abdali did not march on Pune � his greatest strategic mistake. Madhavrao I (Peshwa 1761-1772, aged 17) rebuilt the state with minister Nana Phadnis. He crushed the Nizam at Udgir (1763), forcing cession of Ahmadnagar, Bijapur, and parts of Carnatic. General Mahadaji Sindhia reoccupied Delhi (1771), restored Emperor Shah Alam II, and became power behind the Mughal throne. Madhavrao suppressed his uncle Raghunathrao's rebellion and died of tuberculosis in 1772 at age 27, leading to a succession crisis and Peshwa decline.

The Maratha Confederacy: Five Houses

Under the later Peshwas, the Maratha Empire transformed into a confederacy of five semi-autonomous houses: (1) Peshwa at Pune � nominal head, directly controlling the Deccan heartland (Pune, Satara, Kolhapur, parts of Gujarat, Konkan coast), ruling from the magnificent Shaniwar Wada palace built by Bajirao I in 1732; (2) Sindhia (Scindia) at Gwalior � founded by Ranoji Sindhia, a general of Bajirao I. Under Mahadaji Sindhia (1761-1794), the Sindhias became dominant in northern India, controlling the Mughal Emperor in Delhi and reorganising their army on European lines with French general Beno�t de Boigne; (3) Holkar at Indore � founded by Malharrao Holkar, controlling Malwa and parts of Rajasthan. The most famous ruler was Ahilyabai Holkar (1767-1795), one of India's greatest female rulers, who built Maheshwar fort, patronised the Somnath and Kashi temples, and administered justice with legendary fairness; (4) Bhonsle at Nagpur � ruling Berar and central India, related to Shivaji's family; they led the Maratha raids into Bengal and Odisha; (5) Gaikwad at Baroda � Maratha governors of Gujarat who became hereditary rulers; they were the most pro-British house, signing a separate treaty with the British in 1802. This confederal structure � with no single leader capable of commanding all five houses � proved to be the Maratha Empire's greatest weakness against the unified and disciplined British East India Company.

The Anglo-Maratha Wars (1775-1818)

The British fought three wars against the Marathas. The First Anglo-Maratha War (1775-1782) was triggered by British support for Raghunathrao, a claimant to the Peshwa throne, against Nana Phadnis's regency. The Treaty of Salbai (1782) restored the status quo � the British abandoned Raghunathrao, and the Marathas recognised British influence. Twenty years of peace followed, during which Mahadaji Sindhia renewed Maratha power in the north. After his death in 1794, internal rivalries fragmented the confederacy.

The Second Anglo-Maratha War (1803-1805) was decisive. Governor-General Lord Wellesley used the weakness of Peshwa Baji Rao II (reigned from 1796) � an incompetent ruler facing rebellions � to impose the Subsidiary Alliance. In 1802, Yashwantrao Holkar defeated the combined Peshwa-Sindhia army at the Battle of Poona, forcing Baji Rao II to flee to British protection. He signed the Treaty of Bassein (31 December 1802), accepting a British garrison at Pune, ceding territories for its maintenance, surrendering foreign policy to the British, and becoming a British puppet. The Sindhia, Bhonsle, and Holkar refused to accept this treaty and prepared for war. Arthur Wellesley (the future Duke of Wellington) defeated the combined Sindhia-Bhonsle army at the Battle of Assaye (23 September 1803) � Wellesley with 4,500 soldiers (mostly Indian sepoys) defeated a Maratha army of over 30,000 with 100 cannon trained by French officers. Wellesley described Assaye as his greatest victory, even greater than Waterloo. He followed up with victory at Argaon (29 November 1803). Meanwhile, Sir Gerard Lake defeated the Sindhia's northern army at Laswari (1 November 1803), capturing 150 Maratha cannon. The British annexed the Ganga-Yamuna Doab, Delhi (the Mughal emperor placed under British protection), Odisha, and parts of Gujarat. The Holkar continued fighting, inflicting a rare British defeat at Bharatpur (1805), but Lord Cornwallis made peace, allowing Holkar and Sindhia to retain their territories as subordinate allies.

The Third Anglo-Maratha War (1817-1818) eliminated the last remnants of Maratha independence. Governor-General Lord Hastings pursued aggressive expansion. Baji Rao II, chafing under British control, formed a coalition with the Bhonsle of Nagpur and the Holkar of Indore. The British struck first: they defeated the Bhonsle at Sitabaldi (1817) and the Holkar at Mahidpur (1817). Baji Rao II fought a brave guerrilla campaign � including the Battle of Koregaon (1 January 1818), where 800 British-Indian troops held off a much larger Maratha force in a famous defensive action � but was defeated at Ashtee (1818) and surrendered in June 1818. He was pensioned off to Bithoor (near Kanpur) with an annual pension of 8 lakh rupees. The Peshwa title was abolished, and Maratha territories were annexed by the British. The Maratha states (Sindhia, Holkar, Gaikwad, Bhonsle) became princely states under British suzerainty. Of all Indian powers that fought the British, the Marathas resisted the longest � from Shivaji's first struggle (1650) until Baji Rao II's surrender (1818), a period of 168 years.

The Sikh Confederacy (1708-1849)

Foundations: The Ten Gurus

The Sikh religion emerged in Punjab under ten Gurus (1469-1708). The term 'Guru' means a spiritual guide who leads from darkness (gu) to light (ru). The Gurus are considered not as avatars but as enlightened beings who manifested the divine word.

Guru Nanak (1469-1539) � founder of Sikhism. He undertook four major journeys (Udasis) covering Sri Lanka, Tibet, Mecca, and Baghdad, preaching monotheism, equality of all humans, and rejection of caste and ritualism. His 974 hymns are recorded in the Guru Granth Sahib. He instituted the sangat (congregation) and pangat (communal dining, later the langar).

Guru Angad (1539-1552) � formalised the Gurmukhi script (from the Landa script) for writing Punjabi, collected Guru Nanak's hymns, and established the langar permanently.

Guru Amardas (1552-1574) � established the manji system (dioceses headed by preachers) and piri system (female missionaries). He strengthened the langar, making it compulsory for all visitors including Emperor Akbar. He abolished sati and purdah among his followers and promoted widow remarriage.

Guru Ramdas (1574-1581) � founded Amritsar (Ramdaspur) around the sacred Amrit Sarovar (Pool of Nectar) and began construction of the Harmandir Sahib.

Guru Arjan (1581-1606) � completed the Harmandir Sahib with four doors symbolising welcome to all castes and religions. He compiled the Adi Granth (1604), incorporating hymns of the first five Gurus and Hindu-Muslim saints (Kabir, Farid, Namdev, Ravidas). He was executed by Jahangir in 1606 for refusing to convert to Islam � the first Guru martyr. This marked a turning point, transforming the pacifist Sikhs into a militarised movement.

Guru Hargobind (1606-1644) � introduced Miri-Piri (the doctrine of two swords � temporal and spiritual authority), wearing both swords symbolically. He built the Akal Takht (Throne of the Timeless One) facing the Harmandir Sahib as the seat of temporal authority. He militarised the community, built the Lohgarh Fort, and fought four battles against the Mughals.

Guru Har Rai (1644-1661) � maintained a standing army of 2,200 horsemen but avoided conflict, consolidating the community. He maintained diplomatic relations with Dara Shikoh.

Guru Har Krishan (1661-1664) � became Guru at age five and died of smallpox in Delhi at age eight. The Gurdwara Bangla Sahib in Delhi marks his stay.

Guru Tegh Bahadur (1664-1675) � travelled extensively across India (Assam, Dhaka, Bihar) to spread Sikhism. He was executed by Aurangzeb in November 1675 at Chandni Chowk, Delhi, for refusing to convert and for defending the Kashmiri Pandits' right to practice their faith. His martyrdom at age 54 demonstrated that the Mughal Empire threatened all non-Muslims. The Gurdwara Sis Ganj Sahib in Delhi commemorates the site.

Guru Gobind Singh (1666-1708) � the tenth and last human Guru, who transformed the Sikhs into the Khalsa. He was a scholar (Persian, Sanskrit, Punjabi, Braj Bhasha), poet (author of the Dasam Granth and the Zafarnama � the Epistle of Victory � condemning Aurangzeb's betrayal), and military leader who fought 14 battles. His four sons were all martyred: Zorawar Singh (7) and Fateh Singh (5) were bricked alive into a wall at Sirhind by Governor Wazir Khan; Ajit Singh (18) and Jujhar Singh (14) were killed at the Battle of Chamkaur (1705). Before his death at Nanded (1708), he declared the line of personal Gurus ended, establishing the Guru Granth Sahib as the eternal Guru and the Guru Panth (the Khalsa community) as the temporal authority.

The Khalsa (1699)

Guru Gobind Singh founded the Khalsa (the 'Pure') at the Baisakhi festival of 1699 at Anandpur Sahib. According to tradition, the Guru emerged from his tent with a drawn sword, demanding a head for the faith. After three calls, five men volunteered. The five, known as the Panj Pyare (Five Beloved Ones), were Daya Ram (Khatri, Lahore), Dharam Das (Jat, Meerut), Mohkam Chand (tailor, Dwarka), Himmat Chand (water-carrier, Puri), and Sahib Chand (barber, Bidar) � representing all four varnas and different regions of India, symbolising the Khalsa's transcendence of caste.

The Guru performed the Amrit Sanchar: he poured clean water into an iron bowl and stirred it with a double-edged sword (khanda) while reciting scriptures. His wife Mata Sahib Kaur added sugar crystals (patasas), symbolising divine grace. The resulting Amrit (nectar of immortality) was sprinkled on the Panj Pyare, who drank it. The Guru then knelt before the Panj Pyare and asked them to administer Amrit to him, establishing the revolutionary principle that the Guru was subsumed into the Panth � there was no hereditary hierarchy; all Khalsa members were equal regardless of caste.

Khalsa members adopted the Five Ks (panj kakke): (1) Kesh (uncut hair and beard, sign of accepting God's will, rejecting Mughal shaving practice); (2) Kangha (wooden comb worn in the hair, symbolising discipline and cleanliness); (3) Kara (iron/steel bracelet on the right wrist, symbol of restraint and God's infinity � no beginning or end); (4) Kacchera (shorts worn as undergarment, symbolising sexual restraint and readiness for action); (5) Kirpan (ceremonial sword/dagger, symbolising the obligation to protect the weak and fight injustice). They adopted blue attire (bana) and the dastar (turban) as a mark of honour. They took the title Singh (lion) for men and Kaur (princess) for women. The Khalsa was bound by a strict code (Rahit Maryada) prohibiting tobacco, alcohol, halal meat, and adultery. The institution abolished caste within the Sikh community, established Miri-Piri as the guiding principle (every Sikh was both saint and soldier), and transformed the pacifist Sikhs into a warrior community. The Guru Granth Sahib � a 1,430-page scripture of hymns by Sikh Gurus and Hindu-Muslim saints, written in Gurmukhi � was declared the eternal Guru, making Sikhism the first religion to invest a book with the authority of a living Guru.

The Sikh Misls (1716-1801)

After Guru Gobind Singh's death, the Sikhs fought under Banda Singh Bahadur (a Jat peasant turned military leader). He conquered Sirhind (1710, avenging the murder of the Guru's sons), established the first independent Sikh state (from the Sutlej to the Yamuna), and minted coins in the name of Guru Nanak and Guru Gobind Singh. He was defeated and executed by the Mughals in 1716. The Sikhs then fragmented into 12 Misls (autonomous chiefdoms) between the 1720s and 1760s. The word 'Misl' derives from Arabic misl meaning 'similar' or 'equal', reflecting their egalitarian nature. Each Misl was led by a Sardar and operated as a small republic with its own territory, army, and administration:

The 12 Misls and their approximate territories: (1) Sukerchakia (Gujranwala, central Punjab) � the most powerful, under Maha Singh and his son Ranjit Singh; (2) Sandhawalia (Jalandhar Doab); (3) Bhangi (Amritsar, Lahore, western Punjab) � most powerful in the 1760s, it captured Amritsar and Lahore; (4) Kanhaiya (Batala, Gurdaspur); (5) Ramgarhia (Amritsar region) � its leader Jassa Singh Ramgarhia built the Ramgarhia Bunga around the Golden Temple; (6) Singhpuria (Khurdpur, near Jalandhar); (7) Ahluwalia (Kapurthala) � led by Jassa Singh Ahluwalia, who minted coins and patronised the Harmandir Sahib; (8) Nishanpuria (Ambala region); (9) Karor Singhia (Ravi river region); (10) Dulewalia (Beas river region); (11) Shahid (Shaheedan Misl � the 'Martyrs' Misl', composed of Akali Nihangs, veterans of the struggle against Mughals and Afghans); (12) Nakai (Sutlej river region). The Misls met in the Sarbat Khalsa (general assembly of the Khalsa) at the Akal Takht in Amritsar, which decided war and peace, appointed leaders, and settled inter-Misl disputes. The Dal Khalsa (unified Sikh army) was organised into two divisions: the Budha Dal (army of veterans, about 5,000 old soldiers who guarded the Harmandir Sahib and Akal Takht) and the Tarna Dal (army of young soldiers, about 10,000-15,000 troops, the main fighting force). The Sikhs resisted Ahmad Shah Abdali's nine invasions of the Punjab (1747-1769) and always recovered their territories after he withdrew. By the 1760s, they had captured Lahore (1761) and Amritsar, controlling most of central Punjab. The Misls represent a unique experiment in decentralised republican governance, each autonomous but accountable to the Sarbat Khalsa.

Maharaja Ranjit Singh (1780-1839): The Lion of Punjab

Ranjit Singh of the Sukerchakia Misl � born in 1780 in Gujranwala (now Pakistan) � was the greatest Sikh ruler. He conquered the other Misls between 1801 and 1810, establishing the Sikh Empire (the Khalsa Raj) with his capital at Lahore. He was crowned Maharaja on 12 April 1801 (Baisakhi) with the authority of the Sarbat Khalsa.

Annexations: He conquered the Bhangi territories (including Amritsar, 1802), the Kanhaiya territory (Batala, 1803-05), Sialkot (1807), the Jalandhar Doab (1811), and then expanded outwards: Kangra (1810), Multan (1818, after a long siege destroying the Multan fort), Kashmir (1819, defeating Jabbar Khan at Shopian), Peshawar (1823, defeating Afghan Ghazis), Dera Ghazi Khan (1820), Ladakh (1834-42 under Zorawar Singh), Baltistan and Gilgit (1840s). He also captured the Khyber Pass (1834), controlling the traditional invasion route into India. At its peak, his empire stretched from the Indus to the Sutlej, from the Himalayas to Sindh � about 200,000 square miles with 5 million people.

Army Modernisation: Ranjit Singh modernised the Sikh Khalsa Army (also called Fauj-i-Khas) on European lines, hiring European mercenaries. The most famous were French generals Jean-Fran�ois Allard (trained the cavalry in French style � blue uniforms with yellow facings) and Claude Auguste Court (modernised the artillery and cannon foundries); Italian general Paolo Avitabile (governor of Peshawar, known for harsh administration); German physician Johann Honigberger (personal doctor to Ranjit Singh); and American adventurer Josiah Harlan (governor of an Afghan district � claimed as the first American in Afghanistan). The army at its peak comprised 60 infantry battalions (40,000 men), 12,000 regular cavalry (including the elite Gardner Horse and Furlong Horse), and 150 field guns (including the famous Zamzama cannon, the 'Gun of Lahore', now in the Lahore Museum, inspiration for Kipling's Kim). It was arguably the best Indian army of its time.

Treaty of Amritsar (1809): Negotiated by British envoy Charles Metcalfe amid fears of a Napoleonic invasion of India (after the Treaty of Tilsit, 1807), the treaty established the Sutlej River as the permanent boundary between British India and the Sikh Empire. Ranjit Singh agreed to remain north of the Sutlej; the British agreed not to interfere north of it. This secured his eastern frontier, allowing him to expand westwards without British interference, and gave the British a buffer against Afghans and Russians. The treaty remained in force throughout his reign.

Religious Tolerance and Patronage: Ranjit Singh was a remarkably tolerant ruler. Although a devout Sikh, he employed Hindus (Prime Minister Dhian Singh Dogra), Muslims (Foreign Minister Fakir Azizuddin, one of his closest advisors), and Europeans in his administration. His Finance Minister was Dina Nath (a Kashmiri Pandit). He banned cow slaughter out of respect for Hindu sentiments. He patronised the Harmandir Sahib (the Golden Temple), covering it with gold leaf and marble between 1835 and 1843 (partly from Afghan loot). He also built the Hazuri Bagh Baradari (a marble pavilion in Lahore Fort), the Ram Bagh Palace (summer palace in Amritsar, now a museum), and the Samadhi of Ranjit Singh (his mausoleum in Lahore, blending Sikh and Islamic architectural styles). The Koh-i-Noor diamond � one of the world's most famous diamonds (105.6 carats in its original cut) � was acquired from the Afghan ruler Shah Shuja in 1813 in exchange for military support. Ranjit Singh wore it as a ceremonial armlet. After the British annexation (1849), the Koh-i-Noor was seized by the British East India Company and presented to Queen Victoria; it is now part of the British Crown Jewels in the Tower of London. Ranjit Singh died in Lahore on 27 June 1839 after a prolonged illness, leaving a stable and powerful empire. His death was followed by a brutal succession war among his sons and nobles, which destroyed the Sikh state within a decade.

The Anglo-Sikh Wars (1845-1849)

After Ranjit Singh's death (1839), the Sikh Empire plunged into chaos: four successive Maharajas (Kharak Singh, Nau Nihal Singh, Chand Kaur, Sher Singh) were assassinated within five years (1839-1843). The army became unruly, and the Dogra and Sandhawalia factions competed for control. The disciplined British army on the Sutlej frontier prepared for war. The Sikh Khalsa Army, trained on European lines, crossed the Sutlej in December 1845, triggering the First Anglo-Sikh War (1845-46). Four major battles followed: (1) Mudki (18 December 1845) � a confused night battle; the Sikh general Lal Singh was suspected of treachery; (2) Ferozeshah (21-22 December 1845) � the Sikhs fortified their positions with huge entrenchments and 100 cannon. Sir Hugh Gough's initial attack was repulsed, but after desperate fighting on the second day, the British captured the Sikh positions � suffering 2,400 casualties, the highest in any single engagement in India up to that time; (3) Aliwal (28 January 1846) � Sir Harry Smith defeated a Sikh force under Ranjodh Singh Majithia, restoring British morale; (4) Sabraon (10 February 1846) � the decisive battle. The Sikhs fought with extraordinary courage but were overwhelmed by British artillery. The great general Sham Singh Attariwala was killed fighting to the end, and thousands of Sikh soldiers drowned trying to cross the flooded Sutlej. The Treaty of Lahore (9 March 1846) forced the Sikhs to cede Kashmir and the Jalandhar Doab and pay a huge indemnity of 15 million rupees. Since the Sikhs could not pay, Kashmir was sold to Raja Gulab Singh Dogra for 7.5 million rupees under the separate Treaty of Amritsar (16 March 1846), creating the princely state of Jammu and Kashmir under British suzerainty.

The Second Anglo-Sikh War (1848-49) was triggered by the rebellion of Multan governor Mulraj (April 1848), who killed British officers. The rebellion spread to the entire Sikh army under Sardar Chattar Singh Attariwala and his son Sher Singh Attariwala, who fought with great skill and chivalry. Governor-General Lord Dalhousie (determined to annex the Sikh Empire) sent Sir Hugh Gough, who decisively defeated the Sikhs at the Battle of Gujarat (21 February 1849) � the Sikhs were bombarded from long range and could not close with the British. The Sikh army of about 20,000 surrendered at Rawalpindi on 14 March 1849. On 29 March 1849, Lord Dalhousie announced the annexation of the Sikh Empire. The 11-year-old Maharaja Duleep Singh (the youngest son of Ranjit Singh) was removed from Lahore, placed under British guardianship, and eventually exiled to England (1854), where he became a country gentleman in Norfolk. The Koh-i-Noor diamond was seized and sent to Queen Victoria. The annexation of the Punjab completed the British conquest of India.

UPSC Tip: Maratha & Sikh comparison
Both the Marathas and Sikhs represent indigenous Indian resistance to Mughal expansion and British conquest. Key differences: (1) The Marathas used guerrilla warfare (ganimi kava), while the Sikhs used traditional cavalry charges. (2) The Marathas had the Chauth/Sardeshmukhi system of protection money, while the Sikhs had the Rakhi system. (3) The Marathas declined due to internal disunity (competing Maratha houses: Sindhia, Holkar, Bhonsle, Gaikwad) and the British Subsidiary Alliance, while the Sikhs declined due to succession wars after Ranjit Singh's death. (4) Both adopted European military methods (Marathas hired French and Portuguese mercenaries; Sikhs hired French and Italian generals). For UPSC, focus on Shivaji's administration (Ashta Pradhan) and the causes of Maratha decline (the Third Battle of Panipat, the Anglo-Maratha Wars), and on the Sikh Gurus (especially the founding of the Khalsa in 1699), the Misls, and Ranjit Singh's reforms.

15. Advent of Europeans (1498-1765)

The Advent of Europeans in India began with the arrival of the Portuguese navigator Vasco da Gama off the coast of Calicut (Kerala) on 20 May 1498. The Portuguese were followed by the Dutch (1602), the British East India Company (1600 — but they entered India in 1608), the Danish (1616), and the French (1664). The competition between these European trading companies over the lucrative spice and textile trade of India eventually led to the British conquest of India after the Battle of Plassey (1757) and the Battle of Buxar (1764), which gave the British East India Company effective control over Bengal, Bihar, and Odisha — the richest provinces of the Mughal Empire.

Portuguese (1498-1961)

The Portuguese were the first Europeans to establish a colonial presence in India. Their arrival marked the beginning of European imperialism in Asia and fundamentally altered global trade networks that had existed for millennia.

Vasco da Gama's Voyage (1497-1499)

Vasco da Gama departed from Lisbon on 8 July 1497 with four ships (the São Gabriel, São Rafael, Berrio, and a supply ship) under the patronage of King Manuel I of Portugal. His mission was to find a sea route to India by sailing around Africa, bypassing the Ottoman-controlled overland routes that had enriched Venetian and Arab middlemen for centuries. He rounded the Cape of Good Hope in November 1497, sailed up the east coast of Africa (calling at Mozambique, Mombasa, and Malindi), and with the help of the Gujarati pilot Ibn Majid (or Malam Kannu, as per some traditions — a skilled navigator who knew the monsoon wind patterns of the Indian Ocean), crossed the Arabian Sea and reached Kappad Beach, near Calicut (Kozhikode), on 20 May 1498. He was received by the Hindu ruler of Calicut, the Zamorin (Samudri Raja — Lord of the Sea). Trade negotiations failed due to the hostility of the Arab merchants who dominated Calicut's spice trade and because Gama's gift offerings (striped cloth, hats, sugar, oil, honey) were deemed inadequate for a royal audience. Gama returned to Lisbon in 1499 with a small cargo of pepper and cinnamon — the profit from the voyage was estimated at 60 times its cost, sparking a frenzy of Portuguese expeditions to India. A second fleet under Pedro �?lvares Cabral (1500) reached Calicut with 13 ships, established a factory, but after a conflict with the Arabs, the factory was attacked and 50 Portuguese killed. Cabral bombarded Calicut and then sailed to Cochin, where he established friendly relations with the Raja of Cochin, a rival of the Zamorin.

Portuguese Expansion and Administration

In 1502, Vasco da Gama returned with a heavily armed fleet and forced the Zamorin to sign a treaty, demanded the expulsion of all Arab merchants from Calicut, and bombarded the city when the Zamorin hesitated. He established the Portuguese factory at Cochin (1503 — Fort Manuel, the first Portuguese fort in India) and the trading post at Kollam (Quilon). The Portuguese king appointed Francisco de Almeida as the first Portuguese Viceroy of India (1505-1509), who established the policy of Blue Water Policy (dominance of the sea rather than territorial conquest). Almeida defeated a combined Mamluk-Gujarati-Calicut fleet at the Battle of Diu (3 February 1509), a decisive naval battle that established Portuguese naval supremacy in the Indian Ocean for the next century. The Portuguese viceroyalty was based at Goa, which was captured by Afonso de Albuquerque (the second Viceroy, 1509-1515) on 25 November 1510 from the Adil Shah of Bijapur. Albuquerque shifted the capital from Cochin to Goa and established it as the nerve centre of the Estado da �?ndia (Portuguese State of India). He captured Malacca (1511 — the gateway to the East Indies spice trade), Hormuz (1515 — controlling the Persian Gulf entry), and tried to capture Aden (failed). He also established friendly relations with the Vijayanagara Empire (the Portuguese were allowed to trade at San Thome/Mylapore and maintained a fort at Bhatkal). He encouraged Portuguese soldiers to marry local women (the policy of miscegenation) and established a uniform legal code for the Portuguese in India.

The Portuguese established a string of fortified trading stations (feitorias) along the Indian coast. The major Portuguese settlements in India were: Goa (1510 — the administrative and ecclesiastical capital; known as Goa Dourada or Golden Goa, described as the 'Rome of the East' for its magnificent churches, cathedrals, monasteries, and palaces); Diu (1535 — ceded by Bahadur Shah of Gujarat through the Treaty of Bassein in exchange for Portuguese military help against the Mughals; the fort of Diu is a masterpiece of military architecture); Daman (1559 — captured from the Gujarat Sultanate by Governor Constantino de Braganza); Bassein (1534 — captured from Bahadur Shah, a major trading centre, known as the 'City of the North'; the Portuguese built the massive Bassein Fort with its distinctive Corinthian columns); Mumbai (the seven islands of Bombay were part of the Bassein territory but were given to the British as the dowry of Catherine of Braganza on her marriage to Charles II in 1661); Hugli (Bengal, 1579 — a prosperous factory exporting silk and textiles, with the famous Bandel Church built in 1599); Nagapatam (Coromandel Coast); Mylapore/San Thome (near Madras); Kochi/Cochin (1503 — with the Fort Emmanuel and the St. Francis Church, the oldest European church in India where Vasco da Gama was originally buried); Quilon/Kollam; Cranganore; Cannanore/Kannur (with the Fort St. Angelo built in 1505); and Chittagong (Bengal, temporarily).

The Cartaze System and Trade Monopoly

The Portuguese enforced a strict monopoly over the spice trade of the Indian Ocean through the cartaze system — all Indian ships carrying spices, silk, or other valuable goods were required to purchase a license (cartaz) from the Portuguese authorities, and any ship found without a cartaz was seized or sunk. The Portuguese also controlled the major choke points of the Indian Ocean: the Cape of Good Hope, the entrance to the Red Sea (Socotra), the Persian Gulf (Hormuz), and the Strait of Malacca. This gave them an effective stranglehold on the spice trade for most of the 16th century. Portugal's main exports from India were: black pepper (the most important, sourced from the Malabar Coast), cinnamon (from Ceylon/Sri Lanka), ginger, cardamom, cloves (from the Moluccas), nutmeg and mace (from the Banda Islands), indigo (from Gujarat), cotton textiles (from the Coromandel Coast and Gujarat), and precious stones (diamonds from Golconda). The Portuguese also traded in Chinese silk, Japanese silver, and Southeast Asian sandalwood through their network of Asian ports. The Casa da �?ndia in Lisbon regulated all trade and shipping between Portugal and Asia.

Missionary Activities and the Inquisition

The Portuguese combined commerce with evangelisation. The major missionary orders that operated in Portuguese India were the Franciscans (arriving 1517), Dominicans (1548), Jesuits (1542, with the arrival of St. Francis Xavier, a founding member of the Society of Jesus, who reached Goa in 1542 and spent the next decade baptising thousands on the Fishery Coast (Tuticorin), in Travancore, Ceylon, and Malacca, before dying near Canton in 1552), and the Augustinians (1572). St. Francis Xavier is called the 'Apostle of the Indies' — he is said to have baptised tens of thousands using a bell, a basin of water, and a formula. The Portuguese also introduced the Goa Inquisition (1560-1812), established by King Sebastian of Portugal at the request of the Jesuits, to investigate and punish converted Christians suspected of practising their former religions (Hinduism or Islam) in secret. The Inquisition in Goa was more severe than the European version — it targeted converted Hindus and Jews (New Christians) accused of crypto-Hinduism/Judaism. The inquisitional tribunal was based at the Palacio da Inquisi��o near the Old Goa cathedral. The Inquisition also destroyed many Hindu temples, banned Hindu religious festivals, and ordered the destruction of Sanskrit and Marathi manuscripts deemed heretical. It was finally abolished in 1812.

Decline of the Portuguese in India

The Portuguese Empire in India declined from the late 16th century due to a combination of factors. The most important were: (1) Spanish domination of Portugal (1580-1640) — the Iberian Union under Philip II of Spain diverted Portuguese resources to Spain's European wars and neglected the Asian empire; (2) Military defeats by the Dutch and English — the Dutch VOC captured Portuguese Malacca (1641), Colombo (1656), Cochin (1663), and Nagapatnam (1665), while the English captured Hormuz and established trading stations in Portuguese territories; (3) Corruption and administrative decay — the cartaze system was increasingly evaded, officials enriched themselves, and the Portuguese navy decayed; (4) The loss of crucial settlements — the Portuguese lost Hugli to the Mughals (1632, when Shah Jahan captured the Portuguese fort at Hugli for slaving activities), and Muscat to the Omani Arabs (1650); (5) Economic stagnation — Portugal's limited population and capital could not sustain a global empire against the richer and better-organised Dutch and English companies. The Portuguese left behind a lasting cultural legacy in India: the Goan Catholic community, the Konkani language (written in the Roman script), distinctive Goan cuisine (vindaloo, sorpotel), Goan architecture (Basilica of Bom Jesus with the body of St. Francis Xavier, Se Cathedral, Church of St. Francis of Assisi), and the Portuguese Creole languages (the Korlai Portuguese Creole in Maharashtra and the Daman and Diu Portuguese Creoles). Portuguese words entered Indian languages: almari (cupboard), janela (window), sabao (soap), batata (potato), ananas (pineapple), padre (priest), igreja (church), and mesa (table). The Portuguese also introduced European firearms, shipbuilding techniques, and printing to India. Their remaining Indian possessions (Goa, Daman, Diu) were annexed by the Republic of India through Operation Vijay on 19 December 1961, a 36-hour military campaign in which 31 Indians and 30 Portuguese were killed.

Dutch (1602-1825)

The Dutch East India Company (Vereenigde Oost-Indische Compagnie — VOC) was established on 20 March 1602 through a charter granted by the States-General of the Dutch Republic. It is widely considered the first multinational corporation in history — it was the first company to issue shares publicly, the first true transnational corporation, and the first company to be granted sovereign powers (the right to wage war, negotiate treaties, mint coins, and administer justice in its territories). The VOC was formed through the merger of competing Dutch trading companies (the Compagnie van Verre, the Nieuwe Compagnie, etc.) at the instigation of Johan van Oldenbarnevelt, the Grand Pensionary of Holland, to eliminate internal competition and pool resources for long-distance trade.

The VOC in India

The VOC established its Indian factories in the early 17th century. The first Dutch settlement in India was Pulicat (1610, on the Coromandel Coast, about 60 km north of Madras), which served as the Dutch headquarters in India until 1690 (when it was replaced by Nagapatnam). The Dutch established factories at: Surat (1616 — the first in Mughal territory, with permission from Jahangir through the efforts of the Dutch ambassador Pieter van den Broecke), Bimlipatam (1641, on the Odisha-Andhra coast, for cloth and saltpetre), Chinsura (1653, near Hugli in Bengal — the Dutch Bengal headquarters, with the famous Dutch cemetery and the Dutch-style houses that still stand), Baleshwar (Odisha, for saltpetre), Patna (Bihar, for saltpetre and opium), Nagapatam (1658, captured from the Portuguese — became the Dutch Coromandel headquarters after 1690), Sadras (Coromandel Coast, south of Madras), Cochin (1663, captured from the Portuguese after a siege by Admiral Rijckloff van Goens — the Dutch held Cochin for over 130 years, the longest of any European power there), Quilon/Kollam, Cranganore (Kerala), Kannur/Cannanore, and Tranquebar (temporarily leased from the Danes). The Dutch captured the Portuguese settlements in Ceylon (Sri Lanka) in stages: Batticaloa (1638), Colombo (1656), Jaffna (1658), and Galle (1640), gaining control of the cinnamon trade (the most valuable spice in the 17th century).

Trade and Economy

The VOC was the most powerful European trading company in the 17th century (the 'Golden Century' of the Netherlands). Its fleet numbered over 1,500 ships at its peak, more than the combined fleets of England, France, Spain, and Portugal. The company employed over 50,000 people worldwide, with an army of 10,000-15,000 soldiers. The VOC's main exports from India were: black pepper (from the Malabar Coast), cinnamon (from Ceylon, which the Dutch cultivated systematically), cotton textiles (the famous Coromandel chintz and painted calicoes, highly prized in the Indonesian spice islands and Europe), saltpetre (potassium nitrate, the essential component of gunpowder, sourced from Bihar and Bengal — the Dutch supplied the entire European gunpowder industry), indigo (from Gujarat and Agra), silk (from Bengal, including the famous Bengali silk taffetas), opium (from Bengal and Bihar, traded for Indonesian spices), diamonds (from Golconda), and sugar (from Bengal). The VOC also controlled the nutmeg and clove trade of the Moluccas (Spice Islands) through a brutal monopoly that included the deliberate destruction of nutmeg trees on all islands except the Banda Islands (whose entire native population was killed or enslaved in 1621 under Jan Pieterszoon Coen).

Dutch Decline in India

Although the Dutch were commercially successful, they were more interested in the Indonesian archipelago (where the profits from the spice monopoly were immense) than in India. Their Indian factories were important suppliers of textiles, saltpetre, and opium for the Indonesian trade, but the Dutch never aimed to conquer Indian territory (unlike the British and French). Several factors led to Dutch decline in India: (1) The Anglo-Dutch Wars (1652-1784) — the British navy fought four wars with the Dutch (First: 1652-54, Second: 1665-67, Third: 1672-74, Fourth: 1780-84), gradually reducing Dutch naval and commercial power; (2) The Fourth Anglo-Dutch War (1780-84) — the British captured all major Dutch settlements in India, including Nagapatam, Pulicat, Sadras, and Chinsura, during this war (the British East India Company feared that the Dutch would ally with the American rebels and the French during the American Revolutionary War); (3) The Battle of Colachel (10 August 1741) — the Travancore king Marthanda Varma decisively defeated the Dutch East India Company at Colachel (in present-day Kanyakumari district), capturing the Dutch commander Eustachius De Lannoy (who later became the Travancore army commander and modernised the Travancore military); this was the first significant defeat of a European power by an Indian kingdom and marked the end of Dutch expansion on the Malabar Coast; (4) The decline of the Netherlands as a world power — by the late 18th century, the Netherlands was overshadowed by Britain and France; the VOC was dissolved in 1800 (it had been bankrupt for decades, weighed down by corruption, mismanagement, and the costs of the Anglo-Dutch Wars). By the Anglo-Dutch Treaty of 1824, the Dutch ceded all their Indian settlements to the British in exchange for British withdrawal from Sumatra and the Dutch recognition of British control over the Straits Settlements (Singapore, Malacca). The Dutch legacy in India is relatively modest: Dutch-style architecture in Chinsura, Nagapatam, and Cochin (the Dutch Palace at Mattancherry, actually built by the Portuguese and presented to the Raja of Cochin, with later Dutch additions), and Dutch loanwords in Indian languages (like the Malayalam word 'kappal' for ship, derived from the Dutch 'kapitein').

British East India Company (1600-1858)

The English East India Company (EIC), later known as the British East India Company, was the most successful and dominant European power in India. It transformed from a trading enterprise into the de facto ruler of the Indian subcontinent over the course of 250 years.

Foundation and Early Trade (1600-1708)

The EIC was founded on 31 December 1600 by a Royal Charter granted by Queen Elizabeth I, under the title 'The Governor and Company of Merchants of London Trading into the East Indies'. The charter granted the Company a monopoly on English trade with Asia for 15 years (renewed periodically). Unlike the Portuguese crown-controlled enterprise or the Dutch VOC, the EIC was a private company owned by shareholders (initially 218 shareholders, with a capital of £68,000). The EIC sent its first voyage to the East Indies (under James Lancaster) in 1601. After founding the Bantam factory in Java (1603), the Company sent Captain William Hawkins to the Mughal Emperor Jahangir's court in 1608 to seek permission for trade at Surat. Hawkins arrived at Agra in 1609 and stayed for three years, learning Persian and gaining Jahangir's favour (he was given a mansab of 400 zat and a Mughal wife). However, the Portuguese (who were at war with the English at sea) successfully blocked the English from establishing a permanent factory at Surat. In 1612, the English won the Battle of Swally (or Suvali, off Surat), a naval battle against the Portuguese, convincing Jahangir of English naval power. In 1613, the Mughal Emperor Jahangir granted the EIC permission to establish a factory at Surat (the first English factory in India). In 1615, Sir Thomas Roe arrived at Jahangir's court as the English ambassador, with the aim of negotiating a formal commercial treaty for the Company. Roe stayed until 1619, obtained trading privileges, and established the principle of British diplomatic representation at the Mughal court. The EIC established factories at Masulipatnam (1611, on the Coromandel Coast, with permission from the Sultan of Golconda), Armagaon (1625, near Pulicat), Baleshwar (1633, Odisha), Hariharpur (1633, Odisha delta), Fort St. George (1644, Madras — the first fortified English settlement in India, built by Francis Day with permission from the Nayak of Poonamallee), Hugli (1651, Bengal, after the Company obtained permission from Shah Shuja, the Mughal governor of Bengal), Bombay (1661 — acquired by King Charles II as part of the dowry of Catherine of Braganza; Charles II leased it to the EIC in 1668 for an annual rent of £10 in gold), Fort William (1690, Calcutta/Kolkata — founded by Job Charnock, an EIC agent who is traditionally credited as the founder of the city; the EIC obtained the zamindari rights of the three villages of Sutanuti, Kalikata, and Gobindapur from the Mughal emperor in 1698). The English factories were governed by the principle of Presidencies: the Presidency of Bombay (administered from Bombay), the Presidency of Madras (from Fort St. George), and the Presidency of Fort William in Bengal (from Calcutta). Each presidency operated with considerable autonomy until the Regulating Act of 1773 unified the administration under a Governor-General in Bengal.

The Struggle for Supremacy (1708-1757)

In the early 18th century, the EIC faced stiff competition from the French in India. The French East India Company under Joseph François Dupleix (Governor of Pondicherry from 1742) developed a system of using Indian troops trained in European methods (the 'sepoy' army) and allied with Indian princes to extend French influence. The British under Robert Clive (who arrived in India in 1744 as a writer, i.e., clerk, in the EIC) matched the French strategy. The decisive moment came with the Carnatic Wars (detailed below). By 1761, the British had decisively defeated the French, making the EIC the only serious European contender for power in India. However, the EIC also had to defend itself against Indian powers. The most serious threat was from the young Nawab of Bengal, Siraj-ud-Daula (who became Nawab in 1756 at age 23). Siraj was angered by the EIC's fortification of Calcutta (without his permission) and by the Company's abuse of its trade privileges (they refused to pay the standard duties and their private servants traded duty-free). In June 1756, Siraj attacked and captured Fort William, Calcutta, and imprisoned the English garrison in the infamous Black Hole of Calcutta (a small prison cell, 18 ft by 14 ft, in which 146 prisoners were allegedly confined overnight on 20 June 1756; only 23 survived — though the veracity of the story is disputed; the 'Black Hole' narrative, promoted by John Zephaniah Holwell, is now widely seen as a propaganda exaggeration to justify the British conquest of Bengal).

The Battles of Plassey and Buxar (1757-1765)

The Battle of Plassey (Palashi, on the banks of the Bhagirathi River, about 150 km north of Calcutta) on 23 June 1757 was the turning point of British history in India. Robert Clive, with about 3,000 soldiers (including 800 Europeans and 2,200 Indian sepoys) and 9 cannons, faced Siraj-ud-Daula's army of about 50,000 soldiers (including infantry, cavalry, and artillery) and 53 French cannons. The outcome was decided not by military force but by treason. Siraj's commander-in-chief Mir Jafar (who had secretly agreed to betray Siraj in exchange for being made Nawab) ordered his troops to withdraw, creating a gap in the Bengali lines. The battle was effectively a coup, not a real battle — the Bengali army melted away with minimal fighting. The British installed Mir Jafar as Nawab of Bengal (who paid them £2 million as compensation and granted the EIC the zamindari of 24 Parganas). The Treaty of Alinagar (1757) was imposed on the new Nawab, confirming the EIC's privileges. Mir Jafar was a weak ruler who could not satisfy the EIC's escalating demands for money. In 1760, the British replaced him with his son-in-law Mir Qasim, who paid a massive bribe for the appointment. Mir Qasim, however, proved independent-minded and tried to reform his administration, curb the EIC's abuse of trade privileges, and build an independent army. He fought the British at the Battle of Buxar (22 October 1764) on the banks of the Ganges in Bihar. The allied army of Mir Qasim (Bengal), Shuja-ud-Daulah (Nawab of Awadh), and the Mughal Emperor Shah Alam II was decisively defeated by a smaller EIC force under Major Hector Munro (7,000 soldiers including 857 Europeans, vs. the allied army of 40,000). Munro's victory was the most decisive single battle in Indian colonial history. The Treaty of Allahabad (1765) was the most important treaty of the 18th century for India: the Mughal Emperor Shah Alam II granted the EIC the Diwani (the right to collect revenue) of Bengal, Bihar, and Odisha. This made the EIC the sovereign power in eastern India, although in theory it was the Diwan (revenue collector) acting on behalf of the Mughal Emperor. The Nawab of Awadh was made a British protectorate (he paid a huge war indemnity). The EIC now controlled the richest provinces of India — with a revenue of £4 million per year. The British had become the paramount power in India.

Consolidation and Expansion (1765-1858)

After the Diwani grant, the EIC's internal administration in Bengal was chaotic. The Company's officials (known as 'Nabobs') enriched themselves through corruption, private trade, and extortion. The Company prevented the entry of Indian merchants into profitable trades. The Bengal Famine of 1770 (in which an estimated 10 million people — one-third of Bengal's population — died) was caused by the EIC's rapacious revenue policies (heavy taxation, exclusion of Indian merchants from the grain trade, the Company's hoarding of rice for export, and the remittance of tax revenues to Britain). The British Parliament passed a series of Acts to regulate the Company: the Regulating Act of 1773 (created the post of Governor-General of Bengal with supervisory authority over the other presidencies; established a Supreme Court at Calcutta), Pitt's India Act of 1784 (established a Board of Control in London to oversee the Company's political activities; created the dual system of government), and the Charter Acts of 1793, 1813, 1833, and 1853 (which progressively reduced the Company's monopoly and increased Parliament's control). The most effective Governor-General was Lord Wellesley (1798-1805), who introduced the policy of Subsidiary Alliance (an Indian ruler could keep his throne only by accepting the EIC's protection, paying for a British garrison, and surrendering foreign policy to the British). Wellesley used this to make Hyderabad, Mysore, Awadh, the Maratha states (Gwalior, Indore, Nagpur), and many Rajput states into British protectorates. The Anglo-Maratha Wars (1775-1818) broke Maratha power, and the Anglo-Sikh Wars (1845-46, 1848-49) annexed the Sikh Empire. By 1856, under Lord Dalhousie (who aggressively used the Doctrine of Lapse — annexing states without a natural heir), the EIC ruled directly over two-thirds of India, with the remaining third under princely states that were de facto British protectorates.

French (1664-1793)

The French East India Company (Compagnie Française des Indes Orientales) was founded by Jean-Baptiste Colbert, the finance minister of King Louis XIV, in 1664, as part of Colbert's ambitious programme to make France a global commercial and naval power equal to the Dutch and English. The French Company was a state-controlled enterprise (unlike the British EIC which was privately owned) — it was heavily dependent on royal patronage and government subsidies. The French established their first Indian factory at Surat (1668), followed by Masulipatnam (1669, on the Coromandel Coast). In 1673, the French purchased the village of Pondicherry (Puducherry, called 'Le Comptoir de Pondichéry') from the Sultan of Bijapur, making it the capital of French India. The French built the city with a planned grid layout (the French Quarter and the Indian Quarter, separated by a canal), a massive fort (the Fort of Pondicherry, destroyed and rebuilt multiple times), and a beautiful cathedral (the Basilica of the Sacred Heart of Jesus). Other French factories were established at Chandernagore (Bengal, 1690 — the second most important French settlement, a prosperous centre of the silk and saltpetre trade), Mahe (Malabar Coast, 1721 — named after the French hero François Mahé de La Bourdonnais), Yanam (Andhra Coast, 1723), Karaikal (Tamil Nadu, 1739), and a brief presence at Balasore (Odisha). The French Company was also active in the Indian Ocean islands (Mauritius and Bourbon/Réunion), which served as naval bases for operations against the British.

The French were the main rivals of the British in India. The most dynamic period of French activity was under Joseph François Dupleix, Governor-General of Pondicherry from 1742 to 1754. Dupleix had a grand vision: to create a French empire in India by forging alliances with Indian princes and by using Indian soldiers trained in European methods (the sepoy infantry, which he innovated and used extensively). Dupleix's method was to intervene in succession disputes of Indian kingdoms, support a claimant to the throne, and demand territorial and commercial privileges in return. He successfully placed Muzaffar Jang on the throne of Hyderabad (which made him the effective master of the Deccan) and Chanda Sahib as the Nawab of Carnatic (the region around Madras). At its height (1750-1754), French influence under Dupleix extended from the Krishna River to Cape Comorin, and Pondicherry became one of the most prosperous European settlements in India. However, Dupleix was undermined by the British (under the military genius of Robert Clive) and by the French Company's reluctance to fund his expensive imperial ambitions. After the Battle of Arcot (1751), where Clive defended Arcot with 200 soldiers against 10,000 French-supported Indian troops, and the recall of Dupleix to France (1754, where he died in poverty and disgrace), the French lost the initiative. The Battle of Wandiwash (1760) and the Treaty of Paris (1763) ended French political ambitions in India forever. After this, the French maintained their five settlements (Pondicherry, Chandernagore, Mahe, Yanam, Karaikal) as trading stations with no military significance. Pondicherry was captured and recaptured multiple times during the Anglo-French wars of the 18th-19th centuries (the British captured it in 1761, 1778, 1793, and 1803, each time returning it after peace treaties). The French presence in India ultimately ended in 1954 (de facto transfer to India) and 1956 (de jure treaty of cession). French culture left a lasting legacy: the grid-planned Pondicherry with its French architecture, boulevards, street names, and the French language still spoken by the Franco-Pondichérien community; French loanwords in Indian languages; and the Aurobindo Ashram and Auroville (an experimental international township founded in 1968).

Danish (1616-1845)

The Danish East India Company (Ostindisk Kompagni) was founded in 1616 by King Christian IV of Denmark-Norway. It was the smallest and least powerful of the European trading companies operating in India but maintained a presence for over 200 years. The Danish established their first Indian factory at Tranquebar (Tarangambadi, in Tamil Nadu) in 1620 (the city was renamed Fort Dansborg; the Danish purchased the village of Tranquebar from the Nayak of Tanjore for an annual rent of 2,000 pagodas). Tranquebar remained the Danish capital in India, with the well-preserved Fort Dansborg (built 1620) serving as the governor's residence and administrative centre.

Danish Settlements

The Danish colonies in India included: Tranquebar (1620-1845 — the principal settlement, with its Fort Dansborg, Zion Church (the first Protestant church in India, 1701), and the New Jerusalem Church; the town of Tranquebar had a printed town plan and a well-organised colonial administration); Serampore (Frederiksnagore, in Bengal, 1755-1845 — the Danish acquired it from the Nawab of Bengal; it was the most prosperous Danish settlement with a thriving shipbuilding industry, weaving factories, and the famous Serampore Mission Press established by the Baptist missionaries William Carey, Joshua Marshman, and William Ward, who served Danish territory to avoid British restrictions on missionary work — Carey translated the Bible into Bengali, Sanskrit, and 29 other languages, published Bengali magazines, and established Serampore College for training Indian pastors); Nicobar Islands (1756-1848 — the Danish attempted to colonise the Nicobar Islands and established a settlement at Nancowry, but the colony was repeatedly abandoned due to disease; the Danish sold their rights to the British in 1868); and a brief presence at Balasore (Odisha) and Calicut (Kerala). The Danish also had a single ship's factory at Gosaba (Sunderbans) and a settlement at Portonovo (Parangipettai, Tamil Nadu).

Trade and Activities

The Danish East India Company was never a major trading power. It struggled with limited capital (the King of Denmark was personally involved in its financing), a small fleet (never more than a dozen ships per year), and stiff competition from the British and Dutch. Danish exports from India included cotton textiles, pepper, cinnamon, indigo, sugar, saltpetre, and (controversially) slaves (the Danish were involved in the slave trade from the Nicobar Islands and the African coast). The Danish Company was dissolved and re-founded several times: the first company (1616-1639), second (1670-1729), and third (1732-1772). By the late 18th century, the Danish settlements were largely dependent on British goodwill for survival (the British did not invade them because they were neutral and useful for trade). Denmark's alliance with France during the Napoleonic Wars led the British to attack Danish shipping, and the British occupied Serampore (1801-1802, 1808-1815). After the Napoleonic Wars, the Danish settlements were economically unviable. In 1845, the Danish ceded all their Indian territories to the British for a sum of £20,000 (Tranquebar, Serampore, and all other settlements were formally transferred to the British East India Company). The Danish legacy in India is disproportionately large for such a small presence: the Tranquebar Mission (the first Protestant mission in India, established by the German Lutherans Bartholomäus Ziegenbalg and Heinrich Plütschau in 1706 under Danish patronage) pioneered the printing of Tamil and other Indian languages; the Serampore Mission Press printed the first Bengali newspaper (the Samachar Darpan, 1818); and Serampore College (1817) was the first modern degree-granting institution in Asia. The Tranquebar fort (Fort Dansborg) is now a museum managed by the Tamil Nadu Archaeological Department.

The Carnatic Wars (1746-1763)

The Carnatic Wars were a series of three military conflicts between the British and the French East India Companies in the Carnatic region (the coastal districts of present-day Tamil Nadu and Andhra Pradesh) in the mid-18th century. These wars established British supremacy over the French in India and demonstrated the decisive role of European military technology (especially disciplined infantry, flintlock muskets, bayonets, and field artillery) in Indian politics. The wars were a direct extension of the Anglo-French rivalry in Europe and America (the War of Austrian Succession, 1740-1748; and the Seven Years' War, 1756-1763).

WarEuropean ContextKey Events in IndiaOutcome
1st Carnatic War
(1746-1748)		War of Austrian Succession (1740-1748) — France vs. Britain in Europe and America (King George's War)Naval battle off Negapatam (1746) between the French fleet under La Bourdonnais and the British fleet under Peyton; French capture of Madras (September 1746) — La Bourdonnais captured Madras and agreed to ransom it back to the British for a sum; however, Dupleix overruled him and refused to return it. The French attacked the British fort at Fort St. David (near Cuddalore) but were repulsed with the help of the Nawab of Carnatic, Anwar-ud-Din (who was alarmed at French aggression in his territory). The Nawab's army of 10,000 was routed by the French at the Battle of the Adyar River (October 1746) — 300 French soldiers defeated 10,000 Indian troops, demonstrating the military superiority of European-trained infantry.Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle (1748) returned Madras to the British in exchange for Louisbourg (in Canada, captured by the British). The war established no clear supremacy but showed the effectiveness of European methods.
2nd Carnatic War
(1749-1754)		No major European war (peace between France and Britain from 1748 to 1756)A proxy war in India through succession disputes: in Hyderabad, Muzaffar Jang (supported by Dupleix) vs. Nasir Jang (supported by the British); in Carnatic, Chanda Sahib (supported by Dupleix) vs. Anwar-ud-Din (supported by the British). Muzaffar Jang became Nizam of Hyderabad (1751) after Nasir Jang was assassinated. Anwar-ud-Din was killed at the Battle of Ambur (1749) fighting Chanda Sahib. Chanda Sahib became Nawab of Carnatic. The British rallied around Muhammad Ali Khan Walajah, the son of Anwar-ud-Din, who took refuge at Trichinopoly. Robert Clive (a young EIC writer/ clerk turned military commander) captured the fort of Arcot (the capital of the Carnatic) in August 1751 with 200 British soldiers and 300 sepoys, as a diversion to relieve the siege of Trichinopoly. Clive defended Arcot for 53 days against a 10,000-strong Franco-Indian force (the siege of Arcot). Clive's victory at Arcot was one of the most famous military feats in British Indian history.The Treaty of Pondicherry (1754) confirmed Muhammad Ali as Nawab of Carnatic. Dupleix was recalled to France in disgrace (1754). The French Company was bankrupted by the war. British prestige in India was enormously enhanced.
3rd Carnatic War
(1758-1763)		Seven Years' War (1756-1763) — global war between France and Britain, fought in Europe, America, India, and AfricaThe French sent a military expedition under the Comte de Lally, who arrived in 1758, to recapture French influence. Lally captured Fort St. David (1758), but failed to capture Madras (the siege of Madras, 1758-59, was relieved by a British fleet). The decisive battle was the Battle of Wandiwash (22 January 1760) — Sir Eyre Coote (British) decisively defeated Lally (French) in a pitched battle. Lally withdrew to Pondicherry, which was besieged by the British for eight months (1760-61). Pondicherry surrendered in January 1761, and the British razed the city (it was rebuilt later).The Treaty of Paris (1763) ended the Seven Years' War. The French were forced to recognise the British as the paramount European power in India. The French were restricted to their five settlements (Pondicherry, Chandernagore, Mahe, Yanam, Karaikal) without any military presence or fortifications. French imperial ambitions in India were permanently ended.

Significance of the Carnatic Wars

The Carnatic Wars established the military supremacy of the British over the French in India and set the stage for British political supremacy over Indian rulers. The wars demonstrated: (1) the decisive superiority of European military technology (disciplined infantry using flintlock muskets and bayonets, supported by field artillery); (2) the effectiveness of the sepoy system (Indian soldiers trained and commanded by European officers); (3) the importance of naval power (the British Royal Navy's control of the Indian Ocean ensured that French reinforcements from Europe were delayed or blocked, while British reinforcements arrived freely); (4) the willingness of Indian princes to ally with European powers in their own succession disputes (which gave the Europeans a foothold in Indian politics); and (5) the high cost of European-style warfare for Indian states (the Nawab of Arcot's debts to the British Company made him a puppet of his creditors). The wars transformed the EIC from a trading company into the most powerful military force in the Indian subcontinent.

Impact of European Arrival on India

The arrival of European trading companies had a profound and lasting impact on India's economy, society, culture, and politics:

  • Economic Impact: The Portuguese, Dutch, English, and French injected significant amounts of silver and gold into the Indian economy through their purchases of Indian goods (spices, textiles, etc.), creating a trade surplus for India. However, the nature of trade shifted from overland caravan routes to maritime routes, leading to the decline of inland trading centres (such as Delhi, Agra, Lahore) and the rise of coastal port cities (Bombay, Madras, Calcutta, Surat, Pondicherry). The European companies brought Indian goods to European markets on an unprecedented scale, integrating India into the emerging global economy. However, by the late 18th century, the British EIC's policies began to de-industrialise India — the export of Indian textiles was actively discouraged, the Indian artisan class was destroyed, and the 'drain of wealth' (revenues collected in India but remitted to Britain) began under the Company's rule.
  • Social and Cultural Impact: The Europeans introduced Christianity through missionary work (the Portuguese Inquisition, the Protestant Tranquebar Mission), leading to the growth of Christian communities in Goa, the Fishery Coast (Tamil Nadu), Kerala, Bengal, and the Punjab. The missionaries also pioneered education, printing, and mass communication in Indian languages (Ziegenbalg's Tamil printing press, Carey's Bengali press). The Portuguese introduced European plants to India (chillies, potatoes, tomatoes, tobacco, maize, cashew nuts, papayas, pineapples, and guavas from the Americas), which transformed Indian cuisine and agriculture.
  • Military Impact: The Europeans introduced the technology of the flintlock musket and bayonet, the European system of drill and discipline (foot drill, linear tactics, volley fire), and an organised military hierarchy (the sepoy system, with European officers commanding Indian troops). Indian rulers, from the Mughals (who employed European gunners and engineers) to the Marathas (who built a navy after European models), the Sikhs (who employed European mercenaries to train the Khalsa Army), and the Mysore Sultanate (Tipu Sultan's modernisation of his army), all adopted European military methods, leading to a military revolution in 18th-century India.
  • Political Impact: The European trading companies introduced the concept of extraterritoriality and diplomatic immunity (the right of European nations to be governed by their own laws in Indian territory). The European presence fragmented the Indian political landscape — Indian princes allied with rival European companies (the Nizam allied with the French, the Marathas with both French and British, the Nawab of Arcot with the British) in complex patronage networks. This external intervention became the decisive factor in Indian politics by the mid-18th century.
  • Technological Impact: The Europeans introduced printing presses, modern shipbuilding techniques (including copper-bottoming of ships, which reduced marine growth and increased speed), modern cartography (the first accurate maps of India), and improved methods of fortification (the bastion fort system, as seen in Fort St. George, Fort William, and Fort Dansborg). The Portuguese also introduced the first modern hospitals in India (the Royal Hospital in Goa, 1511, and the Hospital of the Misericordia). The British introduced the first modern postal system, the first modern banking (the Bank of Bengal, 1809), and the first newspapers (the Bengal Gazette, 1780).
  TIMELINE OF EUROPEAN ARRIVAL IN INDIA
  
  1498   Vasco da Gama lands at Calicut
  1505   Portuguese Viceroyalty established (Almeida)
  1510   Capture of Goa (Albuquerque)
  1600   English East India Company founded
  1602   Dutch VOC founded
  1613   English factory at Surat
  1620   Danish factory at Tranquebar
  1664   French East India Company founded
  1673   French purchase Pondicherry
  1746   First Carnatic War begins

  1765   Diwani of Bengal granted to EIC
  → By 1765, British control Bengal, Bihar, Odisha
  → By 1818, British control all of India

16. The Revolt of 1857

The Revolt of 1857 � called the First War of Independence by V. D. Savarkar, the Sepoy Mutiny by British historians, and the Great Rebellion by modern Indian historiography � was the largest, most widespread, and most serious armed challenge to British colonial rule in India before the 20th-century national movement. It began as a mutiny of Indian soldiers (sepoys) in the British East India Company's Bengal Army at Meerut on 10 May 1857 and rapidly escalated into a series of civilian uprisings across northern and central India. The revolt drew in a coalition of disaffected princes, dispossessed zamindars, peasants, artisans, and religious leaders, and was suppressed only after more than a year of intense fighting, ending formally with the fall of Gwalior on 20 June 1858. The revolt prompted the British Parliament to pass the Government of India Act 1858, which abolished the East India Company and transferred authority directly to the British Crown � inaugurating the British Raj, a period of direct imperial rule that lasted until 1947. The rebellion remains the defining event in 19th-century Indian history and the subject of enduring historiographical controversy.

Background: India under Company Rule (1765�1856)

By the mid-19th century, the East India Company had transformed from a trading corporation into the paramount military and administrative power in India. Through a combination of outright conquest, subsidiary alliances, and the Doctrine of Lapse, the Company had absorbed most of the subcontinent by 1856. The Punjab (annexed 1849 after the Second Anglo-Sikh War), Pegu (Lower Burma) (1852), Jhansi (1853), Nagpur (1854), and Awadh (1856) were among the most recent acquisitions. The Company's administration was increasingly intrusive, extractive, and culturally insensitive. A series of Anglo-Afghan Wars (1839�42), Anglo-Sikh Wars (1845�46, 1848�49), and the Second Anglo-Burmese War (1852) had drained the treasury and placed a heavy tax burden on the peasantry. The Company's land-revenue settlements � the Permanent Settlement in Bengal (1793), the Ryotwari system in Madras and Bombay (1820s), and the Mahalwari system in the North-Western Provinces (1833) � had impoverished the peasantry and dispossessed many traditional zamindars. The introduction of Western education (English Education Act 1835), Christian missionary activity (Charter Act 1813), and social-reform legislation (abolition of sati 1829, Hindu Widows' Remarriage Act 1856) was perceived by conservative Hindus and Muslims as a deliberate assault on indigenous religious and social structures. The annexation of Awadh in February 1856 � on the pretext of 'misgovernment' � was the final straw: it deprived the Nawab of his throne, threw 60,000 soldiers and retainers out of employment, and deeply alienated the substantial body of sepoys who came from Awadh's Brahmin and Rajput communities.

Causes of the Revolt

Political Causes

  • Doctrine of Lapse: Formulated by Lord Dalhousie (Governor-General 1848�1856), the Doctrine of Lapse allowed the Company to annex any princely state whose ruler died without a natural male heir. States annexed under this policy included Satara (1848), Sambalpur (1849), Jhansi (1853), Nagpur (1854), and Udaipur (1852, though later restored). The Doctrine was widely seen as a betrayal of longstanding treaty obligations and a thinly disguised land-grab. The most egregious case was Jhansi, where the Rani's adopted son was denied recognition as the rightful heir.
  • Annexation of Awadh: The largest and most controversial annexation. The Company claimed that the Nawab, Wajid Ali Shah, had misgoverned his kingdom. Awadh was annexed on 7 February 1856. The Nawab was exiled to Calcutta, and the kingdom's court, army, and administration were dissolved. Thousands of sepoys recruited from Awadh's landed classes felt personally betrayed � their families' estates were threatened by the new revenue assessments, and their ruler had been deposed.
  • Subsidiary Alliance and loss of sovereignty: Even states that were not formally annexed had been reduced to vassalage through the Subsidiary Alliance system (introduced by Lord Wellesley, 1798�1805). Indian rulers could no longer maintain their own armies, conduct independent foreign policy, or administer justice in cases involving Europeans. The Mughal emperor Bahadur Shah Zafar, though still nominally the sovereign, had been told that his dynasty would end with him � the British planned to discontinue the Mughal title after his death.
  • Displacement of the traditional elite: The British land-revenue settlements and administrative reforms had dispossessed a vast number of zamindars, talukdars, and chieftains. In the North-Western Provinces and Awadh, the 'summary settlement' of 1855�56 transferred land from the traditional talukdars to the cultivating ryots, creating a powerful class of aggrieved landowners.

Economic Causes

  • Heavy land revenue demand: Under all three revenue systems (Permanent Settlement, Ryotwari, Mahalwari), the state's share of the produce was set at 50�66% of the rental value, leaving peasants with barely enough for subsistence. The revenue was rigidly collected � failure to pay led to forfeiture of land. In the North-Western Provinces, revenue demand increased by 30�50% after the 1833 settlement.
  • Destruction of traditional industries: British free-trade policies (Charter Act 1813, which ended the Company's monopoly on Indian trade, and the 1830s removal of protective tariffs) flooded India with machine-made textiles from Lancashire. India's once-great textile industry � Bengal muslin, Dacca cotton, Madras chintz � was destroyed. Artisans, weavers, and spinners were thrown out of work in millions. By 1850, the population of Dacca (once the 'Manchester of India') had declined from 200,000 to 40,000.
  • Drain of Wealth: Dadabhai Naoroji later theorised that India's wealth was systematically drained to Britain through 'home charges' � salaries and pensions of British officials in India, interest on the Company's debt, dividends to shareholders, and purchases of British goods and services. This drain, estimated at £3�5 million annually by the 1850s, deprived India of capital that could have been used for internal development.
  • Disruption of traditional credit and commerce: The Company's legal system favoured British merchants and Indian moneylenders over traditional bankers and traders. The introduction of the 'Lex Loci' Act (1845) and the frequent auctioning of defaulted estates enriched the new commercial classes (banias, moneylenders, and speculators) at the expense of the old landed aristocracy.

Social and Religious Causes

  • Christian missionary activity and fear of forced conversion: The Charter Act of 1813 permitted Christian missionaries to enter India freely. The Company built churches, supported missionary schools, and displayed open favouritism toward converts. The Religious Disabilities Act (1850) allowed Christian converts to inherit ancestral property, which many Hindus saw as an incentive to convert. Rumours spread that the British planned to forcibly convert all Indians � the greased cartridge was interpreted as a deliberate conspiracy to destroy both Hindu and Muslim religious identities.
