  • Social reform legislation as cultural interference: The abolition of sati (1829, Lord William Bentinck), the legalisation of widow remarriage (1856, Hindu Widows' Remarriage Act, drafted by Lord Dalhousie and backed by Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar), and the suppression of female infanticide and human sacrifice (e.g., the Meriah sacrifices of the Khonds in Odisha) were seen not as progressive measures but as unwarranted interference in age-old Hindu customs. The British were also accused of undermining the caste system by requiring all government employees, regardless of caste, to perform the same duties.
  • Educational and administrative reforms: Macaulay's Minute on Education (1835) made English the medium of instruction in all higher education, displacing Persian and Sanskrit. The traditional Indian educational system (pathshalas, madrasas, tols) was marginalised. The Lex Loci Act of 1845 (also called the Religious Disabilities Act) and the Caste Disabilities Removal Act 1850 removed legal bars on conversion, further inflaming conservative opinion.
  • Propaganda and rumour: Throughout 1856 and early 1857, an elaborate network of chapatis (small unleavened bread) was circulated from village to village across northern India � a traditional method of spreading news of impending danger. There were prophecies that the Company's rule would end in 1857 (the centenary of the Battle of Plassey), and stories circulated that the British had mixed ground bone into the flour and the atta (wheat flour) supplied to sepoys.

Military Causes

  • Discrimination against Indian sepoys: Indian soldiers were paid less than their British counterparts (a sepoy received Rs 7 per month, a British private Rs 23). Promotion was blocked at the rank of subedar (the highest native rank); the highest commissioned officer positions were reserved for Europeans. Sepoys were subject to harsher discipline and could be flogged more severely than British soldiers.
  • General Service Enlistment Act (1856): This Act required all new recruits to the Bengal Army to accept the obligation of service overseas if ordered. High-caste Hindus (Brahmins and Rajputs), who considered crossing the 'kala pani' (the black ocean) a violation of caste purity, were outraged. Though the Act applied only to new recruits, it symbolised the British disregard for Indian religious sensitivities.
  • Imbalance in the Bengal Army: The Bengal Army, the largest of the three Presidency armies, had approximately 86,000 Indian soldiers to only 27,000 British soldiers (a ratio of roughly 3:1). The army was dominated by high-caste Brahmins and Rajputs from Awadh and Bihar � the same communities most affected by the annexation of Awadh and the new land settlements. This social homogeneity made collective action easier.
  • Loss of allowances and privileges: The British had abolished the batta (extra pay for field service) in 1833 and imposed new service conditions that sepoys found onerous. The abolition of treaty allowances and the introduction of new pension regulations in the 1840s reduced the economic attractiveness of military service.

Immediate Cause and the Outbreak

The Greased Cartridge Incident

In early 1857, the British introduced the Pattern 1853 Enfield rifle to replace the older Brown Bess musket. The new rifle used a paper cartridge that held both gunpowder and the bullet; the soldier had to bite open the cartridge, pour the powder into the barrel, and ram the bullet down. British ordnance depots at Dum Dum (near Calcutta) began manufacturing cartridges that were greased with animal fat to keep them waterproof. In January 1857, a low-caste worker at Dum Dum asked a high-caste sepoy for a drink of water. The sepoy refused, saying the worker's touch would pollute him. The worker retorted, 'You will soon lose your caste anyway, for the new cartridges are greased with cow and pig fat, and you will have to put them in your mouth.' The story spread like wildfire through the cantonments of the Bengal Army.

The British military authorities, realising the danger, issued orders that sepoys could grease their own cartridges with clarified butter (ghee) or use a new paper-tearing method that did not require biting. But the damage was done � the rumour had already reinforced the widespread suspicion that the British intended to destroy both Hindu and Muslim religious identities.

Mangal Pandey and the Barrackpore Incident

On 29 March 1857, at the Barrackpore garrison near Calcutta, a sepoy of the 34th Native Infantry named Mangal Pandey (born 1827, in a Bhumihar Brahmin family from Ballia district) went berserk while on parade. He shouted at his comrades to rise up, attacked his British sergeant-major, and then turned his musket on the adjutant, Lieutenant Baugh. Baugh was thrown from his horse but not killed. Pandey then attempted to shoot himself but was overpowered. He was court-martialled and hanged on 8 April 1857. The entire 34th Native Infantry was disbanded as a punishment. Pandey became a martyr and a symbol of resistance � his name is now a byword for rebellion in Indian popular culture.

The Meerut Outbreak (10 May 1857)

On 24 April 1857, at the Meerut cantonment (60 km from Delhi), 90 soldiers of the 3rd Bengal Light Cavalry refused to use the greased cartridges during a parade. They were court-martialled on 9 May and sentenced to 10 years' hard labour in chains. On the evening of 10 May � a Sunday � the remaining sepoys of the Meerut garrison rose up. They released their imprisoned comrades, shot their British officers, and set fire to the cantonment. The Meerut outbreak was spontaneous and unorganised, but it had a clear target: the crowd attacked the symbols of British authority � the officers, the jail, the treasury, and the telegraph office. After sunset, the sepoys marched the 60 km to Delhi, arriving early on 11 May 1857.

Spread and Course of the Revolt

Delhi: The Symbolic Heart of the Revolt

The Meerut sepoys reached Delhi on the morning of 11 May. They were joined by the Delhi garrison (the 38th, 54th, and 74th Native Infantry) and attacked the Red Fort, the seat of the Mughal emperor. The British officers and civilians in Delhi were slaughtered. The sepoys proclaimed the octogenarian Bahadur Shah Zafar (born 1775) as the Emperor of all India. Bahadur Shah, initially reluctant, was forced to accept the leadership of the revolt. He issued a proclamation calling on all Indians � Hindus and Muslims alike � to unite against the British. The proclamation promised land rights, religious freedom, and the restoration of the old order.

In Delhi, a Court of Administration was set up, with Bahadur Shah's eldest son, Mirza Mughal, as the commander-in-chief. However, the court was divided by factional infighting. The military leadership of the Delhi rebels was provided by Bakht Khan, a Rohilla artillery officer from Bareilly who arrived in Delhi in July 1857 with 4,000 troops. Bakht Khan became the de facto military commander, but his authority was constantly challenged by the older noblemen around the emperor. Delhi's defence was hampered by a lack of coordination, shortage of supplies, and the absence of a unified command.

The British, after the initial shock, moved swiftly to recapture Delhi. A British force under Major-General Henry Barnard occupied the Ridge (a high ground north of Delhi) on 8 June 1857. For three months, the British besieged Delhi � a siege marked by intense fighting, constant sorties by the rebels, and severe hardship on both sides. The British received reinforcements including the Corps of Guides (under Henry Daly), Gurkha regiments, and Sikh troops from the Punjab. On 14 September 1857, the British launched a final assault, breaching the city walls at the Kashmere Gate (where the British sappers � including the young John Nicholson � distinguished themselves). After six days of street-to-street fighting, Delhi fell on 20 September 1857. The British carried out savage reprisals: the Mughal princes were executed in cold blood (shot at the Delhi Gate), the city was looted, and thousands of civilians were killed. Bahadur Shah was captured at the tomb of Humayun, tried for treason and rebellion, and exiled to Rangoon, where he died in 1862 at the age of 87. The British demolished large parts of Delhi, including the Jama Masjid area, to clear fields of fire for the artillery.

Kanpur (Cawnpore)

At Kanpur, the rebellion was led by Nana Saheb (born 1824), the adopted son of the last Maratha Peshwa, Baji Rao II. The British had refused to continue the Peshwa's pension after Baji Rao's death (the Company had annexed the Peshwa's domains in 1818 but had promised a pension of £80,000 per year; after Baji Rao's death in 1851, the Company discontinued it, and Nana Saheb's legal challenge failed). On 5 June 1857, the Indian troops at Kanpur revolted. They surrounded the British entrenchment � a hastily fortified collection of barracks where about 900 Europeans and 300 Indian loyalists had taken refuge. The siege lasted 21 days (6�27 June 1857), with the British garrison suffering heavy casualties from heat, disease, and rebel fire. On 25 June, General Sir Hugh Wheeler, the British commander, agreed to a ceasefire proposed by Nana Saheb, who offered safe passage to the British and their families.

The British soldiers and civilians � men, women, and children � were taken in boats from the river ghat (the Satichaura Ghat) on 27 June. As the boats pushed off, rebel sepoys opened fire from the banks. A massacre ensued � nearly all the men were killed (the exact number is disputed; historians estimate 300�400 deaths). The surviving women and children (about 200) were taken back to Kanpur and held captive. In July, as a British relief force under Major General Henry Havelock approached Kanpur, Nana Saheb ordered the execution of the prisoners. The women and children were hacked to death with axes (the Bibighar massacre, 15 July 1857). Havelock's force entered Kanpur on 17 July to find the massacre site. The British were incensed, and the Kanpur massacre became the defining atrocity of the revolt in the British imagination, used to justify brutal reprisals.

Nana Saheb proclaimed himself Peshwa and tried to establish a Maratha-led government, but he was forced to retreat as British reinforcements continued to arrive. He fled to Nepal in 1858 and was never captured � his ultimate fate remains a mystery.

Lucknow: The Siege of the Residency

In Lucknow, the capital of recently annexed Awadh, the rebellion was led by Begum Hazrat Mahal (born c. 1820), the wife of the deposed Nawab Wajid Ali Shah. The Begum took charge of the administration, appointing her 11-year-old son, Birjis Qadr, as the Nawab. The rebellion in Awadh had massive popular support � it was the only region where the revolt became a genuine mass uprising.

On 4 July 1857, the Indian troops in Lucknow revolted and besieged the British Residency, where about 2,000 British soldiers, civilians, and loyal Indian sepoys had taken shelter. The defender was Sir Henry Lawrence, the Chief Commissioner of Awadh, who died of wounds on 4 July (his last words: 'Let there be no petty retaliation � no humbug'). The siege of the Residency lasted an extraordinary 87 days (1 July � 25 September 1857). The British garrison held out under constant fire, enduring disease, starvation, and bombardment.

A relief force under General Havelock and Sir James Outram fought its way into Lucknow on 25 September but was too weak to evacuate the garrison. The defenders were now reinforced but still besieged. A second, larger relief force under the new Commander-in-Chief, Sir Colin Campbell, reached Lucknow in November 1857. On 17 November, Campbell evacuated the Residency � with the women and children leaving first � and withdrew from Lucknow. Begum Hazrat Mahal held out in the city until March 1858, when Campbell returned with a massive force and recaptured Lucknow. The Begum fled to Nepal, where she died in 1879.

Jhansi and Rani Lakshmibai

The Rani of Jhansi � born Manikarnika Tambe (1828), popularly known as Lakshmibai � was one of the most iconic figures of the revolt. Her husband, Raja Gangadhar Rao, died in 1853, and their adopted son, Damodar Rao, was denied recognition by the British under the Doctrine of Lapse. Jhansi was annexed by Lord Dalhousie. The Rani was granted a pension of Rs 60,000 per year and ordered to leave the fort.

In June 1857, the Indian troops at Jhansi mutinied and massacred the British officers. The Rani initially maintained an ambiguous position � she assured the British that she was protecting their interests, but she also took command of the fort and organised the defence of the city. In March 1858, the British sent a force under Sir Hugh Rose to capture Jhansi. The Rani defended the fort for two weeks (21 March � 4 April 1858). She personally led sorties and fought alongside her soldiers. The walls were breached on 4 April, and the Rani escaped on horseback at night, her adopted son strapped to her back � the legendary leap from the fort (a popular embellishment, but the escape itself is historical fact). She joined Tantia Tope at Kalpi and then moved to Gwalior.

On 17 June 1858, in the battle at Kotah-ki-Sarai near Gwalior, the Rani was killed in action. She was dressed as a cavalry soldier and died on the battlefield. Sir Hugh Rose described her as 'the best and bravest of the rebel leaders.' Her courage became a symbol of Indian resistance and inspired the future generation of freedom fighters.

Tantia Tope and the Guerrilla Phase

Tantia Tope (born 1814, real name Ramachandra Panduranga Tope, of a Maratha Brahmin family) was the most capable military leader of the revolt. A close associate of Nana Saheb, he led the rebel forces at Kanpur, fought at the Second Battle of Kanpur, and escaped to central India. In 1858, he captured the fort of Gwalior (1 June 1858) with the help of Rani Lakshmibai and Nana Saheb's nephew, Rao Saheb. But Sir Hugh Rose recaptured Gwalior on 20 June 1858. Tantia Tope escaped and waged a brilliant guerrilla campaign across the jungles of central India for ten months (June 1858 � April 1859), constantly evading British columns. He was betrayed by his trusted servant, Man Singh, captured at Paron (near Shivpuri) on 7 April 1859, and executed on 18 April 1859 at Sipri (now Shivpuri).

Other Centres of the Revolt

  • Bareilly: Khan Bahadur Khan, a Rohilla chieftain, led the revolt in Rohilkhand. He captured Bareilly in May 1857 and ruled the region independently until the British recaptured Bareilly in May 1858. He was executed in 1860.
  • Arrah (Bihar): Kunwar Singh (born 1777), the 80-year-old zamindar of Jagdispur, was the most important leader from eastern India. He led the revolt in Bihar, fought the famous Battle of Arrah, and conducted a brilliant campaign of guerrilla warfare. He died on 26 April 1858, after cutting off his own wounded arm to prevent it from being captured � a legendary act of defiance.
  • Central India: The Rani of Ramgarh, Raja of Narwar, and the Bhils and Kolis tribal communities rose up in various parts of central India. The Raja of Banpur and Raja of Shahgarh joined the rebellion.
  • Jagdishpur: After Kunwar Singh's death, his brother Amar Singh continued the struggle until November 1859.
  • Assam: Maniram Dewan and the Assamese nobles attempted an uprising (1857�58) but were quickly suppressed. Maniram was hanged in 1858.
  • Other notable leaders: Feroz Shah (a descendant of the Mughal dynasty, active in central India), Shahzada Muhammad Ibrahim (a claimant to the throne of Mysore), Nawab of Banda, Rana Beni Madho (a talukdar of Awadh), and Maulvi Liaquat Ali of Allahabad.

Regions That Did Not Join

  • Punjab: The Punjab remained largely loyal, thanks to the firm administration of Sir John Lawrence and the alliance with the Sikh aristocracy. The Sikhs, who had been defeated by the British in the Anglo-Sikh Wars (1845�46, 1848�49), saw an opportunity to gain British favour by remaining loyal. The British raised new regiments from the Punjab, and these Sikh and Punjabi Muslim troops played a crucial role in suppressing the revolt.
  • Bengal: The Bengal intelligentsia (the 'bhadralok'), the zamindars (who had been created by the Permanent Settlement and were loyal to the British), and the Bengali peasantry largely stayed out of the revolt. The educated Bengali middle class � including figures who would later lead the Indian National Congress � saw the revolt as a backward-looking feudal movement and did not participate.
  • Madras Presidency and Bombay Presidency: The Madras Army and the Bombay Army (separate from the Bengal Army) remained loyal. The princely states of Hyderabad (under the Nizam), Mysore, Baroda, Gwalior (under Sindhia, who remained loyal), and Indore (under Holkar) did not join the revolt. Their armies were used to suppress the rebellion.
  • Rajputana and Central India: The Rajput princes � the Maharaja of Jaipur, Maharaja of Jodhpur, Maharana of Udaipur � remained loyal to the British, as did the Nawab of Bhopal.

Suppression of the Revolt: British Military Campaign

The British suppression of the revolt was methodical, brutal, and effective. The key phases were:

  • Recapture of Delhi (September 1857): The fall of Delhi broke the back of the rebellion. The British force was reinforced by the Punjab Movable Column (under Neville Chamberlain, John Nicholson, and others), the Corps of Guides, and Sikh and Gurkha regiments. The assault on Delhi cost the British 1,100 casualties.
  • Relief of Lucknow (November 1857): Sir Colin Campbell's force of 5,000 relieved the Residency and evacuated its defenders. The second capture of Lucknow (March 1858) required 30,000 British troops and heavy artillery.
  • Central India Campaign (1858): Sir Hugh Rose's campaign through central India � capturing Jhansi (April 1858), Kalpi (May 1858), and Gwalior (June 1858) � was the most brilliant British operation of the war. Rose marched 1,000 miles in five months, fought over 30 engagements, and destroyed the rebel military capability in central India.
  • Guerrilla phase (June 1858 � April 1859): After the fall of Gwalior, the revolt entered a guerrilla phase. Tantia Tope, Kunwar Singh, and other leaders conducted hit-and-run operations in central India, Awadh, and Bihar. The British used mobile columns, systematic deforestation, and offers of amnesty (with the exception of those directly involved in the murder of British women and children) to suppress the last pockets of resistance.
  • British reprisals: The British response to the revolt was marked by extreme brutality. General Neill ordered the execution of thousands of Indians in Kanpur � captured rebels were forced to lick the blood of the victims before being hanged. In Delhi, the British executed all male members of the Mughal royal family and indiscriminately killed thousands of civilians. The British used the 'noose and the cannon' � rebels were blown from the mouths of cannons (a Mughal punishment, adopted by the British). Villages suspected of harbouring rebels were burned. The scale of British reprisals shocked even contemporary European observers (e.g., Benjamin Disraeli criticised the brutality in Parliament).

Causes of Failure of the Revolt

  • Lack of unified leadership and coordination: The revolt had no central command, no common strategy, and no unified political objective. Different leaders fought for different goals � Bahadur Shah wanted to restore the Mughal Empire, Nana Saheb wanted to revive the Maratha Confederacy, the Awadh talukdars wanted to restore the old landed order, the religious leaders wanted to defend Islam and Hinduism against Christian encroachment. There was no coordination between centres: Delhi, Kanpur, Lucknow, Jhansi, and Bareilly all fought their own separate campaigns.
  • Limited geographical spread: The revolt was confined to the Gangetic plain and central India. The south, the Deccan, Bengal, and the Bombay Presidency remained quiet. The British were therefore able to draw on the resources of the loyal presidencies and princely states to reinforce their armies.
  • Superior British resources and technology: The British had superior artillery (heavier guns, better ammunition), the telegraph (which allowed them to communicate faster than the rebels), the postal system, and a functioning logistical chain. The British controlled the sea routes and could bring in reinforcements from Britain, China (after the Second Opium War ended in 1860, British troops were diverted to India), and other imperial outposts.
  • Loyalty of key Indian allies: The revolt was defeated in large part because of the loyalty of Indian soldiers and rulers who did not join. The Sikh and Gurkha regiments, the armies of the Nizam of Hyderabad, the Patiala state, and other loyalist rulers provided the British with a reliable Indian military force. The British also recruited extensively from the 'martial races' � a policy that originated during the revolt and continued through the Raj.
  • Lack of mass support among the educated classes: The emerging Western-educated middle class � the future leaders of the Indian National Congress � did not support the revolt. They saw it as a reactionary, feudal uprising that wanted to turn the clock back to the pre-British era. Dadabhai Naoroji, the future president of the INC, described the revolt as 'a struggle for power between two sets of masters: the British and the native princes. The people had nothing to gain from either.' The merchants and moneylenders, who had profited from British legal and economic reforms, also remained loyal.
  • No positive program: The rebels had no vision for India's future. Their proclamations called for a return to the pre-British order � the Mughal Empire, the Maratha Peshwa, the old feudal hierarchy. This backward-looking program had no appeal for the emerging modern sectors of Indian society.
  • Communal divisions exploited by the British: Though the revolt witnessed remarkable Hindu-Muslim unity (the British were the common enemy), the British actively worked to divide the two communities. The British favoured the Sikhs over both Hindus and Muslims, and after the revolt, the 'divide and rule' policy was systematically applied.

Consequences of the Revolt

End of the East India Company and the Proclamation of the Crown

  • Government of India Act 1858: The most important constitutional consequence. The Act abolished the East India Company (which had ruled India for 101 years, 1757�1858) and transferred all authority to the British Crown. The Secretary of State for India (a Cabinet minister, originally called the President of the Board of Control) was given supreme authority over Indian affairs, assisted by a Council of India (an advisory body of 15 members). The Governor-General of India was now also given the title Viceroy (the personal representative of the Crown). Lord Canning, who had been Governor-General since 1856, became the first Viceroy (1858�1862).
  • The Queen's Proclamation (1 November 1858): Read out at a durbar in Allahabad by Lord Canning, the Proclamation was a landmark document that set out the new policy of the British Crown towards India. Its key provisions: (a) The Crown solemnly promised not to interfere in the religious beliefs and practices of Indians � a direct reversal of the reformist policies of the pre-1857 era. (b) The Doctrine of Lapse was formally abandoned � princes were guaranteed the right to adopt heirs, and no further annexations of princely states would occur. (c) Indians were promised equal opportunity in public service (an empty promise, as the ICS examination was held only in London and was virtually inaccessible to Indians). (d) Amnesty was granted to all rebels except those directly involved in the murder of British subjects. (e) The Crown declared its desire to promote the 'moral and material improvement' of India.

Military Reorganisation

  • Ratio of British to Indian troops: The proportion of British soldiers in India was increased from roughly 1:6 (pre-1857) to roughly 1:2. The British army in India was permanently fixed at 60,000�80,000 soldiers, paid for by Indian taxpayers.
  • Artillery monopoly: All artillery � the most effective weapon in 19th-century warfare � was placed exclusively in British hands. No Indian regiment was allowed to have its own artillery.
  • Diversification of recruitment: The British abandoned the policy of recruiting from the traditional 'martial' communities of Awadh and Bihar (Brahmins and Rajputs). Instead, they developed the 'martial races' theory, which held that Sikhs, Gurkhas, Pathans, and Punjabi Muslims were more 'loyal' and 'martial' than the high-caste Hindus of the Gangetic plain. The Punjab, Nepal, and the North-West Frontier became the primary recruiting grounds for the British Indian Army. The Bengal Army was reorganised to include a more diverse mix of castes, regions, and religions.
  • Regimental system reformed: The corps and regiments were reorganised to prevent the concentration of soldiers from a single region, caste, or community. The system of 'class regiments' (regiments composed entirely of one 'type' of soldier, e.g., a Sikh regiment, a Gurkha regiment) was later developed to create competing loyalties.

Administrative and Policy Changes

  • End of social reform: The British abandoned their earlier policy of social reform. The Religious Disabilities Act (1850) and the Hindu Widows' Remarriage Act (1856) were the last such acts for decades. The British decided that any further interference in Indian social customs was too dangerous. The 'civilising mission' of the early 19th century gave way to a policy of cautious conservatism and non-interference.
  • Policy towards princely states: The Doctrine of Lapse was abandoned. The British now treated the princes as their 'natural allies' � loyal, conservative, and useful props of British rule. The princes were given full autonomy in internal affairs (as long as they remained loyal), and the British guaranteed their titles and territories. The number of princely states was fixed at 562, covering about 40% of India's territory.
  • Policy towards zamindars and talukdars: The British confirmed the rights of the zamindars and talukdars who had remained loyal. In Awadh, the 'lyall settlement' (1860�65) restored the talukdars to their pre-annexation positions � the opposite of the 1856 settlement that had dispossessed them. The zamindars became the most reliable allies of the British in rural India.
  • Racial divide intensified: The revolt deepened the racial divide between Britons and Indians. The British regarded Indians with increased contempt, suspicion, and fear. Whites-only clubs, separate social spaces, and racial discrimination in the civil service and the military became stricter. The British also applied 'divide and rule' more systematically, favouring Muslims, Sikhs, and other minorities over the Hindu majority in a deliberate strategy of divide et impera.
  • Change in the status of the Viceroy: The Viceroy (formerly Governor-General) was now the personal representative of the British monarch and enjoyed greater prestige and ceremonial authority. The Viceroy's Council was expanded, and the Indian Civil Service (ICS) was established as the elite administrative corps � an all-powerful, all-white bureaucracy (the first Indian entered the ICS only in 1864 � Satyendranath Tagore, and even then under exceptional circumstances).

Economic Consequences

  • Cost of the revolt: The revolt cost the British government £36 million (equivalent to about £4 billion today). The entire cost was charged to India � the Indian debt increased from £68 million in 1857 to £108 million in 1860. Indian taxpayers paid for the suppression of their own rebellion.
  • Restructuring of government finances: The British reorganised India's financial administration. The Income Tax Act (1860) was introduced (a temporary measure after the revolt, made permanent later). The Paper Currency Act (1861) gave the government the sole right to issue currency. The Indian Penal Code (1860), drafted by Lord Macaulay and based on English common law, was enacted (it remains the basic criminal code of India, Pakistan, and Bangladesh today).
  • Beginning of modern public works: The British, realising the need for faster troop movements, accelerated railway construction. The railway network grew from 400 miles (1857) to 5,000 miles (1870). The telegraph network was also expanded. The Indian Railways Act (1858) and the Indian Telegraph Act (1859) laid the legal foundation. However, these developments were primarily for military and administrative control, not for Indian economic development.

Social and Cultural Consequences

  • Growth of communal identities: The British policy of divide and rule, the emphasis on 'martial races', the separate electorates (later introduced in 1909), and the official classification of Indians by religion sowed the seeds of modern communalism. The British deliberately promoted the idea that Hindus and Muslims were irreconcilably separate peoples � a view that would eventually lead to the partition of India in 1947.
  • Decline of the old aristocracy: The revolt eliminated the last remnants of the Mughal aristocracy and the Maratha nobility. The old feudal order, which had been the backbone of the rebellion, was destroyed. The vacuum was filled by the new Western-educated middle class, who rejected the feudal past and would lead the future national movement.
  • Missionary activity continues: Despite the Queen's Proclamation of religious non-interference, Christian missionary activity continued in India. However, the British government now distanced itself officially from missionary work.

Impact on the National Movement

  • Inspiration for the first generation of nationalists: The revolt was reinterpreted in the early 20th century as the 'First War of Independence'. V. D. Savarkar's book, The Indian War of Independence, 1857 (published in 1909, banned by the British), argued that the revolt was a national uprising, not a mutiny. This interpretation was adopted by the revolutionary movement (the Ghadar Party, the HSRA) and later by mainstream nationalists.
  • Lessons learned: The early Indian National Congress (founded 1885) deliberately avoided the military path of 1857 and chose constitutional agitation instead. However, the revolutionary wing of the freedom struggle (Bhagat Singh, Chandrasekhar Azad, the Bengal revolutionaries, the INA) drew direct inspiration from the heroes of 1857 � Mangal Pandey, Rani Lakshmibai, Tantia Tope, Kunwar Singh.
  • Symbols and memory: The revolt became a central theme in Indian nationalist art, literature, and popular culture. Bankim Chandra Chattopadhyay's novel Anandamath (1882), which included the song 'Vande Mataram', was set in the background of the Sannyasi Rebellion (an earlier 18th-century uprising) but was inspired by the spirit of 1857. The INA's battle cry 'Delhi Chalo' and the slogan 'Jai Hind' (both popularised by Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose) were echoes of the 1857 rebels' march on Delhi.

Historiography: Interpretations of the Revolt

Historiographical Debate: Was 1857 a National War of Independence?
The interpretation of the 1857 revolt has shifted dramatically across time and political contexts. British imperial historiography (e.g., Sir John Kaye's History of the Sepoy War in India, 1864�76; Sir George Trevelyan's Cawnpore, 1865) portrayed the revolt as a barbaric mutiny by ungrateful sepoys, driven by religious fanaticism and feudal reaction. The British historians emphasised the atrocities committed by the rebels (particularly the Kanpur massacre) and presented British reprisals as necessary and justified acts of justice. This view dominated Western scholarship until the mid-20th century.

Indian nationalist historiography (V. D. Savarkar, The Indian War of Independence, 1909; R. C. Majumdar, The Sepoy Mutiny and the Revolt of 1857, 1957) argued that the revolt was a national uprising � the first coordinated attempt by Indians to overthrow British rule. Savarkar, writing from exile in London, portrayed the revolt as a unified struggle of Hindus and Muslims, princes and peasants, organised under the leadership of Bahadur Shah Zafar. Modern Indian nationalist historians, however, have been more critical � R. C. Majumdar argued that the revolt was not a 'national war of independence' because it lacked modern nationalist consciousness and mass participation from all regions.

Modern academic historiography (Eric Stokes, The Peasant and the Raj, 1978; S. B. Chaudhuri, Theories of the Indian Mutiny, 1965; C. A. Bayly, Indian Society and the Making of the British Empire, 1988) has moved beyond the nationalism-versus-mutiny debate. Stokes, through detailed local studies of village records in Uttar Pradesh, showed that the revolt was a complex, multi-layered phenomenon driven by a combination of economic grievances (excessive land revenue, dispossession of talukdars), social tensions (the rise of a new class of moneylenders and banias who benefited from British legal reforms), and cultural anxieties (fear of forced conversion, pollution of caste). Modern historians emphasise the fragmented, localised nature of the revolt � it was not a single rebellion but a series of interconnected uprisings, each with its own causes, leadership, and dynamics.

Marxist historiography (I. Habib, K. N. Panikkar) interpreted the revolt as a patriotic struggle of the Indian people against colonial exploitation, led by the traditional elites but driven by mass agrarian discontent. For Marx himself (who wrote extensively on the revolt for the New York Tribune), the revolt was a justified anti-colonial uprising, though he was critical of its feudal leadership and lack of a progressive programme.

Subaltern historiography (Ranajit Guha, Gautam Bhadra, and the Subaltern Studies collective, 1980s onwards) shifted the focus away from elite leaders (princes, zamindars, middle-class intellectuals) and towards the ordinary participants � peasants, soldiers, artisans, and tribals. Subaltern historians argue that the revolt was a 'popular' uprising in which the masses acted on their own, independently of the elite leaders, driven by their own specific grievances, worldviews, and political consciousness. The revolt, in this view, reveals the autonomous political domain of the peasantry � a domain that the elite nationalist historiography had ignored.

In summary, the consensus among contemporary historians is that the 1857 revolt was neither a simple mutiny nor a fully formed national war of independence. It was a series of massive, interconnected popular uprisings � the first large-scale, if ultimately unsuccessful, armed challenge to British colonial rule in India. Its legacy is contested but undeniable: it marked the end of the East India Company, it reshaped British colonial policy, and it provided a storehouse of symbols, heroes, and martyrs for the Indian national movement.

Legacy of the Revolt

  • The last stand of the old order: The revolt was the final attempt of the pre-colonial Indian elite � the Mughals, the Marathas, the Muslim nawabs, the Rajput and Bhumihar zamindars � to resist the imposition of British rule. Its defeat marked the definitive end of the medieval political order in India and the full establishment of colonial modernity.
  • Birth of the British Raj: The revolt led to the abolition of Company rule and the establishment of the British Raj (1858�1947). The Raj was a more conservative, cautious, and authoritarian regime than the Company had been. It was also more efficient and more systematically exploitative � the foundations of the modern colonial state were laid in the post-1857 period.
  • Symbol of national unity: Despite its failure, the revolt demonstrated that Hindus and Muslims could unite against a common enemy � the greased cartridge had offended both communities equally, and both had fought side by side. This memory of Hindu-Muslim unity (Bahadur Shah's proclamations explicitly called for Hindu-Muslim brotherhood) was invoked by the Indian National Congress in the 20th century as evidence of India's composite nationalism.
  • Global significance: The 1857 revolt was part of a larger global wave of anti-colonial and popular uprisings in the mid-19th century � the Taiping Rebellion in China (1850�64), the Indian Rebellion of 1857, the Morant Bay Rebellion in Jamaica (1865), and the Polish January Uprising (1863�64). The British response to 1857 influenced colonial policy throughout the British Empire.
  • Memory and commemoration: In independent India, the revolt is commemorated as the beginning of the struggle for freedom. The Indian Museum in Kolkata, the National Museum in New Delhi, and the 1857 Memorial Museum at the Red Fort preserve artefacts of the revolt. Mangal Pandey's image appeared on Indian postage stamps (1984). The Rani Lakshmibai is a central figure in Indian iconography � the Western Uttar Pradesh university is named after her, and her statue adorns many public squares. The revolt is a set text in Indian history curricula at every level of education.

17. Indian Freedom Struggle (1885-1947)

The Indian Freedom Struggle was the world's largest anti-colonial mass movement, distinguished by its unique combination of constitutional agitation, mass civil disobedience, and revolutionary armed struggle. Spanning six decades from the founding of the Indian National Congress (1885) to independence (1947), the movement involved every section of Indian society and produced some of the most influential political leaders and thinkers of the modern world. The struggle unfolded in four overlapping phases: the Moderate phase (1885-1905), the Swadeshi and Revolutionary phase (1905-1915), the Gandhian phase (1915-1935), and the Final phase (1935-1947). The movement ultimately achieved independence on 15 August 1947, but at the cost of the partition of India into India and Pakistan — the largest forced migration in human history, with approximately 15 million people displaced and up to 1 million dead in communal violence.

Phase 1: The Moderate Era (1885-1905)

Foundation of the Indian National Congress

The Indian National Congress (INC) was founded in December 1885 at Bombay's Gokuldas Tejpal Sanskrit College, with 72 delegates from across India. The initiative came from A. O. Hume, a retired British civil servant who believed that a合法 political organisation would serve as a 'safety valve' for growing Indian discontent after the Ilbert Bill controversy (1883-84) and the rise of radical nationalism. The first president was W. C. Bonnerjee, a Calcutta barrister. The founding session was attended by representatives from all major regions and communities, including Hindus, Muslims, Parsis, Christians, and Jains. Important pre-Congress organisations that paved the way included: the Landholders' Society (1837, Calcutta), the Madras Native Association (1852), the Bombay Association (1852), the East India Association (1866, London, founded by Dadabhai Naoroji), the Indian Association (1876, Calcutta, founded by Surendranath Banerjee — the first all-India political organisation before the Congress), the Poona Sarvajanik Sabha (1870, founded by M. G. Ranade), and the Madras Mahajana Sabha (1884).

Key Moderate Leaders and Their Contributions

The Moderate leaders were typically Western-educated professionals — lawyers, journalists, teachers, and civil servants — who believed in gradual constitutional reform within the British Empire. The most prominent included:

  • Dadabhai Naoroji (1825-1917): Called the 'Grand Old Man of India.' He was the first Indian to be elected to the British House of Commons (1892, representing the Liberal Party from Finsbury Central). He served as Congress president three times (1886, 1893, 1906). His seminal work Poverty and Un-British Rule in India (1901) systematically developed the Drain of Wealth theory, arguing that Britain's annual tribute from India (£30-40 million) was the root cause of Indian poverty. The theory became the economic foundation of the nationalist movement.
  • Gopal Krishna Gokhale (1866-1915): Gandhi called him his 'political guru.' A founding member of the Congress's moderate wing, he founded the Servants of India Society in 1905 to train Indian missionaries for national service. He was a member of the Imperial Legislative Council for 13 years, where he expertly criticised the government's budget and financial policies. His influence on the Minto-Morley Reforms of 1909 was considerable.
  • Pherozeshah Mehta (1845-1915): Known as the 'Uncrowned King of Bombay.' He was a leading lawyer and municipal politician who shaped the constitutionalist strand of Indian nationalism. He served as Congress president in 1890 and was instrumental in drafting the Congress's founding constitution.
  • Surendranath Banerjee (1848-1925): Called the 'Surrender Nath' or 'Indian Edmund Burke.' He founded the Indian Association in 1876 and organized the first all-India political conference in 1883. His newspaper The Bengalee was a powerful voice for reform. He wrote the classic memoir A Nation in Making (1925).
  • Madan Mohan Malaviya (1861-1946): Four-time Congress president (1909, 1913, 1919, 1932). He founded the Banaras Hindu University (1916) and the newspaper The Leader. He was a prominent advocate of Hindu religious and educational causes within a broadly nationalist framework.
  • Mahadev Govind Ranade (1842-1901): A judge, scholar, and social reformer who was the intellectual godfather of the early Congress. He founded the Prarthana Samaj, championed widow remarriage, and argued for Indian industrialisation through his writings in the Journal of the Poona Sarvajanik Sabha.

Moderate Demands and Methods

The Moderates pursued a strategy of constitutional agitation: submitting petitions and memoranda to the British Parliament, holding annual Congress sessions to pass resolutions, publishing newspapers, organising lecture tours in England (Naoroji, Banerjee, Gokhale all travelled to Britain to lobby), and occasionally sending deputations to the Viceroy. Their key demands included: expansion of the legislative councils with elected Indian members (achieved partially in the Indian Councils Act 1892, which allowed Indians to elect members to provincial councils through indirect elections by municipalities and district boards); simultaneous holding of the Indian Civil Service (ICS) examinations in India and Britain (the exam remained London-only until 1922); reduction of military expenditure (the military budget consumed 40% of Indian revenues); imposition of protective tariffs for Indian industries (the British followed a policy of Free Trade that destroyed Indian textile manufacturing); and abolition of the salt tax (an oppressive regressive tax that fell heaviest on the poor). The Indian Councils Act 1909 (the Morley-Minto Reforms) marked the Moderates' greatest achievement: it increased the size of legislative councils, allowed Indians to be elected to them through a restricted franchise based on property and education, and introduced the principle of separate electorates for Muslims (a concession that would prove enormously consequential). The Moderates' limitations were undeniable: they represented only the educated elite, had no mass following, and achieved only minimal reforms. However, they created the organisational structure and the ideal of Indian nationhood upon which later mass movements would be built.

Phase 2: The Swadeshi, Revolutionary, and Home Rule Phase (1905-1915)

The Partition of Bengal (1905)

The Partition of Bengal was announced on 19 July 1905 and implemented on 16 October 1905 by Viceroy Lord Curzon (1899-1905). The province of Bengal, with a population of 78 million, was divided into West Bengal (Bihar, Odisha, and western Bengal, with capital at Calcutta) and East Bengal and Assam (eastern Bengal and Assam, with capital at Dhaka). Curzon's stated justification was administrative efficiency — Bengal was too large (189,000 sq miles) for a single provincial administration. The real motive was political: Bengali Hindus, who dominated the province's landowning, professional, and commercial classes, had become the most politically conscious group in India. By creating a Muslim-majority East Bengal, the British aimed to split the Bengali-speaking population along religious lines and reduce the political influence of Calcutta's Hindu bhadralok (respectable middle class). The partition sparked the most intense anti-British agitation India had yet witnessed. The Bengali nationalist leader Surendranath Banerjee declared that the partition was 'a deliberate blow at the nationality of the Bengali people.' The day of partition, 16 October 1905, was observed as a day of mourning across Bengal: shops closed, people fasted, and the rakhi bandhan ceremony (tying of sacred threads) was used to symbolise the unity of the two halves of Bengal.

The Swadeshi and Boycott Movement

The Swadeshi Movement began as a boycott of British manufactured goods, especially Lancashire cotton cloth and Liverpool salt, and the promotion of indigenous products (swadeshi). The movement quickly expanded to include: boycott of British goods (Manchester cloth was publicly burned in bonfires across Bengal's towns and villages); promotion of indigenous industry (the revival of handloom weaving, the establishment of national textile mills, soap factories, tanneries, match factories, and banks — the first Swadeshi bank, the People's Bank, was founded in 1906); national education (the National Council of Education was established in 1906, leading to the founding of the Bengal National College and the Bengal Technical Institute; Rabindranath Tagore's Visva-Bharati University at Santiniketan was inspired by this movement); public meetings and processions that drew crowds of up to 100,000 people; and the cultural revival of Bengali art, music, and literature. The song Bande Mataram (Hail to the Mother), written by Bankim Chandra Chattopadhyay in his novel Anandamath (1882) and set to music by Rabindranath Tagore, became the movement's anthem. The movement spread beyond Bengal to Maharashtra (Tilak's swadeshi campaign), Punjab (Lala Lajpat Rai), and Madras (the Madras Native Association's swadeshi resolution).

The Rise of Extremist or Militant Nationalism

The Swadeshi period also saw the emergence of the Extremist or Militant school of Indian nationalism, led by the 'Lal-Bal-Pal' triumvirate: Bal Gangadhar Tilak (Maharashtra), Bipin Chandra Pal (Bengal), and Lala Lajpat Rai (Punjab), along with Aurobindo Ghosh. Unlike the Moderates, the Extremists believed in Swaraj (self-rule) as the goal and passive resistance as the method. Tilak made Swaraj a mass slogan: 'Swaraj is my birthright and I shall have it.' He used the traditional Maharashtrian Ganpati festival (publicly celebrated from 1894) and Shivaji festivals (from 1896) to mobilise popular sentiment. His newspapers Kesari (Marathi) and Mahratta (English) had enormous influence. Aurobindo Ghosh, the intellectual architect of Extremist nationalism, wrote in Bande Mataram: 'We have to make India free... and we shall do it by our own strength.' The Extremist programme called for Swaraj, boycott of British goods and institutions, national education, and the promotion of swadeshi industries. The Surat Split of the Congress (December 1907) occurred when Extremists attempted to elect Tilak as Congress president; the Moderates (led by Mehta and Gokhale) expelled them by calling in the police to physically remove them from the session. The Congress remained under Moderate control until the Lucknow Pact of 1916.

Revolutionary Terrorism

The failure of both constitutional agitation and mass boycotts to achieve political concessions led a section of the nationalist youth to adopt armed revolution as a method. Revolutionary secret societies emerged across India, especially in Bengal, Maharashtra, and Punjab. In Bengal, the Anushilan Samiti (founded 1902 in Calcutta) and the Jugantar Party (led by Barin Ghosh, Aurobindo's younger brother, and Hemchandra Kanungo) carried out a series of political assassinations, robberies (to fund activities), and attacks on British officials. The first political assassination was the killing of the British magistrate Kingsford by Khudiram Bose and Prafulla Chaki (30 April 1908, Muzaffarpur — Kingsford escaped, but two British women died). Khudiram Bose was hanged at age 18, becoming the first Indian revolutionary martyr. The Alipore Bomb Conspiracy Case (1908) followed the discovery of the Jugantar group's bomb-making factory at Muraripukur garden; Aurobindo Ghosh was arrested and tried but acquitted (his barrister was Chittaranjan Das). In Maharashtra, the Abhinav Bharat Society (founded by V. D. Savarkar and his brother Ganesh Savarkar) carried out revolutionary activities; Savarkar was arrested in London (1910) for possession of firearms and sentenced to 50 years in the Cellular Jail (Kalapani) in Port Blair, Andaman Islands. In Punjab, the Ghadar Party (1913) was formed by Indian revolutionaries in the United States and Canada under Lala Hardayal, Kartar Singh Sarabha, and Sohan Singh Bhakna. The Ghadaris (rebellious) planned a simultaneous armed uprising across India in 1915, but the plot was betrayed to the British. Many Ghadar leaders were executed or imprisoned. The Komagata Maru incident (1914) involved 376 Indian passengers (mostly Sikhs from Punjab) who were denied entry to Canada and, upon forced return to Calcutta, were fired upon by British police, killing 20.

Muslim League and Home Rule Movement

The All-India Muslim League was founded on 30 December 1906 at the Ahsan Manzil Palace in Dhaka, during the annual Muhammadan Educational Conference. Its founding president was Sir Sultan Muhammad Shah Aga Khan III; its chief patron was Nawab Salimullah of Dhaka. The League was loyalist in its early years, supporting the British Raj and working to protect Muslim political interests through separate electorates. The Morley-Minto Reforms (Indian Councils Act 1909) granted Muslims separate electorates, formally introducing communal representation into Indian politics. In 1911, the British reversed the Partition of Bengal (annulled at the Delhi Durbar by King George V) and announced the transfer of the capital from Calcutta to Delhi — a decision that alienated both Bengali Hindus (who had agitated against partition) and the Muslim League (which had benefitted from the partition). The Home Rule League Movement (1916) was launched separately by Annie Besant (an Irish theosophist who had adopted India as her home) and Bal Gangadhar Tilak. Besant's Home Rule League was notably successful in Madras and western India; she became Congress president in 1917, the first woman to hold the office. Tilak's League operated in Maharashtra and Karnataka. The movement demanded self-government for India within the British Empire on the model of the white dominions (Canada, Australia). The Lucknow Pact (December 1916) between the Congress and the Muslim League was a landmark: the reunited Congress (Moderates and Extremists had reunited in 1916) accepted separate electorates for Muslims, while the League agreed to work with the Congress for self-government. The Pact also produced a common scheme of constitutional reforms demanding elected majorities in legislative councils.

Phase 3: The Gandhian Era (1915-1935)

Gandhi's Return and Early Satyagrahas

Mohandas Karamchand Gandhi (1869-1948) returned to India from South Africa on 9 January 1915 (now celebrated as Pravasi Bharatiya Diwas). In South Africa (1893-1914), he had developed his method of Satyagraha (truth-force or soul-force) — non-violent resistance based on moral persuasion rather than physical coercion — leading successful campaigns against racial discrimination (the Natal Indian Congress, the Phoenix Settlement, the Tolstoy Farm, and the 1913 march of 2,000 Indian miners). His political guru Gopal Krishna Gokhale advised him to spend a year travelling across India to understand the country before speaking. Gandhi's first three major satyagrahas in India established his method and his leadership. The Champaran Satyagraha (1917, Bihar): indigo planters under the tinkathia system forced peasants to grow indigo on 3/20th of their land and sell it at fixed low prices. Gandhi arrived in Champaran, was ordered to leave by the District Magistrate, refused, and was arrested. The subsequent agitation forced the government to appoint a commission of inquiry (with Gandhi as a member) that abolished the tinkathia system. The Kheda Satyagraha (1918, Gujarat): when crops failed due to heavy rains, the government refused to suspend land revenue collection. Gandhi led the peasants in a campaign of non-payment; the government eventually issued instructions that revenue should be collected only from those who could afford to pay. The Ahmedabad Mill Strike (1918): textile mill workers demanded a 35% wage increase; Gandhi led them in a strike, supporting them by undertaking his first political fast (three days), which shamed the mill owners into accepting arbitration. The poet Rabindranath Tagore first addressed Gandhi as Mahatma ('Great Soul') during this period.

The Rowlatt Satyagraha and Jallianwala Bagh (1919)

The Rowlatt Act (the Anarchical and Revolutionary Crimes Act, March 1919) extended wartime repressive measures into peacetime: it allowed the government to arrest and detain suspected revolutionaries without trial, try cases without juries, and confiscate property. Gandhi called for a nationwide hartal (cessation of business) on 6 April 1919 as a day of prayer and fasting. The response was unprecedented — the strike was observed across India, from Bombay to Calcutta to Lahore. The most tragic consequence came in Amritsar, Punjab. On 13 April 1919, the Sikh festival of Baisakhi drew an unarmed crowd of 15,000-20,000 men, women, and children to the Jallianwala Bagh, a walled garden with a single narrow entrance. Brigadier-General Reginald Dyer arrived with 90 Gurkha and Baluchi soldiers and ordered them to open fire without warning. The firing continued for 10 minutes until ammunition was exhausted; 1,650 rounds were discharged. Official figures: 379 killed, 1,200 wounded. Indian sources estimate over 1,000 killed. Dyer later stated he had intended 'to produce a moral effect' and would have used machine guns if he could have brought them into the garden. The Hunter Commission (appointed by the government) condemned Dyer's actions, but the British Parliament's House of Lords praised him and the Morning Post raised a public subscription of £20,000 for him. Gandhi called the massacre 'a monster of the deepest dye' and said it 'shook the foundations of the British empire.' The poet Rabindranath Tagore returned his knighthood in protest. The massacre became the great turning point in the national movement — millions of moderate, loyal Indians became convinced that the British system was irredeemably oppressive.

Non-Cooperation and Khilafat Movement (1920-1922)

The Khilafat Movement (1919-1924) was a powerful pan-Islamic protest movement launched by Indian Muslims against the British abolition of the Ottoman Caliphate (Khilafat) after World War I. The leaders — the Ali brothers (Muhammad Ali and Shaukat Ali), Maulana Abul Kalam Azad, and Maulana Hasrat Mohani — organised mass protests and threatened non-cooperation with the British government. Gandhi saw this as a golden opportunity for Hindu-Muslim unity and merged the Khilafat cause with the national movement. The Non-Cooperation Movement (launched August 1920) was Gandhi's first nationwide mass movement. Its programme was comprehensive: surrender of all titles and honorary offices; boycott of legislatures, courts, and government educational institutions; boycott of foreign goods (especially cloth); promotion of hand-spinning and hand-weaving (khadi) as a symbol of economic self-reliance; establishment of national schools and colleges; and popularisation of the charkha (spinning wheel) as the symbol of the movement. Gandhi promised Swaraj within one year if the movement achieved complete non-violence and Hindu-Muslim unity. The response was electric: thousands of students left government schools and colleges (the Gujarat College in Ahmedabad was reduced from 500 to 20 students), lawyers gave up their practices (C. R. Das, Motilal Nehru, and even Muhammad Ali Jinnah initially supported), legislatures were boycotted in the 1921 elections (voter turnout dropped to 6% in some constituencies), and the production of khadi became a nationwide cottage industry. Thousands were arrested. But the movement came to a violent and premature end. On 5 February 1922, at Chauri Chaura in Gorakhpur district, Uttar Pradesh, a police party that had fired on a picketing group of peasants was attacked by the mob; the peasants set fire to the police station, burning 22 policemen alive. Gandhi, who had repeatedly insisted on non-violence as the absolute condition of the movement, immediately suspended it on 12 February 1922. He was arrested on 10 March, tried for sedition ('to disaffection towards the Government established by law in India'), and sentenced to 6 years' imprisonment. He was released on medical grounds in February 1924 after an appendicitis operation. The suspension of the movement demoralised many nationalists: C. R. Das and Motilal Nehru broke away to form the Swaraj Party (1923), arguing that the boycott of legislatures had been a mistake and that elected councils should be entered to obstruct the government from within.

The Swarajists, Simon Commission, and the Rise of Revolutionary Activity

The mid-1920s saw the emergence of the Swaraj Party (1923) within the Congress, led by C. R. Das (president) and Motilal Nehru (secretary). The Swarajists entered the Central Legislative Council and provincial councils to 'wreck the constitution from within' by refusing to accept ministerial positions and by voting against government budgets. The death of C. R. Das in 1925 weakened the party. The Simon Commission (1928) was sent by the British Conservative government under Stanley Baldwin to review the working of the 1919 Government of India Act and recommend further constitutional reforms. The Commission was composed entirely of British Members of Parliament, with no Indian members. The all-white Simon Commission was met with the black-flag protest and the slogan 'Simon Go Back' wherever it went in India (February-April 1928). When the Commission visited Lahore on 30 October 1928, the veteran nationalist leader Lala Lajpat Rai was leading a peaceful protest when the police superintendent James Scott ordered a lathi charge; Rai was beaten severely and died of his injuries on 17 November 1928. The death of Lajpat Rai led directly to the rise of the Hindustan Socialist Republican Association (HSRA), led by Bhagat Singh, Chandrasekhar Azad, Sukhdev, and Rajguru. Bhagat Singh and his associates had founded the HSRA in 1928 (a successor to the Hindustan Republican Association of 1924) with the aim of complete Indian independence through armed revolution. To avenge Lajpat Rai's death, Bhagat Singh, Rajguru, and Azad conspired to kill the police superintendent James Scott. On 17 December 1928, they mistakenly shot the Assistant Superintendent J. P. Saunders instead (the actual assassins were Bhagat Singh and Rajguru). Bhagat Singh fled to Lahore, where on 8 April 1929, he and Batukeshwar Dutt threw two bombs and leaflets in the Central Legislative Assembly from the visitors' gallery, shouting 'Inquilab Zindabad' (Long Live the Revolution). The bombs were designed not to kill but to make a point; no one was killed. Bhagat Singh and Dutt were arrested and sentenced to transportation for life. Meanwhile, the police cracked down on the HSRA, and a series of revolutionary encounters followed. In October 1929, Bhagat Singh was implicated in the Saunders murder case after the police discovered the Lahore Conspiracy. Chandrasekhar Azad died in a shootout with police at Alfred Park, Allahabad, on 27 February 1931, refusing to be captured alive. Bhagat Singh, Rajguru, and Sukhdev were executed on 23 March 1931 in Lahore Central Jail (they were hanged secretly at 7:30 pm, and their bodies were cremated secretly on the banks of the Sutlej River). Their martyrdom made them the most revered figures in the Indian independence movement — their execution sparked massive protests across India, and they remain among the most iconic Indian revolutionaries.

Purna Swaraj and the Civil Disobedience Movement (1930-1934)

The Nehru Report (1928, chaired by Motilal Nehru) proposed a dominion constitution for India that rejected separate electorates for Muslims, leading to the breakdown of the Congress-Muslim League consensus. The Congress decided at its 1929 Lahore session (presided over by Jawaharlal Nehru) to declare Purna Swaraj (Complete Independence) as its goal, abandoning the earlier demand for Dominion Status. On 26 January 1930, the first Independence Day was celebrated across India, with the Purna Swaraj declaration read aloud at public meetings. 26 January later became India's Republic Day (1950). The Salt Satyagraha or Dandi March began on 12 March 1930 when Gandhi departed from his Sabarmati Ashram with 78 carefully selected followers (mostly from the ashram). They walked 241 miles (388 km) over 24 days, passing through 48 villages where Gandhi addressed huge crowds. They reached the coastal village of Dandi on 6 April 1930, where Gandhi broke the salt law by picking up a handful of natural salt. The act was brilliantly symbolic: the British salt tax (a government monopoly on salt manufacture) affected every Indian, rich and poor, and the act of making salt was both illegal and universal. The movement spread with extraordinary speed: salt was manufactured along the entire coast; foreign cloth was boycotted; liquor shops were picketed; government servants resigned; and peasants refused to pay land revenue and chowkidari (village watchman) taxes. The British responded with mass arrests: over 90,000 people, including Gandhi (arrested 5 May 1930), Jawaharlal Nehru, and most Congress leaders, were imprisoned by the end of 1930. The First Round Table Conference (November 1930-January 1931, London) was boycotted by the Congress; it was attended by British politicians, Indian princes, the Muslim League, and representatives of the Depressed Classes (B. R. Ambedkar), Sikhs, Christians, and Anglo-Indians. The conference failed to produce a constitutional formula acceptable to Indian opinion. The Gandhi-Irwin Pact (5 March 1931) was a personal agreement between Gandhi and Viceroy Lord Irwin: the Congress agreed to suspend the Civil Disobedience Movement and participate in the Second Round Table Conference in exchange for the release of political prisoners and the right to make salt for domestic consumption. The truce was controversial: the revolutionary leader Bhagat Singh was executed three days before the pact was announced, and many felt Gandhi should have secured his commutation. The Second Round Table Conference (September-December 1931, London) was the only time Gandhi visited Europe; he attended as the sole Congress representative. The conference broke down on the communal question: the British insisted on separate electorates for minorities (Muslims, Sikhs, Depressed Classes), which Gandhi passionately opposed. The Communal Award (1932) by Prime Minister Ramsay MacDonald granted separate electorates to the Depressed Classes (Scheduled Castes). Gandhi, from Yerawada Jail, began a 'fast unto death' on 20 September 1932, arguing that separate electorates would permanently divide Hindu society. The Poona Pact (24 September 1932) negotiated between B. R. Ambedkar (representing the Depressed Classes) and Gandhi produced a compromise: the Depressed Classes were given 148 reserved seats in provincial legislatures but elected by a joint Hindu electorate, not a separate one. The Civil Disobedience Movement was formally withdrawn in April 1934. The Government of India Act 1935 emerged from the Round Table Conference process: it introduced provincial autonomy (elected Indian governments in eleven provinces, with reserved British powers over defence, foreign affairs, and tribal areas), a bicameral federal legislature at the centre, and separate electorates for Muslims, Sikhs, Christians, Anglo-Indians, and Europeans.

Phase 4: The Final Phase (1935-1947)

Congress Ministries and the Rise of Jinnah (1937-1939)

The 1937 elections, held under the Government of India Act 1935, produced a decisive Congress victory: the Congress won 716 of 1,585 provincial seats and formed ministries in 7 of 11 provinces (Madras, Bombay, United Provinces, Central Provinces, Bihar, Orissa, and Assam; later also in the North-West Frontier Province under Congress-allied Chief Minister Dr. Khan Sahib). The Muslim League won only 108 of 482 Muslim-reserved seats and formed no ministries. The Congress ministries (1937-1939) proved generally popular: they introduced civil liberties reforms (releasing political prisoners), agrarian legislation (relief for indebted peasants), educational expansion, and prohibition in some provinces. However, the period also deepened Hindu-Muslim polarisation. The Muslim League, in its 1938 session, declared that 'the Congress party in the name of nationalism has tried to suppress the Muslim culture and language.' The 'two-nation theory' — that Hindus and Muslims were distinct nations who could not be forced into a single state — gained ground under the leadership of Muhammad Ali Jinnah, who had left the Congress in 1920 and taken over the Muslim League in 1934. When World War II broke out in September 1939, Viceroy Lord Linlithgow unilaterally declared India a belligerent on Britain's side without consulting Indian leaders. The Congress ministries resigned in protest in October-November 1939. The Delhi Resolution (March 1940) of the Muslim League, later called the Lahore Resolution (passed at the League's annual session in Lahore, 22-24 March 1940), formally demanded the creation of independent states for Muslims: 'that geographically contiguous units are demarcated into regions which should be so constituted... that the areas in which the Muslims are numerically in majority, as in the north-western and north-eastern zones of India, should be grouped to constitute independent states.' The word 'Pakistan' was not used in the resolution, but it was the formal birth of the demand for a separate Muslim homeland.

The War Years: August Offer, Cripps Mission, and Quit India (1940-1945)

The August Offer (8 August 1940) by Viceroy Linlithgow offered India dominion status after the war, an expansion of the Viceroy's Executive Council to include more Indians, and the establishment of a war advisory council. The Congress rejected it because it did not provide for immediate self-government. The Individual Satyagraha (1940-41) was launched by Gandhi as a symbolic protest, with selected individuals (Vinoba Bhave, Jawaharlal Nehru) offering non-violent resistance by making anti-war speeches. In 1941, Japan entered the war and advanced rapidly through Southeast Asia, capturing Singapore (February 1942), Burma (March-May 1942), and reaching India's eastern borders. The British government sent Sir Stafford Cripps, a Labour Cabinet Minister, to India in March 1942 with the Cripps Mission, offering dominion status after the war with the right to secede from the British Commonwealth, and the option for provinces to opt out of the Indian Union. The Cripps Offer failed because: the Congress demanded immediate self-government (not a post-war promise); the British refused to place defence under Indian control during the war; and the Cripps Mission's provisions for provincial secession were seen as a concession to the Muslim League's partition demand. The failure of the Cripps Mission led directly to the Quit India Movement. The All-India Congress Committee met at Bombay on 8 August 1942 and passed the Quit India Resolution, demanding an immediate end to British rule. Gandhi, in his historic 'Do or Die' speech, declared: 'I have only one pistol in this fight of truth and non-violence � the weapon of the people. I ask you to keep the light of freedom burning... I have a message for you. It is: Do or Die. We shall either free India or die in the attempt.' The British response was swift and decisive: the very next day (9 August 1942), Gandhi, Nehru, Patel, and all members of the Congress Working Committee were arrested. The movement, leaderless and without the organisational framework Gandhi had insisted upon, turned violent. There were attacks on police stations, railway stations, post offices, and government buildings across northern and central India. The parallel governments were established in parts of Maharashtra (Satara under Krantisinh Nana Patil), Bihar (the 'Kisan Sena'), and eastern Uttar Pradesh (Ballia). The British deployed 57 infantry battalions, crushed the movement with overwhelming force: over 100,000 people were arrested, 10,000 were killed or wounded by police firing, and 60,000 were detained without trial. Underground leaders � Jayaprakash Narayan (JP, who escaped from Hazaribagh Jail), Aruna Asaf Ali (the 'Heroine of 1942' who hoisted the Congress flag at the Gowalia Tank Maidan on 9 August), Ram Manohar Lohia, and Achyut Patwardhan � organised guerrilla resistance and maintained clandestine radio stations. The Quit India Movement was the most massive uprising India had seen; it proved that British India could not be held by force after the war.

Subhas Chandra Bose and the Indian National Army

Subhas Chandra Bose (1897-1945, known as Netaji) was the leader of the radical wing of the Congress. He had been elected Congress president in 1938 and re-elected in 1939 against Gandhi's wishes, defeating Gandhi's preferred candidate Pattabhi Sitaramayya. He was forced to resign because of differences with the Congress Working Committee (most members resigned in protest), and he formed the Forward Bloc (1939) as a left-wing party within the Congress. Arrested by the British in July 1940, he went on hunger strike and was released on parole. On 26 January 1941, he escaped house arrest in Calcutta, disguised as a Pathan insurance agent named 'Mohammad Ziauddin,' and travelled through Afghanistan to the Soviet Union (hoping to enlist Stalin's support against British colonialism). The Soviets, then allied with Britain, refused him. He went to Berlin, where he met Hitler and convinced the Nazis to support the formation of the Free India Legion (2,000 Indian POWs captured in North Africa). In February 1943, he travelled by German submarine to Madagascar, where he transferred to a Japanese submarine that took him to Tokyo. The Japanese Prime Minister Hideki Tojo offered him support. On 21 October 1943, Bose announced the formation of the Provisional Government of Free India (Azad Hind) in Singapore, with himself as Head of State, Prime Minister, and Minister of War. The Indian National Army (INA, Azad Hind Fauj) was formed from Indian prisoners of war captured by the Japanese in Malaya and Singapore, as well as Indian civilians in Southeast Asia. The total INA strength reached about 50,000 soldiers, including a women's regiment (the Rani of Jhansi Regiment led by Captain Lakshmi Sahgal). The INA's battle cry was 'Dilli Chalo' (March to Delhi) and 'Jai Hind' (Victory to India). The INA fought alongside the Japanese in the Burma Campaign (1944), the Battles of Imphal and Kohima, where the Japanese offensive was decisively defeated by the British Fourteenth Army under General William Slim. The INA surrendered with the Japanese in August 1945. Bose reportedly died in a plane crash at Taipei on 18 August 1945, though the circumstances remain a subject of controversy (three official inquiries have been conducted, all concluding that he died in the crash, but persistent conspiracy theories claim he survived). The INA trials at the Red Fort, Delhi (November 1945-May 1946) of three captured INA officers � Shah Nawaz Khan, Prem Sahgal, and Gurbaksh Singh Dhillon � stirred enormous public sympathy across India. The Congress organised a high-powered legal defence team (Bhulabhai Desai, Jawaharlal Nehru, Tej Bahadur Sapru, K. N. Katju). The three were sentenced to death but the sentences were commuted to life imprisonment under massive public pressure. The INA trials and the British decision to demobilise Indian troops controversially led to the Royal Indian Navy Mutiny (February 1946) at Bombay, where 20,000 ratings (sailors) on 78 ships of HMIS Talwar went on strike, demanding better conditions and independence. The mutiny spread to Karachi, Madras, and Calcutta. The British realised that they could no longer rely on the loyalty of Indian armed forces, a critical factor in the decision to grant independence quickly.

The Cabinet Mission and the Road to Partition (1946-1947)

In February 1946, the British government sent a high-level delegation � the Cabinet Mission (Lord Pethick-Lawrence, Sir Stafford Cripps, and A. V. Alexander) � to negotiate a final constitutional settlement. The Cabinet Mission Plan (16 May 1946) proposed: a united India with a weak central government controlling only defence, foreign affairs, and communications; three groups of provinces (Group A: Hindu-majority provinces; Group B: Muslim-majority provinces in the north-west; Group C: Bengal and Assam) with their own legislatures and governments; and the right of any province or group to secede after 10 years. Both the Congress and the Muslim League accepted the plan in principle. However, on 10 July 1946, Jawaharlal Nehru (who had become Congress president, succeeding Maulana Azad) stated at a press conference that the Congress was not bound by the grouping arrangement and could modify the plan after independence. Jinnah interpreted this as a betrayal and withdrew the League's acceptance. He called for Direct Action Day on 16 August 1946 to achieve Pakistan through mass mobilization. The result was the Great Calcutta Killings: four days of communal rioting in Calcutta that left over 5,000 dead and 20,000 injured. The violence spread to Noakhali (East Bengal, October 1946, where Muslims attacked Hindus), then to Bihar (October-November 1946, where Hindus retaliated against Muslims), and finally to Punjab in early 1947. The total death toll by March 1947 exceeded 10,000. The British Labour government, exhausted by World War II and facing financial crisis, decided that immediate withdrawal was necessary. On 20 February 1947, Prime Minister Clement Attlee announced that the British would leave India by June 1948. He appointed Lord Louis Mountbatten as the last Viceroy (arrived 24 March 1947) with a mandate to transfer power quickly. Mountbatten, working with his chief of staff Lord Ismay, concluded that partition was inevitable. The Mountbatten Plan (3 June 1947) proposed the partition of India into two independent dominions � India and Pakistan � with the boundaries to be determined by a Boundary Commission chaired by Sir Cyril Radcliffe, a British lawyer who had never visited India. Radcliffe was given just five weeks to draw the boundaries of the new nations. The Indian Independence Act 1947 was passed by the British Parliament on 18 July 1947. India and Pakistan became independent at midnight on 14-15 August 1947. The Radcliffe Award was announced on 17 August 1947 (two days after independence) and divided the provinces of Bengal and Punjab, creating East Pakistan (now Bangladesh) and West Pakistan. The partition triggered the largest and most violent mass migration in human history: approximately 15 million Hindus, Muslims, and Sikhs crossed the new borders in both directions; between 200,000 and 1 million people died in the ensuing violence. The state of Jammu and Kashmir, a Muslim-majority state ruled by a Hindu Maharaja (Hari Singh), initially remained independent but then acceded to India in October 1947 after a Pakistani tribal invasion, sparking the first India-Pakistan war. Jawaharlal Nehru became the first Prime Minister of India, Muhammad Ali Jinnah became the first Governor-General of Pakistan, and Lord Mountbatten served as India's first Governor-General until June 1948. Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel, the 'Iron Man of India,' as Deputy Prime Minister and Home Minister, integrated 562 princely states into the Indian Union through a combination of diplomacy, financial incentives, and military force (including the 'police action' in Hyderabad in September 1948).

Revolutionary and Social Movements in the Freedom Struggle

Revolutionary Terrorism (1905-1934)

The revolutionary movement in India was a parallel stream to the mainstream Congress-led struggle. It passed through three distinct phases: the first phase (1905-1915) associated with the Bengal revolutionaries (Anushilan Samiti, Jugantar Party) and the Ghadar Party; the second phase (1915-1928) of relative decline due to government repression; and the third phase (1928-1934) associated with the Hindustan Socialist Republican Association (HSRA) and the martyrdom of Bhagat Singh. The revolutionaries of the first phase included: Khudiram Bose (hanged 1908, aged 18, one of the youngest martyrs); Kanailal Dutta (who assassinated the approver Narendra Nath Goswami in Alipore Jail, 1908, and was hanged); Madan Lal Dhingra (executed 1909 for assassinating Sir Curzon Wyllie in London); Vanchinathan (who assassinated the British collector Ashe in Tirunelveli, 1911); and Rash Behari Bose (who attempted to assassinate Viceroy Lord Hardinge in Delhi, 1912, and escaped to Japan). The third-phase revolutionaries (HSRA) included: Bhagat Singh (executed 23 March 1931, aged 23. He was a Marxist revolutionary who wrote extensively on socialism and the theory of revolution; his pamphlet 'Why I Am an Atheist' is a philosophical classic; his final months in jail were spent reading Lenin, Trotsky, and historical biographies; he went on a 64-day hunger strike demanding better treatment for political prisoners); Chandrasekhar Azad (who died in a police shoot-out in 1931, refusing to be captured); Sukhdev Thapar and Shivaram Rajguru (executed with Bhagat Singh); and Jatin Das (who died after a 63-day hunger strike in Lahore Jail in 1929). The revolutionaries were unable to achieve their goals directly, but their courage and sacrifice inspired mass nationalism and forced the British to recognise the depth of Indian opposition.

Social Reform and the National Movement

The Indian freedom struggle was deeply intertwined with social reform movements. The Brahmo Samaj (founded 1828 by Raja Ram Mohan Roy), the Arya Samaj (founded 1875 by Swami Dayanand Saraswati), the Prarthana Samaj (1867), the Ramakrishna Mission (founded 1897 by Swami Vivekananda), and the Theosophical Society (1879, headed by Annie Besant) contributed to the cultural nationalism that underlay political nationalism.
