The women's movement was an integral part of the struggle: women participated in large numbers in the Civil Disobedience Movement (including Sarojini Naidu, the first Indian woman Congress president in 1925, who accompanied Gandhi to the Second Round Table Conference; Kamaladevi Chattopadhyay, who was the first Indian woman to be arrested; Kasturba Gandhi, who led movements in Gandhi's absence; Aruna Asaf Ali, who hoisted the Congress flag at the Quit India Movement launch; and Mithuben Petit, among many others). The Depressed Classes movement led by Dr. B. R. Ambedkar (1891-1956) was a major force: Ambedkar argued that the freedom struggle must also mean liberation from caste oppression. He founded the Independent Labour Party (1936) and later the Scheduled Castes Federation (1942). The Poona Pact (1932) between Ambedkar and Gandhi was a watershed, establishing the principle of reserved seats for Scheduled Castes. The trade union movement also grew in parallel: the All India Trade Union Congress (AITUC) was founded in 1920 by Lala Lajpat Rai, while the left wing of the Congress produced leaders like Jayaprakash Narayan, Ram Manohar Lohia, and Acharya Narendra Dev, who combined nationalism with socialism.

Significance and Legacy

The Indian freedom struggle transformed the Indian subcontinent's relationship with the world. It was unique among anti-colonial movements in its consistent adherence to non-violence as a mass method (Gandhi's most original political contribution), its ability to mobilise all sections of a deeply stratified society, and its success in creating a unified nation-state from diverse linguistic, religious, and regional constituencies. The struggle produced the Constitution of India (adopted 26 November 1949, effective 26 January 1950), the longest-written constitution in the world, establishing a sovereign, socialist, secular, democratic republic with universal adult franchise. The struggle also shaped the post-colonial world order: India's independence inspired decolonisation movements across Asia and Africa, and the non-violent method influenced leaders from Martin Luther King Jr. (American civil rights movement) to Nelson Mandela (the South African anti-apartheid movement). For UPSC, the Indian freedom struggle is a core subject: it tests not only factual recall of dates, names, and events but also the capacity for historical analysis � understanding the interplay of economic forces (the Drain of Wealth), social change (caste, gender, religious identity), political strategy (the success and failure of different methods), and the role of ideas (liberalism, socialism, Gandhian thought) in shaping the modern Indian nation.

18. Post-Independence India (1947-2025)

The History of Independent India is the narrative of how a poor, deeply divided, war-torn colony transformed into the world's largest democracy and fifth-largest economy within 75 years. The challenges at independence were staggering: partition had left up to 1 million dead and 15 million homeless; the economy was stagnant, industry negligible, and agriculture dependent on uncertain monsoons; 565 princely states remained outside the Indian Union; and the new nation had to create a constitutional, political, and economic order from scratch. The period is traditionally divided into the Nehruvian era (1947-1964) of democratic-socialist nation-building, the Indira Gandhi era (1966-1984) of populist radicalism and authoritarian emergency, the coalition era (1984-2014) of economic liberalisation and political fragmentation, and the Modi era (2014-present) of right-wing majoritarianism and rapid economic change.

Integration of Princely States and the Making of the Constitution

The Integration of 562 Princely States

Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel, India's first Deputy Prime Minister and Home Minister, accomplished the seemingly impossible task of integrating the 562 princely states into the Indian Union within two years (1947-1949). The mechanism was the Instrument of Accession, under which each state surrendered only three subjects (defence, foreign affairs, and communications) to the Government of India while retaining internal autonomy. Patel worked through V. P. Menon, the Secretary of the States Department, who drafted the legal instruments and personally negotiated with the princes. Patel's strategy combined: diplomacy (personal persuasion, appealing to patriotism and the princes' desire for a dignified future); political pressure (the threat of popular uprisings by the Congress movements within the states if they did not accede); financial incentives (the princes were guaranteed their privy purses � tax-free annual payments � and their personal properties, titles, and privileges through the Constitution); and military force (used only against the three recalcitrant states). The integration of the states was completed through a three-stage process: accession (1947-48), merger (1948-49, where the states were merged into existing provinces or formed into unions of states), and the final constitutional integration (1949, where all states accepted the Constitution of India).

Three states offered serious resistance. Junagadh: a Hindu-majority state on the Gujarat coast ruled by the Muslim Nawab Muhammad Mahabat Khanji III, who acceded to Pakistan on 15 August 1947. India refused to accept this as valid (arguing that the accession was geographically impossible � Junagadh had no land border with Pakistan). India imposed a blockade, organised a plebiscite (February 1948), and the population voted overwhelmingly for India. The Nawab fled to Pakistan. Hyderabad: the largest (82,000 sq miles, population 17 million) and wealthiest princely state, ruled by the Nizam Usman Ali Khan, the richest man in the world (estimated fortune of $2 billion in 1947, including the Hope Diamond and the Jacob Diamond � 184.7 carats). The Nizam dreamed of an independent state and resisted all pressure to accede. His militias (the Razakars under Kasim Razvi, 100,000 strong) terrorised the Hindu population and killed thousands. After a year of negotiations failed, the Indian Army launched Operation Polo (13-17 September 1948), a 'police action' that defeated the Nizam's forces in five days with minimal casualties (only 32 Indian soldiers killed). The Nizam signed the Instrument of Accession and was retained as the ceremonial head of state (Rajpramukh) until 1956. Jammu and Kashmir: the most complex case. The state was ruled by Maharaja Hari Singh, a Hindu Dogra ruler governing a Muslim-majority population (77% Muslim). He signed a Standstill Agreement with Pakistan (August 1947) but hesitated to accede to either dominion, hoping for independence. In October 1947, Pakistani Pathan tribesmen, supported by the Pakistani army (Operation Gulmarg), invaded Kashmir, looting and massacring their way toward Srinagar. On 24 October, a temporary 'Azad Kashmir' government was formed. The Maharaja appealed to India for military help. On 26 October 1947, he signed the Instrument of Accession to India, and Indian troops were airlifted to Srinagar the next day. The First Indo-Pak War (1947-48) ended with a UN-brokered ceasefire on 1 January 1949, establishing the Line of Control (LoC) that divided Kashmir into Indian-administered Jammu and Kashmir and Pakistan-administered Azad Kashmir and Gilgit-Baltistan. India took the matter to the UN Security Council, which passed resolutions calling for a plebiscite (which was never held). Jammu and Kashmir was granted special autonomous status under Article 370 of the Constitution.

The Framing of the Constitution

The Constituent Assembly of India, elected in July 1946 (indirectly by provincial legislatures), had 299 members representing all communities, regions, and political parties. Its drafting committee, chaired by Dr. B. R. Ambedkar (who is therefore called the 'Chief Architect of the Indian Constitution'), worked from 29 August 1947 to 26 November 1949, meeting for 114 days over 2 years, 11 months, and 17 days. The Constitution was deeply influenced by the Government of India Act 1935 (the administrative and federal framework), the American Constitution (judicial review, fundamental rights, the impeachment of the president), the British Constitution (parliamentary system, the Speaker, rule of law), the Irish Constitution (directive principles of state policy), the Canadian Constitution (federal structure with a strong centre), and the Australian Constitution (concurrent list). The Constitution was adopted on 26 November 1949 and came into effect on 26 January 1950 � now celebrated as Republic Day. It established India as a sovereign, socialist, secular, democratic republic (the words 'socialist' and 'secular' were added by the 42nd Amendment in 1976). The Constitution's key features include: universal adult franchise (every citizen over 21, later reduced to 18, could vote � a radical step when most Western democracies still had property or gender qualifications); a federal structure with a strong unitary bias (single constitution, single citizenship, the Union Parliament's power to reorganise states, the appointment of state governors by the President); a parliamentary system of government; an independent judiciary with the power of judicial review (the Supreme Court and High Courts can strike down laws that violate fundamental rights); fundamental rights (Articles 12-35, enforceable in courts: right to equality, right to freedom, right against exploitation, right to freedom of religion, cultural and educational rights, right to constitutional remedies); and directive principles of state policy (Articles 36-51, non-justiciable but fundamental to the governance of the country). The Constitution is the longest-written constitution in the world (originally 395 articles in 22 parts and 8 schedules; now 448 articles in 25 parts and 12 schedules after over 100 amendments). Dr. Rajendra Prasad was the first President of the Constituent Assembly and later the first President of India.

The Nehru Era (1947-1964)

Foreign Policy and Non-Alignment

Jawaharlal Nehru, India's first Prime Minister, served for 17 years (1947-1964) and shaped virtually every aspect of India's early development. His most distinctive contribution was the Non-Alignment foreign policy: India refused to join either the US-led North Atlantic Treaty Organisation (NATO, 1949) or the Soviet-led Warsaw Pact (1955), maintaining strategic autonomy during the Cold War. With Marshal Tito of Yugoslavia, President Gamal Abdel Nasser of Egypt, President Sukarno of Indonesia, and President Kwame Nkrumah of Ghana, Nehru founded the Non-Aligned Movement (NAM) at the 1955 Bandung Conference (the first Afro-Asian conference) and the formal Belgrade Conference of 1961. The Panchsheel (Five Principles of Peaceful Coexistence) � mutual respect for territorial integrity, mutual non-aggression, mutual non-interference, equality and mutual benefit, and peaceful coexistence � were first formalised in the Sino-Indian Agreement of 1954 regarding Tibet. Nehru condemned colonialism (supporting Indonesia against the Dutch, Algeria against the French, and the Congo against Belgian interests), opposed racial discrimination (strongly criticising South African apartheid at the UN), and attempted to mediate the Korean War and the Suez Crisis (1956). His policy of non-alignment was criticised as moralistic and ineffective (it did not prevent China from attacking India in 1962), but it gave India a leadership role in the post-colonial world and preserved India's strategic flexibility.

Economic Policy: The Planned Economy

Nehru was a convinced socialist who believed that rapid industrialisation under state direction was the only path to national prosperity and independence. The Planning Commission was established in March 1950 by a cabinet resolution (not by the Constitution). The Five Year Plans, modelled on the Soviet Gosplan system, guided economic development. The First Five Year Plan (1951-56) was a cautious plan that prioritised agriculture, irrigation, and power generation (the Bhakra Nangal Dam, Hirakud Dam, Damodar Valley Corporation multi-purpose river valley projects). It achieved a 3.6% GDP growth rate (above the target of 2.1%). The Second Five Year Plan (1956-61), drafted by statistician Prasanta Chandra Mahalanobis, focused on heavy industrialisation. Three integrated steel plants were established with foreign collaboration: Bhilai (with the Soviet Union, 1959 � the largest), Durgapur (with the United Kingdom, 1959), and Rourkela (with West Germany, 1962). The Industrial Policy Resolution 1956 reserved 17 industries (including steel, coal, oil, aircraft, shipbuilding, telecommunications, and mining) exclusively for the public sector, and another 12 were to be progressively state-owned. The government established state-owned enterprises: Hindustan Machine Tools (HMT, 1953), Hindustan Aeronautics Limited (HAL, 1940), Bharat Heavy Electricals Limited (BHEL, 1964), Oil and Natural Gas Corporation (ONGC, 1956), Indian Oil Corporation (IOC, 1964), and the State Trading Corporation (STC, 1956). The government also invested in scientific education: the Indian Institutes of Technology (IITs) were established at Kharagpur (1951, with UNESCO assistance), Bombay (1958, Soviet aid), Madras (1959, West German aid), Kanpur (1959, US consortium aid through the Kanpur Indo-American Programme), and Delhi (1961, UK aid). The Atomic Energy Commission (1948) under Homi Bhabha laid the foundation for India's nuclear programme.

Linguistic Reorganisation of States

The States Reorganisation Commission (SRC), headed by Justice Fazl Ali with H. N. Kunzru and K. M. Panikkar as members, was appointed in 1953 in response to widespread agitations (the most intense being the fast-unto-death of Potti Sriramulu for a separate Telugu-speaking state of Andhra; Sriramulu died on 15 December 1952, and Nehru was forced to concede Andhra state on 1 October 1953). The SRC submitted its report in 1955, recommending the reorganisation of states on linguistic lines. The States Reorganisation Act 1956 created 14 states and 6 union territories: Andhra Pradesh (the first linguistic state, formed from the Telugu-speaking areas of the old Madras Presidency), Assam (retained for the Assamese-speaking region), Bihar, Bombay (controversially bilingual � Marathi and Gujarati � leading to the Samyukta Maharashtra agitation), Jammu and Kashmir, Kerala (formed from the Malayalam-speaking areas of Travancore-Cochin, Malabar district of Madras, and Kasargod), Madhya Pradesh, Madras (renamed Tamil Nadu in 1969), Mysore (renamed Karnataka in 1973, uniting all Kannada-speaking areas), Orissa, Punjab (the demand for Punjabi Suba was rejected, leading to a long Sikh agitation), Rajasthan, Uttar Pradesh, and West Bengal. Bombay state was split into Gujarat and Maharashtra in 1960 (after the Samyukta Maharashtra movement and the deaths of 106 protesters in police firing in Bombay in 1956 and the Jogeshwari firing of 1958). Punjab was reorganised in 1966 (Punjabi Suba for the Sikh-majority Punjabi-speaking area, Haryana for the Hindi-speaking area, and the hilly regions merging with Himachal Pradesh). Later states were created from existing states: Nagaland (1963), Meghalaya, Manipur, Tripura (1972), Sikkim (1975, as the 22nd state, after a referendum), Mizoram, Arunachal Pradesh (1987), Goa (1987), Chhattisgarh, Uttarakhand, Jharkhand (2000), and Telangana (2014, as the 29th state, from Andhra Pradesh after a 50-year-long agitation).

The 1962 Sino-Indian War and Nehru's Decline

The Sino-Indian War of 1962 was the greatest shock of Nehru's tenure. The border dispute arose from two conflicting claims: the McMahon Line (the 1914 boundary between British India and Tibet, which China never accepted) in the eastern sector (Arunachal Pradesh), and the Aksai Chin region in the western sector (Ladakh), which China claimed as part of its Xinjiang-Uighur Autonomous Region. India followed a 'forward policy' of establishing patrol posts in the disputed areas, which China saw as provocative. In October 1962, China launched a simultaneous offensive in both sectors. The Indian Army, poorly trained for high-altitude warfare (at 14,000-17,000 feet), lacking adequate weapons (the Lee-Enfield rifles jammed in the cold) and winter clothing, suffered a humiliating defeat: the Chinese captured the key town of Bomdila in the eastern sector and came within 30 miles of the Assam plains before unilaterally declaring a ceasefire on 21 November 1962. India lost 1,383 soldiers killed (according to Indian figures), 1,696 missing, and 3,000 captured. China lost 722 killed. China kept Aksai Chin (17,000 sq miles) and returned all prisoners of war in 1963. The war destroyed Nehru's reputation (he was a broken man by the time of his death on 27 May 1964) and ended the policy of non-alignment in practice (India turned to the US and USSR for military aid, receiving US radar systems and Soviet MiG-21 fighter jets). The war also marked the permanent deterioration of Sino-Indian relations and the beginning of India's massive military modernisation.

Shastri and Indira Gandhi: The Turbulent Years (1964-1984)

Lal Bahadur Shastri (1964-1966)

Lal Bahadur Shastri, Nehru's successor, was a contrast to the aristocratic, English-educated Nehru: short (under 5 feet), humble, a product of the Hindi heartland, a man of simple habits and unwavering integrity (he resigned as Minister of Railways in 1956 after a train accident killed 144 people, accepting moral responsibility). His brief 19-month tenure was dominated by the Second Indo-Pak War of 1965. Pakistan's President Ayub Khan launched Operation Gibraltar (August 1965), infiltrating 5,000-7,000 trained fighters across the ceasefire line in Kashmir to start a rebellion. When the rebellion failed, Pakistan launched a conventional offensive. India responded by crossing the ceasefire line and attacking Lahore, capturing 450 sq miles of Pakistani territory (including the strategic Haji Pir Pass and the town of Chumb). The largest tank battles since World War II were fought at Asal Uttar (September 1965, a decisive Indian victory where 60 Pakistani Patton tanks were destroyed) and Chawinda (the biggest tank battle in history since Kursk, 700 tanks involved). The war ended with a UN-mandated ceasefire and the Tashkent Agreement (10 January 1966), mediated by Soviet Premier Alexei Kosygin, which restored the status quo ante bellum. Shastri died of a heart attack in Tashkent the night after signing the agreement (the circumstances remain controversial). He gave the enduring slogan 'Jai Jawan, Jai Kisan' (Hail the soldier, Hail the farmer) at the 1965 Lal Quila address.

Indira Gandhi: The Radical Phase (1966-1977)

Indira Gandhi (1917-1984), Nehru's only child, became Prime Minister in January 1966 at age 48. Initially seen as a 'goongi gudiya' (dumb doll) by Congress party bosses (the Syndicate), she rapidly consolidated power. Her first major political act was the nationalisation of 14 major commercial banks (19 July 1969), which she accomplished by ordinance after the Supreme Court struck down the Banking Companies Act. The bank nationalisation was immensely popular (it promised credit to agriculture and small-scale industry) and made her a mass leader. She followed this with the abolition of the privy purses of the princes (1971, through a constitutional amendment after the President's order was struck down by the Supreme Court) and the Monopolies and Restrictive Trade Practices (MRTP) Act 1969, which curbed the concentration of economic power. Indira Gandhi called a snap election in 1971 on the slogan 'Garibi Hatao' (Remove Poverty) and won a massive victory (352 of 518 seats). Her 1971 victory, achieved after she had purged the Congress Syndicate (the Congress had split in 1969 into Congress-R under Indira and Congress-O under the Syndicate), gave her unchallenged control over the party and government.

The 1971 War and the Creation of Bangladesh

The most decisive event of Indira Gandhi's tenure was the India-Pakistan War of 1971, which created Bangladesh. In the 1970 Pakistani general election, the Awami League under Sheikh Mujibur Rahman won 160 of 162 East Pakistani seats in the National Assembly, giving it a majority in Pakistan's parliament. The West Pakistani establishment (President Yahya Khan and Prime Minister Zulfikar Ali Bhutto) refused to allow Mujib to form a government. On 25 March 1971, the Pakistani army launched Operation Searchlight, a brutal crackdown on Bengali nationalists in Dhaka. The Pakistani army (including East Pakistani regiments) systematically targeted intellectuals, students, Hindus, and Awami League supporters. The death toll is disputed: Bangladesh's official figure is 3 million; the Pakistani government's figure is 26,000; most independent estimates suggest 300,000-500,000 killed. Up to 10 million refugees crossed into India (the largest refugee crisis since World War II), creating an enormous humanitarian and economic burden. Indira Gandhi signed the Indo-Soviet Treaty of Friendship and Cooperation (9 August 1971), ensuring Soviet diplomatic cover at the UN Security Council (the USSR vetoed all US-sponsored resolutions). On 3 December 1971, Pakistan launched preemptive air strikes on Indian airfields, and India formally declared war. In the east, the Indian Army, under Lieutenant General Jagjit Singh Aurora, launched the main offensive in coordination with the Mukti Bahini (Bangladeshi freedom fighters). The Pakistani army in East Pakistan, commanded by Lieutenant General A. A. K. Niazi, surrendered on 16 December 1971 at the Racecourse Ground in Dhaka. 93,000 Pakistani soldiers and personnel became prisoners of war. In the west, the Indian Army captured 5,795 sq miles of Pakistani territory (including important areas in Kashmir, Punjab, and Sindh). The war lasted 13 days. India unilaterally released all POWs in 1973. The Shimla Agreement (2 July 1972) between Indira Gandhi and Z. A. Bhutto restored the captured territories in the west (except for some strategic heights) and established the Line of Control in Kashmir, committing both countries to the bilateral resolution of disputes without third-party intervention. Pakistan formally recognised Bangladesh in February 1974 after the OIC summit in Lahore.

The Emergency (1975-1977)

The most controversial period of Indira Gandhi's rule was the Internal Emergency (25 June 1975 to 21 March 1977). The immediate trigger was the Allahabad High Court judgment (12 June 1975) by Justice Jagmohan Lal Sinha, which found Indira Gandhi guilty of electoral malpractices (using government officials to help her election campaign) in the 1971 election and disqualified her from holding office for six years. Opposition parties called for her resignation and launched mass protests under the leadership of Jayaprakash Narayan (the Gandhian veteran who had led a total revolution movement, the 'Sampoorna Kranti', in Bihar since 1974). On 25 June 1975, on the advice of Indira Gandhi, President Fakhruddin Ali Ahmed proclaimed a state of internal emergency under Article 352 of the Constitution. The Emergency saw: the suspension of fundamental rights (including the right to life under Article 21 � the right to habeas corpus was suspended); the arrest of over 100,000 political opponents (including JP Narayan, Morarji Desai, Atal Bihari Vajpayee, L. K. Advani, Chandra Shekhar, and most of the opposition leadership); the imposition of press censorship (the Press Council was abolished; foreign correspondents were expelled; the government's news agency Samachar was given a monopoly); the constitutional amendments (the 42nd Amendment of 1976, called the 'Mini-Constitution', added the words 'socialist' and 'secular' to the Preamble, stripped the Supreme Court and High Courts of much of their power of judicial review, extended the term of the Lok Sabha from 5 to 6 years, and gave Parliament unlimited power to amend the Constitution). The Emergency was enforced with particular brutality through the family planning programme: Sanjay Gandhi, Indira's younger son (who had emerged as a parallel authority to the regular government), launched a forced sterilisation campaign in the summer of 1976, setting a target of 4.3 million sterilisation operations. In some states (particularly Uttar Pradesh, Haryana, Madhya Pradesh), the police and health officials forcibly sterilised millions of men, mostly poor Muslims and Dalits, leading to hundreds of deaths from botched operations. The emergency was lifted on 21 March 1977. The 1977 elections resulted in a landslide defeat for the Congress (won only 154 of 542 seats). The Janata Party, a coalition of five disparate parties (Congress-O, Jana Sangh, Bharatiya Lok Dal, Socialists, and the Congress for Democracy of Jagjivan Ram), formed the first non-Congress government under Prime Minister Morarji Desai (1977-79). The Janata government reversed many excesses (it restored the right to habeas corpus, the independence of the judiciary, and press freedom) but collapsed in 1979 due to internal squabbling and the defection of a Congress faction under Charan Singh. Indira Gandhi returned to power in the January 1980 elections with a massive majority (353 seats).

The Second Indira Gandhi Term and Operation Blue Star (1980-1984)

Indira Gandhi's second term was dominated by a growing insurgency in Punjab. The Khalistan movement, demanding a separate Sikh state, was led by Jarnail Singh Bhindranwale, a Sikh preacher who gathered a following of armed militants in the Golden Temple complex in Amritsar. The governmental response was contradictory: Indira Gandhi allegedly supported Bhindranwale initially to weaken the opposition Akali Dal (the mainstream Sikh political party), but the strategy backfired as Bhindranwale's militancy grew out of control. By 1983-84, the militants had fortified the Akal Takht (the seat of Sikh religious authority) and the surrounding temples with machine guns, mortar bombs, and anti-aircraft guns, using pilgrims as human shields. On 3 June 1984, the Indian Army launched Operation Blue Star under General K. S. Brar to evacuate the Golden Temple and capture Bhindranwale. The operation, using 5,000 soldiers, tanks, and heavy artillery, lasted 4 days (3-6 June 1984). The army fired at the Akal Takht with recoilless rifles and tank shells, reducing it to rubble. Bhindranwale and most of his fighters were killed. Official figures: 493 military casualties and 83 militants killed; other estimates: 83 army personnel killed, 1,200 pilgrims and militants killed. The operation caused irreparable damage to Sikh sentiments. On 31 October 1984, Indira Gandhi was shot dead by her two Sikh bodyguards, Satwant Singh and Beant Singh, in the garden of her Safdarjung Road residence in New Delhi (she was shot 31 times). The assassination triggered the 1984 anti-Sikh riots in Delhi, where mobs killed approximately 3,000-4,000 Sikhs over four days (the official government figure was 2,733; independent estimates range up to 5,000-8,000). The Congress government (under Prime Minister Rajiv Gandhi, who succeeded his mother within hours) was accused of complicity in the riots.

Rajiv Gandhi and the Era of Coalitions (1984-2014)

Rajiv Gandhi (1984-1989)

Rajiv Gandhi became Prime Minister at age 40, swept to power by an unprecedented sympathy wave (the Congress won 414 of 542 seats in the December 1984 elections � the largest majority in Indian history). A former airline pilot who had entered politics reluctantly after his brother Sanjay's death in 1980, Rajiv was seen as a moderniser. His tenure saw significant achievements: the Anti-Defection Law (52nd Amendment, 1985), which prevented MPs and MLAs from changing parties under threat of losing their seats; the Punjab Accord (24 July 1985 with Sant Harchand Singh Longowal), which promised Chandigarh to Punjab, compensation for Operation Blue Star victims, and the transfer of Hindi-speaking areas to Haryana (the Accord collapsed after Longowal was assassinated by Sikh extremists on 20 August 1985); the Assam Accord (15 August 1985), which ended the six-year Assam Movement against illegal Bangladeshi immigration and fixed 25 March 1971 as the cut-off date for detection and deportation of foreigners; the New Education Policy 1986 (expanding the IITs, IIMs, and establishing the Jawahar Navodaya Vidyalayas for rural gifted students); the 73rd and 74th Constitutional Amendments (passed in 1992, after his death, making local self-government a constitutional mandate with regular elections and reservations for women and Scheduled Castes/Tribes); the Telecom Revolution (the computerisation of the Indian telephone system and the massive expansion of the Public Call Office (PCO) network); and the establishment of the Technology Development Fund and the Science Advisory Council. However, his government suffered from major controversies: the Bofors scandal (allegations that the Swedish company A. B. Bofors paid Rs 64 crore in kickbacks to Indian politicians and defence officials for a $1.3 billion howitzer contract � Rajiv was never convicted, but the scandal destroyed his image of personal integrity); the Shah Bano case (the Supreme Court in 1985 granted maintenance to a 73-year-old Muslim divorcee; Rajiv's government passed the Muslim Women (Protection of Rights on Divorce) Act, 1986, overturning the judgment � it was criticised as Muslim appeasement by Hindus and as patriarchal by feminists); and the IPKF deployment in Sri Lanka (the Indo-Sri Lanka Accord of 29 July 1987 sent the Indian Peace Keeping Force to enforce the Liberation Tigers of Tamil Eelam's disarmament; the IPKF was drawn into the civil war, suffered 1,200 casualties over three years, and was withdrawn in March 1990 as a failed mission). Rajiv Gandhi was defeated in the November 1989 elections. He was assassinated on 21 May 1991 at Sriperumbudur, Tamil Nadu, by an LTTE suicide bomber during a public election rally.

The Liberalisation Era (1991-2004)

The 1991 economic crisis was the catalyst for the most important policy change in independent India's economic history. India's foreign exchange reserves had fallen to $1.2 billion � enough for only two weeks of essential imports. The country was on the verge of defaulting on its international payments. The newly elected government of Prime Minister P. V. Narasimha Rao (June 1991) and his Finance Minister Dr. Manmohan Singh (an Oxford-educated economist and former Governor of the Reserve Bank of India) implemented the New Economic Policy (NEP) of 1991. The reforms included: a 20% devaluation of the rupee, the abolition of industrial licensing for all but 18 industries (the 'License Raj' ended), the reduction of import tariffs (peak tariffs fell from 300% to 65%), the opening of the economy to foreign investment (the Foreign Exchange Management Act replaced the draconian FERA), the disinvestment of public sector enterprises, and the abolition of the MRTP Act's restrictions on large private companies. The reforms were implemented covertly (the Budget of 1991 announced them without prior public debate) and vigorously. India's GDP growth, which had averaged 3.5% in the pre-reform decades (the 'Hindu rate of growth'), accelerated to over 6% annually for the next two decades. Millions of Indians were lifted out of poverty. The IT services industry (Infosys, TCS, Wipro, HCL) emerged as a global powerhouse. India became the world's fastest-growing major economy after China.

The Narasimha Rao government also faced the demolition of the Babri Masjid on 6 December 1992. The 16th-century mosque in Ayodhya was demolished by a mob of 150,000 kar sevaks (Hindu volunteers) who believed it was the birthplace of Lord Rama. The demolition triggered the worst communal riots since the Partition: 2,000 people died in the rioting across India, and the March 1993 Bombay serial blasts (a coordinated attack by the Mumbai underworld, including Dawood Ibrahim and the D-Company, in retaliation for the demolition) killed 257 people. The Supreme Court subsequently ordered the acquisition of the disputed land, and the Allahabad High Court's 2010 judgment divided the site three ways. The Narasimha Rao era also saw the first-ever successful launch of the Agni missile (1989), the establishment of diplomatic relations with Israel (January 1992), the look-east policy (initiating economic engagement with ASEAN countries), and the Panchayati Raj Acts (73rd and 74th Constitutional Amendments, 1992-93). The 1996-1998 period was the most unstable in Indian political history: there were three general elections (1996, 1998, 1999) and four Prime Ministers (Atal Bihari Vajpayee served twice, for 13 days in 1996 and 13 months in 1998-99; H. D. Deve Gowda of the United Front served 1996-97; I. K. Gujral 1997-98). The Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP), the Hindu nationalist party that had been a marginal force, emerged as the dominant party after the demolition of the Babri Masjid. The National Democratic Alliance (NDA) coalition under Prime Minister Atal Bihari Vajpayee (1999-2004) completed a full five-year term. Key events: the Kargil War (May-July 1999), where Pakistani soldiers and Kashmiri militants infiltrated into Indian territory in the Kargil district of Jammu and Kashmir; India repelled them in Operation Vijay, losing 527 soldiers (the war exposed India's intelligence failures and lack of adequate high-altitude military capability); the Pokhran II nuclear tests (11 and 13 May 1998 � five underground nuclear explosions at the Pokhran range in Rajasthan, code-named 'Operation Shakti'), which declared India a nuclear weapons state and led to US sanctions (later lifted); the Delhi-Lahore bus journey (February 1999, a peace initiative) and the Agra Summit with Pakistan (July 2001, which failed). The Vajpayee government also passed the Right to Information Act (2005, in the next government, but the bill was drafted during Vajpayee's term), the Prevention of Terrorism Act (POTA), and the Sarva Shiksha Abhiyan (an education-for-all programme).

The United Progressive Alliance Era (2004-2014)

The United Progressive Alliance (UPA) coalition, led by the Congress party and headed by Prime Minister Dr. Manmohan Singh (the first Sikh Prime Minister of India), won the 2004 elections (Congress 145 seats, UPA 219) and 2009 elections (Congress 206, UPA 262). The Left parties (Communist Party of India-Marxist and allies) supported the government externally with 62 seats (2004-2008). The UPA's most notable achievements were legislative and welfare-oriented. The Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Act (MGNREGA), passed in 2005, guaranteed 100 days of wage employment per year to every rural household; in 2022-23, it covered 15.5 crore households. The Right to Information Act (RTI) 2005 gave citizens the right to request information from public authorities, transforming government accountability. The Forest Rights Act 2006 recognised the rights of forest-dwelling Scheduled Tribes and traditional forest dwellers. The National Rural Health Mission (NRHM) 2005 improved healthcare in rural areas and reduced maternal and infant mortality. The Indo-US Civilian Nuclear Agreement (2005-2008) was the most significant foreign policy achievement of the UPA: the US agreed to full civilian nuclear cooperation with India (including the supply of nuclear fuel and technology) despite India not signing the Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty (NPT); India separated its civilian and military nuclear facilities and placed the civilian ones under International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) safeguards. The agreement nearly caused the fall of the government (the Left withdrew support in July 2008, but the government survived a confidence vote 275-256 after the Samajwadi Party's last-minute support). The Manmohan Singh government was also marked by serious corruption scandals: the 2G spectrum scam (2010, the Telecom Ministry under A. Raja was accused of undervaluing 2G telecom licences, causing an estimated loss of Rs 1.76 lakh crore to the exchequer � the Supreme Court cancelled 122 licenses in 2012); the Commonwealth Games scam (2010, allegations of large-scale corruption in the organisation of the Delhi Commonwealth Games under Suresh Kalmadi); and the Coalgate scam (2012, the Comptroller and Auditor General alleged that the government's allocation of coal blocks without auction caused a presumptive loss of Rs 1.86 lakh crore). The Anna Hazare-led anti-corruption movement (2011) forced the government to pass the Lokpal and Lokayuktas Act 2013. The UPA's second term was also marked by high inflation (wholesale price index averaged 8-10%), slowing growth (GDP growth fell from 8% in 2010 to 4.5% in 2013), and policy paralysis.

Contemporary India: The Modi Era (2014-Present)

The 2014-2019 Term

Narendra Modi, the son of a tea-stall owner from Vadnagar, Gujarat, and the former Chief Minister of Gujarat (2001-2014), led the BJP to a historic victory in the 2014 Lok Sabha elections (the BJP won 282 seats on its own, the first single-party majority since 1984). His election campaign was built on the promise of development ('Sabka Saath, Sabka Vikas' � Together with all, Development for all), job creation, and decisive governance. The Modi government's first major policy was the Goods and Services Tax (GST), implemented on 1 July 2017 after nearly two decades of negotiation � India's biggest tax reform, unifying 17 indirect taxes into a single nationwide tax and creating a common national market. The Demonetisation policy (8 November 2016) demonetised Rs 500 and Rs 1,000 notes, which constituted 86% of the currency in circulation. The stated goals were to eliminate black money, counterfeit currency, and terrorist financing. The policy caused massive cash shortages, long queues outside banks, 104 deaths (according to unofficial estimates, from stress-related causes), and a temporary contraction in economic activity (GDP growth fell from 8.2% in 2016 to 6.6% in 2017). The government claimed that demonetisation led to a 50% increase in income tax returns and the formalisation of the economy. Other key initiatives included: the Jan Dhan Yojana (PMJDY, a financial inclusion programme that opened 500 million bank accounts for the poor); the Swachh Bharat Abhiyan (Clean India Mission, which claimed to build 110 million toilets and end open defecation by 2019); the Ujjwala Yojana (providing free LPG connections to 100 million poor households); the Ayushman Bharat (Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana, 2018, the world's largest government-funded healthcare programme, covering 500 million beneficiaries with an annual cover of Rs 5 lakh per family); the UDAY scheme (Ujwal DISCOM Assurance Yojana, to revive state power distribution companies); the Bankruptcy Code (Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016, a comprehensive bankruptcy law to resolve corporate defaults); and the Make in India, Digital India, and Startup India initiatives to boost manufacturing and entrepreneurship. In foreign policy, Modi strengthened ties with the United States (the 2+2 ministerial dialogue), Israel (the first Indian Prime Minister to visit Israel in 2017), and the UAE and Saudi Arabia (the first Indian Prime Minister to visit in 34 years), while pursuing an aggressive policy against Pakistan (the surgical strikes of September 2016 on terrorist launch pads across the LoC, and the Balakot airstrikes of February 2019 in response to the Pulwama terrorist attack that killed 40 CRPF soldiers). The nuclear deal with the US (the 123 Agreement) was operationalised under this term.

The 2019-2024 Term

The 2019 elections gave the BJP an even larger mandate (303 seats, NDA 353), after a campaign dominated by national security (the Balakot airstrikes and the subsequent India-Pakistan air engagement on 27 February 2019 where Wing Commander Abhinandan Varthaman shot down a Pakistani F-16 before being captured and released). The second Modi term saw transformative and controversial policies. The abrogation of Article 370 (5 August 2019) revoked the special autonomous status of Jammu and Kashmir, reorganised the state into two Union Territories (Jammu and Kashmir, and Ladakh), imposed a complete communications and internet blockade for months, and arrested political leaders including former Chief Ministers Farooq Abdullah and Mehbooba Mufti. The Citizenship Amendment Act (CAA) 2019 granted fast-track Indian citizenship to non-Muslim illegal immigrants from Pakistan, Afghanistan, and Bangladesh (Hindus, Sikhs, Buddhists, Jains, Parsis, Christians) who arrived in India before 31 December 2014. The CAA triggered massive nationwide protests (the Shaheen Bagh protest in Delhi, student protests at Jamia Millia Islamia and Aligarh Muslim University, and violent clashes in Uttar Pradesh where 80 people died in December 2019). The government also announced the National Register of Citizens (NRC) process, beginning with Assam (where a 2019 NRC excluded 1.9 million people, mostly Bengali Hindus and Muslims). The Ram Temple in Ayodhya: the Supreme Court delivered its historic judgment on 9 November 2019, awarding the disputed 2.77-acre land to the Ram Janmabhoomi Trust for the construction of a Ram Temple and allocating an alternative 5-acre plot to the Muslim side for the construction of a mosque. The Bhoomi Pujan (groundbreaking ceremony) was performed by PM Modi on 5 August 2020, and the grand temple was inaugurated on 22 January 2024. The COVID-19 pandemic struck India in March 2020. India imposed the world's largest lockdown (21 March-7 June 2020, affecting 1.3 billion people, enforced with 24 hours' notice), causing an estimated 100 million people to lose their livelihoods (the Labour Ministry estimated 122 million jobs lost in April 2020 alone) and triggering a massive reverse migration of millions of migrant workers walking hundreds of kilometres to their villages. The devastating second wave (April-May 2021) overwhelmed the healthcare system: hospitals ran out of oxygen, hospital beds, and medicines; crematoriums ran out of wood; the official death toll reached 530,000 by 2024 (the WHO estimated 4.7 million excess deaths, while the Chicago University study estimated 3.8 million). The government's vaccine programme (Covaxin and Covishield) eventually vaccinated 2.2 billion doses by 2023, but the initial vaccine diplomacy (exporting vaccines) was criticised when India faced a severe domestic shortage during the second wave.

The 2024 Elections and Current Situation

The 2024 Lok Sabha elections (April-June 2024, 642 million voters in seven phases over 44 days � the largest election in human history) saw the BJP winning 240 seats (down from 303) and the NDA coalition winning 293 seats, a reduced majority. The INDIA opposition alliance (Indian National Developmental Inclusive Alliance, led by the Congress party) won 232 seats, with the Congress winning 99 seats � a dramatic recovery from its 2014 and 2019 lows of 44 and 52 seats. Narendra Modi became Prime Minister for a third term, leading a coalition government dependent on the Telugu Desam Party (16 seats) and Janata Dal-United (12 seats) for survival. India in 2025 is a nation of immense achievements and stark contradictions. It is the world's fifth-largest economy (nominal GDP $4.1 trillion, expected to reach $5 trillion by 2027-28) and the most populous country (1.44 billion, surpassing China in April 2023). Its digital economy is the world's most advanced in many respects: the Unified Payments Interface (UPI) processes over 13 billion transactions monthly (more than all global credit card transactions combined). India became the fourth country to land on the Moon (Chandrayaan-3, 23 August 2023, landing near the lunar south pole � a feat no other nation had achieved). The Aditya-L1 solar mission (launched September 2023) is India's first solar observatory. India's space programme, under the Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO), launched the Gaganyaan human spaceflight programme (manned mission planned for 2025-26) and plans to build the Bharatiya Antariksha Station (Indian Space Station) by 2035. However, India faces enormous challenges: unemployment (youth unemployment at 20-25%, according to the Centre for Monitoring Indian Economy � CMIE; the world's largest annual increase in the labour force � 15 million new entrants � but only 5-7 million new formal jobs created annually); inequality (the top 10% owns 65% of the national wealth; the top 1% owns 40% of wealth, one of the highest concentrations in the world, according to the Oxfam India report 2024); environmental degradation (Delhi's air pollution is the world's worst, with PM2.5 levels exceeding 300 micrograms per cubic metre in winter � 20 times the WHO safe limit; 1.67 million Indians died from air pollution in 2019, according to The Lancet); farmer distress (the 2020-2021 farmer protests � the largest in world history � against the three farm laws led to their repeal in November 2021 after a year-long agitation involving 300 million rural Indians); communal polarisation (the majoritarianism of the Modi era has deepened the Hindu-Muslim divide; hate speech against Muslims has increased; targeted violence against minorities is periodically reported); and democratic backsliding (international indices � the Freedom House Index, the Democracy Index by the Economist Intelligence Unit, and the V-Dem Institute � have all downgraded India's democratic status from 'flawed democracy' to 'hybrid regime' or 'electoral autocracy' during the Modi era, citing the erosion of media freedom, the independence of the judiciary, and the space for civil society).

India's Legacy and Future Trajectory

India's journey from independence to the present day is a story of extraordinary resilience and ambition. It has maintained a continuous democracy for seven decades (unlike most post-colonial states), peacefully transferred power between competitive parties at the national and state levels 20+ times, held 18 general elections with universal adult franchise, and elevated hundreds of millions out of poverty (the World Bank estimates that 400 million Indians were lifted out of extreme poverty between 2005 and 2024). India has survived four wars with Pakistan (1947, 1965, 1971, 1999), one with China (1962), the 1975-77 Emergency (the only breakdown of democracy), the Punjab insurgency, the Kashmir insurgency, the Northeast insurgencies, the Left-wing extremist (Naxalite-Maoist) insurgency in central India, and multiple catastrophic natural disasters. Its democratic institutions � the Election Commission, the Supreme Court, the free press (though under increasing pressure), and a vibrant civil society � remain robust despite significant challenges. As India approaches its centenary of independence in 2047, it aims to become a 'developed country' (Viksit Bharat) with a $30 trillion economy, a per capita income comparable to middle-income nations, and a leading role in global affairs. Whether it achieves these goals depends on solving the fundamental challenges of creating productive employment for its young population, managing its environmental and water resources, maintaining social harmony in an increasingly polarised society, and preserving its democratic institutions against the centralisation of power.
